“那你们让他们为他们的土地纳贡吗？”哈西姆急切地问。

“不。”林加德语气一正，答道；“这个，哈西姆老爷，你要明白，不是白人的习俗。我们当然能——可这不是习俗。”

“是吗？”对方带着将信将疑的笑。“他们比我们强，却要我们纳贡。有时候还真收着了——连瓦久都纳过，那里可是人人自由、人人佩克里斯短剑的。”

一时间死一般的沉寂，林加德若有所思，马来人们石头般望着虚空。

“可我们的火药都自己人打自己人烧掉了，”哈西姆温言接着说，“我们的刀剑也都彼此磕钝了。”

他叹了口气，停了停，随后换上轻松的口气，开始怂恿林加德去访问瓦久——“为了做生意，也为了看看朋友。”他一只手按在胸前说着，微微躬身。

“好。跟朋友做生意。”林加德笑着说，“这样一条船”——他挥了挥手臂——“这样一条船就像一户帘幕后面藏着许多人口的人家。它跟老婆孩子一样费钱。”

客人们起身告辞。

“你为我放了三枪，庞格里马·哈西姆。”林加德郑重地说，“我也叫人把三桶火药送上了你的船；一枪一桶。可咱们还没两清。”

===Original===

The Malay’s eyes glittered with pleasure.

“This is indeed a friend’s gift. Come to see me in my country!”

“I promise,” said Lingard, “to see you﻿—some day.”

The calm surface of the bay reflected the glorious night sky, and the brig with the prau riding astern seemed to be suspended amongst the stars in a peace that was almost unearthly in the perfection of its unstirring silence. The last handshakes were exchanged on deck, and the Malays went aboard their own craft. Next morning, when a breeze sprang up soon after sunrise, the brig and the prau left the bay together. When clear of the land Lingard made all sail and sheered alongside to say goodbye before parting company﻿—the brig, of course, sailing three feet to the prau’s one. Hassim stood on the high deck aft.

“Prosperous road,” hailed Lingard.

“Remember the promise!” shouted the other. “And come soon!” he went on, raising his voice as the brig forged past. “Come soon﻿—lest what perhaps is written should come to pass!”

The brig shot ahead.

“What?” yelled Lingard in a puzzled tone, “what’s written?”

He listened. And floating over the water came faintly the words:

“No one knows!”

===Chinese===

马来人的眼睛喜得发亮。

“这才是朋友之赠。到我的国家来看我吧！”

我答应你，”林加德说，“去看你——早晚有一天。”

海湾平静的水面映着瑰丽的夜空，双桅船拖着船尾的普拉帆船，仿佛悬在群星之间，那份安宁，在纹丝不动的寂静的完满中，几乎不属于人间。最后一次握手在甲板上交换完毕，马来人们回了自己的船。第二天早晨，日出后不久起了风，双桅船和普拉帆船一同离了海湾。一出陆地，林加德便张满帆，侧身靠拢道别，然后才分道扬镳——当然，双桅船走三尺，普拉帆船才走一尺。哈西姆站在后面高高的甲板上。

“一路順风。”林加德招呼道。

“记住你的承诺！”对方大喊。“早点来！”双桅船冲到前面时，他又提高了嗓门。“早点来——免得那也许已写就的事真应了验！”

双桅船飞驰而去。

“什么？”林加德困惑地大喊，“什么写就了？”

他側耳听。飘过水面，隐约传来那句话：

“谁也不知道！”

### III / 三
===Original===

“My word! I couldn’t help liking the chap,” would shout Lingard when telling the story; and looking around at the eyes that glittered at him through the smoke of cheroots, this Brixham trawler-boy, afterward a youth in colliers, deep-water man, gold-digger, owner and commander of “the finest brig afloat,” knew that by his listeners﻿—seamen, traders, adventurers like himself﻿—this was accepted not as the expression of a feeling, but as the highest commendation he could give his Malay friend.

“By heavens! I shall go to Wajo!” he cried, and a semicircle of heads nodded grave approbation while a slightly ironical voice said deliberately﻿—“You are a made man, Tom, if you get on the right side of that Rajah of yours.”

“Go in﻿—and look out for yourself,” cried another with a laugh.

A little professional jealousy was unavoidable, Wajo, on account of its chronic state of disturbance, being closed to the white traders; but there was no real ill-will in the banter of these men, who, rising with handshakes, dropped off one by one. Lingard went straight aboard his vessel and, till morning, walked the poop of the brig with measured steps. The riding lights of ships twinkled all round him; the lights ashore twinkled in rows, the stars twinkled above his head in a black sky; and reflected in the black water of the roadstead twinkled far below his feet. And all these innumerable and shining points were utterly lost in the immense darkness. Once he heard faintly the rumbling chain of some vessel coming to an anchor far away somewhere outside the official limits of the harbour. A stranger to the port﻿—thought Lingard﻿—one of us would have stood right in. Perhaps a ship from home? And he felt strangely touched at the thought of that ship, weary with months of wandering, and daring not to approach the place of rest. At sunrise, while the big ship from the West, her sides streaked with rust and grey with the salt of the sea, was moving slowly in to take up a berth near the shore, Lingard left the roadstead on his way to the eastward.

===Chinese===

“老天！我没法不喜欢那家伙。”林加德讲这段故事时总要这样大喊；这个布里克瑟姆拖网渔船上的船童、后来运煤船上的小伙计、远洋水手、淘金者、“海上最棒的双桅船”的船主兼船长——环顾着雪茄烟雾后面朝他闪亮的那些眼睛，知道在这些听客——跟他一样的海员、商人、冒险家——听来，这句话不是一句抒情的表白，而是他能给他的马来友人的最高褒奖。

“天哪！我一定要去瓦久！”他喊道，半圆圈里的脑袋纷纷郑重赞许地点着，同时有一个略带讥诮的声音不紧不慢地说——“汤姆，你要是讨得你那位拉者的欢心，你这辈子就成了。”

“进去吧——自己留神。”另一个笑着喊道。

一点同行的妒忌在所难免，瓦久因为长年乱糟糟，向来不对白人商人开放；但这些人打趣里并无真正的恶意，说罢纷纷起身握手，一个个散去。林加德径直回船，直到天亮，一直迈着均匀的步子在双桅船的艉楼上踱着。锚地四周船的泊灯在他身边闪烁；岸上的灯一排排地闪烁；星星在他头顶的黑色天空中闪烁；倒映在锚地黑色水面里的灯火，在他脚下深处远远地闪烁。而所有这些数不清的光点，都彻底迷失在浩大的黑暗之中。有一次，他隐约听见某条船下锚的锚链声，远远地在港界之外。港外的生船——林加德想——咱们自己人准会一直开进来。也许是从本国来的船？想到那条船，几个月漂泊而疲惫不堪，却不敢靠近这歇脚之地，他心里生出一种奇异的感动。日出时，那条从西方来的大船正缓缓开进、到岸边不远处寻泊位，船壳锈迹道道、覆着海水的盐灰；而林加德离开了锚地，转向东去的航路。

===Original===

A heavy gulf thunderstorm was raging, when after a long passage and at the end of a sultry calm day, wasted in drifting helplessly in sight of his destination, Lingard, taking advantage of fitful gusts of wind, approached the shores of Wajo. With characteristic audacity, he held on his way, closing in with a coast to which he was a stranger, and on a night that would have appalled any other man; while at every dazzling flash, Hassim’s native land seemed to leap nearer at the brig﻿—and disappear instantly as though it had crouched low for the next spring out of an impenetrable darkness. During the long day of the calm, he had obtained from the deck and from aloft, such good views of the coast, and had noted the lay of the land and the position of the dangers so carefully that, though at the precise moment when he gave the order to let go the anchor, he had been for some time able to see no further than if his head had been wrapped in a woollen blanket, yet the next flickering bluish flash showed him the brig, anchored almost exactly where he had judged her to be, off a narrow white beach near the mouth of a river.

He could see on the shore a high cluster of bamboo huts perched upon piles, a small grove of tall palms all bowed together before the blast like stalks of grass, something that might have been a palisade of pointed stakes near the water, and far off, a sombre background resembling an immense wall﻿—the forest-clad hills. Next moment, all this vanished utterly from his sight, as if annihilated and, before he had time to turn away, came back to view with a sudden crash, appearing unscathed and motionless under hooked darts of flame, like some legendary country of immortals, withstanding the wrath and fire of Heaven.

===Chinese===

一场海湾雷暴正肆虐。经过漫长的航程，又在一个闷热无风的日子里、望得见目的地却只能无助地漂流、白白耗掉一天之后，林加德趁着阵阵疾风，逼近了瓦久的海岸。他以他那招牌式的胆气继续前进，在这样骇人的夜里贴近一条他陌生的海岸——换了任何别人都会魂飞魄散；每一次炫目的电闪里，哈西姆的故国仿佛朝双桅船猛扑近一截——随即瞬间消失，仿佛正蹲低了身子，要从透不进光的黑暗里发起下一次腾跃。在无风的长昼里，他从甲板上、从桅顶上把海岸看得清清楚楚，把地势和险处的位置牢记在心；因此，尽管他下达抛锚命令的那一刻，早已什么也看不见——跟脑袋裹了毛毯里一样——但下一道蓝幽幽的闪光还是给他照出了双桅船：锚抛得几乎分毫不差，正落在他所判定的位置上，在一条河口附近白沙滩的外面。

他看得见岸上一高丛架在木桩上的竹屋，一小片高高的椰林一齐朝狂风弯着腰，像一丛草茎，水边像是有一排削尖的木栅，远处，一道阴沉的背景宛如一堵巨大的墙——覆满森林的丘陵。下一刻，这一切从他眼前彻底消失，仿佛被湮灭；他还来不及转头，它们又伴着一声炸响回到视野，在钩状的火焰飞矢之下完好无损、纹丝不动，像某个传说中长生不老的国度，承受着上天的雷霆与烈火。

===Original===

Made uneasy by the nature of his holding ground, and fearing that in one of the terrific offshore gusts the brig would start her anchor, Lingard remained on deck to watch over the safety of his vessel. With one hand upon the lead-line which would give him instant warning of the brig beginning to drag, he stood by the rail, most of the time deafened and blinded, but also fascinated, by the repeated swift visions of an unknown shore, a sight always so inspiring, as much perhaps by its vague suggestion of danger as by the hopes of success it never fails to awaken in the heart of a true adventurer. And its immutable aspect of profound and still repose, seen thus under streams of fire and in the midst of a violent uproar, made it appear inconceivably mysterious and amazing.

Between the squalls there were short moments of calm, while now and then even the thunder would cease as if to draw breath. During one of those intervals. Lingard, tired and sleepy, was beginning to doze where he stood, when suddenly it occurred to him that, somewhere below, the sea had spoken in a human voice. It had said, “Praise be to God﻿—” and the voice sounded small, clear, and confident, like the voice of a child speaking in a cathedral. Lingard gave a start and thought﻿—I’ve dreamed this﻿—and directly the sea said very close to him, “Give a rope.”

The thunder growled wickedly, and Lingard, after shouting to the men on deck, peered down at the water, until at last he made out floating close alongside the upturned face of a man with staring eyes that gleamed at him and then blinked quickly to a flash of lightning. By that time all hands in the brig were wildly active and many ropes-ends had been thrown over. Then together with a gust of wind, and, as if blown on board, a man tumbled over the rail and fell all in a heap upon the deck. Before anyone had the time to pick him up, he leaped to his feet, causing the people around him to step back hurriedly. A sinister blue glare showed the bewildered faces and the petrified attitudes of men completely deafened by the accompanying peal of thunder. After a time, as if to beings plunged in the abyss of eternal silence, there came to their ears an unfamiliar thin, faraway voice saying:

“I seek the white man.”

===Chinese===

林加德对锚地的底质不放心，生怕双桅船在某一阵可怕的岸外狂风里走锚，便留在甲板上照看他的船。他一手搭在测深绳上——绳上一有动静，就能立刻警告他船开始拖锚——就那样立在栏杆边，大部分时间被震得又聋又瞎，却又着了迷似的，一遍遍看那陌生海岸的疾闪掠影；这样的景象总是令人振奋，对一个真正的冒险家，一半是因为它对危险的朦胧暗示，一半是因为它总能唤起的成功的希望。而这海岸在火流的映照下、在震耳欲聋的喧嚣中，依然是一副深沉静穆、亘古不移的面貌，显得神秘得不可思议，令人惊叹。

飑与飑之间有短短的平静时刻，雷声有时也会歇下来，仿佛换气。在一次这样的间歇里，疲惫困倦的林加德正站着打起盹来，忽然发觉，下面的什么地方，海用人的嗓音说了话。那嗓音说：“赞美真主——”声音细小、清亮而笃定，像大教堂里一个孩子说话。林加德一惊，心想——我这是在做梦——紧接着海就在离他很近的地方说：“给根缆。”

雷声恶狠狠地咆哮着，林加德朝甲板上的人喊了一声，俯身往水面张望，终于看清紧挨着船边浮着一张仰起的脸，一双瞪着的眼睛朝他闪光，又在一次闪电里飞快地眨了一下。这时双桅船上所有的人都狂热地行动起来，许多绳头抛了下去。随后，随着一阵风，仿佛是被吹上船来似的，一个人翻过栏杆，一堆似的瘫在甲板上。还没等谁来得及扶他，他已一跃而起，吓得周围的人慌忙后退。一道阴森的蓝光照出一张张惊愕的脸和一个个呆若木鸡的姿势——那一声随行的炸雷把所有人彻底震聋了。过了一会儿，仿佛是对沉在永恒寂静深渊里的生灵，他们的耳朵里传来一个陌生的、细弱而遥远的声音：

“我找白人。”

===Original===

“Here,” cried Lingard. Then, when he had the stranger, dripping and naked but for a soaked waistcloth, under the lamp of the cabin, he said, “I don’t know you.”

“My name is Jaffir, and I come from Pata Hassim, who is my chief and your friend. Do you know this?”

He held up a thick gold ring, set with a fairly good emerald.

“I have seen it before on the Rajah’s finger,” said Lingard, looking very grave.

“It is the witness of the truth I speak﻿—the message from Hassim is﻿—‘Depart and forget!’ ”

“I don’t forget,” said Lingard, slowly. “I am not that kind of man. What folly is this?”

It is unnecessary to give at full length the story told by Jaffir. It appears that on his return home, after the meeting with Lingard, Hassim found his relative dying and a strong party formed to oppose his rightful successor. The old Rajah Tulla died late at night and﻿—as Jaffir put it﻿—before the sun rose there were already blows exchanged in the courtyard of the ruler’s *dalam*. This was the preliminary fight of a civil war, fostered by foreign intrigues; a war of jungle and river, of assaulted stockades and forest ambushes. In this contest, both parties﻿—according to Jaffir﻿—displayed great courage, and one of them an unswerving devotion to what, almost from the first, was a lost cause. Before a month elapsed Hassim, though still chief of an armed band, was already a fugitive. He kept up the struggle, however, with some vague notion that Lingard’s arrival would turn the tide.

“For weeks we lived on wild rice; for days we fought with nothing but water in our bellies,” declaimed Jaffir in the tone of a true fire-eater.

And then he went on to relate, how, driven steadily down to the sea, Hassim, with a small band of followers, had been for days holding the stockade by the waterside.

===Chinese===

“在这儿。”林加德喊。等到把这个浑身滴水、只围一条湿腰布的生客带到舱灯底下，他说：“我不认得你。”

“我叫贾菲尔，从帕塔·哈西姆那里来，他是我的首领，也是你的朋友。你认得这个吗？”

他举起一枚厚实的金戒指，镶着一颗相当不错的祖母绿。

“我见过它，在拉者的手指上。”林加德说，神色非常凝重。

“它是我说的话为真的见证——哈西姆的信是——‘走吧，忘了吧！’”

“我不忘。”林加德缓缓地说。“我不是那种人。这是唱的哪一出？”

贾菲尔讲的经过不必在此全录。原来哈西姆与林加德相遇后回到家中，发现他的亲戚奄奄一息，一伙强势的人已经结成，要阻挠他的合法继承。老拉者图拉深夜死了——照贾菲尔的说法——太阳还没升起，统治者的宅院内就已经交上了手。这是一场由外来阴谋煽起的内战的序战；一场丛林与河流的战争，攻栅与林间伏击的战争。照贾菲尔说，在这场争斗中，双方都表现了极大的勇气，其中一方更是对一件几乎从一开始就已无望的事业矢志不渝。不出一个月，哈西姆虽然还是一支武装的首领，却已经是个逃亡者了。但他仍撑着不肯罢休，怀着某种模糊的指望：林加德的到来会扭转大局。

“好几个星期我们靠野米活命；好几天我们肚子里只有水，照旧打仗。”贾菲尔用真正好汉的腔调朗声道。

接着他讲述他们如何被一步步逼到海边，哈西姆带着一小队追随者，据守着水边的木栅，已经守了好几天。

===Original===

“But every night some men disappeared,” confessed Jaffir. “They were weary and hungry and they went to eat with their enemies. We are only ten now﻿—ten men and a woman with the heart of a man, who are tonight starving, and tomorrow shall die swiftly. We saw your ship afar all day; but you have come too late. And for fear of treachery and lest harm should befall you﻿—his friend﻿—the Rajah gave me the ring and I crept on my stomach over the sand, and I swam in the night﻿—and I, Jaffir, the best swimmer in Wajo, and the slave of Hassim, tell you﻿—his message to you is ‘Depart and forget’﻿—and this is his gift﻿—take!”

He caught hold suddenly of Lingard’s hand, thrust roughly into it the ring, and then for the first time looked round the cabin with wondering but fearless eyes. They lingered over the semicircle of bayonets and rested fondly on musket-racks. He grunted in admiration.

“*Ya-wa*, this is strength!” he murmured as if to himself. “But it has come too late.”

“Perhaps not,” cried Lingard.

“Too late,” said Jaffir, “we are ten only, and at sunrise we go out to die.” He went to the cabin door and hesitated there with a puzzled air, being unused to locks and door handles.

“What are you going to do?” asked Lingard.

“I shall swim back,” replied Jaffir. “The message is spoken and the night can not last forever.”

“You can stop with me,” said Lingard, looking at the man searchingly.

“Hassim waits,” was the curt answer.

“Did he tell you to return?” asked Lingard.

“No! What need?” said the other in a surprised tone.

Lingard seized his hand impulsively.

“If I had ten men like you!” he cried.

“We are ten, but they are twenty to one,” said Jaffir, simply.

Lingard opened the door.

“Do you want anything that a man can give?” he asked.

The Malay had a moment of hesitation, and Lingard noticed the sunken eyes, the prominent ribs, and the worn-out look of the man.

“Speak out,” he urged with a smile; “the bearer of a gift must have a reward.”

“A drink of water and a handful of rice for strength to reach the shore,” said Jaffir sturdily. “For over there”﻿—he tossed his head﻿—“we had nothing to eat today.”

“You shall have it﻿—give it to you with my own hands,” muttered Lingard.

===Chinese===

“可每天夜里都有人消失。”贾菲尔坦言。“他们又累又饿，投到敌人那边吃饭去了。如今我们只剩十个——十个男人，和一个有男人之心的女人，今晚挨餓，明天就得快快赴死。我们一整天都远远望见你们的船；可你们来得太迟了。拉者怕有奸诈，也怕你——他的朋友——遭毒手，就把戒指交给我，我贴着沙地匍匐爬行，又在黑夜里游了过来——我，贾菲尔，瓦久最好的泳者，哈西姆的奴仆，告诉你——他给你的信是‘走吧，忘了吧’——这是他的赠礼——拿去！”

他忽然抓住林加德的手，把戒指硬塞进去，这才头一回用好奇而无畏的目光环视这间餐舱。目光在半环形的刺刀上流连，又爱惜地停在火枪架上。他赞赏地哼了一声。

“真的，这就是力量！”他喃喃着，像自言自语。“可惜来得太迟了。”

“也许不迟。”林加德大声道。

“太迟了。”贾菲尔说，“我们只剩十个，明天日出我们就出去赴死。”他走到舱门口，在那里迟疑，一脸困惑，因为他不惯用门锁和门把手。

“你要做什么？”林加德问。

“我游回去。”贾菲尔回答。“信带到了，夜却不会永远不完。”

“你可以留在我这儿。”林加德说，仔细地打量这个人。

“哈西姆在等。”回答很干脆。

“他叫你回去了？”林加德问。

“没有！何必？”对方一副诧异的口气。

林加德冲动地抓住他的手。

“我要有十个像你这样的人就好了！”他喊道。

“我们倒是十个，可他们一人抵我们二十个。”贾菲尔简单地说。

林加德开了门。

“你想要什么人给得起的东西吗？”他问。

马来人迟疑了片刻，林加德注意到他深陷的眼睛、突出的肋骨和那一脸熬干了的神色。

“直说，”他笑着催道，“送礼的信使总该有赏。”

“一口水、一把米，好有力气游到岸上。”贾菲尔硬朗地说。“因为那边”——他把头一甩——“我们今天断了粮。”

“你会得到的——我亲手拿给你。”林加德咕哝道。

===Original===

He did so, and thus lowered himself in Jaffir’s estimation for a time. While the messenger, squatting on the floor, ate without haste but with considerable earnestness, Lingard thought out a plan of action. In his ignorance as to the true state of affairs in the country, to save Hassim from the immediate danger of his position was all that he could reasonably attempt. To that end Lingard proposed to swing out his longboat and send her close inshore to take off Hassim and his men. He knew enough of Malays to feel sure that on such a night the besiegers, now certain of success, and being, Jaffir said, in possession of everything that could float, would not be very vigilant, especially on the sea front of the stockade. The very fact of Jaffir having managed to swim off undetected proved that much. The brig’s boat could﻿—when the frequency of lightning abated﻿—approach unseen close to the beach, and the defeated party, either stealing out one by one or making a rush in a body, would embark and be received in the brig.

This plan was explained to Jaffir, who heard it without the slightest mark of interest, being apparently too busy eating. When the last grain of rice was gone, he stood up, took a long pull at the water bottle, muttered: “I hear. Good. I will tell Hassim,” and tightening the rag round his loins, prepared to go. “Give me time to swim ashore,” he said, “and when the boat starts, put another light beside the one that burns now like a star above your vessel. We shall see and understand. And don’t send the boat till there is less lightning: a boat is bigger than a man in the water. Tell the rowers to pull for the palm-grove and cease when an oar, thrust down with a strong arm, touches the bottom. Very soon they will hear our hail; but if no one comes they must go away before daylight. A chief may prefer death to life, and we who are left are all of true heart. Do you understand, O big man?”

“The chap has plenty of sense,” muttered Lingard to himself, and when they stood side by side on the deck, he said: “But there may be enemies on the beach, O Jaffir, and they also may shout to deceive my men. So let your hail be *Lightning!* Will you remember?”

For a time Jaffir seemed to be choking.

“Lit-ing! Is that right? I say﻿—is that right, O strong man?” Next moment he appeared upright and shadowy on the rail.

===Chinese===

他果真亲手递了，为此在贾菲尔心目里一时掉了价。信使蹲在地板上，不慌不忙、却相当认真地吃着，林加德趁这工夫想好了行动计划。他对国内局势的实情一无所知，凭他所能，合理的打算只有一个：把哈西姆从眼前的险境里救出来。为此，林加德打算甩出他的长大艇，派它贴着岸边开进去，接出哈西姆和他的人。他对马来人了解得够多，确信在这样一个夜晚，围攻者自以为胜券在握——照贾菲尔说，能浮的东西都到了他们手里——不会太警觉，尤其在木栅临海的一面。贾菲尔能神不知鬼不觉地游出来，这件事本身就证明了这一点。等闪电稀疏下来，双桅船的小艇可以不被察觉地贴近海滩，败方的人或一个个溜出来，或一齐猛冲上艇，被接回双桅船。

这个计划讲给贾菲尔听时，他听着，没有丝毫感兴趣的样子，显然只顾着吃。最后一粒米下了肚，他站起来，对着水瓶长长灌了一口，咕哝道：“我听明白了。好。我去告诉哈西姆。”又紧了紧腰间的破布，准备动身。“给我游回岸的时间，”他说，“等小艇出发时，在你们船上那盏星一样亮着的灯旁边，再点上一盏。我们望见就懂了。闪电没稀下来之前别派艇：一条艇比水里一个人显眼。告诉桨手们朝椰林划，等一条用猛劲插下去的桨碰到底就停桨。很快他们会听见我们的呼号；可要是没人来，他们必须在天亮前离开。头人或许宁死不生，而我们这些活下来的，个个都是真心。你明白吗，大个子？”

“这家伙脑子够用。”林加德暗自咕哝。等两人并肩站在甲板上时，他说：“可海滩上也许有敌人，贾菲尔啊，他们也会呼喊来骗我的人。所以你们的呼号就用‘闪电’！记住了？”

贾菲尔像是噎了一会儿。

“利——廷！对不对？我说——对不对，大力士？”下一刻，他已经笔直地、影子似的立在了栏杆上。

===Original===

“Yes. That’s right. Go now,” said Lingard, and Jaffir leaped off, becoming invisible long before he struck the water. Then there was a splash; after a while a spluttering voice cried faintly, “Lit-ing! Ah, ha!” and suddenly the next thunder-squall burst upon the coast. In the crashing flares of light Lingard had again and again the quick vision of a white beach, the inclined palm-trees of the grove, the stockade by the sea, the forest far away: a vast landscape mysterious and still﻿—Hassim’s native country sleeping unmoved under the wrath and fire of Heaven.

===Chinese===

“对。就是这样。去吧。”林加德说。贾菲尔纵身一跃，早在落水之前就没了影。接着一声水响；过了一会儿，一个咕噜着水的嗓音微弱地喊：“利——廷！啊，哈！”随即下一场雷暴骤然扑上海岸。在一次次炸裂的电光里，林加德一遍又一遍疾闪般望见那片白色沙滩、椰林倾斜的身影、海边的木栅、远处的森林：一片浩瀚而神秘静穆的风景——哈西姆的故国，在上天的雷霆与烈火之下沉睡未醒。

### IV / 四
===Original===

A Traveller visiting Wajo today may, if he deserves the confidence of the common people, hear the traditional account of the last civil war, together with the legend of a chief and his sister, whose mother had been a great princess suspected of sorcery and on her deathbed had communicated to these two the secrets of the art of magic. The chief’s sister especially, “with the aspect of a child and the fearlessness of a great fighter,” became skilled in casting spells. They were defeated by the son of their uncle, because﻿—will explain the narrator simply﻿—“The courage of us Wajo people is so great that magic can do nothing against it. I fought in that war. We had them with their backs to the sea.” And then he will go on to relate in an awed tone how on a certain night “when there was such a thunderstorm as has been never heard of before or since” a ship, resembling the ships of white men, appeared off the coast, “as though she had sailed down from the clouds. She moved,” he will affirm, “with her sails bellying against the wind; in size she was like an island; the lightning played between her masts which were as high as the summits of mountains; a star burned low through the clouds above her. We knew it for a star at once because no flame of man’s kindling could have endured the wind and rain of that night. It was such a night that we on the watch hardly dared look upon the sea. The heavy rain was beating down our eyelids. And when day came, the ship was nowhere to be seen, and in the stockade where the day before there were a hundred or more at our mercy, there was no one. The chief, Hassim, was gone, and the lady who was a princess in the country﻿—and nobody knows what became of them from that day to this. Sometimes traders from our parts talk of having heard of them here, and heard of them there, but these are the lies of men who go afar for gain. We who live in the country believe that the ship sailed back into the clouds whence the Lady’s magic made her come. Did we not see the ship with our own eyes? And as to Rajah Hassim and his sister, Mas Immada, some men say one thing and some another, but God alone knows the truth.”

===Chinese===

今天到瓦焦去的旅客，若是当得起百姓的信任，还可以听到关于上一场内战的传统说法，外加一位头人和他妹妹的传说；他们的母亲是一位被疑会巫术的贵公主，临终时把法术的奥秘传给了这兄妹二人。尤其是那位头人的妹妹，「有着孩童的容貌和大勇士的无畏」，精于施咒。他们被叔父的儿子打败了——讲述人会简单地解释——「咱们瓦焦人的勇武太大了，法术对付不了它。那场仗我打过。我们把她们逼得背靠着大海。」接着他会带着敬畏的口气往下讲：某一夜，「起了前所未闻、后也未见过的雷暴雨」，一条像白人船只的船出现在海岸外，「仿佛是从云端里驶下来的。它走着，」他会一口咬定，「帆顶着风鼓得满满的；个头像一座岛；闪电在它的桅杆之间蹿来蹿去，那桅杆高得同山顶一样；一团星火在它上方的云里低低地烧。我们一望便知那是星火，因为凡人点的火，经不得那一夜的风雨。那样的夜，我们守夜的人几乎不敢朝海上看。大雨直打得我们睁不开眼皮。天亮以后，那条船不见了；前一天围寨里还困着百十来个任凭我们发落的仇人，也一个不剩了。头人哈西姆没了，那位在国内是公主的女眷也没了——从那天起到今天，谁也不知道他们的下落。有时我们那一带的行商说在这儿听见过他们的消息，又在那儿听见过，可那都是出远门逐利之人的谎话。我们住在国里的人相信，那条船又驶回云端里去了，是女眷的法术把它召来的。那条船我们不是亲眼见过的么？至于拉者·哈西姆和他妹妹伊玛达小姐，这个人这么说，那个人那么说，唯有真主知道真相。」

===Original===

Such is the traditional account of Lingard’s visit to the shores of Boni. And the truth is he came and went the same night; for, when the dawn broke on a cloudy sky the brig, under reefed canvas and smothered in sprays, was storming along to the southward on her way out of the Gulf. Lingard, watching over the rapid course of his vessel, looked ahead with anxious eyes and more than once asked himself with wonder, why, after all, was he thus pressing her under all the sail she could carry. His hair was blown about by the wind, his mind was full of care and the indistinct shapes of many new thoughts, and under his feet, the obedient brig dashed headlong from wave to wave.

Her owner and commander did not know where he was going. That adventurer had only a confused notion of being on the threshold of a big adventure. There was something to be done, and he felt he would have to do it. It was expected of him. The seas expected it; the land expected it. Men also. The story of war and of suffering; Jaffir’s display of fidelity, the sight of Hassim and his sister, the night, the tempest, the coast under streams of fire﻿—all this made one inspiring manifestation of a life calling to him distinctly for interference. But what appealed to him most was the silent, the complete, unquestioning, and apparently uncurious, trust of these people. They came away from death straight into his arms as it were, and remained in them passive as though there had been no such thing as doubt or hope or desire. This amazing unconcern seemed to put him under a heavy load of obligation.

He argued to himself that had not these defeated men expected everything from him they could not have been so indifferent to his action. Their dumb quietude stirred him more than the most ardent pleading. Not a word, not a whisper, not a questioning look even! They did not ask! It flattered him. He was also rather glad of it, because if the unconscious part of him was perfectly certain of its action, he, himself, did not know what to do with those bruised and battered beings a playful fate had delivered suddenly into his hands.

===Chinese===

这便是关于林加德到访波尼海岸的传统说法。实情是，他当夜来，当夜就走；因为阴云密布的天空破晓时，双桅船正收着缩帆、裹在飞沫里，一路向南疾驰，奔出海湾。林加德照管着自己船只的疾驰，焦灼地望着前方，不止一次诧异地自问：说到底，他为什么这样逼着她满帆疾赶。他的头发被风吹得乱飞，心里满是牵挂，满是许多新念头影影绰绰的形状；在他脚下，听话的双桅船从一个浪头扑向另一个浪头。

她的船主兼船长并不知道自己在往哪儿去。这位冒险家只有一个模糊的感觉：自己正站在一桩大冒险的门坎上。有件事要办，而他觉得非他来办不可。人们在指望他。海在指望，陆地在指望，人也在指望。战争与苦难的故事；贾菲尔展示的忠义，哈西姆兄妹的身影，那一夜，那场暴风雨，火流下面的海岸——这一切汇成一回事，堂堂地显现在他面前，分明是在召唤他出手。可最打动他的，是这些人那无言的、彻底的、不问所以、仿佛连好奇心都没有的信任。他们才脱死境，就仿佛径直投进了他的怀抱，此后便安安静静待在那里，仿佛世上本不存在怀疑、希望或欲求这类东西。这种惊人的淡然，似乎给他压上了一副沉重的义务。

他心里辩道：倘若这些败亡的人不是把一切都指望在他身上，便不会对他的举动这般漠然。他们那哑默的安静，比最恳切的哀求更打动他。没有一句话，没有一声低语，连探问的一瞥都没有！他们不问！这叫他得意。他也颇为此庆幸，因为他虽然浑身本能地认定了自己的行动，他自己却实在不知道，该拿这些被顽皮的命运突然交到他手里的、遍体鳞伤的人们怎么办。

===Original===

He had received the fugitives personally, had helped some over the rail; in the darkness, slashed about by lightning, he had guessed that not one of them was unwounded, and in the midst of tottering shapes he wondered how on earth they had managed to reach the longboat that had brought them off. He caught unceremoniously in his arms the smallest of these shapes and carried it into the cabin, then without looking at his light burden ran up again on deck to get the brig under way. While shouting out orders he was dimly aware of someone hovering near his elbow. It was Hassim.

“I am not ready for war,” he explained, rapidly, over his shoulder, “and tomorrow there may be no wind.” Afterward for a time he forgot everybody and everything while he conned the brig through the few outlying dangers. But in half an hour, and running off with the wind on the quarter, he was quite clear of the coast and breathed freely. It was only then that he approached two others on that poop where he was accustomed in moments of difficulty to commune alone with his craft. Hassim had called his sister out of the cabin; now and then Lingard could see them with fierce distinctness, side by side, and with twined arms, looking toward the mysterious country that seemed at every flash to leap away farther from the brig﻿—unscathed and fading.

The thought uppermost in Lingard’s mind was: “What on earth am I going to do with them?” And no one seemed to care what he would do. Jaffir with eight others quartered on the main hatch, looked to each other’s wounds and conversed interminably in low tones, cheerful and quiet, like well-behaved children. Each of them had saved his kris, but Lingard had to make a distribution of cotton cloth out of his trade-goods. Whenever he passed by them, they all looked after him gravely. Hassim and Immada lived in the cuddy. The chief’s sister took the air only in the evening and those two could be heard every night, invisible and murmuring in the shadows of the quarterdeck. Every Malay on board kept respectfully away from them.

===Chinese===

他亲自收留了这些逃亡者，搀着几个翻过栏杆；在电光乱劈的黑暗里，他估摸出这些人没有一个是没带伤的，而在这群摇摇晃晃的身影中间，他纳闷他们究竟是怎么设法够到把他们接下海的那条长大艇的。他把其中最小的一个身影不容分说地抱进怀里，送进舱里，看也不看那轻轻的负担，转身又跑上甲板去开船。他大声发号施令的当口，隐约觉得有个人贴近他臂肘旁打转。那是哈西姆。

「我还没准备好打仗，」他扭头急急解释了一句，「明天兴许就没风了。」此后有一阵子，他领着双桅船避开外围那几处险地，把所有的人、所有的事都忘了。可不过半个钟头，船已兜着尾风驶离，把海岸远远抛清，他才畅快地喘了口气。直到这时他才走近艉楼上那两个人——每逢难处，他惯于在艉楼上单独同自己的船只神交。哈西姆已把妹妹叫出了舱房；林加德不时借着电光把他们看得清清楚楚：并肩而立，臂挽着臂，朝那片神秘的国土望去——每闪一下电，那国土仿佛就朝远处跃退一步——完好无损，渐渐隐没。

林加德心里最要紧的念头是：「我到底拿他们怎么办？」可似乎没有一个人在乎他怎么办。贾菲尔带着八个人安置在主舱口上，彼此照料着伤口，低声没完没了地交谈，安静而快活，像听话的孩子。他们人人保住了自己的克力士短剑，可棉布还得林加德从自己的货物里分发。他从他们跟前经过时，他们都庄重地目送他。哈西姆和伊玛达住在艉舱里。头人的妹妹只在傍晚出来透气，每天夜里都听得见那兄妹俩，人影不见，在艉楼的暗影里低低絮语。船上的马来人个个敬而远之。

===Original===

Lingard, on the poop, listened to the soft voices, rising and falling, in a melancholy cadence; sometimes the woman cried out as if in anger or in pain. He would stop short. The sound of a deep sigh would float up to him on the stillness of the night. Attentive stars surrounded the wandering brig and on all sides their light fell through a vast silence upon a noiseless sea. Lingard would begin again to pace the deck, muttering to himself.

“Belarab’s the man for this job. His is the only place where I can look for help, but I don’t think I know enough to find it. I wish I had old Jörgenson here﻿—just for ten minutes.”

This Jörgenson knew things that had happened a long time ago, and lived amongst men efficient in meeting the accidents of the day, but who did not care what would happen tomorrow and who had no time to remember yesterday. Strictly speaking, he did not live amongst them. He only appeared there from time to time. He lived in the native quarter, with a native woman, in a native house standing in the middle of a plot of fenced ground where grew plantains, and furnished only with mats, cooking pots, a queer fishing net on two sticks, and a small mahogany case with a lock and a silver plate engraved with the words “Captain H. C. Jörgenson. Barque *Wild Rose*.”

It was like an inscription on a tomb. The *Wild Rose* was dead, and so was Captain H. C. Jörgenson, and the sextant case was all that was left of them. Old Jörgenson, gaunt and mute, would turn up at meal times on board any trading vessel in the Roads, and the stewards﻿—Chinamen or mulattos﻿—would sulkily put on an extra plate without waiting for orders. When the seamen traders foregathered noisily round a glittering cluster of bottles and glasses on a lighted verandah, old Jörgenson would emerge up the stairs as if from a dark sea, and, stepping up with a kind of tottering jauntiness, would help himself in the first tumbler to hand.

“I drink to you all. No﻿—no chair.”

===Chinese===

林加德在艉楼上听着那轻柔的话音起起落落，带着忧伤的调子；有时那女子像恼怒、又像疼痛似的喊一声。他会猛地站住。一声深长的叹息会从夜的静定里飘到他身边。专注的繁星环绕着这条漂泊的双桅船，四面八方，星光穿过浩大的寂静，落在无声的海上。林加德便又踱起甲板来，独自咕哝。

「这档子事得找贝拉拉布。要寻帮手，只有他那个地方；可我怕自己知道得不够，寻不着门路。真希望老约根森在这儿——哪怕十分钟也好。」

这位约根森，知道许多陈年旧事，住在一些男人中间——那些人应付眼下的意外个个在行，却不管明天会怎样，也没工夫记得昨天。严格说来，他并不算住在他们中间。他只是不时在那儿露个面。他住在土著区，同一个土著女人一起，住在一栋土著房子里；房子立在一圈围起来的地当中，地里种着芭蕉，屋里陈设只有席子、炊锅、一副架在两根棍上的怪渔网，还有一只小小的红木匣子，带锁，镶着一块银牌，刻着「H·C·约根森船长，『野玫瑰号』三桅帆船」的字样。

那活像一块墓志铭。「野玫瑰号」死了，H·C·约根森船长也死了，这只六分仪匣子便是他们留下的一切。老约根森，瘦长而寡言，到饭点会在锚地里任何一条商船上现身，侍者——不论是中国人还是混血儿——会阴着脸不等吩咐就添上一副盘碟。当行商水手们在灯火通明的游廊上，围着亮闪闪的一簇酒瓶酒杯喧闹聚谈时，老约根森会从楼梯口冒上来，仿佛从一片黑海里浮出，迈着一种摇晃的洒脱走上前，伸手就够着哪只酒杯便给自己斟上一杯。

「敬诸位。不——不必让座。」

===Original===

He would stand silent over the talking group. His taciturnity was as eloquent as the repeated warning of the slave of the feast. His flesh had gone the way of all flesh, his spirit had sunk in the turmoil of his past, but his immense and bony frame survived as if made of iron. His hands trembled but his eyes were steady. He was supposed to know details about the end of mysterious men and of mysterious enterprises. He was an evident failure himself, but he was believed to know secrets that would make the fortune of any man; yet there was also a general impression that his knowledge was not of that nature which would make it profitable for a moderately prudent person.

This powerful skeleton, dressed in faded blue serge and without any kind of linen, existed anyhow. Sometimes, if offered the job, he piloted a home ship through the Straits of Rhio, after, however, assuring the captain:

“You don’t want a pilot; a man could go through with his eyes shut. But if you want me, I’ll come. Ten dollars.”

Then, after seeing his charge clear of the last island of the group he would go back thirty miles in a canoe, with two old Malays who seemed to be in some way his followers. To travel thirty miles at sea under the equatorial sun and in a cranky dugout where once down you must not move, is an achievement that requires the endurance of a fakir and the virtue of a salamander. Ten dollars was cheap and generally he was in demand. When times were hard he would borrow five dollars from any of the adventurers with the remark:

“I can’t pay you back, very soon, but the girl must eat, and if you want to know anything, I can tell you.”

It was remarkable that nobody ever smiled at that “anything.” The usual thing was to say:

“Thank you, old man; when I am pushed for a bit of information I’ll come to you.”

Jörgenson nodded then and would say: “Remember that unless you young chaps are like we men who ranged about here years ago, what I could tell you would be worse than poison.”

===Chinese===

他会立在谈笑的人群上首，一言不发。他的缄默，如同宴席上那位反复示警的侍奴一般雄辩。他的血肉早已随众生的血肉去了，他的精神也沉沦在他往日的动荡里；可他那副嶙峋的巨大骨架还在，仿佛是铁打的。他的手发颤，眼睛却稳。人们认定他知道那些神秘人物与神秘勾当的下场的细节。他本人是个明摆着的失败者，可人们又相信他知道着足以让任何人发财的秘密；不过还有一种普遍的印象：他那套知识，对于稍微谨慎一点的人来说，可不是有利可图的哪一种。

这副强有力的骷髅，穿着褪色的蓝哔叽，贴身的布件一概没有，就这么凑合活着。有时，若有人雇他，他便领一条回航船通过里奥海峡——不过总先向船长声明：

「您用不着引水员；闭着眼睛也过得去。可您要我，我就来。十块钱。」

送完船、过了群岛最后一座岛之后，他会坐着独木舟回程三十英里，同船的是两个老马来人，仿佛是他的什么追随者。在赤道的烈日下、在一条翻掉之后连动也不敢动的窄长独木舟里走三十英里海路，这份本事要有苦行僧的忍耐和火蜥蜴的德行。十块钱实在便宜，所以他向来不愁没人雇。手头拮据时，他会向随便哪位冒险家借五块钱，说一句：

「一时半会儿还不上；可那丫头总得吃饭。您要是想知道什么，我可以告诉您。」

奇怪的是，从没有人对他那句「什么」笑过。大家照例只说：

「多谢，老哥；哪天我缺个信儿，就去找您。」

约根森便点点头，说：「记住喽——除非你们这帮小子能像我们当年在这一带闯荡的人，我要告诉你们的东西，比毒药还毒。」

===Original===

It was from Jörgenson, who had his favourites with whom he was less silent, that Lingard had heard of Darat-es-Salam, the “Shore of Refuge.” Jörgenson had, as he expressed it, “known the inside of that country just after the high old times when the white-clad Padris preached and fought all over Sumatra till the Dutch shook in their shoes.” Only he did not say “shook” and “shoes” but the above paraphrase conveys well enough his contemptuous meaning. Lingard tried now to remember and piece together the practical bits of old Jörgenson’s amazing tales; but all that had remained with him was an approximate idea of the locality and a very strong but confused notion of the dangerous nature of its approaches. He hesitated, and the brig, answering in her movements to the state of the man’s mind, lingered on the road, seemed to hesitate also, swinging this way and that on the days of calm.

It was just because of that hesitation that a big New York ship, loaded with oil in cases for Japan, and passing through the Billiton passage, sighted one morning a very smart brig being hove-to right in the fairway and a little to the east of Carimata. The lank skipper, in a frock-coat, and the big mate with heavy moustaches, judged her almost too pretty for a Britisher, and wondered at the man on board laying his topsail to the mast for no reason that they could see. The big ship’s sails fanned her along, flapping in the light air, and when the brig was last seen far astern she had still her mainyard aback as if waiting for someone. But when, next day, a London tea-clipper passed on the same track, she saw no pretty brig hesitating, all white and still at the parting of the ways. All that night Lingard had talked with Hassim while the stars streamed from east to west like an immense river of sparks above their heads. Immada listened, sometimes exclaiming low, sometimes holding her breath. She clapped her hands once. A faint dawn appeared.

“You shall be treated like my father in the country,” Hassim was saying. A heavy dew dripped off the rigging and the darkened sails were black on the pale azure of the sky. “You shall be the father who advises for good﻿—”

“I shall be a steady friend, and as a friend I want to be treated﻿—no more,” said Lingard. “Take back your ring.”

“Why do you scorn my gift?” asked Hassim, with a sad and ironic smile.

===Chinese===

林加德正是从约根森嘴里——约根森也有他看得上的人，对他们话多些——听说了达拉特-埃斯-萨拉姆，「避难之岸」。照约根森自己的说法，他「在那轰轰烈烈的老时光刚过去时，就熟悉那片地方的内情；那时白衣帕德里们满苏门答腊传教又打仗，打得荷兰人两腿发抖」。他原话用的自然不是「发抖」「两腿」这些字眼，但上面的转述足以传达他那股轻蔑的意思。林加德此刻竭力回想、拼缀老约根森那些奇谈里切实可用的零碎；可留在他心里的，只有一个大约的方位概念，和一股强烈而模糊的印象——那地方的进路凶险。他犹豫着；双桅船随着主人心灵的动静而动作，也在路上流连，仿佛同样在迟疑，无风的日子里这边一荡、那边一摆。

正因这份迟疑，一条装着成箱煤油、运往日本的大纽约船，从比利顿水道穿行时，一天早晨望见一条十分漂亮的双桅船，正顶风停船，恰恰堵在航路正中、卡里马塔以东一点的地方。穿长礼服的瘦长船长和蓄着浓髭的大副，看她的模样几乎漂亮得不像英国船，又纳闷船上那人凭什么把顶帆收到桅上逼停住，看不出一点缘故。大船的帆在微风里噗噗扇送着她前进；最后望见那条双桅船时，她已远远落在船尾之后，主桁仍然背着风，像在等什么人。可第二天，一条伦敦的运茶快帆走在同一航路上，却再没望见那条在岔路口白静静、一动不动地踌躇着的漂亮双桅船。那一整夜，林加德都在同哈西姆交谈，头顶上繁星像一条巨大的火花之河，从东流向西。伊玛达听着，时而低低惊呼，时而屏住呼吸。她拍过一次手。淡淡的曙光出现了。

「您在国内会被当作我的父亲相待。」哈西姆正说着。索具上滴着沉重的露水，暗下来的帆衬在天空浅蓝的底色上，黑黢黢的。「您将是那位进良言的父亲——」

「我做一个不变心的朋友，也只求朋友之礼相待——别无其他。」林加德说，「戒指你收回去。」
「为什么看不起我的馈赠？」哈西姆带着忧伤而讥诮的笑问。

===Original===

“Take it,” said Lingard. “It is still mine. How can I forget that, when facing death, you thought of my safety? There are many dangers before us. We shall be often separated﻿—to work better for the same end. If ever you and Immada need help at once and I am within reach, send me a message with this ring and if I am alive I will not fail you.” He looked around at the pale daybreak. “I shall talk to Belarab straight﻿—like we whites do. I have never seen him, but I am a strong man. Belarab must help us to reconquer your country and when our end is attained I won’t let him eat you up.”

Hassim took the ring and inclined his head.

“It’s time for us to be moving,” said Lingard. He felt a slight tug at his sleeve. He looked back and caught Immada in the act of pressing her forehead to the grey flannel. “Don’t, child!” he said, softly.

The sun rose above the faint blue line of the Shore of Refuge.

The hesitation was over. The man and the vessel, working in accord, had found their way to the faint blue shore. Before the sun had descended halfway to its rest the brig was anchored within a gunshot of the slimy mangroves, in a place where for a hundred years or more no white man’s vessel had been entrusted to the hold of the bottom. The adventurers of two centuries ago had no doubt known of that anchorage for they were very ignorant and incomparably audacious. If it is true, as some say, that the spirits of the dead haunt the places where the living have sinned and toiled, then they might have seen a white longboat, pulled by eight oars and steered by a man sunburnt and bearded, a cabbage-leaf hat on head, and pistols in his belt, skirting the black mud, full of twisted roots, in search of a likely opening.

Creek after creek was passed and the boat crept on slowly like a monstrous water-spider with a big body and eight slender legs.﻿ ﻿… Did you follow with your ghostly eyes the quest of this obscure adventurer of yesterday, you shades of forgotten adventurers who, in leather jerkins and sweating under steel helmets, attacked with long rapiers the palisades of the strange heathen, or, musket on shoulder and match in cock, guarded timber blockhouses built upon the banks of rivers that command good trade? You, who, wearied with the toil of fighting, slept wrapped in frieze mantles on the sand of quiet beaches, dreaming of fabulous diamonds and of a far-off home.

===Chinese===

「收下，」林加德说，「它暂时还是我的。你面对死亡的时候还惦着我的安危，这个我怎么能忘？前头有许多凶险。我们还会常常分开——为了同一个目标各自出力。什么时候你和伊玛达急需援手，而我又够得着，就差人把这枚戒指捎信给我；只要我活着，决不误你的事。」他环顾四周惨白的破晓。「我要同贝拉拉布直截了当地谈——像我们白人那样。我没见过他，可我是个有力量的人。贝拉拉布必须帮我们收复你的国家；事成之后，我也不会让他把你们吞了。」

哈西姆接过戒指，低下头去。

「该动身了。」林加德说。他觉出袖子被轻轻一扯。他回头，正撞见伊玛达把额头抵在那灰法兰绒上。「别这样，孩子！」他轻声说。

太阳升上避难之岸那一线淡蓝。

迟疑结束了。人与船同心协力，寻到了通往那淡蓝海岸的路。太阳还没落下一半，双桅船已在一炮之距的地方落了锚，锚地挨着滑腻的红树林——一百多年来，还没有一条白人的船只敢在这里把性命托付给这片水底的掌心。两百年前的冒险家们无疑知道这个锚地，因为他们愚昧无知，却胆大包天。倘若真像有些人说的那样，死者的魂灵常在生者作孽和劳苦过的地方徘徊，那么他们或许看见过一条白色的长大艇，八支桨划着，一个晒得黢黑、蓄着胡子的男人掌着舵，头戴菜叶帽，腰插手枪，贴着满是盘曲树根的黑泥逡巡，寻一处像样的进口。

小湾过了一道又一道，小艇缓缓爬行，像一只大身子、八条细腿的怪水蜘蛛。……被遗忘的冒险家们的阴魂啊——你们当年身穿皮紧身衣、在铁盔下汗流浃背，抡着长剑攻打异教徒的栅寨，或者肩扛火枪、引燃火绳，守卫河岸上扼着好买卖的木寨堡——你们可曾用幽冥的眼睛，追随这个昨日的无名冒险家的求索？你们，这些厮杀得疲乏了的人，裹着粗呢斗篷睡在安静海滩的沙上，梦见神话般的钻石，梦见遥远的家乡。

===Original===

“Here’s an opening,” said Lingard to Hassim, who sat at his side, just as the sun was setting away to his left. “Here’s an opening big enough for a ship. It’s the entrance we are looking for, I believe. We shall pull all night up this creek if necessary and it’s the very devil if we don’t come upon Belarab’s lair before daylight.”

He shoved the tiller hard over and the boat, swerving sharply, vanished from the coast.

And perhaps the ghosts of old adventurers nodded wisely their ghostly heads and exchanged the ghost of a wistful smile.

===Chinese===

「这儿有个口子，」太阳正朝他左手边西沉时，林加德对坐在身旁的哈西姆说，「这儿有个口子，够一条船进。我看这就是我们要找的入口。必要的话，我们整夜沿这条小河往上划；要是天亮前还摸不着贝拉拉布的老巢，那才见鬼。」

他把舵柄猛地一推，小艇陡然一拐，从海岸边消失了。

也许，老冒险家们的鬼魂们真的深有所悟地点了点他们幽灵的头，交换了一个幽幽怅惘的微笑。

### V / 五
===Original===

“What’s the matter with King Tom of late?” would ask someone when, all the cards in a heap on the table, the traders lying back in their chairs took a spell from a hard gamble.

“Tom has learned to hold his tongue, he must be up to some dam’ good thing,” opined another; while a man with hooked features and of German extraction who was supposed to be agent for a Dutch crockery house﻿—the famous “Sphinx” mark﻿—broke in resentfully:

“Nefer mind him, shentlemens, he’s matt, matt as a Marsh Hase. Dree monats ago I call on board his prig to talk pizness. And he says like dis﻿—‘Glear oudt.’ ‘Vat for?’ I say. ‘Glear oudt before I shuck you oferboard.’ Gott-for-dam! Iss dat the vay to talk pizness? I vant sell him ein liddle case first chop grockery for trade and﻿—”

“Ha, ha, ha! I don’t blame Tom,” interrupted the owner of a pearling schooner, who had come into the Roads for stores. “Why, Mosey, there isn’t a mangy cannibal left in the whole of New Guinea that hasn’t got a cup and saucer of your providing. You’ve flooded the market, *savee*?”

Jörgenson stood by, a skeleton at the gaming table.

“Because you are a Dutch spy,” he said, suddenly, in an awful tone.

The agent of the Sphinx mark jumped up in a sudden fury.

“Vat? Vat? Shentlemens, you all know me!” Not a muscle moved in the faces around. “Know me,” he stammered with wet lips. “Vat, fünf year﻿—berfegtly acquaint﻿—grockery﻿—Verfluchte sponsher. Ich? Spy. Vat for spy? Vordamte English pedlars!”

The door slammed. “Is that so?” asked a New England voice. “Why don’t you let daylight into him?”

“Oh, we can’t do that here,” murmured one of the players. “Your deal, Trench, let us get on.”

===Chinese===

“汤姆王最近是怎么了？”牌都堆在桌上、赌客们仰在椅子里歇手的时候，常有人这样问。

“汤姆学会不说话了，他准是在图什么顶了不得的大事。”另一个发表见解；这时一个鹰钩面孔、德国血统、据说是一家荷兰陶瓷行——有名的“斯芬克斯”牌——的代理商的人，忿忿地插嘴：

“别管他，先生们，他疯了，疯得像只沼泽兔。三个月前我到他船上谈生意。他就这么说——‘滚出去。’‘为什么？’我说。‘趁我没把你扔下船，滚出去。’老天爷！这是谈生意的样子吗？我想卖他一小箱上等瓷器做买卖用，可是——”

“哈哈哈！这可怪不着汤姆。”一个进锚地来补给的采珠帆船船主打断道。“啫，毛贼，整个新几内亚连一个长癣的吃人生番都不剩了，谁手里没有一套你供的杯碟。你把市场灌满啦，明白？”

约根森站在旁边，赌桌边的一具骷髅。

“就因为你是个荷兰奸细。”他忽然用一种骇人的腔调说。

斯芬克斯牌的代理商暴怒地跳起来。

“什么？什么？先生们，你们都认得我！”周围一张脸的肌肉都不动。“认得我。”他嘴唇发湿地结巴起来。“什么，五年——熟得不能再熟——瓷器——该死的斯芬克斯。我？奸细。当什么奸细？该死的英国货郎！”

门砰地关上了。“是吗？”一个新英格兰口音问。“怎么不给他开开瓢？”

“嘺，这儿可不行那个。”一个赌客咕哝道。“该你发牌了，特伦奇，接着来吧。”

===Original===

“Can’t you?” drawled the New England voice. “You law-abiding, get-a-summons, act-of﻿—parliament lot of sons of Belial﻿—can’t you? Now, look a-here, these Colt pistols I am selling﻿—” He took the pearler aside and could be heard talking earnestly in the corner. “See﻿—you load﻿—and﻿—see?” There were rapid clicks. “Simple, isn’t it? And if any trouble﻿—say with your divers”﻿—*click, click, click*﻿—“Through and through﻿—like a sieve﻿—warranted to cure the worst kind of cussedness in any nigger. Yes, siree! A case of twenty-four or single specimens﻿—as you like. No? Shotguns﻿—rifles? No! Waal, I guess you’re of no use to me, but I could do a deal with that Tom﻿—what d’ye call him? Where d’ye catch him? Everywhere﻿—eh? Waal﻿—that’s nowhere. But I shall find him some day﻿—yes, siree.”

Jörgenson, utterly disregarded, looked down dreamily at the falling cards. “Spy﻿—I tell you,” he muttered to himself. “If you want to know anything, ask me.”

When Lingard returned from Wajo﻿—after an uncommonly long absence﻿—everyone remarked a great change. He was less talkative and not so noisy, he was still hospitable but his hospitality was less expansive, and the man who was never so happy as when discussing impossibly wild projects with half a dozen congenial spirits often showed a disinclination to meet his best friends. In a word, he returned much less of a good fellow than he went away. His visits to the Settlements were not less frequent, but much shorter; and when there he was always in a hurry to be gone.

===Chinese===

“不行？”新英格兰口音拖长了调子。“你们这帮守法良民、传票伺候、议会法案的所罗门的后代——不行？听着，我卖的这科尔特手枪——”他把采珠船主拉到一边，听得见他在角落里恳切地推销。“瞧——上膛——再——瞧？”几声脆响。“简单不？要是出了乱子——比如跟你的潜水伕——咔、咔、咔——对穿对过——跟筛子似的——保证治好任何黑鬼最难缠的浑劲儿。可不！一箱二十四支，或者单买——随你。不要？猎枪——步枪？也不要！好嘛，我看你对我没用处，可我跟那个汤姆倒能做上一票——他叫什么来着？上哪儿堵他去？哪儿都去——是吧？好嘛——那就等于哪儿也没有。可我早晚找得着他——可不。”

约根森被彻底撇在一边，梦似的望着纷纷落下的牌。“奸细——我说的是。”他自言自语地咕哝。“想打听什么，来问我。”

林加德从瓦久回来——那次出门出奇地久——人人都看出他大变了。话少了，也不那么大嗓门了；依旧好客，可那份好客不那么铺张了；这个从前最爱跟六七个气味相投的人一起畅谈狂得没边计划的人，如今常常不愿见哪怕最好的朋友。一句话，他回来时，比起离去时，大大不算个好好先生了。他去各处商埠的次数没有少，却短得多；一到那里，也总是急着要走。

===Original===

During two years the brig had, in her way, as hard a life of it as the man. Swift and trim she flitted amongst the islands of little known groups. She could be descried afar from lonely headlands, a white speck travelling fast over the blue sea; the apathetic keepers of rare lighthouses dotting the great highway to the east came to know the cut of her topsails. They saw her passing east, passing west. They had faint glimpses of her flying with masts aslant in the mist of a rain-squall, or could observe her at leisure, upright and with shivering sails, forging ahead through a long day of unsteady airs. Men saw her battling with a heavy monsoon in the Bay of Bengal, lying becalmed in the Java Sea, or gliding out suddenly from behind a point of land, graceful and silent in the clear moonlight. Her activity was the subject of excited but low-toned conversations, which would be interrupted when her master appeared.

“Here he is. Came in last night,” whispered the gossiping group.

Lingard did not see the covert glances of respect tempered by irony; he nodded and passed on.

“Hey, Tom! No time for a drink?” would shout someone.

He would shake his head without looking back﻿—far away already.

Florid and burly he could be seen, for a day or two, getting out of dusty gharries, striding in sunshine from the Occidental Bank to the Harbour Office, crossing the Esplanade, disappearing down a street of Chinese shops, while at his elbow and as tall as himself, old Jörgenson paced along, lean and faded, obstinate and disregarded, like a haunting spirit from the past eager to step back into the life of men.

Lingard ignored this wreck of an adventurer, sticking to him closer than his shadow, and the other did not try to attract attention. He waited patiently at the doors of offices, would vanish at tiffin time, would invariably turn up again in the evening and then he kept his place till Lingard went aboard for the night. The police peons on duty looked disdainfully at the phantom of Captain H. C. Jörgenson, Barque *Wild Rose*, wandering on the silent quay or standing still for hours at the edge of the sombre roadstead speckled by the anchor lights of ships﻿—an adventurous soul longing to recross the waters of oblivion.

===Chinese===

那两年里，双桅船照它自己的方式，过的日子跟它的主人一样苦。她轻捷匀称地穿梭在人迹罕至的群岛之间。从孤零零的海岬上远远就能望见她，一个白点在蓝色的海上疾驰；通往东方的大航道上零星灯塔那些淡漠的守灯人，都认得了她顶帆的样子。他们看见她向东去，向西回。有时在雨飑的迷雾里一瞥她桅杆斜曳着飞掠而过，有时又有整天的闲工夫，看她顶着头绪纷乱的风、帆哆嗦着、笔直立着，稳稳前行。人们见过她在孟加拉湾里跟猛烈的季风搏斗，在爪哇海上被无风困住，也见过她忽然从一道陆角后面滑出来，在清朗的月光里优雅而无声。她的活跃是那些压低了嗓门却兴冲冲的谈话的主题，只要她的船长一露面，谈话便戛然而止。

“他来了。昨儿夜里进的港。”扎堆闲话的人们低声道。

林加德没有看见那些掺着讥诮的敬意中的窥视目光；他点点头，径直走了过去。

“喂，汤姆！没工夫喝一杯？”有人喊。

他摇摇头，头也不回——人已经到远处去了。

有那么一两天，人们看得见他满面红光、身躯魁伟，从扑满尘土的马车里跨下来，在太阳底下大步从西方银行走到港务局，穿过广场，消失在中国店铺街上；在他肘边，跟他一般高的，是老约根森，瘦削、褪色、执拗、无人理睬，像一个急于回到人群生活中来的往昔幽灵。

林加德不理会这个冒险家的残骸——它比他的影子还紧地贴着他——对方也不设法引人注意。他在办公楼的门口耐心等候，午饭时间便消失，傍晚准时再冒出来，然后一直守到林加德夜里回船。值班的警察差役轻蔑地望着“野玫瑰号”巴克帆船的H.C.约根森船长的幽灵，在那寂静的码头上游荡，或在点缀着船泊灯的阴沉锚地边上，一站几个钟头——一个渴望重新渡过遗忘之水的冒险灵魂。

===Original===

The sampan-men, sculling lazily homeward past the black hull of the brig at anchor, could hear far into the night the drawl of the New England voice escaping through the lifted panes of the cabin skylight. Snatches of nasal sentences floated in the stillness around the still craft.

“Yes, siree! Mexican war rifles﻿—good as new﻿—six in a case﻿—my people in Baltimore﻿—that’s so. Hundred and twenty rounds thrown in for each specimen﻿—marked to suit your requirements. Suppose﻿—musical instruments, this side up with care﻿—how’s that for your taste? No, no! Cash down﻿—my people in Balt﻿—Shooting seagulls you say? Waal! It’s a risky business﻿—see here﻿—ten percent discount﻿—it’s out of my own pocket﻿—”

As time wore on, and nothing happened, at least nothing that one could hear of, the excitement died out. Lingard’s new attitude was accepted as only “his way.” There was nothing in it, maintained some. Others dissented. A good deal of curiosity, however, remained and the faint rumour of something big being in preparation followed him into every harbour he went to, from Rangoon to Hong Kong.

He felt nowhere so much at home as when his brig was anchored on the inner side of the great stretch of shoals. The centre of his life had shifted about four hundred miles﻿—from the Straits of Malacca to the Shore of Refuge﻿—and when there he felt himself within the circle of another existence, governed by his impulse, nearer his desire. Hassim and Immada would come down to the coast and wait for him on the islet. He always left them with regret.

At the end of the first stage in each trip, Jörgenson waited for him at the top of the boat-stairs and without a word fell into step at his elbow. They seldom exchanged three words in a day; but one evening about six months before Lingard’s last trip, as they were crossing the short bridge over the canal where native craft lie moored in clusters, Jörgenson lengthened his stride and came abreast. It was a moonlight night and nothing stirred on earth but the shadows of high clouds. Lingard took off his hat and drew in a long sigh in the tepid breeze. Jörgenson spoke suddenly in a cautious tone: “The new Rajah Tulla smokes opium and is sometimes dangerous to speak to. There is a lot of discontent in Wajo amongst the big people.”

===Chinese===

舢板夫们懒懒地摇橹回家，从抛锚的双桅船黑色船体边经过，直到深夜还听得见新英格兰口音的慢腔调，从舱室天窗掀起的玻璃板缝里漏出来。一句句鼻音的句子，片片飘散在静止的船四周的静寂里。

“可不！墨西哥战争步枪——跟新的一样——一箱六支——我巴尔的摩的东家——没錯。每支饶一百二十发——唛头随您的要求打。比方说——乐器，此面向上小心轻放——合您口味不？不不！现钱——我巴尔的摩的——你说打海鸥？好嘛！那可是风险营生——听我说——九折——从我自己腰包里贴的——”

时间一天天过去，什么动静也没有——至少外人听不到什么——那阵热闹便冷了。林加德的新做派被大家只当作“他的路数”。有人说这里头没什么。也有人不以为然。不过好奇心留下了不少，而“有件大事正在酝酿”的模糊传闻，跟着他进了从仰光到香港的每一个港口。

他最觉得自在的地方，莫过于双桅船停泊在那大片浅滩内侧的时候。他生活的重心挪了大约四百英里——从马六甲海峡挪到了避难岸——一到那里，他就觉得自己置身在另一种存在的圈子里，为冲动所驱策，离欲望更近。哈西姆和伊玛达会下到海边，在小岛上等他。每次离开他们，他都不免惜惜。

每趟航程的第一段走完，约根森总在船梯顶上等他，一言不发便跟上他的肘边。两人一天难得说上三句话；可是在林加德最后一次出航之前约六个月的一个傍晚，两人正跨过运河上那座小桥——桥下土著船只一簇簇泊着——约根森忽然放快脚步，赶了个并肩。那是个有月亮的夜，地上除了高云的影子，万籁不动。林加德摘下帽子，在温热的风里长叹一声。约根森忽然用谨慎的口吻开口：“新拉者图拉抽上鸦片了，有时候跟他说话有性命之虞。瓦久的贵人们里头，很有些不满。”

===Original===

“Good! Good!” whispered Lingard, excitedly, off his guard for once. Then﻿—“How the devil do you know anything about it?” he asked.

Jörgenson pointed at the mass of praus, coasting boats, and sampans that, jammed up together in the canal, lay covered with mats and flooded by the cold moonlight with here and there a dim lantern burning amongst the confusion of high sterns, spars, masts and lowered sails.

“There!” he said, as they moved on, and their hatted and clothed shadows fell heavily on the queer-shaped vessels that carry the fortunes of brown men upon a shallow sea. “There! I can sit with them, I can talk to them, I can come and go as I like. They know me now﻿—it’s time-thirty-five years. Some of them give a plate of rice and a bit of fish to the white man. That’s all I get﻿—after thirty-five years﻿—given up to them.”

He was silent for a time.

“I was like you once,” he added, and then laying his hand on Lingard’s sleeve, murmured﻿—“Are you very deep in this thing?”

“To the very last cent,” said Lingard, quietly, and looking straight before him.

The glitter of the roadstead went out, and the masts of anchored ships vanished in the invading shadow of a cloud.

“Drop it,” whispered Jörgenson.

“I am in debt,” said Lingard, slowly, and stood still.

“Drop it!”

“Never dropped anything in my life.”

“Drop it!”

“By God, I won’t!” cried Lingard, stamping his foot.

There was a pause.

“I was like you﻿—once,” repeated Jörgenson. “Five and thirty years﻿—never dropped anything. And what you can do is only child’s play to some jobs I have had on my hands﻿—understand that﻿—great man as you are, Captain Lingard of the *Lightning*.﻿ ﻿… You should have seen the *Wild Rose*,” he added with a sudden break in his voice.

Lingard leaned over the guardrail of the pier. Jörgenson came closer.

“I set fire to her with my own hands!” he said in a vibrating tone and very low, as if making a monstrous confession.

“Poor devil,” muttered Lingard, profoundly moved by the tragic enormity of the act. “I suppose there was no way out?”

“I wasn’t going to let her rot to pieces in some Dutch port,” said Jörgenson, gloomily. “Did you ever hear of Dawson?”

===Chinese===

“好！好！”林加德激动地低呼，一时失了警惕。随即——“你到底是怎么知道的？”他问。

约根森朝运河里那一大堆普拉帆船、沿岸货船和舢板一指——它们挤作一团躺在水面上，盖着席子，浸在清冷的月光里，乱糟糟的高船艉、桅杆、帆桁和落下的帆之间，这里那里亮着一盏昏灯。

“瞧见没有！”他们往前走时他说，两人戴帽穿衣的影子沉沉地落在那些奇形怪状的船上——这些船在浅海上驮着棕色人的身家。“瞧见没有！我能跟他们坐在一起，跟他们说话，来去随我的便。他们如今认得我——三十五年了。有的人家还给白人一盘米、一点鱼。我得的就这些——三十五年——全搭给他们了。”

他沉默了一会儿。

“我从前跟你一样。”他补了一句，随后把手搭在林加德的袖子上，低声问——“这事你陷得深吗？”

“陷到最后一分钱。”林加德平静地说，眼睛望着正前方。

锚地的粼光熄灭了，泊船的桅杆隐进一片云拥来的暗影里。

“撒手吧。”约根森耳语道。

“我背了债。”林加德缓缓地说，站住了。

“撒手！”

“我这辈子没撒过手。”

“撒手！”

“上帝作证，我不！”林加德跺着脚喊。

停了片刻。

“我从前跟你一样——从前。”约根森又说。“三十五年——从没撒过手。而你干的这些，比起我经手过的某些营生，不过是小孩过家家——明白吗——哪怕你是个伟人，‘闪电号’的林加德船长。……你真该见见‘野玫瑰号’。”他添了一句，嗓音突然一哑。

林加德俯在防栏杆上。约根森凑近了些。

“我亲手放火烧了她！”他声音发颤，说得极低，像在作一桩骇人的自白。

“可怜的魔鬼。”林加德喃喃道，被这行为的悲慘与惨烈深深撼动。“我想，是实在没有路了吧？”

“我不能让她在哪个荷兰港口里烂成碎片。”约根森阴郁地说。“你听说过道森吗？”

===Original===

“Something﻿—I don’t remember now﻿—” muttered Lingard, who felt a chill down his back at the idea of his own vessel decaying slowly in some Dutch port. “He died﻿—didn’t he?” he asked, absently, while he wondered whether he would have the pluck to set fire to the brig﻿—on an emergency.

“Cut his throat on the beach below Fort Rotterdam,” said Jörgenson. His gaunt figure wavered in the unsteady moonshine as though made of mist. “Yes. He broke some trade regulation or other and talked big about law-courts and legal trials to the lieutenant of the *Komet*. ‘Certainly,’ says the hound. ‘Jurisdiction of Macassar, I will take your schooner there.’ Then coming into the roads he tows her full tilt on a ledge of rocks on the north side﻿—smash! When she was half full of water he takes his hat off to Dawson. ‘There’s the shore,’ says he﻿—‘go and get your legal trial, you﻿—Englishman﻿—’ ” He lifted a long arm and shook his fist at the moon which dodged suddenly behind a cloud. “All was lost. Poor Dawson walked the streets for months barefooted and in rags. Then one day he begged a knife from some charitable soul, went down to take a last look at the wreck, and﻿—”

“I don’t interfere with the Dutch,” interrupted Lingard, impatiently. “I want Hassim to get back his own﻿—”

“And suppose the Dutch want the things just so,” returned Jörgenson. “Anyway there is a devil in such work﻿—drop it!”

“Look here,” said Lingard, “I took these people off when they were in their last ditch. That means something. I ought not to have meddled and it would have been all over in a few hours. I must have meant something when I interfered, whether I knew it or not. I meant it then﻿—and did not know it. Very well. I mean it now﻿—and do know it. When you save people from death you take a share in their life. That’s how I look at it.”

Jörgenson shook his head.

“Foolishness!” he cried, then asked softly in a voice that trembled with curiosity﻿—“Where did you leave them?”

“With Belarab,” breathed out Lingard. “You knew him in the old days.”

===Chinese===

“好像听说过——现在记不清了——”林加德咕哝道，一想到自己的船在哪个荷兰港口里慢慢朽烂，他背上便掠过一阵寒意。“他死了——不是吗？”他心不在焉地问，一边暗自掂量，真到山穷水尽，自己有没有胆量放火烧了这条双桅船。

“在鹿特丹堡下面的海滩上抹了脖子。”约根森说。他瘦削的身影在晃忽的月光里摇曳，仿佛雾做的。“是。他犯了他们哪条贸易章程，跟‘科梅特号’的大尉大谈什么法院、什么依法审判。‘好啊，’那条狗说。‘望加锡的管辖权，我把你的纵帆船押到那儿去。’船开进锚地时，他全速把它拖上北边一道礁脊——稀烂！船进了半舱水，他向道森脱帽。‘岸就在那儿，’他说——‘去讨你那场依法审判吧，你——英国佬——’”他举起长长的手臂，朝月亮晃着拳头，月亮慌忙躲进一朵云后。“全完了。可怜的道森赤着脚、披着破衣在街上走了好几个月。后来有一天，他跟哪个善心人讨了把刀，下到海边再看他的破船最后一眼，就——”

“我不跟荷兰人过不去。”林加德不耐烦地打断。“我要的是哈西姆拿回他自己的——”

“要是荷兰人要的正是‘就这个样’呢。”约根森回敬。“再说这种营生里有个魔鬼——撒手吧！”

“听我说，”林加德道，“我在那些人山穷水尽的时候救了他们。这总有含义。我本不该插手，那样不出几个钟头一切就了结了。我既然插了手，不管当时知不知道，就必定有所图。那时我有此意——而不自知。很好。如今我有此意——而且自知。你把人从死里救出来，你就在他们的命里占了一份。我是这么看的。”

约根森摇头。

“胡闹！”他喊道，随即又轻声问，声音因好奇而发颤——“你把他们安置在哪儿了？”

“在贝拉拉布那儿。”林加德低声说。“你当年认得他。”

===Original===

“I knew him, I knew his father,” burst out the other in an excited whisper. “Whom did I not know? I knew Sentot when he was King of the South Shore of Java and the Dutch offered a price for his head﻿—enough to make any man’s fortune. He slept twice on board the *Wild Rose* when things had begun to go wrong with him. I knew him, I knew all his chiefs, the priests, the fighting men, the old regent who lost heart and went over to the Dutch, I knew﻿—” he stammered as if the words could not come out, gave it up and sighed﻿—“Belarab’s father escaped with me,” he began again, quietly, “and joined the Padris in Sumatra. He rose to be a great leader. Belarab was a youth then. Those were the times. I ranged the coast﻿—and laughed at the cruisers; I saw every battle fought in the Battak country﻿—and I saw the Dutch run; I was at the taking of Singal and escaped. I was the white man who advised the chiefs of Manangkabo. There was a lot about me in the Dutch papers at the time. They said I was a Frenchman turned Mohammedan﻿—” he swore a great oath, and, reeling against the guardrail, panted, muttering curses on newspapers.

“Well, Belarab has the job in hand,” said Lingard, composedly. “He is the chief man on the Shore of Refuge. There are others, of course. He has sent messages north and south. We must have men.”

“All the devils unchained,” said Jörgenson. “You have done it and now﻿—look out﻿—look out.﻿ ﻿…”

“Nothing can go wrong as far as I can see,” argued Lingard. “They all know what’s to be done. I’ve got them in hand. You don’t think Belarab unsafe? Do you?”

“Haven’t seen him for fifteen years﻿—but the whole thing’s unsafe,” growled Jörgenson.

“I tell you I’ve fixed it so that nothing can go wrong. It would be better if I had a white man over there to look after things generally. There is a good lot of stores and arms﻿—and Belarab would bear watching﻿—no doubt. Are you in any want?” he added, putting his hand in his pocket.

===Chinese===

“认得，我认得他，还认得他父亲。”对方激动地低语迸出。“谁我不认得？森托特还是爪哇南岸之王、荷兰人出赏格买他人头的时候——那赏格够一个人发家——我就认得他。他走背运的时候，在‘野玫瑰号’上睡过两夜。我认得他，认得他所有的头领、阿訇、打仗的好汉、那个失了心转投荷兰人的老摄政，我认得——”他结巴起来，仿佛话堵在喉咙里，索性不说了，叹了口气——后来又平平静静地重新开腔：“贝拉拉布的父亲是跟我一起逃出来的，去投苏门答腊的帕德里派。他后来成了大首领。贝拉拉布那时还是个少年。那才叫年月。我沿着海岸驰骋——把巡防舰不当回事；巴塔克乡里的每一仗我都看见了——我还看见荷兰人溃逃；我经历了辛加尔陷落，逃了出来。我就是给马南沙保众头领出主意的那个白人。当时荷兰的报纸上写了我不少。他们说我是个入了回教的法国人——”他狠狠赌了一个咒，踉跄着靠上栏杆，喘着气，把报纸咒了个够。

“好了，这桩事现在归贝拉拉布经手。”林加德从容地说。“他是避难岸上说话算数的人。当然还有别人。他已往南北各地送了信。咱们需要人手。”

“等于把群魔全放了锁链。”约根森说。“事是你干的，现在——小心——小心……”

“照我看，出不了岔子。”林加德争辩。“该干什么他们全知道。我把他们握在手心里。你不至于觉得贝拉拉布靠不住吧？啊？”

“十五年没见过他了——可整桩事就靠不住。”约根森咕哝。

“我告诉你，我安排得万无一失。要是那边有个白人替我总照应着就更妥了。存着一大批粮秣军火——贝拉拉布嘛，确实——得有人盯着一刻不离。你缺什么吗？”他补了一句，手往口袋里摸。

===Original===

“No, there’s plenty to eat in the house,” answered Jörgenson, curtly. “Drop it,” he burst out. “It would be better for you to jump overboard at once. Look at me. I came out a boy of eighteen. I can speak English, I can speak Dutch, I can speak every cursed lingo of these islands﻿—I remember things that would make your hair stand on end﻿—but I have forgotten the language of my own country. I’ve traded, I’ve fought, I never broke my word to white or native. And, look at me. If it hadn’t been for the girl I would have died in a ditch ten years ago. Everything left me﻿—youth, money, strength, hope﻿—the very sleep. But she stuck by the wreck.”

“That says a lot for her and something for you,” said Lingard, cheerily.

Jörgenson shook his head.

“That’s the worst of all,” he said with slow emphasis. “That’s the end. I came to them from the other side of the earth and they took me and﻿—see what they made of me.”

“What place do you belong to?” asked Lingard.

“Tromso,” groaned out Jörgenson; “I will never see snow again,” he sobbed out, his face in his hands.

Lingard looked at him in silence.

“Would you come with me?” he said. “As I told you, I am in want of a﻿—”

“I would see you damned first!” broke out the other, savagely. “I am an old white loafer, but you don’t get me to meddle in their infernal affairs. They have a devil of their own﻿—”

“The thing simply can’t fail. I’ve calculated every move. I’ve guarded against everything. I am no fool.”

“Yes﻿—you are. Good night.”

“Well, goodbye,” said Lingard, calmly.

He stepped into his boat, and Jörgenson walked up the jetty. Lingard, clearing the yoke lines, heard him call out from a distance:

“Drop it!”

“I sail before sunrise,” he shouted in answer, and went on board.

When he came up from his cabin after an uneasy night, it was dark yet. A lank figure strolled across the deck.

“Here I am,” said Jörgenson, huskily. “Die there or here﻿—all one. But, if I die there, remember the girl must eat.”

===Chinese===

“不缺，家里的吃食管够。”约根森干脆地答。“撒手吧。”他忽然发作。“你还不如这就跳海去。看看我。我十八岁出来的。我会说英语，会说荷兰话，这些岛上每一种该死的土话我都会——我记得的事能叫你头发根根倒竖——可我把我自己祖国的语言忘了。我经过商，打过仗，对白人土人，我从来没赖过账。再看看我。若不是那个女人，我十年前就死在沟里了。什么都离了我——青春、钱、力气、指望——连觉都离了我。可她守着这条破船没走。”

“这为她添彩，也为你添了几分。”林加德爽朗地说。

约根森摇头。

“这正是最惨的。”他一字一顿缓缓地说。“这是结局。我从地球那一边来到他们中间，他们收留了我，然后——瞧他们把我弄成了什么。”

“你老家是哪儿？”林加德问。

“特罗姆瑟。”约根森呻吟出来；“我再也见不着雪了。”他两手捂着脸啜泣。

林加德默默看了他半晌。

“你肯跟我去吗？”他说。“我说过，我正缺一个——”

“我宁可先看你下地狱！”对方凶狠地爆发。“我是个老白人无赖，可你别想让我去搅和他们的鬼营生。他们自有他们的魔鬼——”

“这事根本败不了。每一步我都算到了。处处都防到了。我不是傻子。”

“是——你就是。晚安。”

“那么，再会。”林加德平静地说。

他跨进小艇，约根森走上栈桥。林加德一边解叉绳，一边听见他从远处喊：

“撒手！”

“日出前我就开船。”他高声回答，上了船。

一夜睡得不安稳，他从舱里上来时，天还没亮。一条瘦长的人影在甲板上踱过来。

“我来了。”约根森声音沙哑地说。“死在那边还是这边——都一样。可要是我死在那边，记住，那女人得有饭吃。”

===Original===

Lingard was one of the few who had seen Jörgenson’s girl. She had a wrinkled brown face, a lot of tangled grey hair, a few black stumps of teeth, and had been married to him lately by an enterprising young missionary from Bukit Timah. What her appearance might have been once when Jörgenson gave for her three hundred dollars and several brass guns, it was impossible to say. All that was left of her youth was a pair of eyes, undimmed and mournful, which, when she was alone, seemed to look stonily into the past of two lives. When Jörgenson was near they followed his movements with anxious pertinacity. And now within the sarong thrown over the grey head they were dropping unseen tears while Jörgenson’s girl rocked herself to and fro, squatting alone in a corner of the dark hut.

“Don’t you worry about that,” said Lingard, grasping Jörgenson’s hand. “She shall want for nothing. All I expect you to do is to look a little after Belarab’s morals when I am away. One more trip I must make, and then we shall be ready to go ahead. I’ve foreseen every single thing. Trust me!”

In this way did the restless shade of Captain H. C. Jörgenson recross the water of oblivion to step back into the life of men.

===Chinese===

见过约根森那个女人的，林加德是少数几个。她有一张打皱的棕色脸，一头乱蓬蓬的白发，几颗发黑的残牙；不久前，武吉知马来的一个富有进取心的年轻传教士给他们证了婚。当年约根森为她掏了三百块大洋外加几门黄铜炮时，她长什么模样，已无从说起。她的青春只剩下那一双眼睛，未曾黯淡而含着愁，独处的时候，那双眼睛仿佛直直地望进两条命的过去。约根森在近旁时，它们便焦灼不捨地追随他的一举一动。此刻，灰白的头上罩着纱笼，那双眼睛正悄悄落泪，没有人看见——约根森的女人独自蜷坐在黑屋子的一个角落里，前后摇着自己。

“这个你放心。”林加德握住约根森的手说。“她什么都不缺。我只望你替我看着点贝拉拉布的德行，趁我不在。我再跑一趟，然后咱们就好开工了。每一件小事我都预见到了。信我！”

H.C.约根森船长的不安之魂，就这样重新渡过遗忘之水，回到了人群的生活之中。

### VI / 六
===Original===

For two years, Lingard, who had thrown himself body and soul into the great enterprise, had lived in the long intoxication of slowly preparing success. No thought of failure had crossed his mind, and no price appeared too heavy to pay for such a magnificent achievement. It was nothing less than bringing Hassim triumphantly back to that country seen once at night under the low clouds and in the incessant tumult of thunder. When at the conclusion of some long talk with Hassim, who for the twentieth time perhaps had related the story of his wrongs and his struggle, he lifted his big arm and shaking his fist above his head, shouted: “We will stir them up. We will wake up the country!” he was, without knowing it in the least, making a complete confession of the idealism hidden under the simplicity of his strength. He would wake up the country! That was the fundamental and unconscious emotion on which were engrafted his need of action, the primitive sense of what was due to justice, to gratitude, to friendship, the sentimental pity for the hard lot of Immada﻿—poor child﻿—the proud conviction that of all the men in the world, in his world, he alone had the means and the pluck “to lift up the big end” of such an adventure.

Money was wanted and men were wanted, and he had obtained enough of both in two years from that day when, pistols in his belt and a cabbage-leaf hat on head, he had unexpectedly, and at early dawn, confronted in perfect silence that mysterious Belarab, who himself was for a moment too astounded for speech at the sight of a white face.

The sun had not yet cleared the forests of the interior, but a sky already full of light arched over a dark oval lagoon, over wide fields as yet full of shadows, that seemed slowly changing into the whiteness of the morning mist. There were huts, fences, palisades, big houses that, erected on lofty piles, were seen above the tops of clustered fruit trees, as if suspended in the air.

===Chinese===

两年来，林加德把整个身心都投进了这项宏图大业，一直活在缓缓酝酿成功的长醉里。失败的念头从没掠过他的心头，为这样一桩辉煌的功业，多重的代价似乎都不算贵。这功业不是别的，就是把哈西姆凯旋式地送回那个国度——那个他曾在夜里、在低垂的乌云下、在不息的雷声喧嚣中见过一面的国度。有时同哈西姆长谈到末了——那大概已是第二十次听他讲自己的冤屈与抗争——他会抬起粗壮的胳膊，拳头在头顶上挥着，喊道：「我们要把他们鼓动起来。我们要把这个国家唤醒！」殊不知，他这正是在全盘招认藏在他那朴拙力量之下的理想主义。他要唤醒这个国家！这便是那最根本、最不自知的情感；在这情感上，嫁接着他行动的癖性，他对公道、恩义、友情的那份原始的是非心，对伊玛达苦命的多情怜惜——可怜的孩子——还有那份骄傲的确信：天底下、他的世界里的所有男人当中，唯有他一个人有本钱、有胆量「扛起这桩冒险的大头」。

要钱，要人；从那天算起，两年里两样他都凑足了足数。那一天，他腰插手枪、头戴菜叶帽，在破晓时分悄没声息地突然站到了那位神秘的贝拉拉布面前——贝拉布自己乍见一张白人脸，一时也惊得说不出话来。

太阳还没照临内陆的森林，可已盛满光亮的天空，已拱覆在黑沉沉的椭圆形潟湖上，覆在还满是阴影的广阔田野上；那阴影仿佛正缓缓化作晨雾的白色。有茅屋，有围篱，有栅寨，还有一些大屋，架在高高的桩柱上，从成簇的果树顶上露出来，仿佛悬在半空。

===Original===

Such was the aspect of Belarab’s settlement when Lingard set his eyes on it for the first time. There were all these things, a great number of faces at the back of the spare and muffled-up figure confronting him, and in the swiftly increasing light a complete stillness that made the murmur of the word “*Marhaba*” (welcome), pronounced at last by the chief, perfectly audible to every one of his followers. The bodyguards who stood about him in black skullcaps and with long-shafted lances, preserved an impassive aspect. Across open spaces men could be seen running to the waterside. A group of women standing on a low knoll gazed intently, and nothing of them but the heads showed above the unstirring stalks of a maize field. Suddenly within a cluster of empty huts near by the voice of an invisible hag was heard scolding with shrill fury an invisible young girl:

“Strangers! You want to see the strangers? O devoid of all decency! Must I so lame and old husk the rice alone? May evil befall thee and the strangers! May they never find favour! May they be pursued with swords! I am old. I am old. There is no good in strangers! O girl! May they burn.”

“Welcome,” repeated Belarab, gravely, and looking straight into Lingard’s eyes.

Lingard spent six days that time in Belarab’s settlement. Of these, three were passed in observing each other without a question being asked or a hint given as to the object in view. Lingard lounged on the fine mats with which the chief had furnished a small bamboo house outside a fortified enclosure, where a white flag with a green border fluttered on a high and slender pole but still below the walls of long, high-roofed buildings, raised forty feet or more on hardwood posts.

Far away the inland forests were tinted a shimmering blue, like the forests of a dream. On the seaward side the belt of great trunks and matted undergrowth came to the western shore of the oval lagoon; and in the pure freshness of the air the groups of brown houses reflected in the water or seen above the waving green of the fields, the clumps of palm trees, the fenced-in plantations, the groves of fruit trees, made up a picture of sumptuous prosperity.

===Chinese===

林加德头一眼看见贝拉拉布的聚落时，它便是这副模样。这一切之外，在那具迎着他、裹得严严的身影背后还有许许多多张脸；而在迅速增亮的天光里，四下一片彻底的寂静，以致首领终于吐出的那声「*玛尔哈巴*」（欢迎），他身后的每一个人都听得清清楚楚。卫兵们头戴黑色小帽、持着长柄矛，立在他四周，面无表情。隔着开阔地，看得见男人们奔向水边。一群女人立在一座矮丘上凝望，玉米田纹丝不动的秸秆上，只露出她们的头。忽然，近旁一簇空茅屋里，响起一个看不见的老泼妇的声音，尖刻凶狠地骂一个看不见的姑娘：

「生人！你想看生人？哦，不知羞耻的东西！我这么老这么瘸，稻子就得我一个人舂？咒你和那些生人遭殃！咒他们永远讨不着好！咒他们被刀剑追杀！我老了，我老了。生人没一个好玩意儿！哦，丫头！咒他们烧死。」

「欢迎。」贝拉拉布又说了一遍，神情庄重，直直望着林加德的眼睛。

那一回，林加德在贝拉拉布的聚落里住了六天。其中头三天，两人都在互相打量，谁也不发一问，谁也不露一点口风。林加德懒懒地卧在头人为他备下的细席上——那是一间小竹屋，坐落在一圈设防的围子外头；围子里一根高而细的杆上，飘着一面镶绿边的白旗，可还矮于那一片长长的高顶屋宇的墙——那些屋子架在硬木桩柱上，离地四十英尺还多。

远处，内陆的森林染着一层闪动的蓝色，像梦中的森林。临海一侧，大树干和缠结的灌木组成的地带，一直延到椭圆形潟湖的西岸；在纯净清新的空气里，成群的棕色屋影倒映水中，或从摇曳的青葱田野上方探出，还有一丛丛棕榈、圈起来的种植园、一片片果林，构成一幅富丽繁盛的图景。

===Original===

Above the buildings, the men, the women, the still sheet of water and the great plain of crops glistening with dew, stretched the exalted, the miraculous peace of a cloudless sky. And no road seemed to lead into this country of splendour and stillness. One could not believe the unquiet sea was so near, with its gifts and its unending menace. Even during the months of storms, the great clamour rising from the whitened expanse of the Shallows dwelt high in the air in a vast murmur, now feeble now stronger, that seemed to swing back and forth on the wind above the earth without anyone being able to tell whence it came. It was like the solemn chant of a waterfall swelling and dying away above the woods, the fields, above the roofs of houses and the heads of men, above the secret peace of that hidden and flourishing settlement of vanquished fanatics, fugitives, and outcasts.

Every afternoon Belarab, followed by an escort that stopped outside the door, entered alone the house of his guest. He gave the salutation, inquired after his health, conversed about insignificant things with an inscrutable mien. But all the time the steadfast gaze of his thoughtful eyes seemed to seek the truth within that white face. In the cool of the evening, before the sun had set, they talked together, passing and repassing between the rugged pillars of the grove near the gate of the stockade. The escort away in the oblique sunlight, followed with their eyes the strolling figures appearing and vanishing behind the trees. Many words were pronounced, but nothing was said that would disclose the thoughts of the two men. They clasped hands demonstratively before separating, and the heavy slam of the gate was followed by the triple thud of the wooden bars dropped into iron clamps.

On the third night, Lingard was awakened from a light sleep by the sound of whispering outside. A black shadow obscured the stars in the doorway, and a man entering suddenly, stood above his couch while another could be seen squatting﻿—a dark lump on the threshold of the hut.

“Fear not. I am Belarab,” said a cautious voice.

“I was not afraid,” whispered Lingard. “It is the man coming in the dark and without warning who is in danger.”

“And did you not come to me without warning? I said ‘welcome’﻿—it was as easy for me to say ‘kill him.’ ”

===Chinese===

在屋宇、男人、女人、静止的水面和露光闪闪的辽阔庄稼原野之上，伸展着无云天空那超拔而神奇的安宁。仿佛没有一条路能通进这荣华而静谧的国度。人简直不能相信，那不安分的大海会近在咫尺，带着它的馈赠和无尽的威胁。即使风暴连月的时节，浅滩那片白茫茫的水域升起的大喧嚣，也高高地悬在半空，化作一片浩大的低鸣，忽弱忽强，仿佛随风在大地上空来回摇荡，谁也说不出它起自何处。它像一道瀑布的庄严吟唱，在林木、田野之上，在屋脊和人头之上，涨起又消歇，笼罩着这个由败亡的狂信徒、逃亡者与亡命徒组成的隐秘而兴旺的聚落的那份隐秘的安宁。

每天下午，贝拉拉布由一队扈从陪着——扈从停在门外——独自走进客人的屋子。他道一声问候，问起居，带着高深莫测的神情谈些无关紧要的事。可自始至终，他那双若有所思的眼睛凝定的目光，仿佛要在这张白脸里探寻真相。傍晚凉爽时分，日落之前，两人来来回回踱步交谈，在栅寨大门近旁那片果林粗砺的柱干之间穿行。扈从们远远地在斜阳里，眼睛追着那两个时隐时现于树后的身影。话说了许多，可没有一句足以泄露两个男人的心思。分手前，两人郑重地握手；随后大门沉沉一响关上，木闩落进铁卡，咚、咚、咚三声闷响。

第三夜，林加德从浅睡中被门外的低语声惊醒。一个黑色的身影遮住了门口的星光；一个人突然进屋，立在他的卧席上方；门口还看得见另一个蹲着的——茅屋门槛上一团黑影。

「别怕。我是贝拉拉布。」一个谨慎的声音说。

「我不怕，」林加德低声说，「倒是摸黑进来、又不打招呼的人才有危险。」

「你不也是不打招呼就来了么？我说了『欢迎』——说一声『杀了他』，对我原是一样容易。」

===Original===

“You were within reach of my arm. We would have died together,” retorted Lingard, quietly.

The other clicked his tongue twice, and his indistinct shape seemed to sink halfway through the floor.

“It was not written thus before we were born,” he said, sitting cross-legged near the mats, and in a deadened voice. “Therefore you are my guest. Let the talk between us be straight like the shaft of a spear and shorter than the remainder of this night. What do you want?”

“First, your long life,” answered Lingard, leaning forward toward the gleam of a pair of eyes, “and then﻿—your help.”

===Chinese===

「你在我胳膊够得着的地方。咱们本可以死在一块儿。」林加德不动声色地回敬。

对方咂了两下舌头，那模糊的身影仿佛往地下沉了半截。

「我们降生之前，天书上写的不是这样的结局。」他说着，在席子边盘腿坐下，声音压得死沉。「所以你是我的客人。咱们之间的谈话，要像矛杆一样直，要短过今夜剩下的时辰。你要什么？」

「先祝你长寿，」林加德朝那对眸子的微光俯身答道，「然后——要你的帮手。」

### VII / 七
===Original===

The faint murmur of the words spoken on that night lingered for a long time in Lingard’s ears, more persistent than the memory of an uproar; he looked with a fixed gaze at the stars burning peacefully in the square of the doorway, while after listening in silence to all he had to say, Belarab, as if seduced by the strength and audacity of the white man, opened his heart without reserve. He talked of his youth surrounded by the fury of fanaticism and war, of battles on the hills, of advances through the forests, of men’s unswerving piety, of their unextinguishable hate. Not a single wandering cloud obscured the gentle splendour of the rectangular patch of starlight framed in the opaque blackness of the hut. Belarab murmured on of a succession of reverses, of the ring of disasters narrowing round men’s fading hopes and undiminished courage. He whispered of defeat and flight, of the days of despair, of the nights without sleep, of unending pursuit, of the bewildered horror and sombre fury, of their women and children killed in the stockade before the besieged sallied forth to die.

“I have seen all this before I was in years a man,” he cried, low.

His voice vibrated. In the pause that succeeded they heard a light sigh of the sleeping follower who, clasping his legs above his ankles, rested his forehead on his knees.

“And there was amongst us,” began Belarab again, “one white man who remained to the end, who was faithful with his strength, with his courage, with his wisdom. A great man. He had great riches but a greater heart.”

The memory of Jörgenson, emaciated and grey-haired, and trying to borrow five dollars to get something to eat for the girl, passed before Lingard suddenly upon the pacific glitter of the stars.

“He resembled you,” pursued Belarab, abruptly. “We escaped with him, and in his ship came here. It was a solitude. The forest came near to the sheet of water, the rank grass waved upon the heads of tall men. Telal, my father, died of weariness; we were only a few, and we all nearly died of trouble and sadness﻿—here. On this spot! And no enemies could tell where we had gone. It was the Shore of Refuge﻿—and starvation.”

===Chinese===

那一夜低语的余音，在林加德耳畔萦绕了很久，比一场喧嚣的记忆更难消散。他凝望着门洞那一方块里安详燃烧的星星；贝拉拉布默然听罢他要说的这一切，仿佛被这个白人的魄力和胆量所折服，敞开心扉，再无保留。他讲起自己被狂热与战火包围的青年时代，讲山丘上的会战，讲穿越森林的进军，讲人们矢志不渝的虔诚，讲他们扑不灭的仇恨。没有一丝流云遮蔽那方块星光的柔和辉耀——它嵌在茅屋不透光的黑暗里。贝拉拉布低声絮絮地讲着一连串的挫败，讲灾祸的包围圈如何在人们渐渐黯淡的希望与不曾稍减的勇气四周收拢。他悄声讲着溃逃，讲绝望的日子，讲无眠的长夜，讲无休止的追捕，讲惶乱的恐怖与阴沉的狂怒，讲被围的人在出寨赴死之前、先死在寨里的妻儿。

「我年纪还没长成个男人，这一切我就都见过了。」他低低地喊道。

他的声音发颤。随后的静默里，两人听见那个睡着的随从轻轻叹了口气——他双手抱着脚踝，额头搁在膝盖上。

「我们中间那时候，」贝拉拉布又开了口，「有一个白人，留到了最后，以他的力量、他的勇气、他的智慧尽忠。一个伟人。他有偌大的家财，心却更大。」

约根森的记忆——那个形容枯槁、头发花白、为了给那丫头弄口吃的而想借五块钱的约根森——忽然从安宁闪烁的星空前掠过林加德眼前。

「他像你。」贝拉拉布唐突地接着说，「我们随他逃出来，坐他的船到了这里。那时这儿是一片荒僻。森林一直逼到水边，茂草在高个男人的头顶上摇曳。我父亲特拉勒，死于劳顿；我们只剩寥寥几个人，全差点儿死在忧愁和悲伤里——就在这儿，在这个地方！任何敌人都摸不清我们的去向。这里是避难之岸——也是饥饿之岸。」

===Original===

He droned on in the night, with rising and falling inflections. He told how his desperate companions wanted to go out and die fighting on the sea against the ships from the west, the ships with high sides and white sails; and how, unflinching and alone, he kept them battling with the thorny bush, with the rank grass, with the soaring and enormous trees. Lingard, leaning on his elbow and staring through the door, recalled the image of the wide fields outside, sleeping now, in an immensity of serenity and starlight. This quiet and almost invisible talker had done it all; in him was the origin, the creation, the fate; and in the wonder of that thought the shadowy murmuring figure acquired a gigantic greatness of significance, as if it had been the embodiment of some natural force, of a force forever masterful and undying.

“And even now my life is unsafe as if I were their enemy,” said Belarab, mournfully. “Eyes do not kill, nor angry words; and curses have no power, else the Dutch would not grow fat living on our land, and I would not be alive tonight. Do you understand? Have you seen the men who fought in the old days? They have not forgotten the times of war. I have given them homes and quiet hearts and full bellies. I alone. And they curse my name in the dark, in each other’s ears﻿—because they can never forget.”

This man, whose talk had been of war and violence, discovered unexpectedly a passionate craving for security and peace. No one would understand him. Some of those who would not understand had died. His white teeth gleamed cruelly in the dark. But there were others he could not kill. The fools. He wanted the land and the people in it to be forgotten as if they had been swallowed by the sea. But they had neither wisdom nor patience. Could they not wait? They chanted prayers five times every day, but they had not the faith.

“Death comes to all﻿—and to the believers the end of trouble. But you white men who are too strong for us, you also die. You die. And there is a Paradise as great as all earth and all Heaven together, but not for you﻿—not for you!”

===Chinese===

他在黑夜里絮絮地讲下去，声调一起一伏。他讲他那些绝望的同伴怎样要出去、死在同西方来的船只——那些高舷白帆的船——的海上决战里；他怎样毫不退缩、孤身一人，带领他们同带刺的灌木搏斗，同疯长的茂草搏斗，同参天的巨树搏斗。林加德支着胳膊肘，眼睛望着门外，想起外头那片广阔田野的模样——此刻正在一片浩大的安宁与星光里沉睡。这一切都是这个几乎看不见的低语者干成的；在他身上，有着这一切的起源、创造与命数；在这个念头的惊奇里，那个影影绰绰、低声絮语的身影获得了一种巨人般的意义，仿佛是某种自然力的化身，一种永远威严不灭的力量。

「直到如今，我的性命也同他们的敌人一样不保险。」贝拉拉布凄然说，「目光杀不死人，怒骂也杀不死人；咒诅若无灵验，荷兰人就不会在我们土地上吃得脑满肠肥，我今夜也活不成。你明白么？你见过当年打仗的那些人么？他们没有忘记战时的年月。我给了他们家园、安稳的心和吃饱的肚子。只我一个人给的。他们还在黑暗里、在彼此耳边咒我的名字——因为他们永远忘不了。」

这个人，谈的净是战争与暴力，此刻却出乎意料地流露出一股对安稳与太平的炽烈渴求。没有人会理解他。有些不理解他的人已经死了。他的白牙在黑暗里闪着凶光。可还有些人他杀不得。那班蠢货。他要这片土地和土地上的人被世人忘掉，仿佛已被大海吞没。可他们既无智慧又无耐性。他们就不能等等么？他们一天诵五遍经，却偏偏没有那个信德。

「人皆有死——对信士而言，死是烦恼的终结。可你们这些强得过我们的白人，你们也要死的。你们要死的。有一座天堂，大得抵得上整个尘世加整个天庭——可没你们的份儿——没你们的份儿！」

===Original===

Lingard, amazed, listened without a sound. The sleeper snored faintly. Belarab continued very calm after this almost involuntary outburst of a consoling belief. He explained that he wanted somebody at his back, somebody strong and whom he could trust, some outside force that would awe the unruly, that would inspire their ignorance with fear, and make his rule secure. He groped in the dark and seizing Lingard’s arm above the elbow pressed it with force﻿—then let go. And Lingard understood why his temerity had been so successful.

Then and there, in return for Lingard’s open support, a few guns and a little money, Belarab promised his help for the conquest of Wajo. There was no doubt he could find men who would fight. He could send messages to friends at a distance and there were also many unquiet spirits in his own district ready for any adventure. He spoke of these men with fierce contempt and an angry tenderness, in mingled accents of envy and disdain. He was wearied by their folly, by their recklessness, by their impatience﻿—and he seemed to resent these as if they had been gifts of which he himself had been deprived by the fatality of his wisdom. They would fight. When the time came Lingard had only to speak, and a sign from him would send them to a vain death﻿—those men who could not wait for an opportunity on this earth or for the eternal revenge of Heaven.

He ceased, and towered upright in the gloom.

“Awake!” he exclaimed, low, bending over the sleeping man.

Their black shapes, passing in turn, eclipsed for two successive moments the glitter of the stars, and Lingard, who had not stirred, remained alone. He lay back full length with an arm thrown across his eyes.

===Chinese===

林加德惊愕地听着，一声不吭。睡着的人轻轻打着鼾。贝拉拉布在这阵几乎是情不自禁的、聊以自慰的宣泄之后，又变得十分平静。他解释说，他要一个能撑在他背后的人，一个强有力的、信得过的人，一股外来的力量，好镇住那些不安分的，叫愚昧之徒心生畏惧，好叫他的治理稳如磐石。他在黑暗中摸索着，一把攥住林加德的胳膊肘上方，用力按了一按——随即松开。林加德这才明白，自己那番莽撞为什么这样顺遂。
