“Why, do you think, you are alive yet?” he cried. “Jörgenson has been arguing with them on shore,” he went on, more calmly, with a swing of his arm toward where the night seemed darkest. “Do you think he would have kept them back if they hadn’t expected me every day? His words would have been nothing without my fist.”

She heard a dull blow struck on the side of the yacht and concealed in the same darkness that wrapped the unconcern of the earth and sea, the fury and the pain of hearts; she smiled above his head, fascinated by the simplicity of images and expressions.

Lingard made a brusque movement, the lively little boat being unsteady under his feet, and she spoke slowly, absently, as if her thought had been lost in the vagueness of her sensations.

“And this﻿—this﻿—Jörgenson, you said? Who is he?”

“A man,” he answered, “a man like myself.”

“Like yourself?”

===Chinese===

「您竟敢！」特拉弗斯太太俯身向下，声音急促而激烈；紧接着，林加德的头又在她下方冒出来，仰着一张脸。

「不豁出去——就得撒手。反正要是没有保全你们的本事，海峡来的援手也到得太迟；要是有这本事，我独自就能送你们脱险。我原指望能遇上一个讲道理的人谈谈。我早该明白不可能。你们来自太远的地方，不懂这些事。得，我豁出去了；我派人追你们那条船去了——那人只要有我在背后撑着，连海峡总督本人他也敢拦。他会拦住的。兴许已经拦住了。你们没什么可指望的了。可我在这里。您说过，您相信我没有恶意——」

「是。」她喃喃道。

「所以我才想，索性把一切都告诉您。总得从截船这桩事说起。现在您怎么看我？我把你们同整个世界切断了。你们这些人会像一块石头落进水里一样消失。你们从一个外国港口开往另一个外国港口。谁会操心你们的下落？谁知道？谁猜得着？等他们动起来，早过了几个月了。」

「我明白，」她稳稳地说，「我们无能为力。」

「而且孤立无援。」他补上一句。

停了一停，她用一种克制而从容的声音说：

「这意味着什么？劫掠？囚禁？」

「要不是我在这里，那就意味着死。」他答道。

「可您有力量——」

「不然，你们凭什么还活着？」他喊道。「约根森一直在岸上同他们讲理。」他朝夜色最黑的那边挥了挥手臂，语气平静了些，接着说，「要不是他们天天盼着我来，你以为他拦得住他们？没有我这只拳头，他的话一文不值。」

她听见游艇船舷上被沉沉击了一拳，同样藏进那包裹着大地与海洋的漠然、包裹着心的狂怒与痛楚的黑暗里；她在他头顶上方笑了，被这比喻和措辞的质朴迷住了。

林加德猛地动了一下——轻快的小艇在他脚下不稳——她于是慢慢地、恍恍惚惚地开口，仿佛思路已迷失在感觉的迷茫里。

「那么这位——这位——约根森，您说的是？他是谁？」

「一个人，」他回答，「一个像我一样的人。」

「像您？」

===Original===

“Just like myself,” he said with strange reluctance, as if admitting a painful truth. “More sense, perhaps, but less luck. Though, since your yacht has turned up here, I begin to think that my luck is nothing much to boast of either.”

“Is our presence here so fatal?”

“It may be death to some. It may be worse than death to me. And it rests with you in a way. Think of that! I can never find such another chance again. But that’s nothing! A man who has saved my life once and that I passed my word to would think I had thrown him over. But that’s nothing! Listen! As true as I stand here in my boat talking to you, I believe the girl would die of grief.”

“You love her,” she said, softly.

“Like my own daughter,” he cried, low.

Mrs. Travers said, “Oh!” faintly, and for a moment there was a silence, then he began again:

“Look here. When I was a boy in a trawler, and looked at you yacht people, in the Channel ports, you were as strange to me as the Malays here are strange to you. I left home sixteen years ago and fought my way all round the earth. I had the time to forget where I began. What are you to me against these two? If I was to die here on the spot would you care? No one would care at home. No one in the whole world﻿—but these two.”

“What can I do?” she asked, and waited, leaning over.

He seemed to reflect, then lifting his head, spoke gently:

“Do you understand the danger you are in? Are you afraid?”

“I understand the expression you used, of course. Understand the danger?” she went on. “No﻿—decidedly no. And﻿—honestly﻿—I am not afraid.”

“Aren’t you?” he said in a disappointed voice. “Perhaps you don’t believe me? I believed you, though, when you said you were sure I meant well. I trusted you enough to come here asking for your help﻿—telling you what no one knows.”

“You mistake me,” she said with impulsive earnestness. “This is so extraordinarily unusual﻿—sudden﻿—outside my experience.”

“Aye!” he murmured, “what would you know of danger and trouble? You! But perhaps by thinking it over﻿—”

===Chinese===

「同我一模一样。」他说得有一种奇怪的勉强，仿佛在承认一个痛心的真相。「论见识兴许比我强，论运气不如我。不过，自打你们这条游艇闯到这儿，我也开始觉得，我的运气实在没什么可夸的。」

「我们在这儿就这么致命么？」

「对某些人，也许就是死。对我，也许比死还糟。而这件事，某种程度上系在您手里。想想吧！这样的机会，我再也遇不上第二个了。可那算不得什么！有一个救过我命的人，一个我许过诺的人，会当我把他甩了。那也算不得什么！听着！我站在这艇里同您说话，句句当真：我相信，那姑娘会伤心死的。」

「您爱她。」她轻声说。

「像爱我亲女儿。」他低低喊道。

特拉弗斯太太轻轻「哦」了一声，一时寂静；随后他又开口：

「您听我说。我还是个在拖网船上当小子的时候，在海峡各港望见你们这班游艇上的老爷太太，你们于我，就像这里的马来人于你们一样陌生。我十六年前离家，一路打拼，绕了地球一整圈。早有工夫忘了自己是从哪儿起的步。同这两个比，你们于我算什么？我若当场死在这儿，您会在意么？家乡没有一个人会在意。全世界没有一个人——只有这两个。」

「我能做什么？」她问，俯身等着。

他似乎想了想，然后抬起头，温和地说：

「您明白您身处的是什么样的危险么？您怕不怕？」

「您用词的意思，我当然明白。至于明白危险？」她接着说，「不——断然不怕。而且——说实话——我不怕。」

「是么？」他的声音带着失望。「也许您不信我？可您说您确信我没有恶意时，我倒信了您。我信得过您，才上这儿来求您帮忙——把谁也不知道的事告诉您。」

「您误会我了。」她冲动而恳切地说，「这一切太不寻常了——太突然——完全在我的经验之外。」

「唉！」他喃喃道，「危险和苦难，您能知道什么呢？您！不过，也许细细想过之后——」

===Original===

“You want me to think myself into a fright!” Mrs. Travers laughed lightly, and in the gloom of his thought this flash of joyous sound was incongruous and almost terrible. Next moment the night appeared brilliant as day, warm as sunshine; but when she ceased the returning darkness gave him pain as if it had struck heavily against his breast. “I don’t think I could do that,” she finished in a serious tone.

“Couldn’t you?” He hesitated, perplexed. “Things are bad enough to make it no shame. I tell you,” he said, rapidly, “and I am not a timid man, I may not be able to do much if you people don’t help me.”

“You want me to pretend I am alarmed?” she asked, quickly.

“Aye, to pretend﻿—as well you may. It’s a lot to ask of you﻿—who perhaps never had to make-believe a thing in your life﻿—isn’t it?”

“It is,” she said after a time.

The unexpected bitterness of her tone struck Lingard with dismay.

“Don’t be offended,” he entreated. “I’ve got to plan a way out of this mess. It’s no play either. Could you pretend?”

“Perhaps, if I tried very hard. But to what end?”

“You must all shift aboard the brig,” he began, speaking quickly, “and then we may get over this trouble without coming to blows. Now, if you were to say that you wish it; that you feel unsafe in the yacht﻿—don’t you see?”

“I see,” she pronounced, thoughtfully.

“The brig is small but the cuddy is fit for a lady,” went on Lingard with animation.

“Has it not already sheltered a princess?” she commented, coolly.

“And I shall not intrude.”

“This is an inducement.”

“Nobody will dare to intrude. You needn’t even see me.”

“This is almost decisive, only﻿—”

“I know my place.”

“Only, I might not have the influence,” she finished.

“That I can not believe,” he said, roughly. “The long and the short of it is you don’t trust me because you think that only people of your own condition speak the truth always.”

“Evidently,” she murmured.

“You say to yourself﻿—here’s a fellow deep in with pirates, thieves, niggers﻿—”

“To be sure﻿—”

“A man I never saw the like before,” went on Lingard, headlong, “a﻿—ruffian.”

He checked himself, full of confusion. After a time he heard her saying, calmly:

“You are like other men in this, that you get angry when you can not have your way at once.”

“I angry!” he exclaimed in deadened voice. “You do not understand. I am thinking of you also﻿—it is hard on me﻿—”

===Chinese===

「您是要我把自己想出一场惊吓来！」特拉弗斯太太轻轻笑了。在他幽暗的思绪里，这一串快活的声音突兀得几乎可怕。下一刻，夜亮得如同白昼，暖得如同阳光；可她笑声一止，重新合拢的黑暗便撞在他胸口，撞得他生疼。「我看我办不到。」末了她正色说。

「办不到么？」他困惑地迟疑着。「事情已经够糟了，怕也不丢人。我告诉您，」他急速地说，「我可不是胆小的人；可要是你们这班人不帮我，我兴许也无能为力。」

「您是要我装出吃惊害怕的样子？」她急问。

「对，装一装——尽可以装。这对您可真是抬举——您这辈子兴许从没装过一件事——是不是？」

「是的。」过了一会儿她说。

她语气里这份出乎意料的苦涩，叫林加德慌了。

「别见怪，」他央求道，「我得从这团乱麻里谋划出一条出路。这可不是儿戏。您能装么？」

「也许，下狠劲的话能。可图什么呢？」

「你们全体得挪到双桅船上去，」他急急地说起来，「这样我们兴许不动刀兵就渡过这一关。这样一来，只要您开口说您愿意如此；说您在游艇上觉得不安全——您明白么？」

「我明白。」她若有所思地说。

「双桅船小，可艉舱配得上一位女士。」林加德兴致勃勃地接着说。

「它不是已经庇护过一位公主了么？」她淡淡地评了一句。

「我也不会来搅扰。」

「这倒是个诱饵。」

「谁也不敢来搅扰。您连见都不必见到我。」

「这几乎是决定性的了，只是——」

「我知道自己的斤两。」

「只是，我未必有那个影响力。」她收了尾。

「这我不信。」他粗声说，「说白了，您就是不信任我，因为您以为只有同您身份相当的人，说起话来才句句是真。」

「显然。」她喃喃道。

「您心里在说——瞧这家伙，同海盗、贼、黑鬼打得火热——」

「可不是——」

「这样一个人我见都没见过，」林加德不管不顾地说下去，「一个——粗坯。」

他猛地收住，狼狈至极。过了一阵，他听见她平静地说：

「在这一点上您同别的男人一样：一不如意，立刻就动火。」

「我动火！」他压着嗓子喊，「您不明白。我也是为您着想——我心里苦——」

===Original===

“I mistrust not you, but my own power. You have produced an unfortunate impression on Mr. Travers.”

“Unfortunate impression! He treated me as if I had been a long-shore loafer. Never mind that. He is your husband. Fear in those you care for is hard to bear for any man. And so, he﻿—”

“What Machiavellism!”

“Eh, what did you say?”

“I only wondered where you had observed that. On the sea?”

“Observed what?” he said, absently. Then pursuing his idea﻿—“One word from you ought to be enough.”

“You think so?”

“I am sure of it. Why, even I, myself﻿—”

“Of course,” she interrupted. “But don’t you think that after parting with you on such﻿—such﻿—inimical terms, there would be a difficulty in resuming relations?”

“A man like me would do anything for money﻿—don’t you see?”

After a pause she asked:

“And would you care for that argument to be used?”

“As long as you know better!”

His voice vibrated﻿—she drew back disturbed, as if unexpectedly he had touched her.

“What can there be at stake?” she began, wonderingly.

“A kingdom,” said Lingard.

Mrs. Travers leaned far over the rail, staring, and their faces, one above the other, came very close together.

“Not for yourself?” she whispered.

He felt the touch of her breath on his forehead and remained still for a moment, perfectly still as if he did not intend to move or speak any more.

“Those things,” he began, suddenly, “come in your way, when you don’t think, and they get all round you before you know what you mean to do. When I went into that bay in New Guinea I never guessed where that course would take me to. I could tell you a story. You would understand! You! You!”

He stammered, hesitated, and suddenly spoke, liberating the visions of two years into the night where Mrs. Travers could follow them as if outlined in words of fire.

===Chinese===

「我不信不过您，我是信不过我自己的力量。您给特拉弗斯先生的印象太坏了。」

「坏印象！他待我，就像待一个码头上的混混。这且不论。他是您丈夫。眼看着自己放在心上的人担惊受怕，哪个男人都受不住。所以，他——」

「好一套马基雅维利！」

「嗯，您说什么？」

「我只是奇怪，您这是在哪儿看出来的？在海上？」

「看出什么？」他心不在焉地说，随即接着自己的思路——「您一句话就该够了。」

「您这么想？」

「我拿得准。嗐，连我自己——」

「那当然，」她打断道，「可您不觉得，同您那样——那样——敌意地分手之后，再要恢复来往，会有难处么？」

「像我这样一个人，为了钱什么都肯干——您明白么？」

停了停，她问：

「那您愿意这个理由被人用出来么？」

「只要您自己心里有数就行！」

他的声音震颤——她不安地缩回身，仿佛被他出其不意地碰了一下。

「这里面能有什么利害？」她惊疑地开口。

「一个王国。」林加德说。

特拉弗斯太太远远探出栏杆，瞪着下面；两张脸一上一下，凑得极近。

「不是为您自己？」她低声问。

他觉出她的呼吸拂在额头上，有一瞬纹丝不动，仿佛打算再也不动、再也不说了。

「这种事，」他忽然开口，「你没留心的时候撞上来，等你明白自己要干什么，它已经把你团团围住了。我驶进新几内亚那个海湾时，做梦也没想到那条路会把 我引到哪儿。我能给您讲一个故事。您会懂的！您！您！」

他结结巴巴，欲言又止，忽然说了起来，把两年的种种幻象一齐释放进黑夜里；在那夜里，特拉弗斯太太追随着它们，仿佛它们用火写成的字句描画在夜幕上。

### VII / 七
===Original===

His tale was as startling as the discovery of a new world. She was being taken along the boundary of an exciting existence, and she looked into it through the guileless enthusiasm of the narrator. The heroic quality of the feelings concealed what was disproportionate and absurd in that gratitude, in that friendship, in that inexplicable devotion. The headlong fierceness of purpose invested his obscure design of conquest with the proportions of a great enterprise. It was clear that no vision of a subjugated world could have been more inspiring to the most famous adventurer of history.

From time to time he interrupted himself to ask, confidently, as if he had been speaking to an old friend, “What would you have done?” and hurried on without pausing for approval.

It struck her that there was a great passion in all this, the beauty of an implanted faculty of affection that had found itself, its immediate need of an object and the way of expansion; a tenderness expressed violently; a tenderness that could only be satisfied by backing human beings against their own destiny. Perhaps her hatred of convention, trammelling the frankness of her own impulses, had rendered her more alert to perceive what is intrinsically great and profound within the forms of human folly, so simple and so infinitely varied according to the region of the earth and to the moment of time.

What of it that the narrator was only a roving seaman; the kingdom of the jungle, the men of the forest, the lives obscure! That simple soul was possessed by the greatness of the idea; there was nothing sordid in its flaming impulses. When she once understood that, the story appealed to the audacity of her thoughts, and she became so charmed with what she heard that she forgot where she was. She forgot that she was personally close to that tale which she saw detached, far away from her, truth or fiction, presented in picturesque speech, real only by the response of her emotion.

===Chinese===

他的讲述像发现一个新世界那样令人震惊。她正被人领着沿一条激动人心的生存的边界行走，透过讲述人那毫无机心的热情向里张望。那些感情里的英雄气概，遮住了那感激、那友谊、那说不清的忠忱之中不成比例和荒唐的部分。那不顾一切的炽烈目的，给他那个晦暗的征服图景注入了一番大事业的规模。显然，对史上最著名的冒险家而言，再没有一个征服了世界的幻象比这更动人了。

他不时停下来，满有把握地问，仿佛在对一个老朋友说话：“换了你，你会怎么办？”不等对方认可，又匆匆讲下去。

她感到这一切里有一种巨大的激情，一种与生俱来的爱的禀赋之美——它找到了自己，急需一个对象，也找到了舒展的路；一种以暴烈方式表达的温柔；一种只有把人从他们自己的命运手里押回去才能满足的温柔。也许正因为痛恨束缚她自己冲动之坦率的种种规矩，她才更敏于洞察人的愚蠢形式之内那本质上伟大而深刻的东西——那些愚蠢如此简单，又随地域与时代而无穷变幻。

讲述人不过是个跑船的水手，那不过是个丛林王国、林中之民、晦暗的生命，又有什么要紧！那个简单的灵魂被那意念的伟大攫住了；它燃烧的冲动里没有一点卑污。她一旦明白了这一点，这故事便投合了她思想里的胆气，她听得心醉神迷，忘了自己身在何处。她忘了自己亲身贴近着这个故事——在她眼里，这故事是脱开的、遥远的，是真也好是幻也好，用如画的话语呈现在她面前，只凭她情感的响应而成为真实。

===Original===

Lingard paused. In the cessation of the impassioned murmur she began to reflect. And at first it was only an oppressive notion of there being some significance that really mattered in this man’s story. That mattered to her. For the first time the shadow of danger and death crossed her mind. Was that the significance? Suddenly, in a flash of acute discernment, she saw herself involved helplessly in that story, as one is involved in a natural cataclysm.

He was speaking again. He had not been silent more than a minute. It seemed to Mrs. Travers that years had elapsed, so different now was the effect of his words. Her mind was agitated as if his coming to speak and confide in her had been a tremendous occurrence. It was a fact of her own existence; it was part of the story also. This was the disturbing thought. She heard him pronounce several names: Belarab, Daman, Tengga, Ningrat. These belonged now to her life and she was appalled to find she was unable to connect these names with any human appearance. They stood out alone, as if written on the night; they took on a symbolic shape; they imposed themselves upon her senses. She whispered as if pondering: “Belarab, Daman, Ningrat,” and these barbarous sounds seemed to possess an exceptional energy, a fatal aspect, the savour of madness.

“Not one of them but has a heavy score to settle with the whites. What’s that to me! I had somehow to get men who would fight. I risked my life to get that lot. I made them promises which I shall keep﻿—or﻿—! Can you see now why I dared to stop your boat? I am in so deep that I care for no Sir John in the world. When I look at the work ahead I care for nothing. I gave you one chance﻿—one good chance. That I had to do. No! I suppose I didn’t look enough of a gentleman. Yes! Yes! That’s it. Yet I know what a gentleman is. I lived with them for years. I chummed with them﻿—yes﻿—on goldfields and in other places where a man has got to show the stuff that’s in him. Some of them write from home to me here﻿—such as you see me, because I﻿—never mind! And I know what a gentleman would do. Come! Wouldn’t he treat a stranger fairly? Wouldn’t he remember that no man is a liar till you prove him so? Wouldn’t he keep his word wherever given? Well, I am going to do that. Not a hair of your head shall be touched as long as I live!”

===Chinese===

林加德停住了。激动的低语一歇，她开始思索。起初只是一种压抑的感觉：这个人的故事里，有一样真正要紧的含义。对她要紧。危险与死亡的影子头一回掠过她的心头。那含义就是这个吗？忽然，在敏锐的一闪念之间，她看见自己无助地卷进了这个故事，就像人被卷进一场天灾一样。

他又讲起来。他统共没沉默一分钟。可在特拉弗斯太太感觉里已过了好几年，他的话语此刻效果全然不同了。她的心绪激荡，仿佛他来向她倾诉这件事本身就是一桩惊天动地的大事。这是她自己存在的一桩事实；也是那个故事的一部分。这就是那扰人的念头。她听见他念出几个名字：贝拉拉布、达曼、滕加、宁拉特。这些名字如今属于她的生命了，而她惊恐地发现，自己无法把这些名字跟任何人的模样连在一起。它们孤零零地立着，仿佛写在夜幕上；它们呈现出象征的形状；强行占住她的感官。她若有所思地低语：“贝拉拉布、达曼、宁拉特”，这些蛮荒的音节似乎带着异乎寻常的能量，一种命定的神色，一股疯癫的味道。

“他们哪一个都跟白人有一笔重账要算。这与我何干！我总得想办法弄到肯打仗的人。为了收拢这帮人，我把命都押上了。我许下了诺言，我要兑现——否则——！现在你明白我为什么敢拦你们的船了吧？我陷得这么深，天底下什么约翰爵士我都不放在眼里。一想到前头的大事业，我什么都豁得出去。我给了你们一次机会——一次真正的机会。这是我必须做的。不！大概是我看着不够绅士派头。对！对！就是这个。可我知道绅士是什么。我跟他们同住过多年。我跟他们称兄道弟——是的——在金矿上，在别的那些男人得亮出真本事的地方。他们有的还从国内给我这儿写信——给照现在的我这样的人，因为我——不提了！我知道绅士会怎么做。来！绅士难道不会公道地对待生人？难道不记得拿不出证据就不能说人撒谎？难道不管对谁许的诺都会兑现？好，这些我都要做到。只要我活着，你们头上的一根头发都不会有人碰！”

===Original===

She had regained much of her composure but at these words she felt that staggering sense of utter insecurity which is given one by the first tremor of an earthquake. It was followed by an expectant stillness of sensations. She remained silent. He thought she did not believe him.

“Come! What on earth do you think brought me here﻿—to﻿—to﻿—talk like this to you? There was Hassim﻿—Rajah Tulla, I should say﻿—who was asking me this afternoon: ‘What will you do now with these, your people?’ I believe he thinks yet I fetched you here for some reason. You can’t tell what crooked notion they will get into their thick heads. It’s enough to make one swear.” He swore. “My people! Are you? How much? Say﻿—how much? You’re no more mine than I am yours. Would any of you fine folks at home face black ruin to save a fishing smack’s crew from getting drowned?”

Notwithstanding that sense of insecurity which lingered faintly in her mind she had no image of death before her. She felt intensely alive. She felt alive in a flush of strength, with an impression of novelty as though life had been the gift of this very moment. The danger hidden in the night gave no sign to awaken her terror, but the workings of a human soul, simple and violent, were laid bare before her and had the disturbing charm of an unheard-of experience. She was listening to a man who concealed nothing. She said, interrogatively:

“And yet you have come?”

“Yes,” he answered, “to you﻿—and for you only.”

The flood tide running strong over the banks made a placid trickling sound about the yacht’s rudder.

“I would not be saved alone.”

“Then you must bring them over yourself,” he said in a sombre tone. “There’s the brig. You have me﻿—my men﻿—my guns. You know what to do.

“I will try,” she said.

“Very well. I am sorry for the poor devils forward there if you fail. But of course you won’t. Watch that light on the brig. I had it hoisted on purpose. The trouble may be nearer than we think. Two of my boats are gone scouting and if the news they bring me is bad the light will be lowered. Think what that means. And I’ve told you what I have told nobody. Think of my feelings also. I told you because I﻿—because I had to.”

He gave a shove against the yacht’s side and glided away from under her eyes. A rippling sound died out.

===Chinese===

她已恢复了泰半的镇定，可听到这些话，她又感到那种天塌地陷般全然无靠的晕眩——那是地震的第一次震颤给人的感觉。随后是一种期待的、静止的感觉。她沉默着。他以为她不信。

“来！你到底以为是什么把我引到这儿来——来——来这样跟你说话？哈西姆——我该说拉者图拉——今天下午还问我：‘如今你打算把你这些同胞怎么办？’我看他到这会儿还疑心我是为了什么缘故把你们引来的。那些脑袋里会钻进什么歪念头，你根本料不到。真叫人赌咒。”他真赌了个咒。“我的同胞！你们算吗？算几分？说——算几分？你们不是我的，就像我不是你们的。你们那些上流人物里，可有一个肯为救一条渔船上的船员免于淹死，去面对灭顶之灾？”

尽管那种无靠之感还在她心里隐隐不去，她眼前并没有死亡的影像。她感到活得无比真切。她活在一股力量的热潮里，带着一种新奇之感，仿佛生命是此刻刚刚馈赠的。藏在夜色里的危险没有一点动静来唤醒她的恐怖，可一颗简单而暴烈的人的灵魂的运行，赤裸裸地摊在她面前，具有一次闻所未闻的经历的扰人魅力。她听着的是一个毫不隐瞒的人。她问：

“可你还是来了？”

“是，”他答，“来找你——只为你一个人。”

涨潮的急流漫过浅滩，在游艇舵周围发出安详的汩汩声。

“我不肯独自得救。”

“那你就得亲手把他们带过来。”他阴郁地说。“有那条双桅船。你有我——我的人——我的炮。你知道该怎么做。

“我试试。”她说。

“好。要是你失败，前头那些可怜鬼可就遭殃了。可你当然不会失败。盯着双桅船上那盏灯。我是特意挂上去的。麻烦也许比我们想的更近。我的两条艇出去侦察了，要是他们带回的消息坏，灯就会降下来。想想那意味着什么。我把只对你一个人说过的话都说了。也想想我的处境。我告诉你，是因为我——因为我不能不说。”

他往游艇船身上一搡，从她眼前滑了开去。一阵梿梾的水声渐渐消逝。

===Original===

She walked away from the rail. The lamp and the skylights shone faintly along the dark stretch of the decks. This evening was like the last﻿—like all the evenings before.

“Is all this I have heard possible?” she asked herself. “No﻿—but it is true.”

She sat down in a deck chair to think and found she could only remember. She jumped up. She was sure somebody was hailing the yacht faintly. Was that man hailing? She listened, and hearing nothing was annoyed with herself for being haunted by a voice.

“He said he could trust me. Now, what is this danger? What is danger?” she meditated.

Footsteps were coming from forward. The figure of the watchman flitted vaguely over the gangway. He was whistling softly and vanished. Hollow sounds in the boat were succeeded by a splash of oars. The night swallowed these slight noises. Mrs. Travers sat down again and found herself much calmer.

She had the faculty of being able to think her own thoughts﻿—and the courage. She could take no action of any kind till her husband’s return. Lingard’s warnings were not what had impressed her most. This man had presented his innermost self unclothed by any subterfuge. There were in plain sight his desires, his perplexities, affections, doubts, his violence, his folly; and the existence they made up was lawless but not vile. She had too much elevation of mind to look upon him from any other but a strictly human standpoint. If he trusted her (how strange; why should he? Was he wrong?) she accepted the trust with scrupulous fairness. And when it dawned upon her that of all the men in the world this unquestionably was the one she knew best, she had a moment of wonder followed by an impression of profound sadness. It seemed an unfortunate matter that concerned her alone.

Her thought was suspended while she listened attentively for the return of the yacht’s boat. She was dismayed at the task before her. Not a sound broke the stillness and she felt as if she were lost in empty space. Then suddenly someone amidships yawned immensely and said: “Oh, dear! Oh, dear!” A voice asked: “Ain’t they back yet?” A negative grunt answered.

===Chinese===

她离开栏杆。灯光和天窗沿着漆黑的甲板泛着微光。这个傍晚像昨天一样——像此前的每一个傍晚一样。

“我听到的这一切，可能吗？”她问自己。“不可能——可它是真的。”

她在躺椅里坐下想思考，却发现只能回忆。她跳起来。她确信有人在低低地呼唤游艇。是那个人在喊吗？她听了听，什么也没听见，恼自己被一个嗓音缠住了。

“他说他信得过我。那么，这危险是什么？危险又是什么？”她思量着。

有脚步声从船头方向过来。值夜人的身影模糊地掠过舷门。他轻轻吹着口哨，走远了。艇里空笼的声响之后，是一阵桨落水的哗啦。夜吞掉了这些细微的声音。特拉弗斯太太重新坐下，发觉自己平静多了。

她有想自己的心思的本事——也有这份胆量。丈夫回来之前，她什么行动也不能采取。林加德的警告不是最触动她的东西。这个人把自己最内在的自我一丝不挂地摆了出来，不加任何遮掩。他的欲望、他的困惑、他的感情、他的疑虑、他的暴烈、他的荒唐，都在明处；它们构成的那个生存无法无天，却不卑污。她的心智足够高，只会从一个严格属人的立场看他。既然他信任她（多奇怪；凭什么？他错了吗？），她就以一丝不苟的公道接受这份信托。而当她恍然明白，普天之下的男人中，她最了解的竟偏偏是这一个时，她先是惊愕片刻，随即是一阵深深的悲凉。这似乎是一桩只牵涉她一个人的不幸事。

她的思绪停住了，专心听游艇的小艇回来。她被眼前的任务吓住了。万籁无声，她仿佛迷失在空荡荡的空间里。忽然，船腰那里有人打了个大呵欠，说：“哎呀！哎呀！”一个声音问：“还没回来？”一声否定的哼哼作了答。

===Original===

Mrs. Travers found that Lingard was touching, because he could be understood. How simple was life, she reflected. She was frank with herself. She considered him apart from social organization. She discovered he had no place in it. How delightful! Here was a human being and the naked truth of things was not so very far from her notwithstanding the growth of centuries. Then it occurred to her that this man by his action stripped her at once of her position, of her wealth, of her rank, of her past. “I am helpless. What remains?” she asked herself. Nothing! Anybody there might have suggested: “Your presence.” She was too artificial yet to think of her beauty; and yet the power of personality is part of the naked truth of things.

She looked over her shoulder, and saw the light at the brig’s foreyard arm burning with a strong, calm flame in the dust of starlight suspended above the coast. She heard the heavy bump as of a boat run headlong against the ladder. They were back! She rose in sudden and extreme agitation. What should she say? How much? How to begin? Why say anything? It would be absurd, like talking seriously about a dream. She would not dare! In a moment she was driven into a state of mind bordering on distraction. She heard somebody run up the gangway steps. With the idea of gaining time she walked rapidly aft to the taffrail. The light of the brig faced her without a flicker, enormous amongst the suns scattered in the immensity of the night.

===Chinese===

特拉弗斯太太觉得林加德动人，因为他是可以理解的。生活多么简单啊，她思量。她对自己坦白。她把他放到社会组织之外来看。她发现他在那里面没有位置。多可喜！这里是一个人，而事物的赤裸真相，尽管隔了几个世纪的滋长，离她并不那么远。接着她想到，这个人用他的行动一下子剥去了她的地位、她的财富、她的身份、她的过去。“我一无凭靠。还剩什么？”她问自己。什么也不剩！随便哪个旁人都可能提醒：“你本人还在。”她当时还太造作，想不到自己的美；然而人格的力量正是事物赤裸真相的一部分。

她回头一望，看见双桅船前桅桁端那盏灯，在悬于海岸之上的星尘里，燃着一柱沉静有力的火焰。她听见小艇一头撞上绳梯的一声闷响。他们回来了！她霍地站起，激动到了极点。该说什么？说多少？怎么开口？何必说？那会荒唐透顶，像一本正经地谈论一场梦。她不敢！转眼间她被逼到近乎发狂的心境。她听见有人跑上舷门台阶。为了拖一拖时间，她快步走向船尾栏杆。双桅船的那盏灯正对着她，纹丝不摇，在漫夜繁星的浩瀚里显得巨大。

===Original===

She fixed her eyes on it. She thought: “I shan’t tell him anything. Impossible. No! I shall tell everything.” She expected every moment to hear her husband’s voice and the suspense was intolerable because she felt that then she must decide. Somebody on deck was babbling excitedly. She devoutly hoped d’Alcacer would speak first and thus put off the fatal moment. A voice said roughly: “What’s that?” And in the midst of her distress she recognized Carter’s voice, having noticed that young man who was of a different stamp from the rest of the crew. She came to the conclusion that the matter could be related jocularly, or﻿—why not pretend fear? At that moment the brig’s yardarm light she was looking at trembled distinctly, and she was dumbfounded as if she had seen a commotion in the firmament. With her lips open for a cry she saw it fall straight down several feet, flicker, and go out. All perplexity passed from her mind. This first fact of the danger gave her a thrill of quite a new emotion. Something had to be done at once. For some remote reason she felt ashamed of her hesitations.

She moved swiftly forward and under the lamp came face to face with Carter who was coming aft. Both stopped, staring, the light fell on their faces, and both were struck by each other’s expression. The four eyes shone wide.

“You have seen?” she asked, beginning to tremble.

“How do you know?” he said, at the same time, evidently surprised.

Suddenly she saw that everybody was on deck.

“The light is down,” she stammered.

“The gentlemen are lost,” said Carter. Then he perceived she did not seem to understand. “Kidnapped off the sandbank,” he continued, looking at her fixedly to see how she would take it. She seemed calm. “Kidnapped like a pair of lambs! Not a squeak,” he burst out with indignation. “But the sandbank is long and they might have been at the other end. You were on deck, ma’am?” he asked.

“Yes,” she murmured. “In the chair here.”

“We were all down below. I had to rest a little. When I came up the watchman was asleep. He swears he wasn’t, but I know better. Nobody heard any noise, unless you did. But perhaps you were asleep?” he asked, deferentially.

“Yes﻿—no﻿—I must have been,” she said, faintly.

===Chinese===

她定睛望着它。她想：“我什么都不告诉他。不可能。不！我要全说出来。”她时时刻刻等着听见丈夫的嗓音，这悬而未决简直难以忍受，因为她知道，到那时她就必须拿定主意了。甲板上有人在兴奋地喋喋不休。她虔敬地盼望达尔卡塞尔先开口，好把那致命的时刻推一推。一个嗓音粗声问：“那是什么？”在慌乱之中她认出是卡特的声音——这个年轻人跟船上其余人不是一路，她早注意到了。她拿定主意，这件事可以当笑话讲，或者——何不装出害怕的样子？就在这时，她正望着的双桅船桁端的灯清晰地颤了一下，她惊呆了，仿佛看见天庭里起了一场骚动。她张嘴正要喊，看见那灯笔直落下去好几英尺，闪了闪，熄灭了。一切彷徨从她心里退得干干净净。这危险的头一个事实给了她一种全然陌生的情绪的震颤。必须马上行动。出于某种说不清的缘由，她为自己的犹豫感到羞愧。

她快步向前，在灯下与正往后走的卡特撞了个对面。两人都停住，愕然相视，灯光照在两张脸上，两人都被对方的神情惊住了。四只眼睛睁得溜圆。

“您看见了？”她问，开始发抖。

“您怎么知道的？”他同时开口，显然吃了一惊。

忽然她看见所有人都上了甲板。

“灯降下来了。”她结结巴巴地说。

“两位先生丢了。”卡特说。见她似乎没有懂，他又说：“在沙洲上被绑走了”，一面紧盯着她，看她的反应。她显得镇定。“像两只羊羔似的被人绑走了！一声都没吭。”他愤慨地迸出这么一句。“不过沙洲很长，他们也许在另一头。您刚才在甲板上，夫人？”他问。

“是。”她喃喃道。“就在这椅子上。”

“我们都下舱了。我得歇一会儿。我上来的时候，值夜人睡着了。他赌咒说没睡，可我心里有数。谁也没听见动静，除非您听见了。可您也许睡着了？”他恭恭敬敬地问。

“是——不——我准是睡着了。”她轻声说。

### VIII / 八
===Original===

Lingard’s soul was exalted by his talk with Mrs. Travers, by the strain of incertitude and by extreme fatigue. On returning on board he asked after Hassim and was told that the Rajah and his sister had gone off in their canoe promising to return before midnight. The boats sent to scout between the islets north and south of the anchorage had not come back yet. He went into his cabin and throwing himself on the couch closed his eyes thinking: “I must sleep or I shall go mad.”

At times he felt an unshaken confidence in Mrs. Travers﻿—then he remembered her face. Next moment the face would fade, he would make an effort to hold on to the image, fail﻿—and then become convinced without the shadow of a doubt that he was utterly lost, unless he let all these people be wiped off the face of the earth.

“They all heard that man order me out of his ship,” he thought, and thereupon for a second or so he contemplated without flinching the lurid image of a massacre. “And yet I had to tell her that not a hair of her head shall be touched. Not a hair.”

And irrationally at the recollection of these words there seemed to be no trouble of any kind left in the world. Now and then, however, there were black instants when from sheer weariness he thought of nothing at all; and during one of these he fell asleep, losing the consciousness of external things as suddenly as if he had been felled by a blow on the head.

When he sat up, almost before he was properly awake, his first alarmed conviction was that he had slept the night through. There was a light in the cuddy and through the open door of his cabin he saw distinctly Mrs. Travers pass out of view across the lighted space.

“They did come on board after all,” he thought﻿—“how is it I haven’t been called!”

He darted into the cuddy. Nobody! Looking up at the clock in the skylight he was vexed to see it had stopped till his ear caught the faint beat of the mechanism. It was going then! He could not have been asleep more than ten minutes. He had not been on board more than twenty!

===Chinese===

同特拉弗斯太太的一番话、悬而未决的紧张和极度的疲乏，使林加德的灵魂亢奋着。回到船上，他问起哈西姆，得知拉者和妹妹已乘独木舟走了，答应午夜前回来。派往锚地南北小岛之间侦察的小艇还没回来。他进了舱，一头倒在睡榻上，阖上眼想：「我得睡，不然要疯的。」

他时而对特拉弗斯太太怀着不动摇的信任——随即又想起她的脸。下一刻那脸又淡去了，他用力想抓住那影像，抓不住——于是又毫不怀疑地确信，自己彻底完了，除非任凭这些人从地面上被抹掉。

「他们全听见那个人把我赶下他的船了。」他想；有一两秒钟，他毫不畏缩地端详着一场屠杀的血淋淋的图景。「可我偏偏还得告诉她：她头上的一根头发也不会让人碰。一根也不会。」

而不合常理的是，一想起这几句话，世上仿佛便什么烦恼也没有了。不过时不时的也有漆黑的瞬息，纯粹由于困乏，他什么也不想；就在其中一瞬里他睡着了，对外界事物的知觉骤然消失，仿佛被人当头打了一闷棍。

他坐起身时，人还没真正醒透，第一个惊慌的念头便是自己睡了一整夜。艉舱里有灯光，透过敞开的舱门，他分明看见特拉弗斯太太的身影穿过那片亮处，出了视野。

「他们到底还是上船来了，」他想——「怎么没人叫我！」

他冲进艉舱。没人！抬头看天窗里的时钟，见它停了他正恼，耳朵却捕到机件轻微的嗒嗒声。还在走呢！那么他睡着了不过十分钟。他上船也不过二十分钟！

===Original===

So it was only a deception; he had seen no one. And yet he remembered the turn of the head, the line of the neck, the colour of the hair, the movement of the passing figure. He returned spiritlessly to his stateroom muttering, “No more sleep for me tonight,” and came out directly, holding a few sheets of paper covered with a high, angular handwriting.

This was Jörgenson’s letter written three days before and entrusted to Hassim. Lingard had read it already twice, but he turned up the lamp a little higher and sat down to read it again. On the red shield above his head the gilt sheaf of thunderbolts darting between the initials of his name seemed to be aimed straight at the nape of his neck as he sat with bared elbows spread on the table, poring over the crumpled sheets. The letter began:

Hassim and Immada are going out tonight to look for you. You are behind your time and every passing day makes things worse.

===Chinese===

原来只是一场错觉；他谁也没看见。可他偏偏记得那侧头的模样、那脖颈的线条、那头发的颜色、那身影走过时的动态。他恹恹地回到舱房，咕哝着「今夜再没我的睡了」，随即又走了出来，手里拿着几页纸，上面爬满高而棱角分明的字迹。

这是约根森三天前写的信，托哈西姆捎来的。林加德已读过两遍，可他还是把灯芯捻高了一点，坐下再读一遍。他头顶上方那面红盾上，他那姓名缩写之间迸射的一束镀金雷电，仿佛正瞄着他后颈；他光着胳膊肘撑在桌上，俯身对着那几页皱巴巴的信纸。信是这样开头的：

哈西姆和伊玛达今夜出去找你。你误了期，每过一天，事情就糟一分。

===Original===

Ten days ago three of Belarab’s men, who had been collecting turtles’ eggs on the islets, came flying back with a story of a ship stranded on the outer mudflats. Belarab at once forbade any boat from leaving the lagoon. So far good. There was a great excitement in the village. I judge it must be a schooner﻿—probably some fool of a trader. However, you will know all about her when you read this. You may say I might have pulled out to sea to have a look for myself. But besides Belarab’s orders to the contrary, which I would attend to for the sake of example, all you are worth in this world, Tom, is here in the *Emma*, under my feet, and I would not leave my charge even for half a day. Hassim attended the council held every evening in the shed outside Belarab’s stockade. That holy man Ningrat was for looting that vessel. Hassim reproved him saying that the vessel probably was sent by you because no white men were known to come inside the shoals. Belarab backed up Hassim. Ningrat was very angry and reproached Belarab for keeping him, Ningrat, short of opium to smoke. He began by calling him “O! son,” and ended by shouting, “O! you worse than an unbeliever!” There was a hullabaloo. The followers of Tengga were ready to interfere and you know how it is between Tengga and Belarab. Tengga always wanted to oust Belarab, and his chances were getting pretty good before you turned up and armed Belarab’s bodyguard with muskets. However, Hassim stopped that row, and no one was hurt that time. Next day, which was Friday, Ningrat after reading the prayers in the mosque talked to the people outside. He bleated and capered like an old goat, prophesying misfortune, ruin, and extermination if these whites were allowed to get away. He is mad but then they think him a saint, and he had been fighting the Dutch for years in his young days. Six of Belarab’s guard marched down the village street carrying muskets at full cock and the crowd cleared out. Ningrat was spirited away by Tengga’s men into their master’s stockade. If it was not for the fear of you turning up any moment there would have been a party-fight that evening. I think it is a pity Tengga is not chief of the land instead of Belarab. A brave and foresighted man, however treacherous at heart, can always be trusted to a certain extent. One can never get anything clear from Belarab. Peace! Peace! You know his fad.

===Chinese===

十天前，贝拉拉布手下三个在小岛上拾龟蛋的人，飞跑回来，说外头泥滩上搁浅了一条船。贝拉拉布当即禁止任何船只驶出潟湖。到此为止都好。村里大为骚动。我估摸那必是条纵帆船——多半是哪个蠢货行商。反正你读到这封信时，它的一切你都知道了。你也许要问，我何不驶出去亲自瞧瞧。可一来贝拉拉布有令，为了服众我不敢不遵；二来，汤姆，你在这世上值钱的一切都在这儿，在这条「埃玛号」里，在我脚下，哪怕半天我也不能撇下我的职守。哈西姆参加了每晚在贝拉拉布栅寨外凉棚里开的议事会。那位圣人宁格拉主张抢那条船。哈西姆申斥了他，说那船多半是你派来的，因为从没有白人进过这片浅滩。贝拉拉布帮着哈西姆说话。宁格拉大怒，怪贝拉拉布克扣他抽的鸦片。他起头还称他「哦！儿子」，末了竟喊出「哦！你连异教徒都不如！」闹了个底朝天。滕加的党羽跃跃欲试要插手，滕加和贝拉拉布之间什么局面你是知道的。滕加一直想把贝拉拉布挤走，你来之前、给贝拉拉布的卫队配上火枪之前，他的胜算已经很是不小。不过那一场叫哈西姆平息了，没人受伤。第二天是礼拜五，宁格拉在清真寺里领完祷告，对外面的信众讲了一通。他像只老山羊又咩又跳，预言说若放走这些白人，必有大祸、覆灭和族灭。他是个疯子，可他们把他当圣人，他年轻时同荷兰人打过好多年仗。贝拉拉布的卫队出动了六个人，端着上了膛的火枪走过村街，人群一哄而散。宁格拉被滕加的人劫走，弄进了他们主子的栅寨。要不是怕你随时露面，那天晚上就要来一场党派火并了。我看，滕加与其做贝拉拉布，倒真不如做这地方的头人。一个有胆有识的人，哪怕心里再奸，总还信得过几分。贝拉拉布那儿，永远问不出一句痛快话。和平！和平！你知道他那毛病。

===Original===

And this fad makes him act silly. The peace racket will get him into a row. It may cost him his life in the end. However, Tengga does not feel himself strong enough yet to act with his own followers only and Belarab has, on my advice, disarmed all villagers. His men went into the houses and took away by force all the firearms and as many spears as they could lay hands on. The women screamed abuse of course, but there was no resistance. A few men were seen clearing out into the forest with their arms. Note this, for it means there is another power beside Belarab’s in the village: the growing power of Tengga.

===Chinese===

这毛病叫他行事糊涂。这场和平的把戏早晚给他惹出乱子，末了兴许要他的命。不过，滕加眼下还觉得光凭自己的党羽不够硬；而贝拉拉布依我的主意，已把全村解除武装。他的人挨家挨户进去，强收了所有的火器和能搜到的长矛。女人们自然哭骂个不了，倒没人反抗。有几条汉子夹着胳膊遁进了林子。记住这一条：这说明村里在贝拉拉布之外还有一股势力，就是滕加那股日益坐大的势力。

===Original===

One morning﻿—four days ago﻿—I went to see Tengga. I found him by the shore trimming a plank with a small hatchet while a slave held an umbrella over his head. He is amusing himself in building a boat just now. He threw his hatchet down to meet me and led me by the hand to a shady spot. He told me frankly he had sent out two good swimmers to observe the stranded vessel. These men stole down the creek in a canoe and when on the sea coast swam from sandbank to sandbank until they approached unobserved﻿—I think﻿—to about fifty yards from that schooner. What can that craft be? I can’t make it out. The men reported there were three chiefs on board. One with a glittering eye, one a lean man in white, and another without any hair on the face and dressed in a different style. Could it be a woman? I don’t know what to think. I wish you were here. After a lot of chatter Tengga said: “Six years ago I was ruler of a country and the Dutch drove me out. The country was small but nothing is too small for them to take. They pretended to give it back to my nephew﻿—may he burn! I ran away or they would have killed me. I am nothing here﻿—but I remember. These white people out there can not run away and they are very few. There is perhaps a little to loot. I would give it to my men who followed me in my calamity because I am their chief and my father was the chief of their fathers.” I pointed out the imprudence of this. He said: “The dead do not show the way.” To this I remarked that the ignorant do not give information. Tengga kept quiet for a while, then said: “We must not touch them because their skin is like yours and to kill them would be wrong, but at the bidding of you whites we may go and fight with people of our own skin and our own faith﻿—and that is good. I have promised to Tuan Lingard twenty men and a prau to make war in Wajo. The men are good and look at the prau; it is swift and strong.” I must say, Tom, the prau is the best craft of the kind I have ever seen. I said you paid him well for the help. “And I also would pay,” says he, “if you let me have a few guns and a little powder for my men. You and I shall share the loot of that ship outside, and Tuan Lingard will not know. It is only a little game. You have plenty of guns and powder under your care.” He meant in the *Emma*.

===Chinese===

一天早晨——四天前——我去见滕加。我看见他在岸边用一把小斧修一块船板，一个奴隶替他打着伞。他眼下正拿造船消遣。他撂下斧头迎我，拉着手把我领到一处荫凉地。他坦白告诉我，已派出两个好水性的人去察看那条搁浅的船。这两人驾着独木舟偷偷下了小河，到了海岸边，便从一个沙洲泅到另一个沙洲，直到——我想——神不知鬼不觉地挨到离那条纵帆船五十码上下。那能是条什么船？我猜不透。那两人回报说船上有三位头人：一个目光炯炯，一个穿白的瘦子，还有一个脸上没胡子、装束又是另一种样式。莫非是个女人？我不知道该怎么想。你要是在就好了。东拉西扯了半天，滕加说：「六年前我还是一国之主，荷兰人把我赶了出来。国虽小，可再小的东西他们也不嫌小。他们假意把国土还给我侄子——叫他去挨烧吧！我要不逃，早叫他们杀了。我在这儿什么也不是——可我记着。外头那几个白人跑不掉，人又那么少。抢一把兴许有点油水。我要分给我的随从，他们是我倒霉时跟着我的，因为我是他们的头人，我父亲是他们父亲的头人。」我指出这打算不审慎。他说：「死人不指路。」我回敬说：「无知者不给信儿。」滕加静了一阵，然后说：「咱们不能动他们，因为他们同你一样肤色，杀他们是罪过；可你们白人一声令下，咱们倒可以去同自己肤色、自己教门的人厮杀——那就是好的。我已答应林加德老爷出二十个人和一条帆船去瓦焦打仗。人是好汉，你瞧那条船，又快又结实。」我得说，汤姆，那条船是我见过的同类里最好的。我说，那点帮手你已重金酬过他了。「我也愿出酬，」他说，「只要你让我的人得几支枪、一点药。外头那条船的浮财，你我平分，林加德老爷不会知道。小小一局罢了。你手底下经管的枪和药多得很。」他指的是「埃玛号」里的。

===Original===

On that I spoke out pretty straight and we got rather warm until at last he gave me to understand that as he had about forty followers of his own and I had only nine of Hassim’s chaps to defend the *Emma* with, he could very well go for me and get the lot. “And then,” says he, “I would be so strong that everybody would be on my side.” I discovered in the course of further talk that there is a notion amongst many people that you have come to grief in some way and won’t show up here any more. After this I saw the position was serious and I was in a hurry to get back to the *Emma*, but pretending I did not care I smiled and thanked Tengga for giving me warning of his intentions about me and the *Emma*. At this he nearly choked himself with his betel quid and fixing me with his little eyes, muttered: “Even a lizard will give a fly the time to say its prayers.” I turned my back on him and was very thankful to get beyond the throw of a spear. I haven’t been out of the *Emma* since.

===Chinese===

听了这话我毫不客气地说开了，两人越说越热，末了他让我明白：他自己有四十来个随从，而我只有哈西姆那九个人守「埃玛号」，他要对我下手、一锅端，易如反掌。「到那时，」他说，「我就势大得人人都要投靠我了。」在往下的话里我又探知一件事：不少人认定你已经栽了，再不会上这儿来了。此后我明白局面严重，急着要回「埃玛号」；可我装作满不在乎，笑着一谢滕加，谢他把他对我和「埃玛号」的打算预先知会了我。他听了差点叫槟榔渣噎住，一双小眼睛钉着我，咕哝道：「就是壁虎，也让苍蝇念完经。」我掉头就走，能走出长矛一掷之地，已是万幸。打那以后我再没出过「埃玛号」。」

### IX / 九
===Original===

The letter went on to enlarge on the intrigues of Tengga, the wavering conduct of Belarab, and the state of the public mind. It noted every gust of opinion and every event, with an earnestness of belief in their importance befitting the chronicle of a crisis in the history of an empire. The shade of Jörgenson had, indeed, stepped back into the life of men. The old adventurer looked on with a perfect understanding of the value of trifles, using his eyes for that other man whose conscience would have the task to unravel the tangle. Lingard lived through those days in the Settlement and was thankful to Jörgenson; only as he lived not from day to day but from sentence to sentence of the writing, there was an effect of bewildering rapidity in the succession of events that made him grunt with surprise sometimes or growl﻿—“What?” to himself angrily and turn back several lines or a whole page more than once. Toward the end he had a heavy frown of perplexity and fidgeted as he read:

—and I began to think I could keep things quiet till you came or those wretched white people got their schooner off, when Sherif Daman arrived from the north on the very day he was expected, with two Illanun praus. He looks like an Arab. It was very evident to me he can wind the two Illanun *pangerans* round his little finger. The two praus are large and armed. They came up the creek, flags and streamers flying, beating drums and gongs, and entered the lagoon with their decks full of armed men brandishing two-handed swords and sounding the war cry. It is a fine force for you, only Belarab who is a perverse devil would not receive Sherif Daman at once. So Daman went to see Tengga who detained him a very long time. Leaving Tengga he came on board the *Emma*, and I could see directly there was something up.

===Chinese===

信接着详述滕加的阴谋、贝拉拉布的摇摆和民心的向背。它记下每一阵舆情的波动和每一桩事件，那种对其重要性的郑重信念，配得上一个帝国的历史危机的编年。约根森的幽魂确实已重新踏进人间。这个老冒险家以对琐事价值的完美把握旁观着一切，替另一个人用着眼睛——那人的良心将担起解开这团乱麻的重任。林加德在商埠一天天过活着，心里感激约根森；只是他不是按天活，而是按信里一句一句的话活着，于是事件的接连到来有一种令人晕头转向的迅疾，有时使他惊得直哼，有时愤怒地朝自己咆哮一声“什么？”不止一次折回去重读几行或一整页。读到末尾，他紧锁眉头，烦躁不安地读到：

——我本来以为，在你回来之前、在那帮倒霉的白人把船弄脱之前，我能压住局面，谁知谢里夫·达曼偏偏在他被盼着的那天从北边到了，带着两条伊拉努人的普拉帆船。他长相像阿拉伯人。我看得很清楚，那两个伊拉努藩王都被他玩弄于股掌之上。两条普拉船又大又武装齐全。它们敲锣打鼓、旗帆飘扬地驶上小河，进了潟湖，甲板上站满了挥舞双手剑、高喊战号的人群。对你来说这是一支好力量，只是贝拉拉布那个别扭的魔鬼不肯马上接待谢里夫·达曼。于是达曼去见滕加，滕加留他谈了很久很久。离开滕加后他上了“艾玛号”，我一眼就看出有事了。

===Original===

He began by asking me for the ammunition and weapons they are to get from you, saying he was anxious to sail at once toward Wajo, since it was agreed he was to precede you by a few days. I replied that that was true enough but that I could not think of giving him the powder and muskets till you came. He began to talk about you and hinted that perhaps you will never come. “And no matter,” says he, “here is Rajah Hassim and the Lady Immada and we would fight for them if no white man was left in the world. Only we must have something to fight with.” He pretended then to forget me altogether and talked with Hassim while I sat listening. He began to boast how well he got along the Bruni coast. No Illanun prau had passed down that coast for years.

Immada wanted me to give the arms he was asking for. The girl is beside herself with fear of something happening that would put a stopper on the Wajo expedition. She has set her mind on getting her country back. Hassim is very reserved but he is very anxious, too. Daman got nothing from me, and that very evening the praus were ordered by Belarab to leave the lagoon. He does not trust the Illanuns﻿—and small blame to him. Sherif Daman went like a lamb. He has no powder for his guns. As the praus passed by the *Emma* he shouted to me he was going to wait for you outside the creek. Tengga has given him a man who would show him the place. All this looks very queer to me.

===Chinese===

他开口就向我要你们答应给他们的弹药和武器，说他急着立刻开船去瓦久，因为讲定了他比你先走几天。我回答说，这倒不假，可你人不来，我不能把火药和火枪交给他。他开始议论你，暗示你也许永远不来了。“那也不要紧，”他说，“这儿有哈西姆拉者和伊玛达小姐，哪怕世上一个白人也不剩，我们也要为他们打仗。只是总得有打仗的家什。”随后他假装把我撇在一边，跟哈西姆谈起来，我坐在一边听着。他吹嘘自己在布鲁尼海岸如何畅通无阻。多少年来没有一条伊拉努的普拉船从那条海岸下来过。

伊玛达也劝我把他要的武器给他。这姑娘简直急疯了，生怕出什么岔子断了瓦久之行。她一心想回国。哈西姆城府很深，可也非常焦急。达曼什么也没从我手里拿去，而当天傍晚，贝拉拉布就命令那两条普拉船离开潟湖。他不信伊拉努人——这也怪不得他。谢里夫·达曼乖乖地走了。他的炮没有火药。普拉船经过“艾玛号”时，他朝我喊话说他要在河口外头等你。滕加给了他一个带路人。这一切在我看来都很蹊跷。

===Original===

Look out outside then. The praus are dodging amongst the islets. Daman visits Tengga. Tengga called on me as a good friend to try and persuade me to give Daman the arms and gunpowder he is so anxious to get. Somehow or other they tried to get around Belarab, who came to see me last night and hinted I had better do so. He is anxious for these Illanuns to leave the neighbourhood. He thinks that if they loot the schooner they will be off at once. That’s all he wants now. Immada has been to see Belarab’s women and stopped two nights in the stockade. Belarab’s youngest wife﻿—he got married six weeks ago﻿—is on the side of Tengga’s party because she thinks Belarab would get a share of the loot and she got into her silly head there are jewels and silks in that schooner. What between Tengga worrying him outside and the women worrying him at home, Belarab had such a lively time of it that he concluded he would go to pray at his father’s tomb. So for the last two days he has been away camping in that unhealthy place. When he comes back he will be down with fever as sure as fate and then he will be no good for anything. Tengga lights up smoky fires often. Some signal to Daman. I go ashore with Hassim’s men and put them out. This is risking a fight every time﻿—for Tengga’s men look very black at us. I don’t know what the next move may be. Hassim’s as true as steel. Immada is very unhappy. They will tell you many details I have no time to write.

The last page fluttered on the table out of Lingard’s fingers. He sat very still for a moment looking straight before him, then went on deck.

“Our boats back yet?” he asked Shaw, whom he saw prowling on the quarterdeck.

“No, sir, I wish they were. I am waiting for them to go and turn in,” answered the mate in an aggrieved manner.

“Lower that lantern forward there,” cried Lingard, suddenly, in Malay.

“This trade isn’t fit for a decent man,” muttered Shaw to himself, and he moved away to lean on the rail, looking moodily to seaward. After a while: “There seems to be commotion on board that yacht,” he said. “I see a lot of lights moving about her decks. Anything wrong, do you think, sir?”

“No, I know what it is,” said Lingard in a tone of elation. She has done it! he thought.

===Chinese===

所以你在外面要当心。那些普拉船在小岛之间躲来躲去。达曼常去访滕加。滕加以好朋友的名义来劝我，要我把他巴望的武器火药交给达曼。他们又想方设法去绕贝拉拉布，昨儿晚上贝拉拉布来见我，暗示我最好照办。他巴望这些伊拉努人早早离开左近。他想的是，他们若把那条纵帆船抢了，马上就会走。如今他要的只有这个。伊玛达去看过贝拉拉布的女眷，在栅寨里歇了两夜。贝拉拉布最年轻的太太——他六个星期前才娶的——站在滕加一党那边，因为她想，船上的赃贝拉拉布能分一份，她那颗糊涂脑袋认定那纵帆船里全是珠宝绸缎。外头滕加缠他，家里女人们缠他，贝拉拉布被折腾得苦了，索性说要去他父亲坟上祈祷。这两天他一直在外面那处瘴气地里露营。回来准定害热病，一害上就什么也干不成了。滕加时常点起浓烟的火。多半是给达曼的信号。我带着哈西姆的人上岸去扑灭。回回都是冒开仗的风险——滕加的人瞧我们的脸色黑得很。下一步会怎样我说不上。哈西姆坚定不移。伊玛达很不快活。许多细枝末节他们会当面讲给你听，我没工夫写了。

最后一页从林加德指间滑脱，在桌上飘了飘。他一动不动坐了片刻，直望前方，随后上了甲板。

“咱们的小艇回来了吗？”他问在后甲板踱步的肖。

“没有，先生，可惜没回。我正等他们回来好去睡。”大副委屈地答。

“把前头那盏灯降下来。”林加德忽然用马来语喊。

“这营生不是正经人干的。”肖自咕哝着走开了，倚到栏杆上，阴郁地朝海那边望。过了一会儿他说：“游艇上像是有动静。我看见好些灯在她甲板上晃来晃去。您看出了什么事吗，先生？”

“不，我知道是怎么回事。”林加德用轻快的口气说。她办到了！他想。

===Original===

He returned to the cabin, put away Jörgenson’s letter and pulled out the drawer of the table. It was full of cartridges. He took a musket down, loaded it, then took another and another. He hammered at the waddings with fierce joyousness. The ramrods rang and jumped. It seemed to him he was doing his share of some work in which that woman was playing her part faithfully. “She has done it,” he repeated, mentally. “She will sit in the cuddy. She will sleep in my berth. Well, I’m not ashamed of the brig. By heavens﻿—no! I shall keep away: never come near them as I’ve promised. Now there’s nothing more to say. I’ve told her everything at once. There’s nothing more.”

He felt a heaviness in his burning breast, in all his limbs as if the blood in his veins had become molten lead.

“I shall get the yacht off. Three, four days﻿—no, a week.”

He found he couldn’t do it under a week. It occurred to him he would see her every day till the yacht was afloat. No, he wouldn’t intrude, but he was master and owner of the brig after all. He didn’t mean to skulk like a whipped cur about his own decks.

“It’ll be ten days before the schooner is ready. I’ll take every scrap of ballast out of her. I’ll strip her﻿—I’ll take her lower masts out of her, by heavens! I’ll make sure. Then another week to fit out﻿—and﻿—goodbye. Wish I had never seen them. Goodbye﻿—forever. Home’s the place for them. Not for me. On another coast she would not have listened. Ah, but she is a woman﻿—every inch of her. I shall shake hands. Yes. I shall take her hand﻿—just before she goes. Why the devil not? I am master here after all﻿—in this brig﻿—as good as anyone﻿—by heavens, better than anyone﻿—better than anyone on earth.”

He heard Shaw walk smartly forward above his head hailing:

“What’s that﻿—a boat?”

A voice answered indistinctly.

“One of my boats is back,” thought Lingard. “News about Daman perhaps. I don’t care if he kicks. I wish he would. I would soon show her I can fight as well as I can handle the brig. Two praus. Only two praus. I wouldn’t mind if there were twenty. I would sweep ’em off the sea﻿—I would blow ’em out of the water﻿—I would make the brig walk over them. ‘Now,’ I’d say to her, ‘you who are not afraid, look how it’s done!’ ”

===Chinese===

他回到舱里，收好约根森的信，拉开桌子的抽屉。里面满是弹药筒。他取下一支火枪，装上弹药，然后又一支，又一支。他敲打填塞时带着凶狠的欢快。推弹杆铿然作响，蹦来蹦去。他觉得自己是在分担一项工作的一部分，而那个女人正在其中忠实地扮演她的角色。“她办到了。”他在心里反复说。“她要坐在我的餐舱里。她要睡在我的铺位上。好，我这条双桅船不丢人。老天作证——不丢人！我会躲开：照我许诺的，绝不凑近他们。现在再没什么可说的了。我把一切都一下子告诉了她。再没什么了。”

他感到滚烫的胸膛里、四肢里一阵沉重，仿佛血管里的血化成了熔化的铅。

“我要把游艇弄下来。三四天——不，一个星期。”

他发现一个星期之内办不成。他想到，游艇浮起来之前，他天天都会见到她。不，他不会去打扰，可他到底是双桅船的船长兼船主。他不打算像挨了鞭子的狗一样在自己的甲板上溜边儿。

“纵帆船备好之前得十天。我要把她压舱的东西一粒不剩全卸出来。我要扒光她——老天作证，我要把她的下桅都拔出来！我要万无一失。然后再用一个星期整修——然后——水别了。真后悔见过他们。水别了——永远。家是他们的地方。不是我的。换一处海岸，她是不会听的。啊，可她是个女人——彻头彻尾的女人。我要握手。是的。临走之前我要握她的手。凭什么不？我终究是这里的主人——在这条双桅船上——不输给任何人——老天作证，比谁都强——比世上任何人都强。”

他听见头顶上肖轻快地走向船头，喊道：

“那是什么——一条艇？”

一个声音含混地答了话。

“我的一条艇回来了。”林加德想。“多半是达曼的消息。我才不管他闹不闹。倒巴不得他闹。我正好让她瞧瞧，我不光会开船，也会打仗。两条普拉船。不过两条普拉船。就是二十条我也不怕。我把它们从海上扫掉——把它们从水上轰上天——让双桅船从它们身上碾过去。‘瞧着，’我会对她说，‘你这无所畏惧的人，看看这是怎么干的！’”

===Original===

He felt light. He had the sensation of being whirled high in the midst of an uproar and as powerless as a feather in a hurricane. He shuddered profoundly. His arms hung down, and he stood before the table staring like a man overcome by some fatal intelligence.

Shaw, going into the waist to receive what he thought was one of the brig’s boats, came against Carter making his way aft hurriedly.

“Hullo! Is it you again?” he said, swiftly, barring the way.

“I come from the yacht,” began Carter with some impatience.

“Where else could you come from?” said Shaw. “And what might you want now?”

“I want to see your skipper.”

“Well, you can’t,” declared Shaw, viciously. “He’s turned in for the night.”

“He expects me,” said Carter, stamping his foot. “I’ve got to tell him what happened.”

“Don’t you fret yourself, young man,” said Shaw in a superior manner; “he knows all about it.”

They stood suddenly silent in the dark. Carter seemed at a loss what to do. Shaw, though surprised by it, enjoyed the effect he had produced.

“Damn me, if I did not think so,” murmured Carter to himself; then drawling coolly asked﻿—“And perhaps you know, too?”

“What do you think? Think I am a dummy here? I ain’t mate of this brig for nothing.”

“No, you are not,” said Carter with a certain bitterness of tone. “People do all kinds of queer things for a living, and I am not particular myself, but I would think twice before taking your billet.”

“What? What do you in-si-nu-ate. My billet? You ain’t fit for it, you yacht-swabbing brass-buttoned imposter.”

“What’s this? Any of our boats back?” asked Lingard from the poop. “Let the seacannie in charge come to me at once.”

“There’s only a message from the yacht,” began Shaw, deliberately.

“Yacht! Get the deck lamps along here in the waist! See the ladder lowered. Bear a hand, serang! Mr. Shaw! Burn the flare up aft. Two of them! Give light to the yacht’s boats that will be coming alongside. Steward! Where’s that steward? Turn him out then.”

Bare feet began to patter all round Carter. Shadows glided swiftly.

“Are these flares coming? Where’s the quartermaster on duty?” shouted Lingard in English and Malay. “This way, come here! Put it on a rocket stick﻿—can’t you? Hold over the side﻿—thus! Stand by with the lines for the boats forward there. Mr. Shaw﻿—we want more light!”

===Chinese===

他感到身子轻飘飘的。他有一种在一片喧嚣中被高高卷起的感觉，像飓风里的一根羽毛那样无依无靠。他深深打了个寒噤。两臂垂着，他站在桌前，直勾勾地望着，像一个被某个致命的消息击垮的人。

肖走到船腰去接他以为是双桅船的小艇，一头撞上了匆匆往船尾来的卡特。

“哦！又是你？”他迅速拦住去路说。

“我从游艇上来。”卡特有点不耐烦地开口。

“还能从哪儿来？”肖说。“这回又要干什么？”

“我要见你们船长。”

“不成。”肖恶狠狠地说。“他睡觉了。”

“他在等我。”卡特跺着脚说。“有大事得告诉他。”

“你别着急，年轻人。”肖一副居高临下的派头；“他全知道了。”

两人在黑暗里忽然都不作声。卡特一时不知如何是好。肖虽奇怪自己竟有这效果，倒也受用。

“见鬼，我就知道是这么回事。”卡特自语道；随即懒懒地问——“兴许你也知道？”

“你猜呢？当我是木头？我当这条船的大副不是白当的。”

“是啊，不是白当的。”卡特带着点刻薄说。“人为口饭什么怪事都干，我自己也不挑，可要我接你这份差事，我得思量思量。”

“什么？你含——沙——射——影什么。我这份差事？你还不配呢，你个游艇上擦甲板的铜扣子骗子。”

“什么事？咱们的艇回来了？”林加德在艉楼上问。“叫带队的土著海员立刻来见我。”

“不过是游艇捎来的口信。”肖不紧不慢地开口。

“游艇！把甲板灯搬到船腰来！放绳梯。快点，水手长！肖先生！艉尾点火把。两支！给要靠拢的游艇小艇照亮。服务员！服务员呢？把他叫起来。”

光脚板开始在卡特四周啪嗒啪嗒跑动。影子飞快地滑来滑去。

“火把呢？值班的舵手呢？”林加德用英语和马来语大喊。“这边，过来！把它安在信号火箭杆上——行不行？探出舷外——这样！船头那边看好缆绳。肖先生——再亮点！”

===Original===

“Aye, aye, sir,” called out Shaw, but he did not move, as if dazed by the vehemence of his commander.

“That’s what we want,” muttered Carter under his breath. “Imposter! What do you call yourself?” he said half aloud to Shaw.

The ruddy glare of the flares disclosed Lingard from head to foot, standing at the break of the poop. His head was bare, his face, crudely lighted, had a fierce and changing expression in the sway of flames.

“What can be his game?” thought Carter, impressed by the powerful and wild aspect of that figure. “He’s changed somehow since I saw him first,” he reflected. It struck him the change was serious, not exactly for the worse, perhaps﻿—and yet.﻿ ﻿… Lingard smiled at him from the poop.

Carter went up the steps and without pausing informed him of what had happened.

“Mrs. Travers told me to go to you at once. She’s very upset as you may guess,” he drawled, looking Lingard hard in the face. Lingard knitted his eyebrows. “The hands, too, are scared,” Carter went on. “They fancy the savages, or whatever they may be who stole the owner, are going to board the yacht every minute. I don’t think so myself but﻿—”

“Quite right﻿—most unlikely,” muttered Lingard.

“Aye, I daresay you know all about it,” continued Carter, coolly, “the men are startled and no mistake, but I can’t blame them very much. There isn’t enough even of carving knives aboard to go round. One old signal gun! A poor show for better men than they.”

“There’s no mistake I suppose about this affair?” asked Lingard.

“Well, unless the gentlemen are having a lark with us at hide and seek. The man says he waited ten minutes at the point, then pulled slowly along the bank looking out, expecting to see them walking back. He made the trunk of a tree apparently stranded on the sand and as he was sculling past he says a man jumped up from behind that log, flung a stick at him and went off running. He backed water at once and began to shout, ‘Are you there, sir?’ No one answered. He could hear the bushes rustle and some strange noises like whisperings. It was very dark. After calling out several times, and waiting on his oars, he got frightened and pulled back to the yacht. That is clear enough. The only doubt in my mind is if they are alive or not. I didn’t let on to Mrs. Travers. That’s a kind of thing you keep to yourself, of course.”

===Chinese===

“是，是，先生。”肖应着，却没动窝，仿佛被船长的火气震慒了。

“这才像话。”卡特低声咕哝。“骗子！你自己算老几？”他半对着肖说。

火把的红光把林加德从头到脚照了出来，他站在艉楼台阶口。光着头，脸在生硬的光照下、在摇曳的火光里，带着一种凶悍而变幻的表情。

“他打的是什么主意？”卡特想，被那身影威猛狂野的气概慑住了。“自打我头回见他，他变了个人似的。”他寻思。他觉得这变化非同小可，说不上变坏——可又……林加德从艉楼上朝他笑了。

卡特走上台阶，开门见山把事情讲了。

“特拉弗斯太太叫我马上来找您。她乱得很，您想也知道。”他拖长声调说，直盯着林加德的脸。林加德皱起眉。“水手们也吓着了。”卡特继续说。“他们琢磨着，抢走船主的那些野人——不管是谁吧——随时会登上游艇。我自己倒不这么想，可是——”

“想得对——多半不会。”林加德咕哝道。

“是，您想必门儿清。”卡特不紧不慢地接着说，“那帮人是吓着了，没跑儿，可我也不能多怪他们。船上连切菜刀都不够一人一把。一门旧信号炮！比他们强的人也够呛。”

“这件事没搞错吧？”林加德问。

 “嗯，除非两位先生存心跟咱们捉迷藏。那人说他在登岸点等了十分钟，然后沿着沙洲慢慢划，一路张望，指望看见他们走回来。他瞧见一段像是冲上沙滩的树干，正划过去，他说树干后面跳起一个人，朝他掼来一根棍子，撒腿就跑。他立刻倒桨，喊‘您在吗，先生？’没人应。他听得见灌木丛卒卒响，还有些耳语似的怪声。天黑得很。喊了几回、停桨等了一阵之后，他害了怕，划回游艇去了。这就够清楚了。我心里唯一的疑问是他们还活着没有。我没向特拉弗斯太太透半个字。那种事当然得烂在自己肚里。”

===Original===

“I don’t think they are dead,” said Lingard, slowly, and as if thinking of something else.

“Oh! If you say so it’s all right,” said Carter with deliberation.

“What?” asked Lingard, absently; “fling a stick, did they? Fling a spear!”

“That’s it!” assented Carter, “but I didn’t say anything. I only wondered if the same kind of stick hadn’t been flung at the owner, that’s all. But I suppose you know your business best, Captain.”

Lingard, grasping his whole beard, reflected profoundly, erect and with bowed head in the glare of the flares.

“I suppose you think it’s my doing?” he asked, sharply, without looking up.

Carter surveyed him with a candidly curious gaze. “Well, Captain, Mrs. Travers did let on a bit to me about our chief-officer’s boat. You’ve stopped it, haven’t you? How she got to know God only knows. She was sorry she spoke, too, but it wasn’t so much of news to me as she thought. I can put two and two together, sometimes. Those rockets, last night, eh? I wished I had bitten my tongue out before I told you about our first gig. But I was taken unawares. Wasn’t I? I put it to you: wasn’t I? And so I told her when she asked me what passed between you and me on board this brig, not twenty-four hours ago. Things look different now, all of a sudden. Enough to scare a woman, but she is the best man of them all on board. The others are fairly off the chump because it’s a bit dark and something has happened they ain’t used to. But she has something on her mind. I can’t make her out!” He paused, wriggled his shoulders slightly﻿—“No more than I can make you out,” he added.

“That’s your trouble, is it?” said Lingard, slowly.

“Aye, Captain. Is it all clear to you? Stopping boats, kidnapping gentlemen. That’s fun in a way, only﻿—I am a youngster to you﻿—but is it all clear to you? Old Robinson wasn’t particular, you know, and he﻿—”

“Clearer than daylight,” cried Lingard, hotly. “I can’t give up﻿—”

===Chinese===

“我看他们没死。”林加德缓缓地说，仿佛想着别的什么。

“哦！您这么说，那就稳了。”卡特斟酌着说。

“什么？”林加德心不在焉地问；“掼棍子，是吗？掼的是矛！”

“正是！”卡特附和道，“可我没吭声。我只是寻思，朝船主掼的，兴许也是同一种‘棍子’，就这么回事。不过您的事您最清楚，船长。”

林加德抓着整把大胡子，在火把的光照里低头沉思，身子笔挺。

“你八成以为这是我的手笔？”他猛然问，头也不抬。

卡特以坦白的好奇打量他。“这个嘛，船长，咱们大副那条艇的事，特拉弗斯太太倒是向我露过一点口风。您把它拦下了，对吧？她怎么打听出来的只有天知道。她说漏了嘴又后悔。可这事对我来说也没她想的那么新鲜。有时候我也会二加二。昨儿夜里那些火箭，嗯？我悔不该把一号艇的事告诉您，悔得想把舌头咬下来。可我冷不防被拿住了。是不是？我问您：是不是？后来她问我不到二十四小时前在这船上您跟我之间说了些什么，我也是这么回她的。事情如今一下子全变了样。吓坏一个女人绰绰有余，可她是那条船上最有种的。其余那帮纯粹是掉了魂儿，因为天有点黑，又出了他们没习惯的事。可她有心事。我摸不透她！”他顿了顿，微微耸了耸肩——“就跟摸不透您一样。”他补了一句。

“这就是你的烦恼，是吗？”林加德缓缓地说。

“可不，船长。您心里门儿清吗？拦艇，绑先生们。这玩儿法不能说不痛快，只是——我在您面前是后生——可您心里真门儿清吗？老鲁滨逊那可是不讲究的人，您知道，可他——”

“比白昼还清楚。”林加德激动地喊。“我不能撒手——”

===Original===

He checked himself. Carter waited. The flare bearers stood rigid, turning their faces away from the flame, and in the play of gleams at its foot the mast near by, like a lofty column, ascended in the great darkness. A lot of ropes ran up slanting into a dark void and were lost to sight, but high aloft a brace block gleamed white, the end of a yardarm could be seen suspended in the air and as if glowing with its own light. The sky had clouded over the brig without a breath of wind.

“Give up,” repeated Carter with an uneasy shuffle of feet.

“Nobody,” finished Lingard. “I can’t. It’s as clear as daylight. I can’t! No! Nothing!”

He stared straight out afar, and after looking at him Carter felt moved by a bit of youthful intuition to murmur, “That’s bad,” in a tone that almost in spite of himself hinted at the dawning of a befogged compassion.

He had a sense of confusion within him, the sense of mystery without. He had never experienced anything like it all the time when serving with old Robinson in the *Ly-E-Moon*. And yet he had seen and taken part in some queer doings that were not clear to him at the time. They were secret but they suggested something comprehensible. This affair did not. It had somehow a subtlety that affected him. He was uneasy as if there had been a breath of magic on events and men giving to this complication of a yachting voyage a significance impossible to perceive, but felt in the words, in the gestures, in the events, which made them all strangely, obscurely startling.

He was not one who could keep track of his sensations, and besides he had not the leisure. He had to answer Lingard’s questions about the people of the yacht. No, he couldn’t say Mrs. Travers was what you may call frightened. She seemed to have something in her mind. Oh, yes! The chaps were in a funk. Would they fight? Anybody would fight when driven to it, funk or no funk. That was his experience. Naturally one liked to have something better than a handspike to do it with. Still﻿—In the pause Carter seemed to weigh with composure the chances of men with handspikes.

“What do you want to fight us for?” he asked, suddenly.

Lingard started.

“I don’t,” he said; “I wouldn’t be asking you.”

“There’s no saying what you would do, Captain,” replied Carter; “it isn’t twenty-four hours since you wanted to shoot me.”

===Chinese===

他把话收住了。卡特等着。举火把的人僵立不动，把脸侧开火焰，在火光底部的闪灭里，近旁那根桅杆像一根高柱，升进巨大的黑暗里。许多缆索斜斜地升进黑沉沉的虚空，望不见了，只有高处一只转向滑车闪着白光，看得见一段桅桁悬在半空，仿佛自己发着光。天在无风之中阴云四合，罩住了双桅船。

“撒手。”卡特不安地倒着脚，重复道。

“谁也不。”林加德把话说完。“我不能。这比白昼还清楚。我不能！不！谁也不！”

他直望向远方，卡特看了看他，一股年轻人的直觉油然而动，低低说了声“这就糟了”，那语气几乎不由自主地透出一点迷雾中的怜惘。

他心里一团乱，身外一层谜。跟老鲁滨逊在“夜莺号”上那么些年，他从没经历过这样的滋味。说起来，他见过也参与过一些当时闹不明白的怪事。那些事虽然隐秘，倒还暗示着可以理解的东西。这件事不然。它有一种说不出的微妙，攫住了他。他惶惶不安，仿佛事件和人的身上吹过了一口魔法之气，给这游艇之旅的乱局添上一种没法看清、却感觉得到的意义——在话语里，在举动里，在事件里，使这一切都古怪地、懵懵地骇人。

他不是那种能细细缕清自己感受的人，再说也没有这份闲空。他还得回答林加德关于游艇上的人的问话。不，他说不上特拉弗斯太太是不是吓着了。她像是有心事。哦，那帮人是慌了。他们会打吗？被逼到头，慌不慌都会打。这是他的经验。当然，有比撬棍更好的家伙谁都喜欢。不过——在停顿中，卡特仿佛从容地掂量着拿撬棍的人们的胜算。

“您干吗要跟我们打？”他忽然问。

林加德一怔。

“我不打，”他说；“不然我何必来问你。”

“您会干出什么，可说不准，船长。”卡特答道；“您要毙我还不满二十四小时呢。”

===Original===

“I only said I would, rather than let you go raising trouble for me,” explained Lingard.

“One night isn’t like another,” mumbled Carter, “but how am I to know? It seems to me you are making trouble for yourself as fast as you can.”

“Well, supposing I am,” said Lingard with sudden gloominess. “Would your men fight if I armed them properly?”

“What﻿—for you or for themselves?” asked Carter.

“For the woman,” burst out Lingard. “You forget there’s a woman on board. I don’t care *that* for their carcases.”

Carter pondered conscientiously.

“Not tonight,” he said at last. “There’s one or two good men amongst them, but the rest are struck all of a heap. Not tonight. Give them time to get steady a bit if you want them to fight.”

He gave facts and opinions with a mixture of loyalty and mistrust. His own state puzzled him exceedingly. He couldn’t make out anything, he did not know what to believe and yet he had an impulsive desire, an inspired desire to help the man. At times it appeared a necessity﻿—at others policy; between whiles a great folly, which perhaps did not matter because he suspected himself of being helpless anyway. Then he had moments of anger. In those moments he would feel in his pocket the butt of a loaded pistol. He had provided himself with the weapon, when directed by Mrs. Travers to go on board the brig.

“If he wants to interfere with me, I’ll let drive at him and take my chance of getting away,” he had explained hurriedly.

He remembered how startled Mrs. Travers looked. Of course, a woman like that﻿—not used to hear such talk. Therefore it was no use listening to her, except for good manners’ sake. Once bit twice shy. He had no mind to be kidnapped, not he, nor bullied either.

“I can’t let him nab me, too. You will want me now, Mrs. Travers,” he had said; “and I promise you not to fire off the old thing unless he jolly well forces me to.”

He was youthfully wise in his resolution not to give way to her entreaties, though her extraordinary agitation did stagger him for a moment. When the boat was already on its way to the brig, he remembered her calling out after him:

“You must not! You don’t understand.”

Her voice coming faintly in the darkness moved him, it resembled so much a cry of distress.

“Give way, boys, give way,” he urged his men.

===Chinese===

“我只是说宁可毙了你，也不能放你去给我招祸。”林加德解释道。

“此一时彼一时。”卡特咕哝道，“可我怎么知道？依我看，您正在拼命给自己招祸呢。”

“好吧，就算是这样。”林加德忽然阴郁地说。“要是我把您的人好好武装起来，他们会打吗？”

“什么——为您还是为他们自己？”卡特问。

“为那个女人。”林加德脫口而出。“你忘了船上有个女人。他们那些皮囊，我不屑一顾。”

卡特认认真真地掂量。

“今儿夜里不行。”他终于说。“里头有一两个好汉，其余的全慌成一堆了。今夜不成。您要他们打仗，得给他们点时间缓过神来。”

他一半存着忠心、一半存着戒心地报着情况和看法。他自己的处境让他大为困惑。他什么都摸不透，不知道该信什么，可他又有一股冲动的、近乎天启的愿望要帮这个人。有时这显得必要——有时又显得是权宜；间或又是一场大糊涂，可糊涂也许无所谓，因为他怀疑自己横竖无能为力。有时他又恼。恼的时候就摸摸口袋里上膛手枪的枪柄。特拉弗斯太太派他上双桅船时，他给自己备下了这家伙。

“他要敢拦我，我就照他放，然后碰运气脱身。”他当时急急地解释。

他记得特拉弗斯太太当时多么惊愕。当然了，那样的女人——不习惯听这种话。所以听她的没用，听也只是为了礼貌。一朝被蛇咬，十年怕井绳。他可不想被绑走，不想，也不想受欺负。

“我不能让他把我也囫圇吞了。您这会儿用得着我，特拉弗斯太太。”他当时说；“我答应您，不把他逼到份儿上，绝不放这老古董响。”

他年轻气盛地认定不能拗她的恳求，尽管她那反常的激动让他一时失措。小艇已经往双桅船去了，他还记得她在身后朝他喊：

“您不能！您不明白。”

她的声音在黑暗里微弱地传来，打动了他，太像一声求救的哭喊。

“划呀，伙计们，划呀。”他催着他的部下。

===Original===

He was wise, resolute, and he was also youthful enough to almost wish it should “come to it.” And with foresight he even instructed the boat’s crew to keep the gig just abaft the main rigging of the brig.

“When you see me drop into her all of a sudden, shove off and pull for dear life.”

Somehow just then he was not so anxious for a shot, but he held on with a determined mental grasp to his fine resolution, lest it should slip away from him and perish in a sea of doubts.

“Hadn’t I better get back to the yacht?” he asked, gently.

Getting no answer he went on with deliberation:

“Mrs. Travers ordered me to say that no matter how this came about she is ready to trust you. She is waiting for some kind of answer, I suppose.”

“Ready to trust me,” repeated Lingard. His eyes lit up fiercely.

Every sway of flares tossed slightly to and fro the massy shadows of the main deck, where here and there the figure of a man could be seen standing very still with a dusky face and glittering eyeballs.

Carter stole his hand warily into his breast pocket:

“Well, Captain,” he said. He was not going to be bullied, let the owner’s wife trust whom she liked.

“Have you got anything in writing for me there?” asked Lingard, advancing a pace, exultingly.

Carter, alert, stepped back to keep his distance. Shaw stared from the side; his rubicund cheeks quivered, his round eyes seemed starting out of his head, and his mouth was open as though he had been ready to choke with pent-up curiosity, amazement, and indignation.

“No! Not in writing,” said Carter, steadily and low.

Lingard had the air of being awakened by a shout. A heavy and darkening frown seemed to fall out of the night upon his forehead and swiftly passed into the night again, and when it departed it left him so calm, his glance so lucid, his mien so composed that it was difficult to believe the man’s heart had undergone within the last second the trial of humiliation and of danger. He smiled sadly:

“Well, young man,” he asked with a kind of good-humoured resignation, “what is it you have there? A knife or a pistol?”

===Chinese===

他精明、果断，又年轻气盛，甚至盼着真能“干一场”。他还有先见之明，甚至嘱咐艇员把小艇就停在双桅船主桅索具的正后方。

“你们看见我突然跳进艇里，就立刻撑开，玩命地划。”

不知怎么，那一刻他倒不那么巴望真开一枪了，可他仍用坚定的意志攥住他那漂亮的决心不放，生怕它从手里滑掉，淹死在怀疑的大海里。

“我是不是该回游艇去了？”他轻声问。

见没有回答，他不紧不慢地继续说：

“特拉弗斯太太吩咐我说，不管这事是怎么弄出来的，她都信得过您。我猜她在等个回话。”

“信得过我。”林加德重复道。他的眼睛狂喜地亮了。

火把每一摆动，主甲板上沉重的黑影便轻轻摇晃，影子里东一个西一个站着人影，一动不动，暗色的脸，闪亮的眼睛。

卡特小心地把手伸进胸袋：

“那么，船长，”他说。船主的太太爱信谁信谁，他是吃软不吃硬的。

“你那儿有写给我的什么字据吗？”林加德跨前一步，喜形于色地问。

卡特机警地退了一步，拉开距离。肖在旁边瞪着眼看；红扑扑的脸颊直哆嗦，圆眼睛像要从脑壳里瞪出来，嘴张着，仿佛憋了一肚子好奇、惊愕和义愤，眼看要噎住。

“没有！没有什么字据。”卡特平稳而低声地说。

林加德像被一声呼喊惊醒。一道浓黑的怒容仿佛从夜里落到他额头上，又倏地回到夜里去；它退去之后，他那样镇静，目光那样清明，神态那样安详，简直难以相信这个人的心在刚才一秒钟里经受了屈辱与危险的双重拷问。他戚然一笑：

“好吧，年轻人，”他带着一种好性气的听天由命问道，“你那儿揣的是什么？刀子还是手枪？”

===Original===

“A pistol,” said Carter. “Are you surprised, Captain?” He spoke with heat because a sense of regret was stealing slowly within him, as stealthily, as irresistibly as the flowing tide. “Who began these tricks?” He withdrew his hand, empty, and raised his voice. “You are up to something I can’t make out. You﻿—you are not straight.”

The flares held on high streamed right up without swaying, and in that instant of profound calm the shadows on the brig’s deck became as still as the men.

“You think not?” said Lingard, thoughtfully.

Carter nodded. He resented the turn of the incident and the growing impulse to surrender to that man.

“Mrs. Travers trusts me though,” went on Lingard with gentle triumph as if advancing an unanswerable argument.

“So she says,” grunted Carter; “I warned her. She’s a baby. They’re all as innocent as babies there. And you know it. And I know it. I’ve heard of your kind. You would dump the lot of us overboard if it served your turn. That’s what I think.”

“And that’s all.”

Carter nodded slightly and looked away. There was a silence. Lingard’s eyes travelled over the brig. The lighted part of the vessel appeared in bright and wavering detail walled and canopied by the night. He felt a light breath on his face. The air was stirring, but the Shallows, silent and lost in the darkness, gave no sound of life.

This stillness oppressed Lingard. The world of his endeavours and his hopes seemed dead, seemed gone. His desire existed homeless in the obscurity that had devoured his corner of the sea, this stretch of the coast, his certitude of success. And here in the midst of what was the domain of his adventurous soul there was a lost youngster ready to shoot him on suspicion of some extravagant treachery. Came ready to shoot! That’s good, too! He was too weary to laugh﻿—and perhaps too sad. Also the danger of the pistol-shot, which he believed real﻿—the young are rash﻿—irritated him. The night and the spot were full of contradictions. It was impossible to say who in this shadowy warfare was to be an enemy, and who were the allies. So close were the contacts issuing from this complication of a yachting voyage, that he seemed to have them all within his breast.

“Shoot me! He is quite up to that trick﻿—damn him. Yet I would trust him sooner than any man in that yacht.”

===Chinese===

“手枪。”卡特说。“意外吗，船长？”他说得动了火气，因为一种悔意正像涨潮一样，悄悄地、不可抗拒地漫进他心里。“这些花招是谁先耍的？”他抽回空着的手，提高了嗓门。“您在图谋什么，我摸不透。您——您不地道。”

高举的火把笔直地烧着，纹丝不摇；在那深入骨髓的静里，双桅船甲板上的影子跟人一样凝固了。

“你不觉得吗？”林加德若有所思地说。

卡特点点头。他对事态的转向感到恼怒，也恼怒自己那越来越强的、想向这个人投降的冲动。

“可特拉弗斯太太信得过我。”林加德接着说，带着温柔的得意，仿佛摆出一条无可辩驳的理由。

“她嘴上这么说。”卡特哼道；“我提醒过她。她是个娃娃。那船上的人个个天真得像娃娃。这您知道。我也知道。您这种人我听说过。只要合您的意，您会把我们整船人都下了海。我就是这么想的。”

“就这么多。”

卡特微微点头，把目光移开。一阵沉默。林加德的目光扫过全船。船被照亮的部分在明灭的细节里显现，四壁和顶棚都是夜。他感到脸上拂过一缕轻风。空气在动，可浅滩沉默着，迷失在黑暗里，没有一点生息。

这份沉寂压着林加德。他的奋斗与希望的世界仿佛死了，仿佛去了。他的欲望无家可归，悬在那吞掉了他的海之一隅、这段海岸、他必成的把握的晦暗里。而在这冒险灵魂的领地中央，站着一个打定主意、一疑心他叛逆就要开枪的毛头小子。来的时候就是准备开枪的！这也有意思！他累得笑不动了——也许也太伤心。再者，那一枪的危险——他相信是真的——年轻人手黑——也让他恼火。这夜、这地方处处是矛盾。这场影子战争里，谁做敌人、谁做盟友，说不清。这场游艇之旅的乱局牵出的瓜葛如此贴近，他仿佛把所有人都揣在了自己胸腔里。

“崩了我！这把戏他真干得出——去他的。可游艇上随便哪个，我都更早信他。”

===Original===

Such were his thoughts while he looked at Carter, who was biting his lips, in the vexation of the long silence. When they spoke again to each other they talked soberly, with a sense of relief, as if they had come into cool air from an overheated room and when Carter, dismissed, went into his boat, he had practically agreed to the line of action traced by Lingard for the crew of the yacht. He had agreed as if in implicit confidence. It was one of the absurdities of the situation which had to be accepted and could never be understood.

“Do I talk straight now?” had asked Lingard.

“It seems straight enough,” assented Carter with an air of reserve; “I will work with you so far anyhow.”

“Mrs. Travers trusts me,” remarked Lingard again.

“By the Lord Harry!” cried Carter, giving way suddenly to some latent conviction. “I was warning her against you. Say, Captain, you are a devil of a man. How did you manage it?”

“I trusted her,” said Lingard.

“Did you?” cried the amazed Carter. “When? How? Where﻿—”

“You know too much already,” retorted Lingard, quietly. “Waste no time. I will be after you.”

Carter whistled low.

“There’s a pair of you I can’t make out,” he called back, hurrying over the side.

===Chinese===

他一边这样想着，一边望着咬着嘴唇、被这长久的沉默弄得懊恼的卡特。两人再开口时，谈得心平气和，带着一种如释重负之感，仿佛从闷热的屋子里走进了凉快的空气里；等被打发走的卡特下到小艇里去时，他实际上已同意了林加德为游艇船员拟定的行动路线。他同意得像是以性命相托。这是局面里那些只能接受、永远无法理解的荒唐处之一。

“我这回说话算数吧？”林加德曾这样问。

“看着够数了。”卡特有所保留地表示同意；“反正我照这个跟您干。”

“特拉弗斯太太信得过我。”林加德又说了一遍。

“老天爷作证！”卡特忽然向某种压在心底的确信屈服了，喊道。“我方才还在她跟前说您的坏话呢。哎，船长，您可真是个魔鬼一样的人物。您是怎么做到的？”

“我信了她。”林加德说。

“是吗？”惊愕的卡特喊道。“什么时候？怎么信的？在哪儿——”

“你知道的已经太多了。”林加德平静地回敬。“别耽搁。我随后就来。”

卡特低低吹了声口哨。

“你们一兑一，我都摸不透。”他一边匆匆翻过船舷一边回头喊。

===Original===

Shaw took this opportunity to approach. Beginning with hesitation: “A word with you, sir,” the mate went on to say he was a respectable man. He delivered himself in a ringing, unsteady voice. He was married, he had children, he abhorred illegality. The light played about his obese figure, he had flung his mushroom hat on the deck, he was not afraid to speak the truth. The grey moustache stood out aggressively, his glances were uneasy; he pressed his hands to his stomach convulsively, opened his thick, short arms wide, wished it to be understood he had been chief-officer of home ships, with a spotless character and he hoped “quite up to his work.” He was a peaceable man, none more; disposed to stretch a point when it “came to a difference with niggers of some kind﻿—they had to be taught manners and reason” and he was not averse at a pinch to﻿—but here were white people﻿—gentlemen, ladies, not to speak of the crew. He had never spoken to a superior like this before, and this was prudence, his conviction, a point of view, a point of principle, a conscious superiority and a burst of resentment hoarded through years against all the successive and unsatisfactory captains of his existence. There never had been such an opportunity to show he could not be put upon. He had one of them on a string and he was going to lead him a dance. There was courage, too, in it, since he believed himself fallen unawares into the clutches of a particularly desperate man and beyond the reach of law.

A certain small amount of calculation entered the audacity of his remonstrance. Perhaps﻿—it flashed upon him﻿—the yacht’s gentry will hear I stood up for them. This could conceivably be of advantage to a man who wanted a lift in the world. “Owner of a yacht﻿—badly scared﻿—a gentleman﻿—money nothing to him.” Thereupon Shaw declared with heat that he couldn’t be an accessory either after or before the fact. Those that never went home﻿—who had nothing to go to perhaps﻿—he interjected, hurriedly, could do as they liked. He couldn’t. He had a wife, a family, a little house﻿—paid for﻿—with difficulty. He followed the sea respectably out and home, all regular, not vagabonding here and there, chumming with the first nigger that came along and laying traps for his betters.

One of the two flare bearers sighed at his elbow, and shifted his weight to the other foot.

===Chinese===

肖趁这个机会凑上前来。他先是嗫嚅着“跟您说句话，先生”，接着便说自己是个体面人。他用响亮而发颤的嗓音申诉着。他成了家，有儿女，他痛恨无法无天。火光在他臃肿的身躯四周晃动，他把蘑菇帽捽在甲板上，他不害怕说真话。灰白的小胡子好斗地橛着，眼神不安；他抽搐地把双手按在肚子上，又把粗短的双臂大张开，希望人家明白，他做过本国航线船的大副，品行无懈可击，而且自信“完全胜任”。他是个和气人，和气到头了；要是“跟黑鬼之类起了冲突——那得教教他们规矩和道理”——他也不介意通融一回，真到万不得已他也不反对——可这回是白人——是绅士、太太，更别说还有船员。他从前从没这样跟上司说过话，而这是谨慎，是他的信念，是一个立场，一条原则，一种自觉的优越，也是多年积攒的一腔怨气——冲着他一生中接连不断、样样不遂意的各位船长。从来没有过这样的机会，可以表明他不是好欺负的。这回他钓住了一个，他要牵着好好遛一遛。这里面也有胆量，因为他相信自己在不知不觉间落进了一个格外亡命之徒的魔掌，落在了法律够不着的地方。

他那抗议的胆气里，也掺着一点小小的算计。也许——他心头一亮——游艇上那班老爷会听说我替他们说了话。这对一个想往上爬的人，未必没有好处。“游艇船主——吓得半死——一位绅士——钱不算回事。”于是肖热烈地声明，他无论如何不做从犯，事前事后都不做。那些从不回国的人——多半也没家可回——他急忙补一句——爱怎么着怎么去。他不行。他有老婆，有一家子，有一栋小房子——好不容易——供清了。他跑船跑得体面，出航回航，一切照章，不是在这儿那儿瞎逛，见个黑鬼就称兄道弟，还给自己的上等人下套子。

他肘边一个举火把的人叹了口气，把重心挪到另一只脚上。

===Original===

These two had been keeping so perfectly still that the movement was as startling as if a statue had changed its pose. After looking at the offender with cold malevolence, Shaw went on to speak of law-courts, of trials, and of the liberty of the subject; then he pointed out the certitude and the inconvenience of being found out, affecting for the moment the dispassionateness of wisdom.

“There will be fifteen years in gaol at the end of this job for everybody,” said Shaw, “and I have a boy that don’t know his father yet. Fine things for him to learn when he grows up. The innocent are dead certain here to catch it along with you. The missus will break her heart unless she starves first. Home sold up.”

He saw a mysterious iniquity in a dangerous relation to himself and began to lose his head. What he really wanted was to have his existence left intact, for his own cherishing and pride. It was a moral aspiration, but in his alarm the native grossness of his nature came clattering out like a devil out of a trap. He would blow the gaff, split, give away the whole show, he would back up honest people, kiss the book, say what he thought, let all the world know﻿ ﻿… and when he paused to draw breath, all around him was silent and still. Before the impetus of that respectable passion his words were scattered like chaff driven by a gale and rushed headlong into the night of the Shallows. And in the great obscurity, imperturbable, it heard him say he “washed his hands of everything.”

“And the brig?” asked Lingard, suddenly.

Shaw was checked. For a second the seaman in him instinctively admitted the claim of the ship.

“The brig. The brig. She’s right enough,” he mumbled. He had nothing to say against the brig﻿—not he. She wasn’t like the big ships he was used to, but of her kind the best craft he ever.﻿ ﻿… And with a brusque return upon himself, he protested that he had been decoyed on board under false pretences. It was as bad as being shanghaied when in liquor. It was﻿—upon his soul. And into a craft next thing to a pirate! That was the name for it or his own name was not Shaw. He said this glaring owlishly. Lingard, perfectly still and mute, bore the blows without a sign.

===Chinese===

这两个方才站得那样纹丝不动，这一动竟像雕像换了姿势一样吓人。肖朝那冒犯者投去冷酷的恶毒一瞥，接着讲起法院、审判、臣民的自由；又指出早晚东窗事发的必然与不便，一时装出一副智者不动声色的派头。

“这营生到了头，人人得蹲十五年大牢，”肖说，“我有个儿子还没见过他爹呢。等他长大，让他学这个？清白人在这里保准跟您一起遭殃。老婆子不当场饿死，也得心碎而死。家也得变卖抵债。”

他在一场与自己安危相关的神秘罪案里越看越怕，开始失态。他真正想要的，是让他这个人原封不动地留着，供自己珍重、自傲。这是一番道德的抱负，可一慌，他天性里原始的粗鄙便像掉进陷阱的魔鬼一样哐哐地蹦了出来。他要揭盖子，要撂挑子，要把整个局子抖搂出去，他要站在老实人一边，吻着《圣经》起誓，想什么说什么，让全世界都知道……他停下来换气的当儿，四下里一片死寂。在那股体面的激情的冲势面前，他的话像大风卷走的糠粃，四散着冲进浅滩的夜色。而那浩大的黑暗不为所动，听他说他“跟这一切洗手不干”。

“那双桅船呢？”林加德忽然问。

肖被问住了。有一秒钟，他身上的海员本能承认了船的这份权利。

“双桅船。双桅船。她没说的。”他咕哝道。他对这条船挑不出毛病——他才不挑呢。她不比他跑惯的大船，可在她那一行里，是他见过最好的船……接着他又猛地回到自家立场上，抗议说自己是被人连哄带骗弄上船的，跟灌醉了被绑架上船差不多。真的——凭良心说。而且是上了一条跟海盗船差不多的船！就这么叫它没错，不然我就不姓肖。他说这话时圆睁着两眼。林加德纹丝不动、一言不发，把这些打击一下下受着，不露一点声色。

===Original===

The silly fuss of that man seared his very soul. There was no end to this plague of fools coming to him from the forgotten ends of the earth. A fellow like that could not be told. No one could be told. Blind they came and blind they would go out. He admitted reluctantly, but without doubt, that as if pushed by a force from outside he would have to try and save two of them. To this end he foresaw the probable need of leaving his brig for a time. He would have to leave her with that man. The mate. He had engaged him himself﻿—to make his insurance valid﻿—to be able sometimes to speak﻿—to have near him. Who would have believed such a fool-man could exist on the face of the sea! Who? Leave the brig with him. The brig!

Ever since sunset, the breeze kept off by the heat of the day had been trying to reestablish in the darkness its sway over the Shoals. Its approaches had been heard in the night, its patient murmurs, its foiled sighs; but now a surprisingly heavy puff came in a free rush as if, far away there to the northward, the last defence of the calm had been victoriously carried. The flames borne down streamed bluishly, horizontal and noisy at the end of tall sticks, like fluttering pennants; and behold, the shadows on the deck went mad and jostled each other as if trying to escape from a doomed craft, the darkness, held up dome-like by the brilliant glare, seemed to tumble headlong upon the brig in an overwhelming downfall, the men stood swaying as if ready to fall under the ruins of a black and noiseless disaster. The blurred outlines of the brig, the masts, the rigging, seemed to shudder in the terror of coming extinction﻿—and then the darkness leaped upward again, the shadows returned to their places, the men were seen distinct, swarthy, with calm faces, with glittering eyeballs. The destruction in the breath had passed, was gone.

A discord of three voices raised together in a drawling wail trailed on the sudden immobility of the air.

“Brig ahoy! Give us a rope!”

===Chinese===

这个人的蠢闹灼着他的灵魂。这从地上被遗忘的角落朝他涌来的瘟疫般的蠢人，没完没了。跟那样的人没法讲。跟谁都没法讲。他们瞎着来，也将瞎着去。他不情愿、却毫不怀疑地承认：仿佛被一股外力推着，他将不得不设法救下他们中间的两个。为此他预见到，很可能需要把双桅船撂下一阵。撂给他——那个大副。大副是他自己雇的——为了保单有效——为了有时候能说说话——为了身边有个人。谁想得到海面上竟有这种蠢人！谁？把船撂给他。这条船！
