He smiled at his white friend. There was something subtle in the smile and afterward an added firmness in the repose of the lips. Immada moved a step forward. She looked at Lingard with terror in her black and dilated eyes. She exclaimed in a voice whose vibration startled the hearts of all the hearers with an indefinable sense of alarm, “He will perish, Hassim! He will perish alone!”

“No,” said Hassim. “Thy fear is as vain tonight as it was at sunrise. He shall not perish alone.”

Her eyelids dropped slowly. From her veiled eyes the tears fell, vanishing in the silence. Lingard’s forehead became furrowed by folds that seemed to contain an infinity of sombre thoughts. “Remember, O Hassim, that when I promised you to take you back to your country you promised me to be a friend to all white men. A friend to all whites who are of my people, forever.”

“My memory is good, O Tuan,” said Hassim; “I am not yet back in my country, but is not everyone the ruler of his own heart? Promises made by a man of noble birth live as long as the speaker endures.”

===Chinese===

“我倒是巴不得把这一切看个透亮。”卡特又咕哝道。“您要离开我们多久，船长？”林加德没有回答。卡特等了一会儿。“来嘛，先生。”他催道。“我总得有个数。多少？两三天？”林加德一怔。

“天。”他重复道。“啊，天。你想知道什么？两……三……那老头怎么说的——说不定是一辈子。”最后几个字说得极低，只有卡特听见。——“您当真？”他低声问。林加德点头。——“能等多久等多久——然后走。”他用同样微弱的声音说。“去哪儿？”——“随便哪儿，最近的港口，哪个港口都行。”——“好。这话总算明白。”年轻人带着宠辱不惊的好脾气评了一句。

“我走了，哈西姆啊！”林加德开口，那位马来人缓缓颔首，直到林加德说完才重新抬起头。林加德用几句话干脆利落地把自己的打算告诉了他：只身促成俘虏的获释。他既不惊讶，也无别的神情。等林加德说到最后一句：“到了为难的时候，你得想法子帮我，哈西姆拉者啊。”他才抬头说：

“好。你以前从没求过我什么。”

他朝他的白人朋友一笑。那笑里有种说不出的微妙，笑过之后，唇角的安详又添了几分坚定。伊玛达向前迈了一步。她望着林加德，乌黑的双眼瞪得大大的，满是恐惧。她喊出的那句话，震得所有听的人心里生出一阵莫名的惊悸：“他会死的，哈西姆！他会孤零零地死的！”

“不会。”哈西姆说。“你的恐惧今夜跟日出时一样是空的。他不会孤零零地死。”

她的眼帘缓缓垂下。泪水从那遮蔽的眼睛里落下来，消失在寂静里。林加德的额头蹙起一道道深纹，仿佛盛着无穷的阴郁思绪。“记住，哈西姆啊，我答应送你回国的时候，你也答应过我，做一切白人的朋友。做我族人的朋友，永远。”

“我的记性很好，老爷。”哈西姆说；“我虽还没回到我的国家，可谁不是自己心灵的主宰？尊贵之人许下的诺言，许诺的人在，诺言就在。”

===Original===

“Goodbye,” said Lingard to Mrs. Travers. “You will be safe here.” He looked all around the cabin. “I leave you,” he began again and stopped short. Mrs. Travers’ hand, resting lightly on the edge of the table, began to tremble. “It’s for you﻿ ﻿… Yes. For you alone﻿ ﻿… and it seems it can’t be.﻿ ﻿…”

It seemed to him that he was saying goodbye to all the world, that he was taking a last leave of his own self. Mrs. Travers did not say a word, but Immada threw herself between them and cried:

“You are a cruel woman! You are driving him away from where his strength is. You put madness into his heart, O! Blind﻿—without pity﻿—without shame!﻿ ﻿…”

“Immada,” said Hassim’s calm voice. Nobody moved.

“What did she say to me?” faltered Mrs. Travers and again repeated in a voice that sounded hard, “What did she say?”

“Forgive her,” said Lingard. “Her fears are for me﻿ ﻿…”﻿—“It’s about your going?” Mrs. Travers interrupted, swiftly.

“Yes, it is﻿—and you must forgive her.” He had turned away his eyes with something that resembled embarrassment but suddenly he was assailed by an irresistible longing to look again at that woman. At the moment of parting he clung to her with his glance as a man holds with his hands a priceless and disputed possession. The faint blush that overspread gradually Mrs. Travers’ features gave her face an air of extraordinary and startling animation.

===Chinese===

“再会。”林加德对特拉弗斯太太说。“你在这儿安全。”他环视了一遍餐舱。“我把你留下——”他重新开口，又猛地收住。特拉弗斯太太轻轻搭在桌沿的手开始发抖。“都是为了你……是。只为你一个人……可看来不行了。……”

他觉得自己是在跟整个世界道别，是跟自己作最后的诀别。特拉弗斯太太一言不发，伊玛达却扑到两人中间，喊道：

“你这个狠心的女人！你把他从他力量的所在赶走。你把疯狂灌进他心里，哦！瞎子——没有怜悯——不知羞耻！……”

“伊玛达。”哈西姆的声音平静地说。谁也没动。

“她对我说了什么？”特拉弗斯太太发着抖问，又用一种发硬的声音重复：“她说什么了？”

“原谅她。”林加德说。“她是为我害怕……”——“是为了您要走的事？”特拉弗斯太太疾声打断。

“是，正是——你得原谅她。”他把眼睛别开了，带着近乎尴尬的神气，可忽然又被一股不可抗拒的渴望攫住，想再看那个女人一眼。诀别之际，他用目光攀住她，像人用双手攥着一件无价的、被人争夺的珍宝。那渐渐漫上特拉弗斯太太面庞的淡淡红晕，给她的脸添了一种异样而惊人的生动。

===Original===

“The danger you run?” she asked, eagerly. He repelled the suggestion by a slighting gesture of the hand.﻿—“Nothing worth looking at twice. Don’t give it a thought,” he said. “I’ve been in tighter places.” He clapped his hands and waited till he heard the cabin door open behind his back. “Steward, my pistols.” The mulatto in slippers, aproned to the chin, glided through the cabin with unseeing eyes as though for him no one there had existed.﻿ ﻿… —“Is it my heart that aches so?” Mrs. Travers asked herself, contemplating Lingard’s motionless figure. “How long will this sensation of dull pain last? Will it last forever.﻿ ﻿…”﻿—“How many changes of clothes shall I put up, sir?” asked the steward, while Lingard took the pistols from him and eased the hammers after putting on fresh caps.﻿—“I will take nothing this time, steward.” He received in turn from the mulatto’s hands a red silk handkerchief, a pocket book, a cigar-case. He knotted the handkerchief loosely round his throat; it was evident he was going through the routine of every departure for the shore; he even opened the cigar-case to see whether it had been filled.﻿—“Hat, sir,” murmured the half-caste. Lingard flung it on his head.﻿—“Take your orders from this lady, steward﻿—till I come back. The cabin is hers﻿—do you hear?” He sighed ready to go and seemed unable to lift a foot.﻿—“I am coming with you,” declared Mrs. Travers suddenly in a tone of unalterable decision. He did not look at her; he did not even look up; he said nothing, till after Carter had cried: “You can’t, Mrs. Travers!”﻿—when without budging he whispered to himself:﻿—“Of course.” Mrs. Travers had pulled already the hood of her cloak over her head and her face within the dark cloth had turned an intense and unearthly white, in which the violet of her eyes appeared unfathomably mysterious. Carter started forward.﻿—“You don’t know this man,” he almost shouted.

===Chinese===

“您要冒的险呢？”她急切地问。他用手轻蔑地一摆，挥开了这层意思。——“不值当看第二眼。想都别想。”他说。“比这凶险的地方我经过多了。”他拍了拍手，直听到身后舱门打开。“服务员，我的手枪。”那穿拖鞋、围裙系到下巴的混血儿，两眼视而不见地穿过餐舱，仿佛舱里没有一个人存在。……——“难道是我的心这么疼？”特拉弗斯太太凝视着林加德一动不动的身影，自问。“这阵隐隐的钝痛要持续多久？会不会持续到永远。……”——“要给您收拾几套换洗衣裳，先生？”服务员问，林加德一面从他手里接手枪，一面给撞针重新装上铜帽、扳开击锤。——“这回什么都不带，服务员。”他又依次从混血儿手里接过一条红绸手帕、一个记事本、一只雪茄匣。他把手帕松松地在脖子上打了个结；显然，他在走每回上岸的老程序；他甚至还打开雪茄匣，看看装满了没有。——“帽子，先生。”混血儿低声道。林加德把它扣到头上。——“听这位太太的吩咐，服务员——等我回来。舱是她的——听见没有？”他叹了口气，像要走了，却抬不动脚。——“我跟您一起去。”特拉弗斯太太忽然宣布，语气斩钉截铁。他不看她；他连头都不抬；他一言不发，直到卡特喊出：“您不能去，特拉弗斯太太！”——他才纹丝不动地对自己低语：——“当然。”特拉弗斯太太已经把斗篷的兜帽拉上头顶，黑布料里的那张脸白得出奇、白得不似人间，衬得那双紫罗兰色的眼睛深不可测、神秘莫测。卡特跨步上前。——“您不了解这个人。”他几乎是喊出来的。

===Original===

“I do know him,” she said, and before the reproachfully unbelieving attitude of the other she added, speaking slowly and with emphasis: “There is not, I verily believe, a single thought or act of his life that I don’t know.”﻿—“It’s true﻿—it’s true,” muttered Lingard to himself. Carter threw up his arms with a groan. “Stand back,” said a voice that sounded to him like a growl of thunder, and he felt a grip on his hand which seemed to crush every bone. He jerked it away.﻿—“Mrs. Travers! stay,” he cried. They had vanished through the open door and the sound of their footsteps had already died away. Carter turned about bewildered as if looking for help.﻿—“Who is he, steward? Who in the name of all the mad devils is he?” he asked, wildly. He was confounded by the cold and philosophical tone of the answer:﻿—“ ’Tain’t my place to trouble about that, sir﻿—nor yours I guess.”﻿—“Isn’t it!” shouted Carter. “Why, he has carried the lady off.” The steward was looking critically at the lamp and after a while screwed the light down.﻿—“That’s better,” he mumbled.﻿—“Good God! What is a fellow to do?” continued Carter, looking at Hassim and Immada who were whispering together and gave him only an absent glance. He rushed on deck and was struck blind instantly by the night that seemed to have been lying in wait for him; he stumbled over something soft, kicked something hard, flung himself on the rail. “Come back,” he cried. “Come back. Captain! Mrs. Travers!﻿—or let me come, too.”

He listened. The breeze blew cool against his cheek. A black bandage seemed to lie over his eyes. “Gone,” he groaned, utterly crushed. And suddenly he heard Mrs. Travers’ voice remote in the depths of the night.﻿—“Defend the brig,” it said, and these words, pronouncing themselves in the immensity of a lightless universe, thrilled every fibre of his body by the commanding sadness of their tone. “Defend, defend the brig.”﻿ ﻿… “I am damned if I do,” shouted Carter in despair. “Unless you come back!﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Travers!”

“… as though﻿—I were﻿—on board﻿—myself,” went on the rising cadence of the voice, more distant now, a marvel of faint and imperious clearness.

===Chinese===

“我了解他。”她说，面对对方那副责备而不信的姿态，她又缓缓地、一字一顿地补上一句：“他一生中的每一个念头、每一个举动，我相信没有一样是我不知道的。”——“是实话——是实话。”林加德自言自语地咕哝。卡特呻吟一声，扬起双臂。“让开。”一个在他听来如雷吼般的声音说，他觉得手上被一攥，攥得每一根骨头都要碎了他猛地抽回手。——“特拉弗斯太太！留下。”他喊。两人已从敞开的门里消失，脚步声也已远去。卡特茫然地四下张望，像在找帮手。——“他是谁，服务员？他到底是何方神圣，这疯魔鬼？”他发急地问。回答的冷淡和达观把他懵住了：——“这不关我的事，先生——也不关您的事，我猜。”——“不关！”卡特喊。“他可是把太太拐走了。”服务员正挑剔地端详着灯，过了一会儿把火苗拧小了。“这样好些。”他咕哝道。——“老天！这叫人怎么办？”卡特接着说，望着正交头接耳、只给他一个心不在焉的眼色的哈西姆和伊玛达。他冲上甲板，立刻被仿佛埋伏在那里的夜撞了个眼前漆黑；他绊上什么软东西，踢上什么硬东西，一头扑到栏杆上。“回来。”他喊。“回来。船长！特拉弗斯太太！——要么让我也去。”

他听着。凉风拂上脸颊。一条黑布蒙着他的眼睛。“走了。”他呻吟道，整个人垮了。忽然他听见特拉弗斯太太的嗓音，在夜的深处远远传来。——“守住双桅船。”那声音说，这几个字在无光的宇宙的浩淼里自行宣告着，以它命令般的哀愁颤动了他全身的每一根纤维。“守住，守住双桅船。”……“偏不，见鬼。”卡特绝望地大喊。“除非你们回来！……特拉弗斯太太！”

“……就像——我本人——在船上——一样。”那声音升起的调子继续着，此刻更远了，微弱而威严的清晰，一个奇迹。

===Original===

Carter shouted no more; he tried to make out the boat for a time, and when, giving it up, he leaped down from the rail, the heavy obscurity of the brig’s main deck was agitated like a sombre pool by his jump, swayed, eddied, seemed to break up. Blotches of darkness recoiled, drifted away, bare feet shuffled hastily, confused murmurs died out. “Lascars,” he muttered. “The crew is all agog.” Afterward he listened for a moment to the faintly tumultuous snores of the white men sleeping in rows, with their heads under the break of the poop. Somewhere about his feet, the yacht’s black dog, invisible, and chained to a deck-ringbolt, whined, rattled the thin links, pattered with his claws in his distress at the unfamiliar surroundings, begging for the charity of human notice. Carter stooped impulsively, and was met by a startling lick in the face.﻿—“Hallo, boy!” He thumped the thick curly sides, stroked the smooth head﻿—“Good boy, Rover. Down. Lie down, dog. You don’t know what to make of it﻿—do you, boy?” The dog became still as death. “Well, neither do I,” muttered Carter. But such natures are helped by a cheerful contempt for the intricate and endless suggestions of thought. He told himself that he would soon see what was to come of it, and dismissed all speculation. Had he been a little older he would have felt that the situation was beyond his grasp; but he was too young to see it whole and in a manner detached from himself. All these inexplicable events filled him with deep concern﻿—but then on the other hand he had the key of the magazine and he could not find it in his heart to dislike Lingard. He was positive about this at last, and to know that much after the discomfort of an inward conflict went a long way toward a solution. When he followed Shaw into the cabin he could not repress a sense of enjoyment or hide a faint and malicious smile.

===Chinese===

卡特不再喊了；他辨认那条小艇辨了一会儿，等终于放弃、从栏杆上跳下来时，双桅船主甲板上沉重的黑暗被他的纵身一跳搅得像一泓黑潭，摇晃、回旋，仿佛要碎开。一团团黑影缩退、飘散，光脚板匆勿窸窣，含混的低语渐渐消逝。“印度水手。”他咕哝。“船员全骚动了。”随后，他又听了一会儿那成排睡在艉楼台阶下、脑袋藏成一排的白人们微微吵闹的鼾声。就在他脚边什么地方，游艇那条黑狗——看不见，拴在甲板系环上——呜咽着，磕碰着细链环，爪子在生疏的环境里急得直挠，乞求人来发一点善心。卡特冲动地弯下腰，脸上猛地挨了一记惊喜的舔。——“喂，小子！”他捶着那厚实的卷毛身侧，抚着那光溜的脑袋——“好小子，罗弗。趴下。躺着，狗。你也闹不明白这是怎么回事——对吧，小子？”那狗静得像死了一样。“唉，我也不明白。”卡特咕哝道。可这种天性，自有一样本事帮忙：对思绪那纠缰不清、没完没了的暗示，来个痛快的蔑视。他告诉自己，很快就会看到这一切的下场，便把所有揣测丢开了。他要是年长几岁，就会觉得这局面远非他所能把握；可他太年轻，看不清全局，也没法把它跟自己分开看。这一连串不可解的事件让他忧心忡忡——可另一面，他手里攥着火药舱的钥匙，心里又实在恨不起林加德来。这一点他终于拿定了，经过一场内心交战的煎熬之后，弄明白这么一点，离解决也就不远了。他跟着肖走进餐舱时，按捺不住一份快意，也藏不住一丝淡淡的、促狭的笑。

===Original===

“Gone away﻿—did you say? And carried off the lady with him?” discoursed Shaw very loud in the doorway. “Did he? Well, I am not surprised. What can you expect from a man like that, who leaves his ship in an open roadstead without﻿—I won’t say orders﻿—but without as much as a single word to his next in command? And at night at that! That just shows you the kind of man. Is this the way to treat a chief mate? I apprehend he was riled at the little al‑ter‑cation we had just before you came on board. I told him a truth or two﻿—but﻿—never mind. There’s the law and that’s enough for me. I am captain as long as he is out of the ship, and if his address before very long is not in one of Her Majesty’s jails or other I au‑tho‑rize you to call me a Dutchman. You mark my words.”

He walked in masterfully, sat down and surveyed the cabin in a leisurely and autocratic manner; but suddenly his eyes became stony with amazement and indignation; he pointed a fat and trembling forefinger.

“Niggers,” he said, huskily. “In the cuddy! In the cuddy!” He appeared bereft of speech for a time.

===Chinese===

“走了——您是说？还把太太一块儿带走了？”肖在门口大声发表演说。“是吗？哼，我一点都不奇怪。那种人还能指望什么，把自己的船撂在一个无遮无拦的锚地里，也不说命令吧——连一句交代都没有，就撂给了自己的副手！还赶在夜里！这就看出他是什么人了。这是对待大副的样子吗？我猜他是恼了，恼咱们在他上船前那点小小的争——论。我跟他说了几句真话——不过——不提了。有王法在，我就够了。他不在船上的这阵子，我就是船长，要是不出几天他的地址不进女王陛下的哪座监狱，你们尽管管我叫荷兰人。记住我的话。”

他大摇大摆走进来坐下，以一副从容而专断的派头环视餐舱；可忽然他的眼睛因惊愕和义愤僵成了两颗石子；他伸出一只肥胖发抖的食指。

“黑鬼。”他哑着嗓子说。“在餐舱里！餐舱里！”他有一阵子像是说不出话来了。

===Original===

Since he entered the cabin Hassim had been watching him in thoughtful and expectant silence. “I can’t have it,” he continued with genuine feeling in his voice. “Damme! I’ve too much respect for myself.” He rose with heavy deliberation; his eyes bulged out in a severe and dignified stare. “Out you go!” he bellowed; suddenly, making a step forward.﻿—“Great Scott! What are you up to, mister?” asked in a tone of dispassionate surprise the steward whose head appeared in the doorway. “These are the Captain’s friends.” “Show me a man’s friends and﻿ ﻿…” began Shaw, dogmatically, but abruptly passed into the tone of admonition. “You take your mug out of the way, bottle-washer. They ain’t friends of mine. I ain’t a vagabond. I know what’s due to myself. Quit!” he hissed, fiercely. Hassim, with an alert movement, grasped the handle of his kris. Shaw puffed out his cheeks and frowned.﻿—“Look out! He will stick you like a prize pig,” murmured Carter without moving a muscle. Shaw looked round helplessly.﻿—“And you would enjoy the fun﻿—wouldn’t you?” he said with slow bitterness. Carter’s distant noncommittal smile quite overwhelmed him by its horrid frigidity. Extreme despondency replaced the proper feeling of racial pride in the primitive soul of the mate. “My God! What luck! What have I done to fall amongst that lot?” he groaned, sat down, and took his big grey head in his hands. Carter drew aside to make room for Immada, who, in obedience to a whisper from her brother, sought to leave the cabin. She passed out after an instant of hesitation, during which she looked up at Carter once. Her brother, motionless in a defensive attitude, protected her retreat. She disappeared; Hassim’s grip on his weapon relaxed; he looked in turn at every object in the cabin as if to fix its position in his mind forever, and following his sister, walked out with noiseless footfalls.

===Chinese===

肖一进舱，哈西姆就在深思而戒备的沉默中注视着他。“这我忍不了。”他带着真情实感继续说。“见鬼！我还尊重我自己呢。”他沉重而郑重地站起来；两眼暴突，严厉而威严地瞪着。“滚出去！”他吼道，忽然向前一步。——“天哪！您要干什么，先生？”服务员在门口露出头，用淡漠而惊讶的口气问。“这几位是船长的朋友。”“把一个人的朋友亮给我看，就……”肖武断地开腔，却又陡然转成教训的口气。“把你那张脸挪开，刷瓶子的。他们不是我的朋友。我不是流氓。我知道自己的身份。滚！”他狠狠地嘶声说。哈西姆眼疾手快，握住了克里斯短剑的柄。肖鼓起腮帮，皱起眉头。——“留神！他会把你像头得奖的猪一样捅了。”卡特一动不动地低声说。肖无助地四下看。——“你瞧着会乐——是不是？”他缓缓地、苦涩地说。卡特那疏远而含混的笑，以其可怕的冷峻彻底压倒了他。极度的沮丧在这位大副原始的灵魂里，取代了种族自尊的正当感情。“我的天！什么命！我造了什么孽，落到这帮人堆里？”他哼哼着坐下，双手抱住那颗灰色的大脑袋。卡特侧身给伊玛达让路——她听哥哥低语了一句，正要退出舱去。她迟疑了一瞬走出去，那一瞬里她抬头看了卡特一眼。她哥哥一动不动，摆着戒备的架势，掩护她退走。她不见了；哈西姆握着剑柄的手松开了；他挨个儿看舱里的每一样东西，仿佛要把它们的位置永远记在心里，然后跟着妹妹，迈着无声的脚步走了出去。

===Original===

They entered the same darkness which had received, enveloped, and hidden the troubled souls of Lingard and Edith, but to these two the light from which they had felt themselves driven away was now like the light of forbidden hopes; it had the awful and tranquil brightness that a light burning on the shore has for an exhausted swimmer about to give himself up to the fateful sea. They looked back; it had disappeared; Carter had shut the cabin door behind them to have it out with Shaw. He wanted to arrive at some kind of working compromise with the nominal commander, but the mate was so demoralized by the novelty of the assaults made upon his respectability that the young defender of the brig could get nothing from him except lamentations mingled with mild blasphemies. The brig slept, and along her quiet deck the voices raised in her cabin﻿—Shaw’s appeals and reproaches directed vociferously to heaven, together with Carter’s inflexible drawl mingled into one deadened, modulated, and continuous murmur. The lockouts in the waist, motionless and peering into obscurity, one ear turned to the sea, were aware of that strange resonance like the ghost of a quarrel that seemed to hover at their backs. Wasub, after seeing Hassim and Immada into their canoe, prowled to and fro the whole length of the vessel vigilantly. There was not a star in the sky and no gleam on the water; there was no horizon, no outline, no shape for the eye to rest upon, nothing for the hand to grasp. An obscurity that seemed without limit in space and time had submerged the universe like a destroying flood.

===Chinese===

他们走进的那片黑暗，正是方才接纳、裹住并藏起了林加德与伊迪丝这两颗惶乱灵魂的黑暗；可对这兄妹二人来说，他们被逐离的那盏灯，如今就像禁忌之望的光；它有着那种岸上灯火对一个精疲力竭、正要把自己交给命定之海的泳者所具有的可怕而安详的明亮。他们回头望；灯已不见；卡特随手关上了身后的舱门，好跟肖把话说透。他想跟这位名义上的指挥官达成某种可操作的妥协，可这位大副被针对他体面的一连串新打击弄得方寸大乱，这位双桅船的年轻保卫者从他那里，除了夹着轻声咒骂的哀叹之外什么也没得到。双桅船睡着了，沿着她安静的甲板，舱里升起的话音——肖朝天挥洒的呼吁与责难，加上卡特不动摇的慢腔调——汇成一股压低的、抑扬的、绵延不断的低语。船腰里的望哨一动不动地探进黑暗，一只耳朵朝向大海，察觉到那奇异的共鸣，像一场口角的幽魂悬在他们背后。瓦苏布把哈西姆和伊玛达送上独木舟后，在整条船的长度上来回巡弋，警惕不移。天上没有一颗星，水上没有一点光；没有天际线，没有轮廓，没有一样可以让眼睛停驻的形状，没有一样可以让手抓住的东西。一种仿佛在空间和时间上都无边的黑暗，像一场毁灭的洪水，淹没了宇宙。

===Original===

A lull of the breeze kept for a time the small boat in the neighbourhood of the brig. The hoisted sail, invisible, fluttered faintly, mysteriously, and the boat rising and falling bodily to the passage of each invisible undulation of the waters seemed to repose upon a living breast. Lingard, his hand on the tiller, sat up erect, expectant and silent. Mrs. Travers had drawn her cloak close around her body. Their glances plunged infinitely deep into a lightless void, and yet they were still so near the brig that the piteous whine of the dog, mingled with the angry rattling of the chain, reached their ears faintly, evoking obscure images of distress and fury. A sharp bark ending in a plaintive howl that seemed raised by the passage of phantoms invisible to men, rent the black stillness, as though the instinct of the brute inspired by the soul of night had voiced in a lamentable plaint the fear of the future, the anguish of lurking death, the terror of shadows. Not far from the brig’s boat Hassim and Immada in their canoe, letting their paddles trail in the water, sat in a silent and invincible torpor as if the fitful puffs of wind had carried to their hearts the breath of a subtle poison that, very soon, would make them die.﻿—“Have you seen the white woman’s eyes?” cried the girl. She struck her palms together loudly and remained with her arms extended, with her hands clasped. “O Hassim! Have you seen her eyes shining under her eyebrows like rays of light darting under the arched boughs in a forest? They pierced me. I shuddered at the sound of her voice! I saw her walk behind him﻿—and it seems to me that she does not live on earth﻿—that all this is witchcraft.”

===Chinese===

风歇了一阵，小艇有一会儿留在双桅船近旁。扬起的帆看不见，只神秘地微微扑动，小艇随着每一道看不见的涌浪整个儿起伏，仿佛卧在一具活着的胸脯上。林加德一手握舵柄，笔直坐着，屏息无声。特拉弗斯太太把斗篷紧裹在身上。两人的目光扎进无光的虚空，深不见底；可他们离双桅船还那么近，那条狗可怜的呜咽混着锁链的恼怒锒铛，还隐约传到他们耳中，唤起痛苦与狂怒的模糊影像。一声尖利的吠叫，收在一个哀怨的长嚎里，仿佛是被过路人眼不可见的幽灵们惊起的，撕开了黑色的寂静，仿佛畜生的本能借着夜之魂，用一声悲怆的哀鸣，道出了对未来的恐惧、潜伏的死亡的痛苦、阴影的恐怖。离双桅船的小艇不远，哈西姆和伊玛达坐在他们的独木舟里，任桨叶拖在水里，陷在一种沉默而不可自拔的麻木中，仿佛风的阵阵吹拂，把一缕微妙的毒气送进了他们的心，很快就要叫他们死去。——“你看见那个白女人的眼睛了吗？”姑娘喊道。她响亮地合起双掌，随即保持着双臂前伸、十指相扣的姿势。“哈西姆啊！你看见她的眼睛在她眉毛底下发光吗，像林子里拱枝下射出的光线？它们刺穿了我。她一开口我就发抖！我看见她跟在他身后走——我总觉得她不是活在世上的人——这一切全是妖法。”

===Original===

She lamented in the night. Hassim kept silent. He had no illusions and in any other man but Lingard he would have thought the proceeding no better than suicidal folly. For him Travers and d’Alcacer were two powerful Rajahs﻿—probably relatives of the Ruler of the land of the English whom he knew to be a woman; but why they should come and interfere with the recovery of his own kingdom was an obscure problem. He was concerned for Lingard’s safety. That the risk was incurred mostly for his sake﻿—so that the prospects of the great enterprise should not be ruined by a quarrel over the lives of these whites﻿—did not strike him so much as may be imagined. There was that in him which made such an action on Lingard’s part appear all but unavoidable. Was he not Rajah Hassim and was not the other a man of strong heart, of strong arm, of proud courage, a man great enough to protect highborn princes﻿—a friend? Immada’s words called out a smile which, like the words, was lost in the darkness. “Forget your weariness,” he said, gently, “lest, O Sister, we should arrive too late.” The coming day would throw its light on some decisive event. Hassim thought of his own men who guarded the *Emma* and he wished to be where they could hear his voice. He regretted Jaffir was not there. Hassim was saddened by the absence from his side of that man who once had carried what he thought would be his last message to his friend. It had not been the last. He had lived to cherish new hopes and to face new troubles and, perchance, to frame another message yet, while death knocked with the hands of armed enemies at the gate. The breeze steadied; the succeeding swells swung the canoe smoothly up the unbroken ridges of water travelling apace along the land. They progressed slowly; but Immada’s heart was more weary than her arms, and Hassim, dipping the blade of his paddle without a splash, peered right and left, trying to make out the shadowy forms of islets. A long way ahead of the canoe and holding the same course, the brig’s dinghy ran with broad lug extended, making for that narrow and winding passage between the coast and the southern shoals, which led to the mouth of the creek connecting the lagoon with the sea.

===Chinese===

她在黑夜里悲叹。哈西姆沉默着。他不抱幻想，若是换了别人而非林加德，他会觉得这举动无异于自取灭亡。在他眼里，特拉弗斯和达尔卡塞尔是两位有权势的拉者——多半是英吉利国君主的亲眷，他知道那君主是个女人；可他们为什么跑来阻碍他收回自己的王国，却是个想不透的难题。他为林加德的安危担心。这风险多半是为他担的——免得大业的前程毁在这几个白人性命的争执上——这一点在他心里激起的波澜，并不像旁人想的那么大。他身上有一种东西，使林加德的这一举动在他看来几乎是义所必至。他不是哈西姆拉者吗？对方不是一个心坚臂强、骄傲无畏、伟大得足以庇护贵胄王孙的人——一个朋友吗？伊玛达的话引出他一个微笑，那微笑跟话语一样，消失在黑暗里。“忘了你的困倦吧。”他温和地说，“免得，妹妹啊，咱们到得太迟。”来日将把它的光投在某桩决定性的事件上。哈西姆想起守卫“艾玛号”的自己人，他愿意待在他们听得见他声音的地方。他遗憾贾菲尔不在。那个曾替他给他朋友捎去他自以为是遗言的人，此刻不在身边，哈西姆为此黯然。那并不是遗言。他又活下来，珍视起新的希望，面对新的麻烦，而且，没准儿，还要再口授一封信，而死亡正用武装敌人的手敲着大门。风稳了；接踵的涌浪托着独木舟，顺着沿岸疾行的一道道完整的水脊平稳地上坡。他们行进得很慢；可伊玛达的心比她的手臂更倦，哈西姆把桨叶无声地插进水里，左右张望，想辨出小岛朦胧的影子。独木舟前方远远的地方，走的同一条航路上，双桅船的小艇张着宽大的斜桁帆在跑，奔向海岸与南面浅滩之间那条通往河口——连通潟湖与海的小河——的狭窄而曲折的水道。

===Original===

Thus on that starless night the Shallows were peopled by uneasy souls. The thick veil of clouds stretched over them, cut them off from the rest of the universe. At times Mrs. Travers had in the darkness the impression of dizzy speed, and again it seemed to her that the boat was standing still, that everything in the world was standing still and only her fancy roamed free from all trammels. Lingard, perfectly motionless by her side, steered, shaping his course by the feel of the wind. Presently he perceived ahead a ghostly flicker of faint, livid light which the earth seemed to throw up against the uniform blackness of the sky. The dinghy was approaching the expanse of the Shallows. The confused clamour of broken water deepened its note.

“How long are we going to sail like this?” asked Mrs. Travers, gently. She did not recognize the voice that pronounced the word “Always” in answer to her question. It had the impersonal ring of a voice without a master. Her heart beat fast.

“Captain Lingard!” she cried.

“Yes. What?” he said, nervously, as if startled out of a dream.

“I asked you how long we were going to sail like this,” she repeated, distinctly.

“If the breeze holds we shall be in the lagoon soon after daybreak. That will be the right time, too. I shall leave you on board the hulk with Jörgenson.”

“And you? What will you do?” she asked. She had to wait for a while.

“I will do what I can,” she heard him say at last. There was another pause. “All I can,” he added.

The breeze dropped, the sail fluttered.

“I have perfect confidence in you,” she said. “But are you certain of success?”

“No.”

The futility of her question came home to Mrs. Travers. In a few hours of life she had been torn away from all her certitudes, flung into a world of improbabilities. This thought instead of augmenting her distress seemed to soothe her. What she experienced was not doubt and it was not fear. It was something else. It might have been only a great fatigue.

===Chinese===

就这样，在那个无星的夜里，浅滩上栖满了不安的灵魂。浓云的厚幕罩在它们上空，把它们同宇宙的其余部分隔开。特拉弗斯太太在黑暗里时而有一种飞驰的晕眩感，时而又仿佛小艇停着不动，世上的一切都停着不动，只有她的遐想挣脱了一切羁绊，自在游荡。林加德在她身旁纹丝不动，把着舵，凭风在脸上的感觉定着航向。不久，他望见前方有一片幽微、惨白的微光在闪烁，仿佛是大地朝天空浑然的黑幕上投起来的。小艇正逼近浅滩的外缘。碎水嘈杂的喧声低了下去，沉了一个调。

“我们还要这样航行多久？”特拉弗斯太太轻声问。回答她那声“永远”的嗓音，她竟没有听出来。那嗓音带着一种无主的、不属于任何人的铿锵。她的心怦怦地跳。

“林加德船长！”她喊。

“嗯。什么？”他紧张地说，像从梦中惊醒。

“我问您，我们还要这样航行多久。”她清楚地重复道。

“风要是不断，天亮后不久我们就进潟湖。那时也正是时候。我把您留在那条废船上，约根森那儿。”

“那您呢？您做什么？”她问。她等了好一会儿。

“我尽力而为。”她终于听见他说。又停了停。“尽我全力。”他补了一句。

风息了，帆抖了抖。

“我对您有完全的信心。”她说。“可您有成功的把握吗？”

“没有。”

特拉弗斯太太这才醒悟自己问得多无谓。生命的几个钟头里，她已被从她全部的确信中撕开，抛进一个尽是不可能性的世界。这念头非但没有加深她的苦恼，反而像是安抚了她。她经历的既不是怀疑，也不是恐惧。是别的什么。也许只是一种巨大的疲惫。

===Original===

She heard a dull detonation as if in the depth of the sea. It was hardly more than a shock and a vibration. A roller had broken amongst the shoals; the livid clearness Lingard had seen ahead flashed and flickered in expanded white sheets much nearer to the boat now. And all this﻿—the wan burst of light, the faint shock as of something remote and immense falling into ruins, was taking place outside the limits of her life which remained encircled by an impenetrable darkness and by an impenetrable silence. Puffs of wind blew about her head and expired; the sail collapsed, shivered audibly, stood full and still in turn; and again the sensation of vertiginous speed and of absolute immobility succeeding each other with increasing swiftness merged at last into a bizarre state of headlong motion and profound peace. The darkness enfolded her like the enervating caress of a sombre universe. It was gentle and destructive. Its languor seduced her soul into surrender. Nothing existed and even all her memories vanished into space. She was content that nothing should exist.

===Chinese===

她听见海底深处似的咚的一声闷响。几乎只是一震一颤。一道浪在浅滩间碎了；林加德方才望见的那片惨白的亮光，闪了闪，在离小艇近得多的地方化开成大张大张的白光。而这一切——那惨淡的爆光，那仿佛某种遥远而巨大之物坍塌的轻微震动——都发生在她生命的界限之外；她的生命仍被无孔不入的黑暗和无孔不入的寂静包围着。一阵阵风绕着她的头吹拂、消散；帆塌下去，簌簌作响，又轮番饱满地静立；而那晕眩的飞速与绝对的静止感越来越快地交替，终于融成一种奇异的状态：一路狂奔的动，与深入骨髓的静。黑暗包裹着她，像一个阴沉宇宙使人瘫软的爱抚。它温柔而具有毁灭性。它的倦怠诱使她的灵魂投降。什么都不存在了，连她全部的记忆也消散进空间。什么都不存在，她满足了。

===Original===

Lingard, aware all the time of their contact in the narrow stern sheets of the boat, was startled by the pressure of the woman’s head drooping on his shoulder. He stiffened himself still more as though he had tried on the approach of a danger to conceal his life in the breathless rigidity of his body. The boat soared and descended slowly; a region of foam and reefs stretched across her course hissing like a gigantic cauldron; a strong gust of wind drove her straight at it for a moment then passed on and abandoned her to the regular balancing of the swell. The struggle of the rocks forever overwhelmed and emerging, with the sea forever victorious and repulsed, fascinated the man. He watched it as he would have watched something going on within himself while Mrs. Travers slept sustained by his arm, pressed to his side, abandoned to his support. The shoals guarding the Shore of Refuge had given him his first glimpse of success﻿—the solid support he needed for his action. The Shallows were the shelter of his dreams; their voice had the power to soothe and exalt his thoughts with the promise of freedom for his hopes. Never had there been such a generous friendship.﻿ ﻿… A mass of white foam whirling about a centre of intense blackness spun silently past the side of the boat.﻿ ﻿… That woman he held like a captive on his arm had also been given to him by the Shallows.

Suddenly his eyes caught on a distant sandbank the red gleam of Daman’s camp fire instantly eclipsed like the wink of a signalling lantern along the level of the waters. It brought to his mind the existence of the two men﻿—those other captives. If the war canoe transporting them into the lagoon had left the sands shortly after Hassim’s retreat from Daman’s camp, Travers and d’Alcacer were by this time far away up the creek. Every thought of action had become odious to Lingard since all he could do in the world now was to hasten the moment of his separation from that woman to whom he had confessed the whole secret of his life.

===Chinese===

林加德自始至终意识着小艇狭窄艉座板上两人的相触，那女人的头垂落在他肩上，让他心头一惊。他把身子挺得更直，仿佛大险临近时，想把自己这条命藏进身体的凝滞僵直里。小艇缓缓腾起、缓缓落下；一片泡沫与礁石的水域横在航路前，嘶响如一口巨大的滚锅；一阵劲风把艇直直朝它推了一瞬，随即掠过去，把她丢还给涌浪规律的摇荡。岩石永远被淹没又永远重新露头，大海永远得胜又永远被击退——这场搏斗让这个人看得出神。他望着它，像望着自己身体里正进行着的什么，而特拉弗斯太太由他的一条手臂托着、贴在他身侧、全然托付给他的支撑，睡着了。守卫着避难岸的浅滩，曾给他成功的头一眼——他的行动所需要的坚实支点。浅滩是他梦想的庇护所；它们的声音有力量安抚并升华他的思想，向他的希望许诺自由。从来没有过这样慷慨的友谊。……一团白沫绕着一个极黑的核心打旋，无声地从艇边转了过去。……他像押着俘虏似的搂在臂弯里的那个女人，也是浅滩给他的。

忽然，他的眼睛在远处一道沙洲上捕捉到达曼营火的红光，它像信号灯沿水面一眨似的，随即熄灭。这使他想起那两个人的存在——另外那两个俘虏。要是载他们进潟湖的战舟在哈西姆撤出达曼营地后不久就离开了沙洲，特拉弗斯和达尔卡塞尔这会儿早已在河口深处了。自从他在世上所能做的，只剩催促他与那个女人分离的时刻早日到来——他已向她倾吐了一生的全部秘密——一切行动的念头都让林加德觉得可憎。

===Original===

And she slept. She could sleep! He looked down at her as he would have looked at the slumbering ignorance of a child, but the life within him had the fierce beat of supreme moments. Near by, the eddies sighed along the reefs, the water soughed amongst the stones, clung round the rocks with tragic murmurs that resembled promises, goodbyes, or prayers. From the unfathomable distances of the night came the booming of the swell assaulting the seaward face of the Shallows. He felt the woman’s nearness with such intensity that he heard nothing.﻿ ﻿… Then suddenly he thought of death.

“Wake up!” he shouted in her ear, swinging round in his seat. Mrs. Travers gasped; a splash of water flicked her over the eyes and she felt the separate drops run down her cheeks, she tasted them on her lips, tepid and bitter like tears. A swishing undulation tossed the boat on high followed by another and still another; and then the boat with the breeze abeam glided through still water, laying over at a steady angle.

“Clear of the reef now,” remarked Lingard in a tone of relief.

“Were we in any danger?” asked Mrs. Travers in a whisper.

“Well, the breeze dropped and we drifted in very close to the rocks,” he answered. “I had to rouse you. It wouldn’t have done for you to wake up suddenly struggling in the water.”

So she had slept! It seemed to her incredible that she should have closed her eyes in this small boat, with the knowledge of their desperate errand, on so disturbed a sea. The man by her side leaned forward, extended his arm, and the boat going off before the wind went on faster on an even keel. A motionless black bank resting on the sea stretched infinitely right in their way in ominous stillness. She called Lingard’s attention to it. “Look at this awful cloud.”

“This cloud is the coast and in a moment we shall be entering the creek,” he said, quietly. Mrs. Travers stared at it. Was it land﻿—land! It seemed to her even less palpable than a cloud, a mere sinister immobility above the unrest of the sea, nursing in its depth the unrest of men who, to her mind, were no more real than fantastic shadows.

===Chinese===

而她睡着了。她竟然睡得着！他低头看她，像看一个孩子酣睡的无知，可他体内的生命，却带着巅峰时刻那猛烈的搏动。近处，涡流沿着礁脉叹息，水在石间呜咽，绕着礁石发出悲怆的低语，像允诺，像诀别，像祈祷。从夜的无从测度的远处，传来涌浪捶击浅滩向海一面的轰鸣。那女人的贴近，他感受得那样强烈，以致什么也听不见。……忽然，他想到了死。

“醒醒！”他凑着她的耳朵大喊，在座位上猛地一转。特拉弗斯太太倒抽一口气；一捧水甩了她满脸，她感到一颗颗水珠顺着面颊淌下，在唇上尝到它们，温热而苦涩，像泪。一道咝咝作响的涌浪把小艇高高抛起，接着又是一道，又一道；随后小艇借着正横的风，以一个平稳的倾角，滑进了静水。

“现在出礁区了。”林加德松了口气说。

“我们方才有危险吗？”特拉弗斯太太低声问。

“唉，风停了，我们漂得离礁石太近。”他答。“我不得不叫醒您。您要是在水里挣扎着惊醒，那可不好。”

这么说她睡着了！她觉得难以置信：自己竟在这条小艇上、明知此行凶险、海面这样颠簸的情形下，合上了眼睛。身旁的男人俯身向前，伸出手臂，小艇顺风跑得更快了，平稳地吃水。一道纹丝不动的黑色堤岸卧在海面上，不祥地静默着，无穷无尽地横在正前方。她唤林加德注意它。“瞧那朵可怕的云。”

“那朵云是海岸，咱们马上就要进河口了。”他平静地说。特拉弗斯太太盯着它看。这是陆地——陆地！在她看来，它比云还要虚幻，只是海上动荡之上的一道不祥的静止，在它的深处孕育着人的动荡，而在她心里，那些人已不比奇幻的影子更真实了。

### V / 五
===Original===

What struck Mrs. Travers most, directly she set eyes on him, was the otherworld aspect of Jörgenson. He had been buried out of sight so long that his tall, gaunt body, his unhurried, mechanical movements, his set face and his eyes with an empty gaze suggested an invincible indifference to all the possible surprises of the earth. That appearance of a resuscitated man who seemed to be commanded by a conjuring spell strolled along the decks of what was even to Mrs. Travers’ eyes the mere corpse of a ship and turned on her a pair of deep-sunk, expressionless eyes with an almost unearthly detachment. Mrs. Travers had never been looked at before with that strange and pregnant abstraction. Yet she didn’t dislike Jörgenson. In the early morning light, white from head to foot in a perfectly clean suit of clothes which seemed hardly to contain any limbs, freshly shaven (Jörgenson’s sunken cheeks with their withered colouring always had a sort of gloss as though he had the habit of shaving every two hours or so), he looked as immaculate as though he had been indeed a pure spirit superior to the soiling contacts of the material earth. He was disturbing but he was not repulsive. He gave no sign of greeting.

Lingard addressed him at once.

“You have had a regular staircase built up the side of the hulk, Jörgenson,” he said. “It was very convenient for us to come aboard now, but in case of an attack don’t you think﻿ ﻿…”

“I did think.” There was nothing so dispassionate in the world as the voice of Captain H. C. Jörgenson, ex Barque *Wild Rose*, since he had recrossed the Waters of Oblivion to step back into the life of men. “I did think, but since I don’t want to make trouble.﻿ ﻿…”

“Oh, you don’t want to make trouble,” interrupted Lingard.

“No. Don’t believe in it. Do you, King Tom?”

“I may have to make trouble.”

“So you came up here in this small dinghy of yours like this to start making trouble, did you?”

“What’s the matter with you? Don’t you know me yet, Jörgenson?”

“I thought I knew you. How could I tell that a man like you would come along for a fight bringing a woman with him?”

“This lady is Mrs. Travers,” said Lingard. “The wife of one of the luckless gentlemen Daman got hold of last evening.﻿ ﻿… This is Jörgenson, the friend of whom I have been telling you, Mrs. Travers.”

===Chinese===

特拉弗斯太太一眼望见约根森，最打眼的是他那副尘世之外的面相。他被埋没得如此之久，以致那瘦高的身躯、那不慌不忙的机械动作、那呆板的脸和目光空茫的眼睛，都透着一种对人间一切可能的意外都无动于衷的气概。这副还魂人的做派，仿佛受着一道咒语的驱遣，在这条船——连特拉弗斯太太的眼里它也不过是具船的尸身——的甲板上来回踱着，朝她转过一双深陷而无表情的眼睛，带着一种近乎非人间的超然。特拉弗斯太太从没有被谁这样带着古怪而意蕴深长的出神凝望过。可她并不讨厌约根森。清晨的光里，他从头到脚一身白，那身洗得干干净净的衣裳里仿佛根本没装着四肢，脸刚刮过（约根森那两腮深陷、颜色枯槁的脸总有一种光泽，仿佛他有每隔两个钟头刮一次脸的习惯），看上去洁净无瑕，真像一个超脱了尘世玷污之触的纯粹精魂。他叫人心神不定，却并不叫人反感。他没有打招呼的意思。

林加德立刻开了口。

「你在废船的船侧搭了一道规规矩矩的楼梯，约根森。」他说，「现在我们上来倒方便；可万一有人来攻，你想过没有……」

「我想过。」自从重新渡过忘川、回到众人的生路以来，世上再没有比这位前「野玫瑰号」三桅帆船的 H·C·约根森船长的嗓音更不动声色的了。「我想过。可我既然不想惹乱子……」

「哦，你不想惹乱子。」林加德打断道。

「不想。不信那个。你信么，汤姆王？」

「我恐怕就得惹点乱子。」

「所以你就坐着你那条小不点儿的小艇跑到这儿来，开始惹乱子了，是么？」

「你是怎么了？约根森，你还不识得我么？」

「我以为识得。可我哪想得到，你这样一个人跑来找架打，居然还随身带个女人？」

「这位是特拉弗斯太太。」林加德说，「达曼昨晚抓去的倒霉先生们之中一位的太太。……这位就是约根森，我同您说起过的那位朋友，特拉弗斯太太。」

===Original===

Mrs. Travers smiled faintly. Her eyes roamed far and near and the strangeness of her surroundings, the overpowering curiosity, the conflict of interest and doubt gave her the aspect of one still new to life, presenting an innocent and naive attitude before the surprises of experience. She looked very guileless and youthful between those two men. Lingard gazed at her with that unconscious tenderness mingled with wonder, which some men manifest toward girlhood. There was nothing of a conqueror of kingdoms in his bearing. Jörgenson preserved his amazing abstraction which seemed neither to hear nor see anything. But, evidently, he kept a mysterious grip on events in the world of living men because he asked very naturally:

“How did she get away?”

“The lady wasn’t on the sandbank,” explained Lingard, curtly.

“What sandbank?” muttered Jörgenson, perfunctorily.﻿ ﻿… “Is the yacht looted, Tom?”

“Nothing of the kind,” said Lingard.

“Ah, many dead?” inquired Jörgenson.

“I tell you there was nothing of the kind,” said Lingard, impatiently.

“What? No fight!” inquired Jörgenson again without the slightest sign of animation.

“No.”

“And you a fighting man.”

“Listen to me, Jörgenson. Things turned out so that before the time came for a fight it was already too late.” He turned to Mrs. Travers still looking about with anxious eyes and a faint smile on her lips. “While I was talking to you that evening from the boat it was already too late. No. There was never any time for it. I have told you all about myself, Mrs. Travers, and you know that I speak the truth when I say too late. If you had only been alone in that yacht going about the seas!”

“Yes,” she struck in, “but I was not alone.”

Lingard dropped his chin on his breast. Already a foretaste of noonday heat staled the sparkling freshness of the morning. The smile had vanished from Edith Travers’ lips and her eyes rested on Lingard’s bowed head with an expression no longer curious but which might have appeared enigmatic to Jörgenson if he had looked at her. But Jörgenson looked at nothing. He asked from the remoteness of his dead past, “What have you left outside, Tom? What is there now?”

===Chinese===

特拉弗斯太太淡淡一笑。她的眼睛远近逡巡；处境的离奇、压倒一切的好奇、利益与疑虑的交战，给了她一副初涉人世的模样，在经验的种种意外面前摆出一副天真无邪的姿态。夹在这两个男人中间，她显得毫无心机、年少轻青。林加德望着她，带着某些男人面对少女时那种不自觉的、搀着惊叹的温柔。他的做派里毫无王国的征服者气象。约根森则保持着他那份惊人的出神，仿佛什么也没听见、什么也没看见。可他显然对活人世界的事保持着一种神秘的掌控，因为他极其自然地问：

「她怎么脱的身？」

「那位太太当时不在沙洲上。」林加德简短地解释。

「哪个沙洲？」约根森敷衍地咕哝……「游艇被抢了没有，汤姆？」

「没有的事。」林加德说。

「啊，死了很多人？」约根森问。

「我说了没有的事。」林加德不耐烦地说。

「怎么？没打起来！」约根森又问，没有丝毫活气。

「没打。」

「亏你还是个能打的人。」

「听我说，约根森。事情赶到那一步：还没到该打的时辰，就已经太迟了。」他转向特拉弗斯太太——她还在焦灼地四下张望，唇边挂着一丝浅笑。「那天傍晚我在艇里同您说话的时候，就已经太迟了。不。从来就没有过打的时候。我已把我的底细全告诉了您，特拉弗斯太太；我说『太迟』的时候，您知道我句句是实。倘若您当时独自一人在那条游艇上闯荡四海就好了！」

「是啊，」她接口道，「可我并不独自一人。」

林加德把下巴垂到胸前。正午暑气的前味，已经败坏了早晨的清新。笑意从伊迪丝·特拉弗斯唇边消逝了，她的眼睛停在林加德低垂的头上，神情已不再好奇；约根森若是看她一眼，或许会觉得那神情难解。可约根森什么也不看。他从他那死去的往昔的迢遥之处发问：「外头你撂下什么，汤姆？如今那儿有什么？」

===Original===

“There’s the yacht on the shoals, my brig at anchor, and about a hundred of the worst kind of Illanun vagabonds under three chiefs and with two war-praus moored to the edge of the bank. Maybe Daman is with them, too, out there.”

“No,” said Jörgenson, positively.

“He has come in,” cried Lingard. “He brought his prisoners in himself then.”

“Landed by torchlight,” uttered precisely the shade of Captain Jörgenson, late of the Barque *Wild Rose*. He swung his arm pointing across the lagoon and Mrs. Travers turned about in that direction.

All the scene was but a great light and a great solitude. Her gaze travelled over the lustrous, dark sheet of empty water to a shore bordered by a white beach empty, too, and showing no sign of human life. The human habitations were lost in the shade of the fruit trees, masked by the cultivated patches of Indian corn and the banana plantations. Near the shore the rigid lines of two stockaded forts could be distinguished flanking the beach, and between them with a great open space before it, the brown roof slope of an enormous long building that seemed suspended in the air had a great square flag fluttering above it. Something like a small white flame in the sky was the carved white coral finial on the gable of the mosque which had caught full the rays of the sun. A multitude of gay streamers, white and red, flew over the half-concealed roofs, over the brilliant fields and amongst the sombre palm groves. But it might have been a deserted settlement decorated and abandoned by its departed population. Lingard pointed to the stockade on the right.

“That’s where your husband is,” he said to Mrs. Travers.

“Who is the other?” uttered Jörgenson’s voice at their backs. He also was turned that way with his strange sightless gaze fixed beyond them into the void.

“A Spanish gentleman I believe you said, Mrs. Travers,” observed Lingard.

“It is extremely difficult to believe that there is anybody there,” murmured Mrs. Travers.

“Did you see them both, Jörgenson?” asked Lingard.

“Made out nobody. Too far. Too dark.”

===Chinese===

「浅滩上是那条游艇，我的双桅船在锚上，还有百十来个最下等的伊兰农光棍，归三个头领，两条战船泊在滩沿。说不定达曼也同他们在一起，在外头。」

「不。」约根森断然说。

「他进来了，」林加德喊道，「那两个俘虏是他亲自押进来的。」

「火把照着上的岸。」前「野玫瑰号」的约根森船长那道身影精确地吐出这几个字。他挥手指向潟湖对岸，特拉弗斯太太转身朝那个方向望去。

满眼只是一大片光和一大片孤寂。她的目光越过那乌亮空阔的水面，望到一线白沙滩镶成的岸——也是空的，不见一点人烟。人居的屋舍隐在果树的荫翳里，遮在玉米地和芭蕉园后头。近岸处，分辨得出两座设栅的炮寨的僵直线条，夹峙着沙滩；两寨之间、面前留出一片大空地的，是一座极大极长的屋子，棕色屋顶的坡面仿佛悬在半空，上头飘着一面大方旗。天空里像一小簇白色火焰的，是清真寺山墙顶上雕琢的白色珊瑚冠，正承满阳光。无数白红相间的彩旗，飞在半掩的屋顶上、亮晃晃的田野上和幽暗的棕榈林间。可这也可能是一座装点停当、被居民抛下的空聚落。林加德指着右边那座栅寨。

「您丈夫就在那儿。」他对特拉弗斯太太说。

「另一座里是谁？」他们背后响起约根森的声音。他也朝着那边，那双古怪的、没有视力的眼睛越过他们，钉在虚空里。

「一位西班牙绅士，我记得您是这么说的，特拉弗斯太太。」林加德说。

「实在很难相信那里头会有什么人。」特拉弗斯太太喃喃道。

「他们两个你都没见着，约根森？」林加德问。

「谁也没辨出来。太远。太黑。」

===Original===

As a matter of fact Jörgenson had seen nothing, about an hour before daybreak, but the distant glare of torches while the loud shouts of an excited multitude had reached him across the water only like a faint and tempestuous murmur. Presently the lights went away processionally through the groves of trees into the armed stockades. The distant glare vanished in the fading darkness and the murmurs of the invisible crowd ceased suddenly as if carried off by the retreating shadow of the night. Daylight followed swiftly, disclosing to the sleepless Jörgenson the solitude of the shore and the ghostly outlines of the familiar forms of grouped trees and scattered human habitations. He had watched the varied colours come out in the dawn, the wide cultivated Settlement of many shades of green, framed far away by the fine black lines of the forest-edge that was its limit and its protection.

Mrs. Travers stood against the rail as motionless as a statue. Her face had lost all its mobility and her cheeks were dead white as if all the blood in her body had flowed back into her heart and had remained there. Her very lips had lost their colour. Lingard caught hold of her arm roughly.

“Don’t, Mrs. Travers. Why are you terrifying yourself like this? If you don’t believe what I say listen to me asking Jörgenson.﻿ ﻿…”

“Yes, ask me,” mumbled Jörgenson in his white moustache.

“Speak straight, Jörgenson. What do you think? Are the gentlemen alive?”

“Certainly,” said Jörgenson in a sort of disappointed tone as though he had expected a much more difficult question.

“Is their life in immediate danger?”

“Of course not,” said Jörgenson.

Lingard turned away from the oracle. “You have heard him, Mrs. Travers. You may believe every word he says. There isn’t a thought or a purpose in that Settlement,” he continued, pointing at the dumb solitude of the lagoon, “that this man doesn’t know as if they were his own.”

“I know. Ask me,” muttered Jörgenson, mechanically.

Mrs. Travers said nothing but made a slight movement and her whole rigid figure swayed dangerously. Lingard put his arm firmly round her waist and she did not seem aware of it till after she had turned her head and found Lingard’s face very near her own. But his eyes full of concern looked so close into hers that she was obliged to shut them like a woman about to faint.

===Chinese===

实情是，约根森在破晓前约一个钟头什么也没看见，只望见远处火把的光，而激动人群的高声呼喊传到他耳中，也只像一阵隐隐的风暴似的低鸣。随后那片灯火排成行列，穿过一片片树丛，进了设栅的寨子。远处的火光在消散的黑暗中隐没，无形人群的嘈杂也蓦地止息，仿佛被退走的夜影一并带走了。天光随后就亮了，向彻夜未眠的约根森揭示出海岸的孤寂，以及成簇的树木和零散屋舍那些熟稔形态的鬼影般的轮廓。他望着黎明里种种颜色显露出来，望着那片深浅不一的绿色的广阔聚落，远处镶着森林边缘的细黑线——那是它的界限，也是它的屏障。

特拉弗斯太太倚着栏杆站着，纹丝不动如一尊像。她的脸失去了全部的活气，两颊死白，仿佛全身的血液都回流进心脏，滞在那里不再出来。连嘴唇都褪了色。林加德一把攥住她的胳膊。

「别这样，特拉弗斯太太。您这是何苦自己吓自己？您不信我的话，那就听我问约根森。……」

「好，问我。」约根森对着白胡子咕哝。

「直说，约根森。你怎么看？两位先生还活着么？」

「当然。」约根森的语气几乎带着失望，仿佛他等的是一个难得多的问题。

「性命眼下有没有危险？」

「当然没有。」约根森说。

林加德从这位神谕跟前转开。「您听见了，特拉弗斯太太。他说的每一个字您都可以信。那聚落里，」他指着潟湖哑默的孤寂接着说，「没有一个念头、一桩图谋，是这个人不像知道自家事一样知道的。」

「我知道。问我。」约根森机械地咕哝。

特拉弗斯太太没作声，只微微一动，僵直的全身便晃得岌岌可危。林加德一把搂住她的腰；她仿佛全无觉察，直到回过头，发觉林加德的脸离自己极近。他那双盛满关切的眼睛直望进她的眼睛，逼得她像将晕倒的女人一样闭上了眼。

===Original===

The effect this produced upon Lingard was such that she felt the tightening of his arm and as she opened her eyes again some of the colour returned to her face. She met the deepened expression of his solicitude with a look so steady, with a gaze that in spite of herself was so profoundly vivid that its clearness seemed to Lingard to throw all his past life into shade.﻿—“I don’t feel faint. It isn’t that at all,” she declared in a perfectly calm voice. It seemed to Lingard as cold as ice.

“Very well,” he agreed with a resigned smile. “But you just catch hold of that rail, please, before I let you go.” She, too, forced a smile on her lips.

“What incredulity,” she remarked, and for a time made not the slightest movement. At last, as if making a concession, she rested the tips of her fingers on the rail. Lingard gradually removed his arm. “And pray don’t look upon me as a conventional ‘weak woman’ person, the delicate lady of your own conception,” she said, facing Lingard, with her arm extended to the rail. “Make that effort please against your own conception of what a woman like me should be. I am perhaps as strong as you are, Captain Lingard. I mean it literally. In my body.”﻿—“Don’t you think I have seen that long ago?” she heard his deep voice protesting.﻿—“And as to my courage,” Mrs. Travers continued, her expression charmingly undecided between frowns and smiles; “didn’t I tell you only a few hours ago, only last evening, that I was not capable of thinking myself into a fright; you remember, when you were begging me to try something of the kind. Don’t imagine that I would have been ashamed to try. But I couldn’t have done it. No. Not even for the sake of somebody else’s kingdom. Do you understand me?”

“God knows,” said the attentive Lingard after a time, with an unexpected sigh. “You people seem to be made of another stuff.”

“What has put that absurd notion into your head?”

“I didn’t mean better or worse. And I wouldn’t say it isn’t good stuff either. What I meant to say is that it’s different. One feels it. And here we are.”

“Yes, here we are,” repeated Mrs. Travers. “And as to this moment of emotion, what provoked it is not a concern for anybody or anything outside myself. I felt no terror. I cannot even fix my fears upon any distinct image. You think I am shamelessly heartless in telling you this.”

===Chinese===

这在林加德身上激起的反应，使她觉出他的胳膊收得更紧；待她重新睁开眼，脸上已恢复了几分血色。她迎着他加深了的关切神情，投去那样平稳的一瞥，那目光不由自主地深沉而鲜活，它的清朗在林加德看来仿佛把他过往的一生都罩进了阴影。——「我不晕。根本不是那么回事。」她用一种彻底平静的声音声明。在林加德听来，那声音冷得像冰。

「好吧。」他带着认命的微笑应道，「可我放手之前，请您先抓住那道栏杆。」她也强使唇边挂上一丝笑。

「好大的不信任。」她说；有一阵子纹丝不动。末了，仿佛是让步似的，她把指尖搭上了栏杆。林加德这才渐渐撤开手臂。「也请您别把我当成俗套的『弱女子』，当成您构想中的娇贵太太。」她说，一条胳膊伸向栏杆，正面对着林加德，「请费心同您自己对『我这样的女人』的构想搏一搏。我说不定同您一样结实呢，林加德船长。这话是就字面说的。就我这副身子。」——「您以为我没早就看出来了么？」她听见他深沉的嗓音抗辩道。——「至于我的胆量，」特拉弗斯太太接着说，神情在蹙眉与微笑之间动人地未定，「几小时前，就在昨晚，我不是才告诉过您，我没法把自己想出一 场惊吓来；您记得么，您那时正求我试一试那类的把戏。别以为我会羞于去试。可我试也办不到。不。哪怕为了别人的王国也办不到。您懂我的意思么？」

「天知道。」专注的林加德过了一会儿才说，叹了一口出人意料的气，「你们这班人，仿佛是另一种料子做的。」

「哪来的这荒唐念头？」

「我不是说好些或坏些。也不能说那料子不好。我要说的是：不一样。这个感觉得出来。所以我们到了这一步。」

「是啊，到了这一步。」特拉弗斯太太重复道，「至于方才那一阵激动，勾起它的并不是对我自身之外的任何人、任何事的牵挂。我没有感到恐惧。我甚至没法把我的怕安在任何清晰的影像上。您一定觉得，我对您说这话，是无耻的无情。」

===Original===

Lingard made no sign. It didn’t occur to him to make a sign. He simply hung on Mrs. Travers’ words as it were only for the sake of the sound.﻿—“I am simply frank with you,” she continued. “What do I know of savagery, violence, murder? I have never seen a dead body in my life. The light, the silence, the mysterious emptiness of this place have suddenly affected my imagination, I suppose. What is the meaning of this wonderful peace in which we stand﻿—you and I alone?”

Lingard shook his head. He saw the narrow gleam of the woman’s teeth between the parted lips of her smile, as if all the ardour of her conviction had been dissolved at the end of her speech into wistful recognition of their partnership before things outside their knowledge. And he was warmed by something a little helpless in that smile. Within three feet of them the shade of Jörgenson, very gaunt and neat, stared into space.

“Yes. You are strong,” said Lingard. “But a whole long night sitting in a small boat! I wonder you are not too stiff to stand.”

“I am not stiff in the least,” she interrupted, still smiling. “I am really a very strong woman,” she added, earnestly. “Whatever happens you may reckon on that fact.”

Lingard gave her an admiring glance. But the shade of Jörgenson, perhaps catching in its remoteness the sound of the word woman, was suddenly moved to begin scolding with all the liberty of a ghost, in a flow of passionless indignation.

“Woman! That’s what I say. That’s just about the last touch﻿—that you, Tom Lingard, red-eyed Tom, King Tom, and all those fine names, that you should leave your weapons twenty miles behind you, your men, your guns, your brig that is your strength, and come along here with your mouth full of fight, barehanded and with a woman in tow.﻿—Well﻿—well!”

“Don’t forget, Jörgenson, that the lady hears you,” remonstrated Lingard in a vexed tone.﻿ ﻿… “He doesn’t mean to be rude,” he remarked to Mrs. Travers quite loud, as if indeed Jörgenson were but an immaterial and feelingless illusion. “He has forgotten.”

“The woman is not in the least offended. I ask for nothing better than to be taken on that footing.”

“Forgot nothing!” mumbled Jörgenson with a sort of ghostly assertiveness and as it were for his own satisfaction. “What’s the world coming to?”

===Chinese===

林加德没有任何表示。他也想不起要有什么表示。他只是挂在特拉弗斯太太的话语上，仿佛只为那声音的缘故。——「我只是对您坦白。」她接着说，「野蛮、暴行、凶杀，我知道什么？我平生连一具尸首也没见过。这地方的光、寂静和神秘的空茫，想必是蓦地攫住了我的想象。我们此刻立身的这片奇异的安宁，究竟是什么意思——只有您和我两个人？」

林加德摇了摇头。他看见那女人微笑时分开的唇间露出一线齿光，仿佛她信念的全部热忧，到了话的尽头，都化作对两人共临未知之物这层伴侣关系的怅然体认。那一丝微笑里某种近乎无依的东西，暖了暖他的心。离他们三英尺之内，约根森那道影子，瘦削而齐整，凝望着虚空。

「是啊。您结实。」林加德说，「可整整坐了一夜小艇！我真奇怪您怎么没僵得站不起来。」

「我一点也不僵。」她含笑打断道，「我实在是个非常结实的女人。」她恳切地补上一句，「不论出什么事，这一点您尽管放心。」

林加德向她投去钦佩的一瞥。可约根森那道影子，也许在它的迢遥里捕到了「女人」这个词的声响，忽然动了气，带着鬼魂的百无禁忌，用一股不动感情的义愤数落起来：

「女人！我就是这话。这可真是绝了顶——你，汤姆·林加德，红眼汤姆，汤姆王，还有那些好听的诨名，竟把兵器撂在二十英里外，撂下你的人、你的炮、你那条吃饭的双桅船，跑到这儿来满嘴开打，两手空空，还拖着一个女人。——好嘛——好嘛！」

「别忘了这位太太听得见，约根森。」林加德懊恼地劝阻道……「他不是存心无礼。」他提高嗓门对特拉弗斯太太说，仿佛约根森当真只是一团无形无觉的幻影。「他是忘了。」

「这位太太一点也不见怪。求之不得被当作这个身份看待呢。」

「什么也没忘！」约根森带着一种幽灵式的执拗咕哝，仿佛说给自己受用，「这世界要变成什么样了？」

===Original===

“It was I who insisted on coming with Captain Lingard,” said Mrs. Travers, treating Jörgenson to a fascinating sweetness of tone.

“That’s what I say! What is the world coming to? Hasn’t King Tom a mind of his own? What has come over him? He’s mad! Leaving his brig with a hundred and twenty born and bred pirates of the worst kind in two praus on the other side of a sandbank. Did you insist on that, too? Has he put himself in the hands of a strange woman?”

Jörgenson seemed to be asking those questions of himself. Mrs. Travers observed the empty stare, the self-communing voice, his unearthly lack of animation. Somehow it made it very easy to speak the whole truth to him.

“No,” she said, “it is I who am altogether in his hands.”

Nobody would have guessed that Jörgenson had heard a single word of that emphatic declaration if he had not addressed himself to Lingard with the question neither more nor less abstracted than all his other speeches.

“Why then did you bring her along?”

“You don’t understand. It was only right and proper. One of the gentlemen is the lady’s husband.”

“Oh, yes,” muttered Jörgenson. “Who’s the other?”

“You have been told. A friend.”

“Poor Mr. d’Alcacer,” said Mrs. Travers. “What bad luck for him to have accepted our invitation. But he is really a mere acquaintance.”

“I hardly noticed him,” observed Lingard, gloomily. “He was talking to you over the back of your chair when I came aboard the yacht as if he had been a very good friend.”

“We always understood each other very well,” said Mrs. Travers, picking up from the rail the long glass that was lying there. “I always liked him, the frankness of his mind, and his great loyalty.”

“What did he do?” asked Lingard.

“He loved,” said Mrs. Travers, lightly. “But that’s an old story.” She raised the glass to her eyes, one arm extended fully to sustain the long tube, and Lingard forgot d’Alcacer in admiring the firmness of her pose and the absolute steadiness of the heavy glass. She was as firm as a rock after all those emotions and all that fatigue.

===Chinese===

「是我自己坚持要同林加德船长一起来的。」特拉弗斯太太对约根森施展出一股迷人的甜腔。

「我就是这话！这世界要变成什么样了？汤姆王自己没有主见了么？他中了什么邪？他疯了！丢下自己的双桅船，隔一道沙洲对面就是两条船里一百二十个天生的、最歹毒的海盗。这一点也是您坚持的么？他把自己交到一个陌生女人手里了？」

约根森仿佛在问自己这些问题。特拉弗斯太太望着那空茫的凝视、那自语自话的嗓音、那非人间的毫无活气。不知怎么，这叫她非常容易对他讲出全部实话。

「不，」她说，「是我整个人都攥在他手里。」

谁也看不出约根森听见了这句斩钉截铁的宣告的半个字——如果他后来没有向林加德发问的话；那问题同他所有的话一样出神：

「那你要带她来做什么？」

「你不明白。这是正理。两位先生之一是这位太太的丈夫。」

「哦，是了。」约根森咕哝，「另一个是谁？」

「告诉你了。一位朋友。」

「可怜的达尔卡塞尔先生。」特拉弗斯太太说，「他真倒运，接受了我们的邀请。不过他实在只能算个泛泛之交。」

「我几乎没留意过他。」林加德阴郁地说，「我上游艇时他正俯在您的椅背上同您说话，像是个很熟的朋友。」

「我们一向彼此很投契。」特拉弗斯太太说着，从栏杆上拾起搁在那里的长望远镜。「我一向喜欢他，喜欢他心地的坦白，还有他的赤诚。」

「他做过什么？」林加德问。

「他爱过。」特拉弗斯太太轻轻地说，「不过那是旧事了。」她举起望远镜贴上眼睛，一条胳膊笔直伸出，托住那根长长的镜筒；林加德把达尔卡塞尔忘在了一边，只顾钦佩她姿势的稳固和那沉重的镜筒的绝对平稳。经了这许多激动、这许多疲乏，她竟还稳如磐石。

===Original===

Mrs. Travers directed the glass instinctively toward the entrance of the lagoon. The smooth water there shone like a piece of silver in the dark frame of the forest. A black speck swept across the field of her vision. It was some time before she could find it again and then she saw, apparently so near as to be within reach of the voice, a small canoe with two people in it. She saw the wet paddles rising and dipping with a flash in the sunlight. She made out plainly the face of Immada, who seemed to be looking straight into the big end of the telescope. The chief and his sister, after resting under the bank for a couple of hours in the middle of the night, had entered the lagoon and were making straight for the hulk. They were already near enough to be perfectly distinguishable to the naked eye if there had been anybody on board to glance that way. But nobody was even thinking of them. They might not have existed except perhaps in the memory of old Jörgenson. But that was mostly busy with all the mysterious secrets of his late tomb.

Mrs. Travers lowered the glass suddenly. Lingard came out from a sort of trance and said:

“Mr. d’Alcacer. Loved! Why shouldn’t he?”

Mrs. Travers looked frankly into Lingard’s gloomy eyes. “It isn’t that alone, of course,” she said. “First of all he knew how to love and then.﻿ ﻿… You don’t know how artificial and barren certain kinds of life can be. But Mr. d’Alcacer’s life was not that. His devotion was worth having.”

“You seem to know a lot about him,” said Lingard, enviously. “Why do you smile?” She continued to smile at him for a little while. The long brass tube over her shoulder shone like gold against the pale fairness of her bare head.﻿—“At a thought,” she answered, preserving the low tone of the conversation into which they had fallen as if their words could have disturbed the self-absorption of Captain H. C. Jörgenson. “At the thought that for all my long acquaintance with Mr. d’Alcacer I don’t know half as much about him as I know about you.”

“Ah, that’s impossible,” contradicted Lingard. “Spaniard or no Spaniard, he is one of your kind.”

“Tarred with the same brush,” murmured Mrs. Travers, with only a half-amused irony. But Lingard continued:

===Chinese===

特拉弗斯太太凭直觉把镜筒对准了潟湖的入口。那边的平水在深色森林的框子里，亮得像一块银。一个黑点扫过她的视界。过了好一会儿她才重新找到它，随后她看见一条小小的独木舟，里面坐着两个人，近得仿佛一唤即应。她望见湿漉漉的船桨在阳光里起落生辉。她清清楚楚辨出了伊玛达的脸，那姑娘仿佛正朝着望远镜的大头直望过来。头人和他的妹妹后半夜在岸影下歇了两个钟头，便进了潟湖，径直朝废船划来。他们已经近得肉眼一望便知——倘若船上有人朝那边看一眼的话。可谁也没想着他们。他们简直可以不存在，也许只存在于老约根森的记忆里。可那记忆大半正忙着应付他新近那座坟墓里全部的神秘隐秘。

特拉弗斯太太蓦地放下了望远镜。林加德从一种出神里醒过来，说：

「达尔卡塞尔先生。爱过！他为什么不能爱？」

特拉弗斯太太坦然直望林加德阴郁的眼睛。「当然不只那一桩。」她说，「首先，他懂得怎样去爱，其次……您不知道某些种生活可以多么造作、多么贫瘠。可达尔卡塞尔先生的生活不是那样。他的爱是值得领受的。」

「您对他的底细知道得真不少。」林加德艳羡地说，「您笑什么？」她又朝他含笑了片刻。搭在她肩头的长长铜筒，衬着她裸露的头颅的白皙，亮得像金子。——「笑一个念头。」她答道，仍压着他们不知不觉落入的低低语调，仿佛他们的话声会搅扰 H·C·约根森船长的自我专注。「笑的是：我同达尔卡塞尔先生相识这么多年，对他的了解，还不及对您的了解一半。」

「啊，那不可能。」林加德反驳道，「西班牙人也罢，不是西班牙人也罢，他是你们一路人。」

「一把刷子刷出来的货色。」特拉弗斯太太喃喃道，讥诮里只有一半的笑意。可林加德接着说：

===Original===

“He was trying to make it up between me and your husband, wasn’t he? I was too angry to pay much attention, but I liked him well enough. What pleased me most was the way in which he gave it up. That was done like a gentleman. Do you understand what I mean, Mrs. Travers?”

“I quite understand.”

“Yes, you would,” he commented, simply. “But just then I was too angry to talk to anybody. And so I cleared out on board my own ship and stayed there, not knowing what to do and wishing you all at the bottom of the sea. Don’t mistake me, Mrs. Travers; it’s you, the people aft, that I wished at the bottom of the sea. I had nothing against the poor devils on board, They would have trusted me quick enough. So I fumed there till﻿—till.﻿ ﻿…”

“Till nine o’clock or a little after,” suggested Mrs. Travers, impenetrably.

“No. Till I remembered you,” said Lingard with the utmost innocence.

“Do you mean to say that you forgot my existence so completely till then? You had spoken to me on board the yacht, you know.”

“Did I? I thought I did. What did I say?”

“You told me not to touch a dusky princess,” answered Mrs. Travers with a short laugh. Then with a visible change of mood as if she had suddenly out of a light heart been recalled to the sense of the true situation: “But indeed I meant no harm to this figure of your dream. And, look over there. She is pursuing you.” Lingard glanced toward the north shore and suppressed an exclamation of remorse. For the second time he discovered that he had forgotten the existence of Hassim and Immada. The canoe was now near enough for its occupants to distinguish plainly the heads of three people above the low bulwark of the *Emma*. Immada let her paddle trail suddenly in the water, with the exclamation, “I see the white woman there.” Her brother looked over his shoulder and the canoe floated, arrested as if by the sudden power of a spell.﻿—“They are no dream to me,” muttered Lingard, sturdily. Mrs. Travers turned abruptly away to look at the further shore. It was still and empty to the naked eye and seemed to quiver in the sunshine like an immense painted curtain lowered upon the unknown.

“Here’s Rajah Hassim coming, Jörgenson. I had an idea he would perhaps stay outside.” Mrs. Travers heard Lingard’s voice at her back and the answering grunt of Jörgenson. She raised deliberately the long glass to her eye, pointing it at the shore.

===Chinese===

「那天他是想在我和你丈夫之间调和，是不是？我当时火太大，没大留意，不过我对他印象不坏。最叫我中意的，是他撒手的样子。那才叫绅士做派。您懂我的意思么，特拉弗斯太太？」

「很懂。」

「是啊，您会懂的。」他简单地说，「可我那时火太大，谁也不愿理。我就回自己船上躲着，不知道该怎么办，直盼着你们全体沉到海底去。别误会，特拉弗斯太太；我盼沉海底的，是你们，是艉楼上的那班人。船上那班可怜鬼，我对他们没有二话。他们本会很快信我的。我就在那儿憋着火，直到——直到……」

「直到九点钟，或者九点过一点。」特拉弗斯太太莫测高深地提示。

「不。直到我想起了您。」林加德以十足的天真说。

「您是说，在那之前您把我的存在忘得那么干净？您可在游艇上同我说过话的，您记得。」

「说过么？我记得说过。我说了什么？」

「您叫我别碰一位黑公主。」特拉弗斯太太短短一笑，答道。随后神色一变，仿佛从一阵轻快里蓦地被唤回对真实处境的知觉：「可我对您那梦中人实在没有恶意。您看那边。她追您来了。」林加德朝北岸瞥了一眼，压下一声悔恨的惊呼。这是他第二次发现，自己把哈西姆和伊玛达的存在忘了。独木舟已经近得舟中人能清清楚楚辨出「埃玛号」低舷墙上的三颗人头。伊玛达猛地让桨叶拖进水里，喊了一声：「我看见那个白人女人在那儿。」她哥哥回头一望，独木舟便停住了，仿佛被一道咒法的力量定住。——「他们对我可不是梦。」林加德倔强地咕哝。特拉弗斯太太猛地转过身去望对岸。肉眼望去，那边静着、空着，在阳光下微微颤动，像一幅垂在未知之前的巨幅画幕。

「哈西姆拉者来了，约根森。我还当他兴许留在外头呢。」特拉弗斯太太听见背后林加德的声音和约根森应承的一声咕噜。她从容举起长镜贴上眼睛，对准了岸那边。

===Original===

She distinguished plainly now the colours in the flutter of the streamers above the brown roofs of the large Settlement, the stir of palm groves, the black shadows inland and the dazzling white beach of coral sand all ablaze in its formidable mystery. She swept the whole range of the view and was going to lower the glass when from behind the massive angle of the stockade there stepped out into the brilliant immobility of the landscape a man in a long white gown and with an enormous black turban surmounting a dark face. Slow and grave he paced the beach ominously in the sunshine, an enigmatical figure in an Oriental tale with something weird and menacing in its sudden emergence and lonely progress.

With an involuntary gasp Mrs. Travers lowered the glass. All at once behind her back she heard a low musical voice beginning to pour out incomprehensible words in a tone of passionate pleading. Hassim and Immada had come on board and had approached Lingard. Yes! It was intolerable to feel that this flow of soft speech which had no meaning for her could make its way straight into that man’s heart.

===Chinese===

此刻她清清楚楚分辨出大聚落棕色屋顶上飘动的彩旗的颜色、棕榈林的摇曳、内陆的黑影，还有那白沙珊瑚滩的灼目白光，在它骇人的神秘里一片辉耀。她扫过整个视野，正要放下望远镜，忽见栅寨沉重的寨角后面，走出一个人，来到那片明亮的静景之中：一袭白色长袍，一大团黑头巾压着一张黝黑的脸。他在阳光里沿着海滩缓缓而郑重地踱步，带着不祥之兆，一个东方故事里的人物，突兀而出、独行踽踽，透着诡异与威胁。

特拉弗斯太太不由自主地一声轻吁，放下了望远镜。就在这时，她背后响起一个低低的、音乐般的声音，用恳切的哀求的调子，倾吐着一连串她听不懂的话。哈西姆和伊玛达已经上了船，走到林加德跟前。是啊！一想到这股软语对她毫无意义，却能径直淌进那个男人的心里，就叫人受不了。

### I / 一
===Original===

“May I come in?”

“Yes,” said a voice within. “The door is open.” It had a wooden latch. Mr. Travers lifted it while the voice of his wife continued as he entered. “Did you imagine I had locked myself in? Did you ever know me lock myself in?”

Mr. Travers closed the door behind him. “No, it has never come to that,” he said in a tone that was not conciliatory. In that place which was a room in a wooden hut and had a square opening without glass but with a half-closed shutter he could not distinguish his wife very well at once. She was sitting in an armchair and what he could see best was her fair hair all loose over the back of the chair. There was a moment of silence. The measured footsteps of two men pacing athwart the quarterdeck of the dead ship *Emma* commanded by the derelict shade of Jörgenson could be heard outside.

Jörgenson, on taking up his dead command, had a house of thin boards built on the after deck for his own accommodation and that of Lingard during his flying visits to the Shore of Refuge. A narrow passage divided it in two and Lingard’s side was furnished with a camp bedstead, a rough desk, and a rattan armchair. On one of his visits Lingard had brought with him a black seaman’s chest and left it there. Apart from these objects and a small looking-glass worth about half a crown and nailed to the wall there was nothing else in there whatever. What was on Jörgenson’s side of the deckhouse no one had seen, but from external evidence one could infer the existence of a set of razors.

The erection of that primitive deckhouse was a matter of propriety rather than of necessity. It was proper that the white men should have a place to themselves on board, but Lingard was perfectly accurate when he told Mrs. Travers that he had never slept there once. His practice was to sleep on deck. As to Jörgenson, if he did sleep at all he slept very little. It might have been said that he haunted rather than commanded the *Emma*. His white form flitted here and there in the night or stood for hours, silent, contemplating the sombre glimmer of the lagoon. Mr. Travers’ eyes accustomed gradually to the dusk of the place could now distinguish more of his wife’s person than the great mass of honey-coloured hair. He saw her face, the dark eyebrows and her eyes that seemed profoundly black in the half light. He said:

===Chinese===

「可以进来么？」

「可以。」里面一个声音说，「门开着。」门上是一根木闩。特拉弗斯先生掀起它进门时，妻子的声音还在继续。「你还当我把自己锁在里头了？你几时见我把自己锁在里头过？」

特拉弗斯先生随手带上门。「没有，还从没到过那一步。」他说，语气毫不缓和。这地方是木屋里的一个单间，有一个不上玻璃的方窗洞，百叶半掩；他一时辨不清妻子。她坐在一把扶手椅里，他看得最清的，是她披散在椅背上的一大蓬浅色头发。片刻寂静。外面听得见两个人匀整的脚步，在死船「埃玛号」的艉楼上踱来踱去——这条船由约根森那道弃船的幽灵统辖着。

约根森重接他那死去的统辖之时，在艉部甲板上叫人用薄板造了一间屋子，供自己住，也供林加德偶尔来避难之岸时落脚。一条窄过道把它隔成两半；林加德那一侧陈设着一张行军床、一张粗木书桌、一把藤扶手椅。有 一回林加德运来一只黑漆水手箱，撂在了那儿。除了这些，加上一面值约两先令六便士、钉在墙上的小镜子，再没有别的东西。甲板室那一边约根森有些什么，谁也没见过；不过凭外在的迹象，可以推断存在着一副剃刀。

搭这间原始的甲板室，图的是体统，不是必需。白人在船上有个自己的地方是体统；可林加德对特拉弗斯太太说他从没在里面睡过一夜，这话千真万确。他惯于睡在甲板上。至于约根森，他若真睡，也睡得极少。与其说他统领「埃玛号」，不如说他出没于「埃玛号」。他那白色的身影夜里到处飘忽，或者一站几个钟头，默默凝望潟湖幽暗的微光。特拉弗斯先生的眼睛渐渐习惯了屋里的昏暗，如今已能从那一大团蜜色头发之外辨出妻子更多的形貌。他看见了她的脸、深色的眉毛，还有半明半暗里显得乌黑的眼睛。他说：

===Original===

“You couldn’t have done so here. There is neither lock nor bolt.”

“Isn’t there? I didn’t notice. I would know how to protect myself without locks and bolts.”

“I am glad to hear it,” said Mr. Travers in a sullen tone and fell silent again surveying the woman in the chair. “Indulging your taste for fancy dress,” he went on with faint irony.

Mrs. Travers clasped her hands behind her head. The wide sleeves slipping back bared her arms to her shoulders. She was wearing a Malay thin cotton jacket, cut low in the neck without a collar and fastened with wrought silver clasps from the throat downward. She had replaced her yachting skirt by a blue check sarong embroidered with threads of gold. Mr. Travers’ eyes travelling slowly down attached themselves to the gleaming instep of an agitated foot from which hung a light leather sandal.

“I had no clothes with me but what I stood in,” said Mrs. Travers. “I found my yachting costume too heavy. It was intolerable. I was soaked in dew when I arrived. So when these things were produced for my inspection.﻿ ﻿…”

“By enchantment,” muttered Mr. Travers in a tone too heavy for sarcasm.

“No. Out of that chest. There are very fine stuffs there.”

“No doubt,” said Mr. Travers. “The man wouldn’t be above plundering the natives.﻿ ﻿…” He sat down heavily on the chest. “A most appropriate costume for this farce,” he continued. “But do you mean to wear it in open daylight about the decks?”

“Indeed I do,” said Mrs. Travers. “D’Alcacer has seen me already and he didn’t seem shocked.”

“You should,” said Mr. Travers, “try to get yourself presented with some bangles for your ankles so that you may jingle as you walk.”

“Bangles are not necessities,” said Mrs. Travers in a weary tone and with the fixed upward look of a person unwilling to relinquish her dream. Mr. Travers dropped the subject to ask:

“And how long is this farce going to last?”

Mrs. Travers unclasped her hands, lowered her glance, and changed her whole pose in a moment.

“What do you mean by farce? What farce?”

“The one which is being played at my expense.”

“You believe that?”

===Chinese===

「你在这儿想锁也锁不成。既没有锁，也没有闩。」

「是么？我没留意。没有锁和闩，我也知道怎么护住自己。」

「听你这么说我很高兴。」特拉弗斯先生瓮声瓮气地说，随即又不作声，打量着椅子里的女人。「由着你的性子穿奇装异服。」他带着淡淡的讥诮接着说。

特拉弗斯太太双手交叠在脑后。宽大的衣袖滑落，臂膀一直裸到肩头。她穿着一件马来薄布短褂，领口开得低，没有领子，从咽喉往下用錾花的银扣一路扣拢。游艇裙子已换成一条金线绣花的蓝格子纱笼。特拉弗斯先生的目光慢慢向下，落在一只躁动不宁的脚的亮闪闪的脚背上，脚上挂着一只轻便的皮凉鞋。

「我身上除了穿着的，什么衣裳也没带。」特拉弗斯太太说，「我嫌游艇的行头太沉，实在受不了。到的时候露水把我浸了个透。所以等这几件东西拿出来给我挑的时候……」

「凭空变出来的。」特拉弗斯先生咕哝，语气沉重得不像讥诮。

「不。从那只箱子里。里头料子都极好。」

「想必。」特拉弗斯先生说，「那人不至于不屑于劫掠土人……」他重重地在箱子上坐下。「配这出闹剧，倒是一身再合适不过的行头。」他接着说，「可你打算白天就这么在甲板上走动？」

「正是。」特拉弗斯太太说，「达尔卡塞尔已经见过我这身，并没有显得吃惊。」

「你该，」特拉弗斯先生说，「去讨几副脚镯戴上，走起路来叮当作响。」

「镯子不算必需品。」特拉弗斯太太倦倦地说，眼睛定定地朝上望着，像一个不肯从梦中撒手的人。特拉弗斯先生撇开这话题，问：

「这出闹剧还要演多久？」

特拉弗斯太太松开双手，垂下目光，整个姿势霎时变了。

「你说的闹剧指什么？什么闹剧？」

「拿我当冤大头演的这一出。」

「你真这么想？」

===Original===

“Not only believe. I feel deeply that it is so. At my expense. It’s a most sinister thing,” Mr. Travers pursued, still with downcast eyes and in an unforgiving tone. “I must tell you that when I saw you in that courtyard in a crowd of natives and leaning on that man’s arm, it gave me quite a shock.”

“Did I, too, look sinister?” said Mrs. Travers, turning her head slightly toward her husband. “And yet I assure you that I was glad, profoundly glad, to see you safe from danger for a time at least. To gain time is everything.﻿ ﻿…”

“I ask myself,” Mr. Travers meditated aloud, “was I ever in danger? Am I safe now? I don’t know. I can’t tell. No! All this seems an abominable farce.”

There was that in his tone which made his wife continue to look at him with awakened interest. It was obvious that he suffered from a distress which was not the effect of fear; and Mrs. Travers’ face expressed real concern till he added in a freezing manner: “The question, however, is as to your discretion.”

She leaned back again in the chair and let her hands rest quietly in her lap. “Would you have preferred me to remain outside, in the yacht, in the near neighbourhood of these wild men who captured you? Or do you think that they, too, were got up to carry on a farce?”

“Most decidedly.” Mr. Travers raised his head, though of course not his voice. “You ought to have remained in the yacht amongst white men, your servants, the sailing-master, the crew whose duty it was to.﻿ ﻿… Who would have been ready to die for you.”

===Chinese===

「何止这么想。我痛感正是如此。拿我当冤大头。这事阴险透顶。」特拉弗斯先生接着说，眼睛仍低垂着，语气毫不宽假。「我得告诉你，我看见你站在那个院场里、夹在一群土人中间、倚着那个人的胳膊，着实吃了一惊。」

「那么，我看上去也阴险么？」特拉弗斯太太说着，头微微朝丈夫偏了偏，「可我向你担保，看见你至少暂时脱了险，我是高兴的，由衷地高兴。争取时间便是一切……」

「我自问，」特拉弗斯先生出声地寻思，「我难道真有过危险？我如今安全了么？我不知道。我说不上来。不！这一切像一出可憎的闹剧。」

他的语气里有种叫妻子继续带着被唤起的兴味注视他的东西。他显然受着一种煎熬，而这煎熬并非出于恐惧；特拉弗斯太太的脸上露出真切的关注，直到他冷冰冰地补了一句：「不过，问题在于你的言行是否检点。」

她又靠回椅子里，两手安静地搁在膝上。「你是愿意我留在外头、留在游艇上、待在这班捉你们的野人近旁么？还是说，你以为他们也是装扮出来演闹剧的？」

「十有八九。」特拉弗斯先生抬起头，当然嗓门并不抬高。「你本该留在游艇上，同白人们、你的仆人们、那位帆船长、那班职责所在的船员们在一起……他们本可以为你去死的。」

===Original===

“I wonder why they should have﻿—and why I should have asked them for that sacrifice. However, I have no doubt they would have died. Or would you have preferred me to take up my quarters on board that man’s brig? We were all fairly safe there. The real reason why I insisted on coming in here was to be nearer to you﻿—to see for myself what could be or was being done.﻿ ﻿… But really if you want me to explain my motives then I may just as well say nothing. I couldn’t remain outside for days without news, in a state of horrible doubt. We couldn’t even tell whether you and d’Alcacer were still alive till we arrived here. You might have been actually murdered on the sandbank, after Rajah Hassim and that girl had gone away; or killed while going up the river. And I wanted to know at once, as soon as possible. It was a matter of impulse. I went off in what I stood in without delaying a moment.”

“Yes,” said Mr. Travers. “And without even thinking of having a few things put up for me in a bag. No doubt you were in a state of excitement. Unless you took such a tragic view that it seemed to you hardly worth while to bother about my clothes.”

“It was absolutely the impulse of the moment. I could have done nothing else. Won’t you give me credit for it?”

Mr. Travers raised his eyes again to his wife’s face. He saw it calm, her attitude reposeful. Till then his tone had been resentful, dull, without sarcasm. But now he became slightly pompous.

“No. As a matter of fact, as a matter of experience, I can’t credit you with the possession of feelings appropriate to your origin, social position, and the ideas of the class to which you belong. It was the heaviest disappointment of my life. I had made up my mind not to mention it as long as I lived. This, however, seems an occasion which you have provoked yourself. It isn’t at all a solemn occasion. I don’t look upon it as solemn at all. It’s very disagreeable and humiliating. But it has presented itself. You have never taken a serious interest in the activities of my life which of course are its distinction and its value. And why you should be carried away suddenly by a feeling toward the mere man I don’t understand.”

===Chinese===

「我不明白他们为什么非去死不可——也不明白我凭什么要他们作这样的牺牲。不过，我毫不怀疑他们死得成。要不，你宁愿我住到那个人的双桅船上去？我们在那边都相当安全。我坚持进到这儿来的真正缘由，是为了离你近些——亲眼看看能做什么、在做什么……可你既要我解释动机，那我还不如什么都不说。我做不到一连几天待在外头没有消息，悬在一团可怕的疑虑里。到这里之前，我们连你和达尔卡塞尔是否还活着都不知道。说不定拉者·哈西姆和那姑娘一走，你们就在沙洲上被杀了；或是上河的路上遇了害。而我要立刻知道，越快越好。那是一时冲动。我穿着一身随身衣裳就走了，一刻也没耽搁。」

「是啊。」特拉弗斯先生说，「连想也没想起给我打一个行李包。你无疑是在兴奋之中。除非你是把事情看得那样悲壮，觉得犯不上再操心我的衣裳。」

「完完全全是一时的冲动。换了我做不到别的。这一点你就不肯体谅么？」

特拉弗斯先生又抬眼望妻子的脸。他看见它平静，她的姿态安详。到那时为止，他的语气一直是怨恨的、沉闷的、不带讥诮的。可此刻他微微摆出了架子。

「不。事实上，凭经验而论，我没法相信你具有同你的出身、你的社会地位、你所属阶层的观念相称的情感。这是我这辈子最沉重的一次失望。我本拿定主意，此生绝口不提。可这一次，似乎是你自己招出来的机会。这绝不是什么庄重的场合。我一点也不把它看作庄重。它非常讨厌，非常丢脸。可它自己找上门来了。你从没对我一生的种种活动有过认真的兴趣——那当然正是我一生的荣光与价值。而你会突然为『我这个人』动起情来，我不明白。」

===Original===

“Therefore you don’t approve,” Mrs. Travers commented in an even tone. “But I assure you, you may safely. My feeling was of the most conventional nature, exactly as if the whole world were looking on. After all, we are husband and wife. It’s eminently fitting that I should be concerned about your fate. Even the man you distrust and dislike so much (the warmest feeling, let me tell you, that I ever saw you display) even that man found my conduct perfectly proper. His own word. Proper. So eminently proper that it altogether silenced his objections.”

Mr. Travers shifted uneasily on his seat.

“It’s my belief, Edith, that if you had been a man you would have led a most irregular life. You would have been a frank adventurer. I mean morally. It has been a great grief to me. You have a scorn in you for the serious side of life, for the ideas and the ambitions of the social sphere to which you belong.”

He stopped because his wife had clasped again her hands behind her head and was no longer looking at him.

“It’s perfectly obvious,” he began again. “We have been living amongst most distinguished men and women and your attitude to them has been always so﻿—so negative! You would never recognize the importance of achievements, of acquired positions. I don’t remember you ever admiring frankly any political or social success. I ask myself what after all you could possibly have expected from life.”

“I could never have expected to hear such a speech from you. As to what I did expect!﻿ ﻿… I must have been very stupid.”

“No, you are anything but that,” declared Mr. Travers, conscientiously. “It isn’t stupidity.” He hesitated for a moment. “It’s a kind of wilfulness, I think. I preferred not to think about this grievous difference in our points of view, which, you will admit, I could not have possibly foreseen before we.﻿ ﻿…”

A sort of solemn embarrassment had come over Mr. Travers. Mrs. Travers, leaning her chin on the palm of her hand, stared at the bare matchboard side of the hut.

“Do you charge me with profound girlish duplicity?” she asked, very softly.

The inside of the deckhouse was full of stagnant heat perfumed by a slight scent which seemed to emanate from the loose mass of Mrs. Travers’ hair. Mr. Travers evaded the direct question which struck him as lacking fineness even to the point of impropriety.

===Chinese===

「所以你不以为然。」特拉弗斯太太用平缓的语气评道，「可我向你担保，你尽管放心。我的情感再合乎常规不过，就仿佛全世界都在旁边看着。说到底，我们是夫妻。我关切你的命运，是最得体的不过的事。就连那个你如此猜忌、如此厌恶的人（我得说，这是我见过的你最热烈的情感）——就连那个人，也觉得我的举止无可挑剔。这是他的原话。『得体』。得体到把他的反对全压了下去。」

特拉弗斯先生在座上不安地挪了挪。

「伊迪丝，我的信念是：你若是个男人，准会过一种极不检点的生活。你会成为一个坦坦荡荡的冒险家。我是就品性而言。这一直是我极大的悲痛。你心里对生活严肃的一面、对你所属社交圈的观念与抱负，藏着一种轻蔑。」

他停住了，因为妻子又把双手叠到了脑后，不再看他。

「再明白不过。」他又开了口，「我们一向生活在最显赫的男女中间，可你对他们的态度总是那么——那么消极！你从不肯承认成就与既得地位的重要。我不记得你可曾坦率地钦佩过任何政治或社交上的成功。我自问，你到底还能指望从生活里得到什么。」

「我从没指望过会从你口中听到这样一篇话。至于我当初指望的是什么！……我必定蠢得很。」

「不，你绝不蠢。」特拉弗斯先生凭良心声明，「那不是蠢。」他迟疑了一下。「我想，是一种任性。我们观点上这可悲的分歧，我原先宁可不去想它——你也会承认，在我们……之前，我根本无从预见。」

一种庄重的窘迫罩上了特拉弗斯先生。特拉弗斯太太用手掌托着下巴，呆望着木屋光板板的板壁。

「你这是指控我怀着少女深藏的两面心肠么？」她极轻地问。

甲板室里满是凝滞的热气，一缕淡淡的香似从特拉弗斯太太那蓬散的头发里散发出来。特拉弗斯先生避开了这个直截了当的问题——它在他听來粗鄙得近乎失体。

===Original===

“I must suppose that I was not in the calm possession of my insight and judgment in those days,” he said. “I﻿—I was not in a critical state of mind at the time,” he admitted further; but even after going so far he did not look up at his wife and therefore missed something like the ghost of a smile on Mrs. Travers’ lips. That smile was tinged with scepticism which was too deep-seated for anything but the faintest expression. Therefore she said nothing, and Mr. Travers went on as if thinking aloud:

“Your conduct was, of course, above reproach; but you made for yourself a detestable reputation of mental superiority, expressed ironically. You inspired mistrust in the best people. You were never popular.”

“I was bored,” murmured Mrs. Travers in a reminiscent tone and with her chin resting in the hollow of her hand.

Mr. Travers got up from the seaman’s chest as unexpectedly as if he had been stung by a wasp, but, of course, with a much slower and more solemn motion.

“The matter with you, Edith, is that at heart you are perfectly primitive.” Mrs. Travers stood up, too, with a supple, leisurely movement, and raising her hands to her hair turned half away with a pensive remark:

“Imperfectly civilized.”

“Imperfectly disciplined,” corrected Mr. Travers after a moment of dreary meditation.

She let her arms fall and turned her head.

“No, don’t say that,” she protested with strange earnestness. “I am the most severely disciplined person in the world. I am tempted to say that my discipline has stopped at nothing short of killing myself. I suppose you can hardly understand what I mean.”

Mr. Travers made a slight grimace at the floor.

“I shall not try,” he said. “It sounds like something that a barbarian, hating the delicate complexities and the restraints of a nobler life, might have said. From you it strikes me as wilful bad taste.﻿ ﻿… I have often wondered at your tastes. You have always liked extreme opinions, exotic costumes, lawless characters, romantic personalities﻿—like d’Alcacer﻿ ﻿…”

“Poor Mr. d’Alcacer,” murmured Mrs. Travers.

“A man without any ideas of duty or usefulness,” said Mr. Travers, acidly. “What are you pitying him for?”

===Chinese===

「我只能假定，那些日子我没有平心静气地拥有我的洞察与判断。」他说，「我——我当时的心境不在批判的状态。」他进一步承认道；可即便说得这样远，他也没有抬眼看妻子，因而错过了特拉弗斯太太唇边那一丝近乎幽灵的微笑。那微笑染着怀疑，那怀疑根深蒂固，只余最淡的表露。于是她一言不发，特拉弗斯先生像自言自语似的接着说：

「你的品行当然无可指摘；可你给自己挣来了一个可憎的名声——心智优越，出之以讥诮。你在最好的人们心里唤起猜疑。你从不讨好。」

「我是烦。」特拉弗斯太太带着追忆的口气喃喃说，下巴仍搁在掌心里。

特拉弗斯先生从水手箱上站起来，突兀得像被黄蜂蜇了一下，当然，动作要迟缓庄重得多。

「你的毛病，伊迪丝，在于骨子里你是个十足的原人。」特拉弗斯太太也站起来，动作柔缓从容，双手抬向头发，半转过身，怅然道：

「文明得不完善。」

「管教得不完善。」特拉弗斯先生经过一阵沉闷的冥想后纠正道。

她放下手臂，回过头。

「不，别这么说。」她带着奇特的恳切抗议道，「我是世上受管教最严的人。我简直想说，我的管教差一点就到了弄死我自己的地步。我想你很难明白我的意思。」

特拉弗斯先生朝地板微微做了个苦脸。

「我也不想试。」他说，「这话听着像一个野蛮人说的——仇恨更高贵生活的精微繁复与约束的野蛮人。出自你的口，我只觉得是任性的恶俗。……我对你的趣味常常纳闷。你一向喜欢极端的见解、异国的装束、无法无天的人物、浪漫的性情——像达尔卡塞尔……」

「可怜的达尔卡塞尔先生。」特拉弗斯太太喃喃道。

「一个毫无责任观念、毫无用处观念的人。」特拉弗斯先生刻薄地说，「你可怜他什么？」

===Original===

“Why! For finding himself in this position out of mere good-nature. He had nothing to expect from joining our voyage, no advantage for his political ambitions or anything of the kind. I suppose you asked him on board to break our tête-à-tête which must have grown wearisome to you.”

“I am never bored,” declared Mr. Travers. “D’Alcacer seemed glad to come. And, being a Spaniard, the horrible waste of time cannot matter to him in the least.”

“Waste of time!” repeated Mrs. Travers, indignantly.

“He may yet have to pay for his good nature with his life.”

Mr. Travers could not conceal a movement of anger.

“Ah! I forgot those assumptions,” he said between his clenched teeth. “He is a mere Spaniard. He takes this farcical conspiracy with perfect nonchalance. Decayed races have their own philosophy.”

“He takes it with a dignity of his own.”

“I don’t know what you call his dignity. I should call it lack of self-respect.”

“Why? Because he is quiet and courteous, and reserves his judgment. And allow me to tell you, Martin, that you are not taking our troubles very well.”

“You can’t expect from me all those foreign affectations. I am not in the habit of compromising with my feelings.”

===Chinese===

「可怜他只因一片好心落到了这步田地。他参加我们这趟航行，本来就无所图，对他的政治抱负或任何这一类的事没有半点好处。我想，你请他上船，是为了拆散我们俩的对坐——那对你必定早已腻烦了。」

「我从不烦。」特拉弗斯先生断言，「达尔卡塞尔来得很乐意。再说他是西班牙人，这可怕的蹉跎光阴，他毫不在乎。」

「蹉跎光阴！」特拉弗斯太太愤然重复。

「他说不定还得拿性命来抵他那片好心呢。」

特拉弗斯先生掩不住一阵怒意。

「啊！我忘了那些前提。」他咬着牙说，「他不过是个西班牙人。他对这场滑稽的阴谋处之泰然。衰败的种族有衰败种族的哲学。」

「他以他自有的尊严承受。」

「我不知道你管那叫什么尊严。我只管它叫没有自尊。」

「为什么？因为他安静、有礼，把判断留着不下？也请容我说一句，马丁，你把我们这场祸事承受得可不怎么好。」

「你不能指望我有那一套外国人的做作。我不惯同自己的情感做交易。」

===Original===

Mrs. Travers turned completely round and faced her husband. “You sulk,” she said.﻿ ﻿… Mr. Travers jerked his head back a little as if to let the word go past.﻿—“I am outraged,” he declared. Mrs. Travers recognized there something like real suffering.﻿—“I assure you,” she said, seriously (for she was accessible to pity), “I assure you that this strange Lingard has no idea of your importance. He doesn’t know anything of your social and political position and still less of your great ambitions.” Mr. Travers listened with some attention.﻿—“Couldn’t you have enlightened him?” he asked.﻿—“It would have been no use; his mind is fixed upon his own position and upon his own sense of power.
