A bitter little laugh floated out into the starlight. Mrs. Travers heard Lingard move suddenly away from her side. She didn’t change her pose by a hair’s breadth. Presently she heard d’Alcacer coming out of the Cage. His cultivated voice asked half playfully:

“Have you had a satisfactory conversation? May I be told something of it?”

“Mr. d’Alcacer, you are curious.”

“Well, in our position, I confess.﻿ ﻿… You are our only refuge, remember.”

===Chinese===

「我叫得上在乎的没有几个。我没有朋友。哦对，他们是这么叫我……」

「你没有朋友？」

「没有。」他断然说，「我这样的人没有伙伴。」

「很有可能。」特拉弗斯太太自言自语地咕哝。

「没有，连约根森也不算。老疯子约根森。他也叫我汤姆王。这名字值多少，你可想见了。」

「是，我看见了。或者不如说听见了。那可怜的人没有分寸，而这一层最要紧。那么，假如我偶尔、就我们两人之间，管你叫汤姆王呢。」特拉弗斯太太的声音在夜里征询着，飘渺而试探；夜已把她整个人裹进一团无色的朦胧里。

她在寂静里等着，臂肘支在栏杆上，脸埋在手里，仿佛已忘了自己说过什么。她听见肘边深沉的低语：

「让我听听你说说看。」

她纹丝没动。幽暗的潟湖微微闪着星星的倒影。

「哦，好，我会让你听见的。」她对着星光朗朗的空际说，声音温软不带一点重音，说着说着却悄悄变了。「汤姆王，我从没指望过，你会从你那没有朋友的神秘里走出来同我说话，往后可别后悔。那是多少天以前的事了！如今又过去一天。告诉我，这样的日子还得有多少？这些晃得人眼花的日子，这些没有一点声响的夜。」

「耐心些。」他喃喃道，「别向我要办不到的事。」

「你我又怎么知道什么办得到？」她带着奇特的轻蔑低声说，「你连猜都不敢猜。可我告诉你，每过一天，都比前一天更受不了。」

那低语里的激愤像一刀扎进他胸膛。「你要我说什么呢？」他仿佛绝望似的喃喃道，「记住，每落一次日，就少一天。你当我愿意你留在这儿？」

一声苦笑飘进星光里。特拉弗斯太太听见林加德猛地离开了她身边。她的姿势分毫未变。过了一会儿，她听见达尔卡塞尔走出围屋。他那有教养的声音半打趣地问：

「谈得还满意么？可以让我略知一二么？」

「达尔卡塞尔先生，你可真好奇。」

「唉，处在咱们的境地，我招认……别忘了，您是我们唯一的依靠。」

===Original===

“You want to know what we were talking about,” said Mrs. Travers, altering slowly her position so as to confront d’Alcacer whose face was almost undistinguishable. “Oh, well, then, we talked about opera, the realities and illusions of the stage, of dresses, of people’s names, and things of that sort.”

“Nothing of importance,” he said courteously. Mrs. Travers moved forward and he stepped to one side. Inside the Cage two Malay hands were hanging round lanterns, the light of which fell on Mr. Travers’ bowed head as he sat in his chair.

===Chinese===

「你想知道我们谈了些什么。」特拉弗斯太太说着，缓缓变换姿势，正对达尔卡塞尔——他的脸几乎辨不出了。「那么好吧，我们谈了歌剧，谈了舞台的真实与幻象，谈了衣裳，谈了人名，诸如此类。」

「都不是要紧事。」他客气地说。特拉弗斯太太向前走，他侧身让开。围屋里两只马来人的手正往四下挂灯，灯光落在特拉弗斯先生低垂的头上，他坐在自己的椅子里。

===Original===

When they were all assembled for the evening meal Jörgenson strolled up from nowhere in particular as his habit was, and speaking through the muslin announced that Captain Lingard begged to be excused from joining the company that evening. Then he strolled away. From that moment till they got up from the table and the camp bedsteads were brought in not twenty words passed between the members of the party within the net. The strangeness of their situation made all attempts to exchange ideas very arduous; and apart from that each had thoughts which it was distinctly useless to communicate to the others. Mr. Travers had abandoned himself to his sense of injury. He did not so much brood as rage inwardly in a dull, dispirited way. The impossibility of asserting himself in any manner galled his very soul. D’Alcacer was extremely puzzled. Detached in a sense from the life of men perhaps as much even as Jörgenson himself, he took yet a reasonable interest in the course of events and had not lost all his sense of self-preservation. Without being able to appreciate the exact values of the situation he was not one of those men who are ever completely in the dark in any given set of circumstances. Without being humorous he was a good-humoured man. His habitual, gentle smile was a true expression. More of a European than of a Spaniard he had that truly aristocratic nature which is inclined to credit every honest man with something of its own nobility and in its judgment is altogether independent of class feeling. He believed Lingard to be an honest man and he never troubled his head to classify him, except in the sense that he found him an interesting character. He had a sort of esteem for the outward personality and the bearing of that seaman. He found in him also the distinction of being nothing of a type. He was a specimen to be judged only by its own worth. With his natural gift of insight d’Alcacer told himself that many overseas adventurers of history were probably less worthy because obviously they must have been less simple. He didn’t, however, impart those thoughts formally to Mrs. Travers. In fact he avoided discussing Lingard with Mrs. Travers who, he thought, was quite intelligent enough to appreciate the exact shade of his attitude. If that shade was fine, Mrs. Travers was fine, too; and there was no need to discuss the colours of this adventure.

===Chinese===

等人齐了要用晚饭时，约根森照他的老习惯不知从哪儿踱了出来，隔着纱网通报说，林加德船长今晚恕不奉陪。说完又踱走了。从那一刻起，直到众人离席、行军床抬进来，网内几个人之间说过的话不足二十句。处境的离奇叫一切交换想法的尝试都变得艰涩；何况各人心里都有一些念头，传给别人分明无用。特拉弗斯先生沉进了受辱之感里。与其说他在郁思，不如说他在以一种沉闷而灰心的方式暗自盛怒。无从以任何方式张扬自己，这扎痛了他的灵魂。达尔卡塞尔则大惑不解。他在某种意义上脱离了众人的生活，也许同约根森本人一样远，却仍对事变的进程怀着一份清醒的兴趣，也还没丢尽自保的本能。他虽掂不出局面的确切分量，却不是那种在任何境况下都全然蒙在鼓里的人。他不幽默，却是个好性子的人。他惯常那温和的微笑是真实的流露。与其说他是西班牙人，不如说更是欧洲人；他有那种真正的贵族天性，惯于把自家的贵气分一份记在每个诚实人账上，下判断时全然不掺阶级的情感。他相信林加德是个诚实的人，也从不费神给他归类，只觉得他是个有趣的人物。他对这个水手的外表人格和做派怀有一种敬意。他还发现他另有一种出众之处：绝不成其为一个类型。他是一件只能凭自身价值来评判的样品。凭着天生的识人眼力，达尔卡塞尔心想，史册上那些海外的冒险家，多半还不及他，因为显而易见，他们必不如他单纯。不过这些想法他并没有郑重地讲给特拉弗斯太太听。实际上他避免同她讨论林加德；他想，以她的聪明，自能掂出他态度的确切色调。那色调既精微，特拉弗斯太太也精微；这场冒险的颜色，无须讨论。

===Original===

Moreover, she herself seemed to avoid all direct discussion of the Lingard element in their fate. D’Alcacer was fine enough to be aware that those two seemed to understand each other in a way that was not obvious even to themselves. Whenever he saw them together he was always much tempted to observe them. And he yielded to the temptation. The fact of one’s life depending on the phases of an obscure action authorizes a certain latitude of behaviour. He had seen them together repeatedly, communing openly or apart, and there was in their way of joining each other, in their poses and their ways of separating, something special and characteristic and pertaining to themselves only, as if they had been made for each other.

===Chinese===

况且她自己似乎也回避一切关于命运中的林加德因素的正面讨论。达尔卡塞尔精微得很，看得出那两个人似乎彼此相知，以一种连他们自己也未必了然的方式。每逢见他们在一起，他总禁不住观察他们。而他也果然纵容了这份禁不住。性命系于一场幽暗行动的种种阶段，这事实授权人享有某种行为上的宽纵。他屡屡见他们在一起，或公开或独处地神交；在他们彼此靠近的方式里、在他们的姿态和分开的方式里，有某种独有的、只属于他们两个的东西，仿佛他们是为彼此而生的。

===Original===

What he couldn’t understand was why Mrs. Travers should have put off his natural curiosity as to her latest conference with the Man of Fate by an incredible statement as to the nature of the conversation. Talk about dresses, opera, people’s names. He couldn’t take this seriously. She might have invented, he thought, something more plausible; or simply have told him that this was not for him to know. She ought to have known that he would not have been offended. Couldn’t she have seen already that he accepted the complexion of mystery in her relation to that man completely, unquestionably; as though it had been something preordained from the very beginning of things? But he was not annoyed with Mrs. Travers. After all it might have been true. She would talk exactly as she liked, and even incredibly, if it so pleased her, and make the man hang on her lips. And likewise she was capable of making the man talk about anything by a power of inspiration for reasons simple or perverse. Opera! Dresses! Yes﻿—about Shakespeare and the musical glasses! For a mere whim or for the deepest purpose. Women worthy of the name were like that. They were very wonderful. They rose to the occasion and sometimes above the occasion when things were bound to occur that would be comic or tragic (as it happened) but generally charged with trouble even to innocent beholders. D’Alcacer thought these thoughts without bitterness and even without irony. With his half-secret social reputation as a man of one great passion in a world of mere intrigues he liked all women. He liked them in their sentiment and in their hardness, in the tragic character of their foolish or clever impulses, at which he looked with a sort of tender seriousness.

He didn’t take a favourable view of the position but he considered Mrs. Travers’ statement about operas and dresses as a warning to keep off the subject. For this reason he remained silent through the meal.

When the bustle of clearing away the table was over he strolled toward Mrs. Travers and remarked very quietly:

“I think that in keeping away from us this evening the Man of Fate was well inspired. We dined like a lot of Carthusian monks.”

“You allude to our silence?”

“It was most scrupulous. If we had taken an eternal vow we couldn’t have kept it better.”

“Did you feel bored?”

===Chinese===

他不明白的是，特拉弗斯太太何以要用一套叫人难以置信的说辞——说什么谈话的内容是衣裳、歌剧、人名——来搪塞他对她同「命运之人」最新一次会话的天生好奇。谈衣裳、歌剧、人名！他没法拿这话当真。他想，她满可以编个更圆些的说法，或者干脆说一句「这个不与你知道」。她该知道他不会见怪的。她难道还没看出来，他对她同那人关系中那一层神秘的色彩，是全盘接受、绝无二话的，仿佛那是天地开辟之初就注定了的？可他并不恼特拉弗斯太太。说到底，那话也许是真的。她爱怎么说话就怎么说话，哪怕说得离奇，只要她高兴，还能叫那个男人仰起脸听；同样，她也凭一股灵感的魔力——无论为着简单的还是叵测的缘由——有本事叫那个男人什么都谈得出来。歌剧！衣裳！是啊——还有莎士比亚和玻璃琴呢！为一时的兴致，或为最深的目的。配得上「女人」这个名分的女人就是这样的。她们奇妙得很。事情临头，她们应付裕如，有时还能凌驾其上；那些事情注定要发生，或喜或悲（看情形），而往往连无辜的旁观者也一并遭殃。达尔卡塞尔想这些时，不带苦味，连讥诮也不带。他半公开地背着一名声：在一个纯属阴谋的世界里，他是个只有一场大爱情的男人；他因此喜欢一切女人。喜欢她们的多情，也喜欢她们的硬心肠；喜欢她们那些或痴或慧的冲动里的悲喜剧性——他怀着一种近乎温柔的严肃去看待它们。

他对局面不抱乐观，可他把特拉弗斯太太那套歌剧衣裳论当作一句「免谈」的警示。因此整顿饭他一言未发。

收拾餐桌的忙乱过后，他踱到特拉弗斯太太跟前，极平静地说：

「我看，命运之人今晚躲开我们，是躲对了。我们这顿饭，吃得像一窝加尔都西会的修士。」

「你是说我们的沉默？」

「规矩得无以复加。哪怕发过终身愿，也守不得更严了。」

「你觉得烦了？」

===Original===

“*Pas du tout*,” d’Alcacer assured her with whimsical gravity. “I felt nothing. I sat in a state of blessed vacuity. I believe I was the happiest of us three. Unless you, too, Mrs. Travers.﻿ ﻿…”

“It’s absolutely no use your fishing for my thoughts, Mr. d’Alcacer. If I were to let you see them you would be appalled.”

“Thoughts really are but a shape of feelings. Let me congratulate you on the impassive mask you can put on those horrors you say you nurse in your breast. It was impossible to tell anything by your face.”

“You will always say flattering things.”

“Madame, my flatteries come from the very bottom of my heart. I have given up long ago all desire to please. And I was not trying to get at your thoughts. Whatever else you may expect from me you may count on my absolute respect for your privacy. But I suppose with a mask such as you can make for yourself you really don’t care. The Man of Fate, I noticed, is not nearly as good at it as you are.”

“What a pretentious name. Do you call him by it to his face, Mr. d’Alcacer?”

“No, I haven’t the cheek,” confessed d’Alcacer, equably. “And, besides, it’s too momentous for daily use. And he is so simple that he might mistake it for a joke and nothing could be further from my thoughts. Mrs. Travers, I will confess to you that I don’t feel jocular in the least. But what can he know about people of our sort? And when I reflect how little people of our sort can know of such a man I am quite content to address him as Captain Lingard. It’s common and soothing and most respectable and satisfactory; for Captain is the most empty of all titles. What is a Captain? Anybody can be a Captain; and for Lingard it’s a name like any other. Whereas what he deserves is something special, significant, and expressive, that would match his person, his simple and romantic person.”

He perceived that Mrs. Travers was looking at him intently. They hastened to turn their eyes away from each other.

“He would like your appreciation,” Mrs. Travers let drop negligently.

“I am afraid he would despise it.”

“Despise it! Why, that sort of thing is the very breath of his nostrils.”

===Chinese===

「*一点也不烦*。」达尔卡塞尔带着古怪的郑重向她保证，「我什么也没觉着。我坐在一阵有福的空明里。我看我是咱们三个里最快活的。除非您也——特拉弗斯太太……」

「您休想从我这里钓出我的心事，达尔卡塞尔先生。我若让您看见，您要吓坏的。」

「思想不过是情感的一个形状罢了。请容我恭维您一句：您说心头养着的那些可怕东西，您却能给它们罩上一副无动于衷的面具，从您脸上什么也看不出来。」

「您永远会说讨喜的话。」

「夫人，我的恭维出自心底最深处。讨人欢喜的念头我早就丢尽了。我也并不是想探您的念头。不论您还能从我身上指望什么，我对您私域的绝对敬重，您尽管放心。不过我想，有您那样一手自制面具的本事，您也真不在乎。那位命运之人，我注意到，就远远不及您的功力。」

「好一个冠冕堂皇的名号。您当着他的面也这么叫么，达尔卡塞尔先生？」

「不，我没这份厚脸皮。」达尔卡塞尔平和地招认，「再说这名号太郑重，不配日用。他又那么单纯，没准会当成玩笑；而再没有比玩笑离我的本意更远的了。特拉弗斯太太，我向您招认：我一点也没有说笑的心思。可他又能知道多少我们这路人呢？而每当我想到我们这路人又能知道多少他这样的人，我就心满意足地只叫他一声林加德船长。这称呼平常、妥帖、体面而令人满意；因为『船长』是一切头衔里最空的一个。什么是船长？谁都当得上船长；对林加德来说，这名字同别的名字没有两样。而他配得上的，是某个特殊的、意味深长的、有表现力的东西，要配得上他这个人，他这个单纯而浪漫的人。」

他察觉特拉弗斯太太正定定地望着他。两人赶忙各自移开目光。

「他会喜欢你的品评的。」特拉弗斯太太淡淡地丢下一句。

「只怕他要嗤之以鼻。」

「嗤之以鼻！那一类东西正是他的命根子。」

===Original===

“You seem to understand him, Mrs. Travers. Women have a singular capacity for understanding. I mean subjects that interest them; because when their imagination is stimulated they are not afraid of letting it go. A man is more mistrustful of himself, but women are born much more reckless. They push on and on under the protection of secrecy and silence, and the greater the obscurity of what they wish to explore the greater their courage.”

“Do you mean seriously to tell me that you consider me a creature of darkness?”

“I spoke in general,” remonstrated d’Alcacer. “Anything else would have been an impertinence. Yes, obscurity is women’s best friend. Their daring loves it; but a sudden flash of light disconcerts them. Generally speaking, if they don’t get exactly at the truth they always manage to come pretty near to it.”

Mrs. Travers had listened with silent attention and she allowed the silence to continue for some time after d’Alcacer had ceased. When she spoke it was to say in an unconcerned tone that as to this subject she had had special opportunities. Her self-possessed interlocutor managed to repress a movement of real curiosity under an assumption of conventional interest. “Indeed,” he exclaimed, politely. “A special opportunity. How did you manage to create it?”

This was too much for Mrs. Travers. “I! Create it!” she exclaimed, indignantly, but under her breath. “How on earth do you think I could have done it?”

Mr. d’Alcacer, as if communing with himself, was heard to murmur unrepentantly that indeed women seldom knew how they had “done it,” to which Mrs. Travers in a weary tone returned the remark that no two men were dense in the same way. To this Mr. d’Alcacer assented without difficulty. “Yes, our brand presents more varieties. This, from a certain point of view, is obviously to our advantage. We interest.﻿ ﻿… Not that I imagine myself interesting to you, Mrs. Travers. But what about the Man of Fate?”

“Oh, yes,” breathed out Mrs. Travers.

“I see! Immensely!” said d’Alcacer in a tone of mysterious understanding. “Was his stupidity so colossal?”

“It was indistinguishable from great visions that were in no sense mean and made up for him a world of his own.”

===Chinese===

「您像是懂得他，特拉弗斯太太。女人有一种非凡的领悟力。我是指她们感兴趣的对象；因为她们的想象一经点燃，就敢于放它去。男人对自己多疑虑，女人天生鲁莽得多。她们在隐秘与沉默的庇护下向前再向前；她们想探的东西越幽暗，她们的胆子越大。」

「你是当真要告诉我，你把我看成一个黑暗的生灵？」

「我说的是泛论。」达尔卡塞尔辩道，「不然就是冒犯了。是啊，幽暗是女人最好的朋友。她们的胆量爱幽暗；骤然一亮，她们就乱了方寸。一般而论，她们即便没有恰恰探到真相，也总能挨得很近。」

特拉弗斯太太一直默默听着，达尔卡塞尔住口之后，她还让寂静继续了好一阵。开口时，她用一种漫不经心的口气说，至于这个题目，她有过特殊的机会。她这位沉着的对答者用一副寻常客套的兴致，压下了一阵真实的好奇。「是么，」他客气地惊叹，「一个特殊的机会。您是怎么设法弄到手的？」

这话叫特拉弗斯太太受不了。「我！设法弄到手！」她压着嗓子愤然喊道，「你当我是怎么弄到手的？」

达尔卡塞尔仿佛自语，听得他毫无悔意地咕哝说，女人确实很少知道自己是怎么「弄到手」的；特拉弗斯太太倦倦地回敬了一句：没有两个男人用同一种方式犯迷糊。对此达尔卡塞尔毫无困难地表示同意。「是啊，我们这一号的品种更多。从某个角度看，这显然是我们的优势。我们叫人感兴趣……倒不是说我自以为叫您感兴趣，特拉弗斯太太。可那位命运之人呢？」

「哦，是。」特拉弗斯太太轻轻吐出。

「我明白了！大大的！」达尔卡塞尔带着神秘的会意说，「他那份痴愚，竟有那样浩大么？」

「那痴愚同宏大的幻象难以分辨，绝无半点卑劣，还替他营造了一个只属于他自己的世界。」

===Original===

“I guessed that much,” muttered d’Alcacer to himself. “But that, you know, Mrs. Travers, that isn’t good news at all to me. World of dreams, eh? That’s very bad, very dangerous. It’s almost fatal, Mrs. Travers.”

“Why all this dismay? Why do you object to a world of dreams?”

“Because I dislike the prospect of being made a sacrifice of by those Moors. I am not an optimist like our friend there,” he continued in a low tone nodding toward the dismal figure of Mr. Travers huddled up in the chair. “I don’t regard all this as a farce and I have discovered in myself a strong objection to having my throat cut by those gorgeous barbarians after a lot of fatuous talk. Don’t ask me why, Mrs. Travers. Put it down to an absurd weakness.”

Mrs. Travers made a slight movement in her chair, raising her hands to her head, and in the dim light of the lanterns d’Alcacer saw the mass of her clear gleaming hair fall down and spread itself over her shoulders. She seized half of it in her hands which looked very white, and with her head inclined a little on one side she began to make a plait.

“You are terrifying,” he said after watching the movement of her fingers for a while.

“Yes﻿ ﻿… ?” she accentuated interrogatively.

“You have the awfulness of the predestined. You, too, are the prey of dreams.”

“Not of the Moors, then,” she uttered, calmly, beginning the other plait. D’Alcacer followed the operation to the end. Close against her, her diaphanous shadow on the muslin reproduced her slightest movements. D’Alcacer turned his eyes away.

“No! No barbarian shall touch you. Because if it comes to that I believe *he* would be capable of killing you himself.”

A minute elapsed before he stole a glance in her direction. She was leaning back again, her hands had fallen on her lap and her head with a plait of hair on each side of her face, her head incredibly changed in character and suggesting something medieval, ascetic, drooped dreamily on her breast.

===Chinese===

「这个我猜到了几分。」达尔卡塞尔自语般咕哝，「可是您知道，特拉弗斯太太，这对我可不是什么好消息。一个梦的世界，嗯？那很糟，很危险。那几乎是致命的，特拉弗斯太太。」

「干吗这样丧气？你为什么反对一个梦的世界？」

「因为我不喜欢被那些摩尔人拿去祭旗的前景。我不像我们那位朋友那样乐观。」他压低声音朝椅子里蜷成一团的特拉弗斯先生那凄惨的身影点点头，「我不把这一切当闹剧看；而且我发现，我极其反感在一通蠢话之后，被那些华服的野蛮人割断喉咙。别问我为什么，特拉弗斯太太。就记在一种荒唐的软弱头上吧。」

特拉弗斯太太在椅子里微微一动，双手抬向头；达尔卡塞尔就着灯光昏暗处，看见她那一大蓬明亮的头发泻下来，披满两肩。她用看上去极白的手抓住半边头发，头微微侧向一边，编起一条辫子。

「你真叫人害怕。」他望着她手指的动作看了一阵，说。

「是么……？」她带着疑问拖了个尾音。

「你有一种命中注定的可怖。你也是梦的猎物。」

「至少不是那些摩尔人的。」她平静地说，动手编另一条辫子。达尔卡塞尔把这道工序看到了底。紧挨着她，她那透明的影子投在纱网上，复现着她最细微的动作。达尔卡塞尔移开了目光。

「不！没有哪个野蛮人碰得着你。因为真到那一步，我相信*他*会亲手杀了你的。」

过了一分钟他才又偷觑她一眼。她又靠了回去，双手落在膝上，脸颊两侧各垂一条辫子；那张脸的气质变得叫人认不出，透着某种中世纪的、苦修的意味，梦一般垂在胸前。

===Original===

D’Alcacer waited, holding his breath. She didn’t move. In the dim gleam of jewelled clasps, the faint sheen of gold embroideries and the shimmer of silks, she was like a figure in a faded painting. Only her neck appeared dazzlingly white in the smoky redness of the light. D’Alcacer’s wonder approached a feeling of awe. He was on the point of moving away quietly when Mrs. Travers, without stirring in the least, let him hear the words:

“I have told him that every day seemed more difficult to live. Don’t you see how impossible this is?”

D’Alcacer glanced rapidly across the Cage where Mr. Travers seemed to be asleep all in a heap and presenting a ruffled appearance like a sick bird. Nothing was distinct of him but the bald patch on the top of his head.

“Yes,” he murmured, “it is most unfortunate.﻿ ﻿… I understand your anxiety, Mrs. Travers, but﻿ ﻿…”

“I am frightened,” she said.

He reflected a moment. “What answer did you get?” he asked, softly.

“The answer was: ‘Patience.’ ”

D’Alcacer laughed a little.﻿—“You may well laugh,” murmured Mrs. Travers in a tone of anguish.﻿—“That’s why I did,” he whispered. “Patience! Didn’t he see the horror of it?”﻿—“I don’t know. He walked away,” said Mrs. Travers. She looked immovably at her hands clasped in her lap, and then with a burst of distress, “Mr. d’Alcacer, what is going to happen?”﻿—“Ah, you are asking yourself the question at last. *That* will happen which cannot be avoided; and perhaps you know best what it is.”﻿—“No. I am still asking myself what he will do.”﻿—“Ah, that is not for me to know,” declared d’Alcacer. “I can’t tell you what he will do, but I know what will happen to him.”﻿—“To him, you say! To him!” she cried.﻿—“He will break his heart,” said d’Alcacer, distinctly, bending a little over the chair with a slight gasp at his own audacity﻿—and waited.

“*Croyez-vous?*” came at last from Mrs. Travers in an accent so coldly languid that d’Alcacer felt a shudder run down his spine.

===Chinese===

达尔卡塞尔屏息等着。她不动。银扣的微芒、金绣的幽光和丝绸的流泽里，她像一幅褪色古画里的人物。只有她的颈项，在那红浊的灯光里白得耀眼。达尔卡塞尔的惊叹近乎敬畏了。他正要悄悄走开，特拉弗斯太太却纹丝未动地让他听见了这样几句：

「我告诉过他，每一天都活得比前一天更难。你看不出这有多么不可能么？」

达尔卡塞尔朝围屋那头飞快瞥了一眼；特拉弗斯先生像是蜷成一团睡着了，一副羽毛蓬乱的病鸟相。他身上清晰可辨的，只有头顶那块秃斑。

「是啊，」他喃喃道，「太不幸了……我明白您的焦灼，特拉弗斯太太，可是……」

「我怕。」她说。

他想了想。「您得到了什么回答？」他轻声问。

「回答是：『耐心。』」

达尔卡塞尔轻轻笑了。——「你笑得有理。」特拉弗斯太太带着哀痛的口气喃喃道。——「所以我才笑。」他低声说。「耐心！他难道看不出其中的可怕？」——「我不知道。他走开了。」特拉弗斯太太说。她一动不动地望着交叠在膝上的双手，随后一阵悲痛迸出来：「达尔卡塞尔先生，将要发生什么？」——「啊，您终于问出这个问题了。将要发生的，是躲不过的那件事；那是什么，也许您自己最清楚。」——「不。我还在问自己，他会做什么。」——「啊，那不是我所能知道的。」达尔卡塞尔声言，「我没法告诉您他会做什么；可我知道会发生在他身上的是什么。」——「在他身上，你说！在他身上！」她喊道。——「他会心碎。」达尔卡塞尔一字一句地说，朝椅子上微微俯身，为自己的大胆轻轻抽了口气——然后等着。

「*你真信么？*」末了，特拉弗斯太太吐出这么一句，语调冷淡倦怠之极，达尔卡塞尔觉得一阵寒战顺着脊梁淌下去。

===Original===

Was it possible that she was that kind of woman, he asked himself. Did she see nothing in the world outside herself? Was she above the commonest kind of compassion? He couldn’t suspect Mrs. Travers of stupidity; but she might have been heartless and, like some women of her class, quite unable to recognize any emotion in the world except her own. D’Alcacer was shocked and at the same time he was relieved because he confessed to himself that he had ventured very far. However, in her humanity she was not vulgar enough to be offended. She was not the slave of small meannesses. This thought pleased d’Alcacer who had schooled himself not to expect too much from people. But he didn’t know what to do next. After what he had ventured to say and after the manner in which she had met his audacity the only thing to do was to change the conversation. Mrs. Travers remained perfectly still. “I will pretend that I think she is asleep,” he thought to himself, meditating a retreat on tiptoe.

He didn’t know that Mrs. Travers was simply trying to recover the full command of her faculties. His words had given her a terrible shock. After managing to utter this defensive “*croyez-vous*” which came out of her lips cold and faint as if in a last effort of dying strength, she felt herself turn rigid and speechless. She was thinking, stiff all over with emotion: “D’Alcacer has seen it! How much more has he been able to see?” She didn’t ask herself that question in fear or shame but with a reckless resignation. Out of that shock came a sensation of peace. A glowing warmth passed through all her limbs. If d’Alcacer had peered by that smoky light into her face he might have seen on her lips a fatalistic smile come and go. But d’Alcacer would not have dreamed of doing such a thing, and, besides, his attention just then was drawn in another direction. He had heard subdued exclamations, had noticed a stir on the decks of the *Emma*, and even some sort of noise outside the ship.

“These are strange sounds,” he said.

“Yes, I hear,” Mrs. Travers murmured, uneasily.

Vague shapes glided outside the Cage, barefooted, almost noiseless, whispering Malay words secretly.

“It seems as though a boat had come alongside,” observed d’Alcacer, lending an attentive ear. “I wonder what it means. In our position.﻿ ﻿…”

“It may mean anything,” interrupted Mrs. Travers.

===Chinese===

她莫非真是那种女人么，他问自己。她眼里除了自己就看不见世界么？她竟高于最寻常的怜悯么？他不能疑心特拉弗斯太太愚蠢；可她也许寡情，同她那个阶层的某些女人一样，世上除了她自己的情感，什么情感也认不得。达尔卡塞尔震惊，同时又松了口气，因为他自认方才走得太远了。好在以她的人品，还不至于庸俗到见怪。她不受小家子气的刻薄辖制。这个念头叫达尔卡塞尔高兴，他一向训练自己不对人指望太多。可他不知道下一步怎么办。在他冒险说了那话、她又以那种方式接下他的大胆之后，唯一的办法就是换个话题。特拉弗斯太太纹丝不动。「我就装作当她睡着了。」他暗自想，盘算着踮脚撤退。

他不知道特拉弗斯太太正在竭力恢复对自己心神的全部掌控。他的话给了她可怕的震动。她勉强吐出那句自卫的「*你真信么*」之后——它出自唇间时又冷又弱，仿佛垂死力气的最后一搏——她觉得自己整个人僵住了，说不出话。她僵着，满心激荡地想：「达尔卡塞尔看出来了！他还看出了多少？」她这样自问，不带恐惧，不带羞惭，只有一种豁出去的听天由命。那阵震动过后，涌来一种安宁。一股暖意流遍四肢。达尔卡塞尔若借着那红浊的灯光细看她的脸，或许能看见她唇边掠过一个宿命论的微笑。可达尔卡塞尔做梦也不会做这种事，何况他的注意力此刻正被引向别处。他听见了压低的惊呼，察觉「埃玛号」的甲板上有一阵骚动，船外也有了些响动。

「这些声响很怪。」他说。

「是，我听见。」特拉弗斯太太不安地喃喃道。

模糊的身影在围屋外滑来滑去，赤着脚，几乎无声，交换着隐秘的马来话。

「像是有条船靠帮了。」达尔卡塞尔侧耳倾听道，「不知道这是什么意思。以咱们的处境……」

「什么意思都可能。」特拉弗斯太太打断道。

===Original===

“Jaffir is here,” said a voice in the darkness of the after end of the ship. Then there were some more words in which d’Alcacer’s attentive ear caught the word “surat.”

“A message of some sort has come,” he said. “They will be calling Captain Lingard. I wonder what thoughts or what dreams this call will interrupt.” He spoke lightly, looking now at Mrs. Travers who had altered her position in the chair; and by their tones and attitudes these two might have been on board the yacht sailing the sea in perfect safety. “You, of course, are the one who will be told. Don’t you feel a sort of excitement, Mrs. Travers?”

“I have been lately exhorted to patience,” she said in the same easy tone. “I can wait and I imagine I shall have to wait till the morning.”

“It can’t be very late yet,” he said. “Time with us has been standing still for ever so long. And yet this may be the hour of fate.”

“Is this the feeling you have at this particular moment?”

“I have had that feeling for a considerable number of moments already. At first it was exciting. Now I am only moderately anxious. I have employed my time in going over all my past life.”

“Can one really do that?”

“Yes. I can’t say I have been bored to extinction. I am still alive, as you see; but I have done with that and I feel extremely idle. There is only one thing I would like to do. I want to find a few words that could convey to you my gratitude for all your friendliness in the past, at the time when you let me see so much of you in London. I felt always that you took me on my own terms and that so kindly that often I felt inclined to think better of myself. But I am afraid I am wearying you, Mrs. Travers.”

“I assure you you have never done that﻿—in the past. And as to the present moment I beg you not to go away. Stay by me please. We are not going to pretend that we are sleepy at this early hour.”

===Chinese===

「贾菲尔来了。」船艉那头的黑暗里一个声音说。接着又是几句话，达尔卡塞尔敏锐的耳朵捕到了「苏拉特」这个词。

「有信函到了。」他说，「他们该去叫林加德船长了。不知道这一声呼唤会打断他什么样的念头、什么样的梦。」他轻轻地说着，此刻望着在椅子里换了姿势的特拉弗斯太太；凭两人的语气和姿态，简直像安安稳稳航行在海上的游艇里。「当然，被告知的只会是您。您不觉得有几分兴奋么，特拉弗斯太太？」

「我近来刚被劝过要有耐心。」她用同样轻松的口气说，「我等得，而且我想，得一直等到天亮。」

「现在还不会太晚。」他说，「对我们来说，时间早就不动了，不知动了多久。可这也许正是命运的时刻。」

「这是您此刻此分此秒的感受么？」

「这感受我已经领了不少时刻了。起初叫人兴奋。如今我只是中等程度的焦虑。这段时间我把过去的一生从头到尾过了一遍。」

「真能做到么？」

「能。不能说烦得要死。您瞧，我还活着；可那件事我做完了，眼下闲得很。只剩一件事想办。我想找几句话，把您往日的种种情谊传达给您——当年在伦敦，您让我那样多地见到您。我总觉得，您是照我本来的样子接纳我的，而且那样厚道，常叫我禁不住高看自己一眼。只怕我烦着您了，特拉弗斯太太。」

「我向您担保，从前您从来没有。——至于此刻，我求您别走。请陪着我。咱们犯不上装出这么早就有困意。」

===Original===

D’Alcacer brought a stool close to the long chair and sat down on it. “Oh, yes, the possible hour of fate,” he said. “I have a request to make, Mrs. Travers. I don’t ask you to betray anything. What would be the good? The issue when it comes will be plain enough. But I should like to get a warning, just something that would give me time to pull myself together, to compose myself as it were. I want you to promise me that if the balance tips against us you will give me a sign. You could, for instance, seize the opportunity when I am looking at you to put your left hand to your forehead like this. It is a gesture that I have never seen you make, and so.﻿ ﻿…”

“Jörgenson!” Lingard’s voice was heard forward where the light of a lantern appeared suddenly. Then, after a pause, Lingard was heard again: “Here!”

Then the silent minutes began to go by. Mrs. Travers reclining in her chair and d’Alcacer sitting on the stool waited motionless without a word. Presently through the subdued murmurs and agitation pervading the dark deck of the *Emma* Mrs. Travers heard a firm footstep, and, lantern in hand, Lingard appeared outside the muslin cage.

“Will you come out and speak to me?” he said, loudly. “Not you. The lady,” he added in an authoritative tone as d’Alcacer rose hastily from the stool. “I want Mrs. Travers.”

“Of course,” muttered d’Alcacer to himself and as he opened the door of the Cage to let Mrs. Travers slip through he whispered to her, “This *is* the hour of fate.”

She brushed past him swiftly without the slightest sign that she had heard the words. On the after deck between the Cage and the deckhouse Lingard waited, lantern in hand. Nobody else was visible about; but d’Alcacer felt in the air the presence of silent and excited beings hovering outside the circle of light. Lingard raised the lantern as Mrs. Travers approached and d’Alcacer heard him say:

“I have had news which you ought to know. Let us go into the deckhouse.”

===Chinese===

达尔卡塞尔把一只凳子挪到长椅边，坐了上去。「哦对，那可能的命运时刻。」他说，「我有个请求，特拉弗斯太太。我不求您泄露什么。那有什么用？结局到来时会明明白白。我只想得到一个预警，只要一点什么，给我工夫定定神，从容收拾一下自己。我想请您答应：倘若天平倒向不利于我们的一边，您给我个暗号。比方说，趁我望着您的时候，把左手像这样抬到额前。这手势我从没见您做过，所以……」

「约根森！」前头传来林加德的声音，一盏灯忽然亮起。停了片刻，又听得林加德喊：「来！」

随后，无声的分钟一分钟一分钟地过去。特拉弗斯太太斜倚在椅中，达尔卡塞尔坐在凳上，两人一动不动，一言不发地等着。过了一会儿，透过弥漫在「埃玛号」黑甲板上的低语与骚动，特拉弗斯太太听见一阵坚定的脚步；林加德提着灯，出现在纱网围屋外面。

「您出来同我谈谈好么？」他大声说，「不是您。是这位太太。」达尔卡塞尔慌忙从凳上起身时，他又用不容分说的口气添了一句，「我要特拉弗斯太太。」

「当然。」达尔卡塞尔自语道；他拉开围屋的门让特拉弗斯太太侧身而出，对她耳语道：「这*正是*命运的时刻。」

她飞快地擦身而过，没有一丝听见这话的表示。艉甲板上，围屋与甲板室之间，林加德提着灯等着。四下看不见别人；可达尔卡塞尔觉得空气里满是缄默而兴奋的人影，在光圈外浮动。特拉弗斯太太走近时，林加德举高了灯，达尔卡塞尔听见他说：

「有了您该知道的消息。我们进甲板室说。」

===Original===

D’Alcacer saw their heads lighted up by the raised lantern surrounded by the depths of shadow with an effect of a marvellous and symbolic vision. He heard Mrs. Travers say “I would rather not hear your news,” in a tone that made that sensitive observer purse up his lips in wonder. He thought that she was overwrought, that the situation had grown too much for her nerves. But this was not the tone of a frightened person. It flashed through his mind that she had become self-conscious, and there he stopped in his speculation. That friend of women remained discreet even in his thoughts. He stepped backward further into the Cage and without surprise saw Mrs. Travers follow Lingard into the deckhouse.

===Chinese===

达尔卡塞尔望见那盏举起的灯照亮的两颗头颅，四周是深不见底的阴影，俨然一幅神奇的、象征性的幻象。他听见特拉弗斯太太说「我宁愿不听你的消息」，那语调叫这位敏感的观察者惊疑地抿紧了嘴唇。他想，她是紧张过度了，局面压垮了她的神经。可那不是受惊的人的语调。他心头闪过一个念头：她变得有了自我意识——想到这里，他的推测便止住了。这位女人的朋友，连在心思里都保持着分寸。他向后退进围屋深处，并不惊讶地望着特拉弗斯太太随林加德进了甲板室。

### IV / 四
===Original===

Lingard stood the lantern on the table. Its light was very poor. He dropped on to the sea-chest heavily. He, too, was overwrought. His flannel shirt was open at the neck. He had a broad belt round his waist and was without his jacket. Before him, Mrs. Travers, straight and tall in the gay silks, cottons, and muslins of her outlandish dress, with the ends of the scarf thrown over her head, hanging down in front of her, looked dimly splendid and with a black glance out of her white face. He said:

“Do you, too, want to throw me over? I tell you you can’t do that now.”

“I wasn’t thinking of throwing you over, but I don’t even know what you mean. There seem to be no end of things I can’t do. Hadn’t you better tell me of something that I could do? Have you any idea yourself what you want from me?”

“You can let me look at you. You can listen to me. You can speak to me.”

“Frankly, I have never shirked doing all those things, whenever you wanted me to. You have led me﻿ ﻿…”

“I led you!” cried Lingard.

“Oh! It was my fault,” she said, without anger. “I must have dreamed then that it was you who came to me in the dark with the tale of your impossible life. Could I have sent you away?”

“I wish you had. Why didn’t you?”

“Do you want me to tell you that you were irresistible? How could I have sent you away? But you! What made you come back to me with your very heart on your lips?”

When Lingard spoke after a time it was in jerky sentences.

“I didn’t stop to think. I had been hurt. I didn’t think of you people as ladies and gentlemen. I thought of you as people whose lives I held in my hand. How was it possible to forget you in my trouble? It is your face that I brought back with me on board my brig. I don’t know why. I didn’t look at you more than at anybody else. It took me all my time to keep my temper down lest it should burn you all up. I didn’t want to be rude to you people, but I found it wasn’t very easy because threats were the only argument I had. Was I very offensive, Mrs. Travers?”

She had listened tense and very attentive, almost stern. And it was without the slightest change of expression that she said:

“I think that you bore yourself appropriately to the state of life to which it has pleased God to call you.”

===Chinese===

林加德把灯放在桌上。灯光很弱。他沉重地一屁股坐到那口水手箱上。他也被熬得过了劲。法兰线衬衫领口敞着。腰间扎一条宽皮带，外衣不在身上。在他面前，特拉弗斯太太穿着那身异域装束——绫罗、棉布、薄纱，色彩明艳——笔挺高挑，披巾的两端罩在头上、垂在胸前，看上去华彩朦胧，白皙的脸上一道黑色的目光。他说：

“你也想把我甩了吗？我告诉你，现在可由不得你了。”

“我没想着甩你，我连你什么意思都不知道。好像我没法做的事没完没了。你不如说说有什么是我能做的？你自己想清楚了吗，你要我做什么？”

“你可以让我看着你。你可以听我说话。你可以对我说话。”

“坦白说，这三样，只要你开口，我从来没有推辞过。是你引着我……”

“我引着你！”林加德喊。

“哦！那是我错了。”她不带怒气地说。“那我一定是做梦了，梦见那天在黑暗里带着你那不可能的人生来找我的是你。我能撵你走吗？”

“我倒希望你撵了。你为什么不？”

“你要我说你让人无法抗拒吗？我怎么撵得走你？可你呢！是什么让你把心捧在唇边，一回又一回地回到我这里来？”

过了一会儿，林加德才开口，一句一頓。

“我没停下来想。我受了伤。我没把你们当作什么太太绅士。我把你们看作性命攥在我手里的人。我遭了难，怎么可能忘掉你？我带回我船上的，就是你的脸。我不知道为什么。我并不比别人多看你几眼。我光是按捺我的火气，就占去了全部的工夫，生怕它把你们全烧了。我不想对你们无礼，可我发现那不容易，因为我手里唯一的道理就是桐吓。我是不是很难看，特拉弗斯太太？”

她一直紧张而专注地听着，近乎严峻。她不带一丝表情变化地说：

“我认为，您的举止，与蒙上帝思典唤您所处的身分，正相称。”

===Original===

“What state?” muttered Lingard to himself. “I am what I am. They call me Rajah Laut, King Tom, and suchlike. I think it amused you to hear it, but I can tell you it is no joke to have such names fastened on one, even in fun. And those very names have in them something which makes all this affair here no small matter to anybody.”

She stood before him with a set, severe face.﻿—“Did you call me out in this alarming manner only to quarrel with me?”﻿—“No, but why do you choose this time to tell me that my coming for help to you was nothing but impudence in your sight? Well, I beg your pardon for intruding on your dignity.”﻿—“You misunderstood me,” said Mrs. Travers, without relaxing for a moment her contemplative severity. “Such a flattering thing had never happened to me before and it will never happen to me again. But believe me, King Tom, you did me too much honour. Jörgenson is perfectly right in being angry with you for having taken a woman in tow.”﻿—“He didn’t mean to be rude,” protested Lingard, earnestly. Mrs. Travers didn’t even smile at this intrusion of a point of manners into the atmosphere of anguish and suspense that seemed always to arise between her and this man who, sitting on the sea-chest, had raised his eyes to her with an air of extreme candour and seemed unable to take them off again. She continued to look at him sternly by a tremendous effort of will.

“How changed you are,” he murmured.

He was lost in the depths of the simplest wonder. She appeared to him vengeful and as if turned forever into stone before his bewildered remorse. Forever. Suddenly Mrs. Travers looked round and sat down in the chair. Her strength failed her but she remained austere with her hands resting on the arms of her seat. Lingard sighed deeply and dropped his eyes. She did not dare relax her muscles for fear of breaking down altogether and betraying a reckless impulse which lurked at the bottom of her dismay, to seize the head of d’Alcacer’s Man of Fate, press it to her breast once, fling it far away, and vanish herself, vanish out of life like a wraith. The Man of Fate sat silent and bowed, yet with a suggestion of strength in his dejection. “If I don’t speak,” Mrs. Travers said to herself, with great inward calmness, “I shall burst into tears.” She said aloud, “What could have happened? What have you dragged me in here for? Why don’t you tell me your news?”

===Chinese===

“什么身分？”林加德喃喃自语。“我就是我。人家叫我海拉者，汤姆王，还有诸如此类。我想你听了只觉得有趣，可我告诉你，这样的名字落到人头上，哪怕只是玩笑，也不是闹着玩的。而这些名字本身，就带着一种东西，让这儿这桩事对任何人都不是小事。”

她站在他面前，绷着一张严厉的脸。——“你这样吓人地把我叫出来，就为了跟我吵架？”——“不，可你为什么偏偏拣这个时候告诉我，我来向你求援在你眼里不过是冒犯？好吧，为冒犯了您的尊严，我向您道歉。”——“你误会了。”特拉弗斯太太说，沉思的严厉一刻也不放松。“这样受宠若惊的事，我此生没有遇到过，也不会再遇到了。可是相信我，汤姆王，你太抬举我了。约根森为你拖带上一个女人而生你的气，气得完全在理。”——“他不是存心无礼。”林加德诚恳地辩道。在这样一个礼节的枝节问题闯进她和这个男人之间似乎总要升起的焦灼与悬虑的空气里时，特拉弗斯太太连笑都没笑一下；那人坐在水手箱上，已把眼睛抬到她脸上，一副极其坦白的神气，仿佛再也放不下来。她凭着一股巨大的意志力，继续严厉地看他。

“你变了好多。”他喃喃道。

他迷失在最简单的惊叹的深处。在他惶乱的悔恨面前，她显得像一个复仇者，像永远化成了石头。永远。忽然，特拉弗斯太太环顾了一下，在椅子里坐下。她的力气不济了，可她仍旧凛然，两手搭在座椅的扶手上。林加德深深叹了口气，垂下眼睛。她不敢松开肌肉，生怕整个人垮掉，泄露那潜伏在她惶乱底下的、不顾一切的冲动：想抓住达尔卡塞尔那位“司命之人”的头，把它按在胸前贴一贴，再把它远远抛开，自己也随之消散，像幽魂一样从生命里消散。司命之人沉默地坐着，垂着头，颓唐中却透着一股劲道。“我要是不开口，”特拉弗斯太太心平气和地对自己说，“我就要哭出来了。”她出声说：“到底出了什么事？你把我拽到这儿来做什么？你怎么不把你的消息告诉我？”

===Original===

“I thought you didn’t want to hear. I believe you really don’t want to. What is all this to you? I believe that you don’t care anything about what I feel, about what I do and how I end. I verily believe that you don’t care how you end yourself. I believe you never cared for your own or anybody’s feelings. I don’t think it is because you are hard, I think it is because you don’t know, and don’t want to know, and are angry with life.”

He flourished an arm recklessly, and Mrs. Travers noticed for the first time that he held a sheet of paper in his hand.

“Is that your news there?” she asked, significantly. “It’s difficult to imagine that in this wilderness writing can have any significance. And who on earth here could send you news on paper? Will you let me see it? Could I understand it? Is it in English? Come, King Tom, don’t look at me in this awful way.”

She got up suddenly, not in indignation, but as if at the end of her endurance. The jewelled clasps, the gold embroideries, gleamed elusively amongst the folds of her draperies which emitted a mysterious rustle.

“I can’t stand this,” she cried. “I can’t stand being looked at like this. No woman could stand it. No woman has ever been looked at like this. What can you see? Hatred I could understand. What is it you think me capable of?”

“You are very extraordinary,” murmured Lingard, who had regained his self-possession before that outburst.

“Very well, and you are extraordinary, too. That’s understood﻿—here we are both under that curse and having to face together whatever may turn up. But who on earth could have sent you this writing?”

“Who?” repeated Lingard. “Why, that young fellow that blundered on my brig in the dark, bringing a boatload of trouble alongside on that quiet night in Carimata Straits. The darkest night I have ever known. An accursed night.”

Mrs. Travers bit her lip, waited a little, then asked quietly:

“What difficulty has he got into now?”

“Difficulty!” cried Lingard. “He is immensely pleased with himself, the young fool. You know, when you sent him to talk to me that evening you left the yacht, he came with a loaded pistol in his pocket. And now he has gone and done it.”

“Done it?” repeated Mrs. Travers blankly. “Done what?”

===Chinese===

“我以为你不想听。我看你是真的不想听。这一切跟你有什么相干？我相信，我的感受、我的作为、我的结局，你一概不在乎。我千真万确地相信，你连你自己的结局都不在乎。我相信你从不曾在乎过自己的或任何人的感情。我想这不是因为你狠心，是因为你不知道，也不想知道，而且对生活怀着怒气。”

他不管不顾地挥了一下手臂，特拉弗斯太太这才第一次看见他手里拿着一张纸。

“那就是你的消息？”她意味深长地问。“很难想象，在这片荒野里，文字能有什么分量。再说，这里究竟能有谁给你递字面上的消息？能给我看看吗？我能看懂吗？是英文吗？行了，汤姆王，别这样吓人地瞪着我。”

她猛地站起来，不是出于义愤，而是像忍到了头。宝石的扣子、金线的绣纹在她衣裙的皱褶间明灭，衣料发出一种神秘的窸窣。

“我受不了了。”她喊。“我受不了被人这样看着。没有女人受得了。从来没有女人被这样看过。你能看见什么？仇恨我懂。你以为我干得出来什么？”

“你真不寻常。”林加德喃喃道，那阵爆发之前他已恢复了自持。

“很好，你也不寻常。这就算说定了——咱们俩都在这道诅咒之下，得一起面对冒出来的一切。可到底是谁给你送来了这封信？”

“谁？”林加德重复道。“就是那个黑夜里误撞上我船的毛头小子，在那个安静的卡里马塔海峡的夜里，拖着一艇的麻烦靠上我的船。那是我经手过的最黑的一夜。倒霉的一夜。”

特拉弗斯太太咬住嘴唇，等了一会儿，才平静地问：

“他这回又惹了什么麻烦？”

“麻烦！”林加德喊。“他正得意得不行呢，这小傻子。您知道吗，您离开游艇那天晚上派他来跟我谈话，他口袋里揣着上膛的手枪来的。现在他真的干了。”

“干了？”特拉弗斯太太发懵地重复。“干了什么？”

===Original===

She snatched from Lingard’s unresisting palm the sheet of paper. While she was smoothing it Lingard moved round and stood close at her elbow. She ran quickly over the first lines, then her eyes steadied. At the end she drew a quick breath and looked up at Lingard. Their faces had never been so close together before and Mrs. Travers had a surprising second of a perfectly new sensation. She looked away.﻿—“Do you understand what this news means?” he murmured. Mrs. Travers let her hand fall by her side. “Yes,” she said in a low tone. “The compact is broken.”

Carter had begun his letter without any preliminaries:

You cleared out in the middle of the night and took the lady away with you. You left me no proper orders. But as a sailorman I looked upon myself as left in charge of two ships while within half a mile on that sandbank there were more than a hundred piratical cutthroats watching me as closely as so many tigers about to leap. Days went by without a word of you or the lady. To leave the ships outside and go inland to look for you was not to be thought of with all those pirates within springing distance. Put yourself in my place. Can’t you imagine my anxiety, my sleepless nights? Each night worse than the night before. And still no word from you. I couldn’t sit still and worry my head off about things I couldn’t understand. I am a sailorman. My first duty was to the ships. I had to put an end to this impossible situation and I hope you will agree that I have done it in a seamanlike way. One misty morning I moved the brig nearer the sandbank and directly the mist cleared I opened fire on the praus of those savages which were anchored in the channel. We aimed wide at first to give those vagabonds that were on board a chance to clear out and join their friends camped on the sands. I didn’t want to kill people. Then we got the long gun to bear and in about an hour we had the bottom knocked out of the two praus. The savages on the bank howled and screamed at every shot. They are mighty angry but I don’t care for their anger now, for by sinking their praus I have made them as harmless as a flock of lambs. They needn’t starve on their sandbank because they have two or three dugouts hauled up on the sand and they may ferry themselves and their women to the mainland whenever they like.

===Chinese===

她从林加德毫不抗拒的掌心里抽走那张纸。她抚平纸的时候，林加德挪过来，紧挨着她肘边站着。她飞快扫过头几行，眼睛随即定住了。读完，她急吸一口气，抬眼望向林加德。两人的脸从没离得这样近过，特拉弗斯太太惊觉一秒钟全然新鲜的感觉。她把眼移开。——“你明白这消息意味着什么吗？”他低声问。特拉弗斯太太让手垂到身侧。“是。”她低声说。“盟约破了。”

卡特的开场白毫无客套：

您半夜里一走了之，还把太太带走了。您没给我留下一句像样的命令。可我是个跑船的，我把自己看作奉命照看两条船的人，而半英里外的沙洲上，一百多个海盗杀手盯着我，像一群就要扑上来的老虎。一天天过去，您和太太音讯全无。把船撂在外头、自己进内陆找您，想都别想——那些海盗就在一扑之距内。您设身处地想想。您想象得到我的焦灼、我一夜一夜睡不着吗？一夜比一夜糟。还是没有您的信儿。我没法干坐着，为我看不懂的事把脑袋愁掉。我是跑船的。我的头一份责任在船上。这局面不能再这样下去了，而我希望您承认，我的了结方式够个海员的派头。一个雾天的早晨，我把双桅船移近沙洲，雾一散，我就朝那些野人泊在水道里的普拉帆船开了火。我们先故意打宽，给船上那些流氓留出跳船逃命、投奔沙滩上他们同伙的工夫。我不想杀人。然后我们把长炮调转了方向，约莫一个钟头，就把两条普拉船的船底敲了个稀烂。沙洲上的野人每一炮都嚎叫。他们气疯了，可如今我才不在乎他们气不气，因为沉了他们的船，我已把他们治得像一群羊羔。他们饿不死，沙洲上还拖有两三条独木舟，他们随时可以把人和娘们儿摆渡到大陆上去。

===Original===

I fancy I have acted as a seaman and as a seaman I intend to go on acting. Now I have made the ships safe I shall set about without loss of time trying to get the yacht off the mud. When that’s done I shall arm the boats and proceed inshore to look for you and the yacht’s gentry, and shan’t rest till I know whether any or all of you are above the earth yet.

I hope these words will reach you. Just as we had done the business of those praus the man you sent off that night in Carimata to stop our chief officer came sailing in from the west with our first gig in tow and the boat’s crew all well. Your serang tells me he is a most trustworthy messenger and that his name is Jaffir. He seems only too anxious to try to get to you as soon as possible. I repeat, ships and men have been made safe and I don’t mean to give you up dead or alive.

“You are quick in taking the point,” said Lingard in a dull voice, while Mrs. Travers, with the sheet of paper gripped in her hand, looked into his face with anxious eyes. “He has been smart and no mistake.”

“He didn’t know,” murmured Mrs. Travers.

“No, he didn’t know. But could I take everybody into my confidence?” protested Lingard in the same low tone. “And yet who else could I trust? It seemed to me that he must have understood without being told. But he is too young. He may well be proud according to his lights. He has done that job outside very smartly﻿—damn his smartness! And here we are with all our lives depending on my word﻿—which is broken now, Mrs. Travers. It is broken.”

Mrs. Travers nodded at him slightly.

“They would sooner have expected to see the sun and the moon fall out of the sky,” Lingard continued with repressed fire. Next moment it seemed to have gone out of him and Mrs. Travers heard him mutter a disconnected phrase.﻿ ﻿… “The world down about my ears.”

“What will you do?” she whispered.

“What will I do?” repeated Lingard, gently. “Oh, yes﻿—do. Mrs. Travers, do you see that I am nothing now? Just nothing.”

===Chinese===

我想我行事像个海员，往后我还要照海员的规矩行事。如今两条船都安全了，我马上动手，不耽误一分钟，设法把游艇从泥里弄出来。办完这个，我就武装起小艇，往岸边开进来找您和游艇的那几位老爷，不弄清你们是死是活、一个不落全须全尾，我决不罢休。

但愿这些话能到您手上。普拉船的事刚办完，您那晚在卡里马塔派去拦我们大副的人，就从西边开回来了，拖着我们的头号艇，艇员个个安好。您那位水手长告诉我，他是个顶可靠的信使，名字叫贾菲尔。他一副急不可耐的样子，只想尽早到您那儿去。我再重复一遍：船和人都保住了，我不打算把您当死人放弃——活的死的都不放。

“你抓要害真快。”林加德用沉闷的嗓音说。特拉弗斯太太手里攥着那张纸，焦灼地端详他的脸。“他干得利落，没说的。”

“他不知道。”特拉弗斯太太喃喃道。

“是，他不知道。可我能把人人都拉进我的信托里吗？”林加德同样低声地抗辩。“再说，我还能信谁？我原以为他不用人说也该明白。可他太年轻。照他那套见识，他尽可以自鸣得意。他把外头那桩活干得真利落——利落得见鬼去！而咱们大伙儿的命如今全悬在我的一句话上——这句话现在断了，特拉弗斯太太。断了。”

特拉弗斯太太朝他微微点头。

“他们宁可亲眼看日月从天上掉下来。”林加德压抑着火气继续说。下一刻，那火气仿佛又熄了，特拉弗斯太太听见他咕哝出一句没头没尾的话。……“天塌在我头上了。”

“您打算怎么办？”她低声问。

“我打算怎么办？”林加德轻声重复。“哦，是——办。特拉弗斯太太，您看不出来吗，我现在什么也不是了？什么也不是。”

===Original===

He had lost himself in the contemplation of her face turned to him with an expression of awed curiosity. The shock of the world coming down about his ears in consequence of Carter’s smartness was so terrific that it had dulled his sensibilities in the manner of a great pain or of a great catastrophe. What was there to look at but that woman’s face, in a world which had lost its consistency, its shape, and its promises in a moment?

Mrs. Travers looked away. She understood that she had put to Lingard an impossible question. What was presenting itself to her as a problem was to that man a crisis of feeling. Obviously Carter’s action had broken the compact entered into with Daman, and she was intelligent enough to understand that it was the sort of thing that could not be explained away. It wasn’t horror that she felt, but a sort of consternation, something like the discomfiture of people who have just missed their train. It was only more intense. The real dismay had yet to make its way into her comprehension. To Lingard it was a blow struck straight at his heart.

He was not angry with Carter. The fellow had acted like a seaman. Carter’s concern was for the ships. In this fatality Carter was a mere incident. The real cause of the disaster was somewhere else, was other, and more remote. And at the same time Lingard could not defend himself from a feeling that it was in himself, too, somewhere in the unexplored depths of his nature, something fatal and unavoidable. He muttered to himself:

“No. I am not a lucky man.”

===Chinese===

他迷失在对她那张脸的凝视里，那张脸转向他，带着一种敬畏的好奇。天塌下来的这一击——卡特利落的手笔招来的——太可怕了，把他的感官震木了，像一场剧痛或一场大灾那样。在一个忽然失去了坚实、失去了形状、失去了诺言的世界上，除了那张女人的脸，还有什么可看的呢？

特拉弗斯太太把目光移开。她明白，自己向林加德提出了一个不可能的问题。在她面前呈现为一道难题的东西，对那个男人是一场感情的危机。显然，卡特的行动撕毁了同达曼订下的盟约，而她的智力足以明白，这种事是无法解释开脱的。她感到的不是恐怖，而是一种惊愕，有点像人们刚误了火车的那种懊恼，只是更强烈。真正的惶恐还要过一阵才会渗进她的理解。对林加德，这是一记直捣心口的打击。

他没有生卡特的气。那家伙行事像个海员。卡特挂念的是船。在这场命数里，卡特不过是一个偶然。灾难的真因在别处，是别的，更遥远的。而与此同时，林加德又无法不感觉到，那原因也在他自己身上，在他天性里未曾探明的深处，某种致命而无可回避的东西。他喃喃自语：

“不。我不是个有运气的人。”

===Original===

This was but a feeble expression of the discovery of the truth that suddenly had come home to him as if driven into his breast by a revealing power which had decided that this was to be the end of his fling. But he was not the man to give himself up to the examination of his own sensations. His natural impulse was to grapple with the circumstances and that was what he was trying to do; but he missed now that sense of mastery which is half the battle. Conflict of some sort was the very essence of his life. But this was something he had never known before. This was a conflict within himself. He had to face unsuspected powers, foes that he could not go out to meet at the gate. They were within, as though he had been betrayed by somebody, by some secret enemy. He was ready to look round for that subtle traitor. A sort of blankness fell on his mind and he suddenly thought: “Why! It’s myself.”

Immediately afterward he had a clear, merciless recollection of Hassim and Immada. He saw them far off beyond the forests. Oh, yes, they existed﻿—within his breast!

“That was a night!” he muttered, looking straight at Mrs. Travers. He had been looking at her all the time. His glance had held her under a spell, but for a whole interminable minute he had not been aware of her at all. At the murmur of his words she made a slight movement and he saw her again.﻿—“What night?” she whispered, timidly, like an intruder. She was astonished to see him smile.﻿—“Not like this one,” he said. “You made me notice how quiet and still it was. Yes. Listen how still it is.”

Both moved their heads slightly and seemed to lend an ear. There was not a murmur, sigh, rustle, splash, or footfall. No whispers, no tremors, not a sound of any kind. They might have been alone on board the *Emma*, abandoned even by the ghost of Captain Jörgenson departed to rejoin the Barque *Wild Rose* on the shore of the Cimmerian sea.﻿—“It’s like the stillness of the end,” said Mrs. Travers in a low, equable voice.﻿—“Yes, but that, too, is false,” said Lingard in the same tone.﻿—“I don’t understand,” Mrs. Travers began, hurriedly, after a short silence. “But don’t use that word. Don’t use it, King Tom! It frightens me by its mere sound.”

===Chinese===

这句话，远不足以道出那个忽然彻悟的真相——仿佛有一种揭示的力量，决意要以此了结他的这场豪赌，把真相径直钉进他的胸膛。可他不是那种肯细细检视自己感受的人。他的天性是去扭住环境不放，此刻他正是这么做的；只是他失去了那种掌控之感，而掌控乃胜券的一半。某种形式的搏斗，本是他生命的本质。可这是他从未经历过的。这是一场他内心里的搏斗。他必须面对不曾料到的力量，面对不能出门到关前迎击的敌人。它们在体内，仿佛有人出卖了他，某个隐秘的仇敌。他简直想四下张望找出那个阴刁的内奸。一阵茫然落在他心上，他忽然想：“咦！是我自己。”

紧接着，哈西姆和伊玛达清清楚楚、毫不留情地回到他记忆里。他看见他们在森林之外的远处。哦，是的，他们存在——在他的胸腔里！

“那才叫一个夜晚！”他直直望着特拉弗斯太太喃喃道。他一直望着她。他的目光把她镇在符咒里，可是整整难熬的一分钟里，他根本没有意识到她。“什么夜晚？”她怯怯地低声问，像个闯入者。她惊讶地看见他笑了。“跟这一夜不一样。”他说。“是你让我注意到它多静。是。你听，多静。”

两人都微微侧了侧头，仿佛侧耳去听。没有一丝低语、叹息、窸窣、泼溅或脚步。没有私语，没有震颤，没有任何声音。他们简直像独自待在“艾玛号”上，连约根森船长的幽魂都弃船而去，回到冥海边上“野玫瑰号”的身边了。——“像终局的寂静。”特拉弗斯太太用低而平稳的声音说。——“是，可那也是假的。”林加德用同样的声调说。——“我不明白。”特拉弗斯太太在一阵短短的沉默后急忙说。“可你别用那个字。别用它，汤姆王！那声音本身就叫我害怕。”

===Original===

Lingard made no sign. His thoughts were back with Hassim and Immada. The young chief and his sister had gone up country on a voluntary mission to persuade Belarab to return to his stockade and to take up again the direction of affairs. They carried urgent messages from Lingard, who for Belarab was the very embodiment of truth and force, that unquestioned force which had permitted Belarab to indulge in all his melancholy hesitations. But those two young people had also some personal prestige. They were Lingard’s heart’s friends. They were like his children. But beside that, their high birth, their warlike story, their wanderings, adventures, and prospects had given them a glamour of their own.

===Chinese===

林加德没有表示。他的心思回到了哈西姆和伊玛达身上。年轻的头领和他的妹妹已深入内地，自愿去劝说贝拉拉布回栅寨来，重新执掌大局。他们带着林加德的急信——在贝拉拉布眼里，林加德是真理与力量的化身，正是那种不容置疑的力量，才容得贝拉拉布沉溺于他种种忧郁的迟疑。可这两个年轻人也有自己的威望。他们是林加德心头的朋友。他们就像他的孩子。不止如此，他们高贵的出身、他们的战史、他们的流亡、历险与前程，也给他们自己镀上了一圈光晕。

### V / 五
===Original===

The very day that Travers and d’Alcacer had come on board the *Emma* Hassim and Immada had departed on their mission; for Lingard, of course, could not think of leaving the white people alone with Jörgenson. Jörgenson was all right, but his ineradicable habit of muttering in his moustache about “throwing a lighted match amongst the powder barrels” had inspired Lingard with a certain amount of mistrust. And, moreover, he did not want to go away from Mrs. Travers.

It was the only correct inspiration on Carter’s part to send Jaffir with his report to Lingard. That stouthearted fighter, swimmer, and devoted follower of the princely misfortunes of Hassim and Immada, had looked upon his mission to catch the chief officer of the yacht (which he had received from Lingard in Carimata) as a trifling job. It took him a little longer than he expected but he had got back to the brig just in time to be sent on to Lingard with Carter’s letter after a couple of hours’ rest. He had the story of all the happenings from Wasub before he left and though his face preserved its grave impassivity, in his heart he did not like it at all.

Fearless and wily, Jaffir was the man for difficult missions and a born messenger﻿—as he expressed it himself﻿—“to bear weighty words between great men.” With his unfailing memory he was able to reproduce them exactly, whether soft or hard, in council or in private; for he knew no fear. With him there was no need for writing which might fall into the hands of the enemy. If he died on the way the message would die with him. He had also the gift of getting at the sense of any situation and an observant eye. He was distinctly one of those men from whom trustworthy information can be obtained by the leaders of great enterprises. Lingard did put several questions to him, but in this instance, of course, Jaffir could have only very little to say. Of Carter, whom he called the “young one,” he said that he looked as white men look when they are pleased with themselves; then added without waiting for a definite question﻿—“The ships out there are now safe enough, O, Rajah Laut!” There was no elation in his tone.

===Chinese===

就在特拉弗斯和达尔卡塞尔被送上“艾玛号”的那一天，哈西姆和伊玛达动身去执行他们的使命；因为林加德当然不能把白人们单独留给约根森。约根森人是靠得住的，可他那改不掉的、对着小胡子咕哝“往火药桶里扔火柴”的老毛病，让林加德对他存了几分不放心。再说，他也不想离开特拉弗斯太太。

卡特唯一的正确直觉，就是派贾菲尔把他的报告送给林加德。这个胆壮心细的战士、泅者、哈西姆与伊玛达王子厄运的忠仆，当初把林加德在卡里马塔交给他的差事——去截游艇的大副——看作一桩小事。这桩事比他预想的多费了些时日，可他回到双桅船上，歇了两个钟头，正赶上被派来给林加德送卡特的信。动身前他从瓦苏布口中听得了全部经过，他脸上虽保持着庄重的木然，心里却一百个不以为然。

贾菲尔无畏而多智，是办难差的人，天生的信使——用他自己的话说——“在伟人之间传递分量沉重的话语”。凭着他那从不出错的记性，他能够一字不差地复述它们，软话硬话、堂上私底，皆无差池；因为他不知恐惧为何物。有他在，就用不着可能落入敌手的书信。他若死在半路，那话便随他一同死去。他还有一眼看透局面的本事和一双善观的眼睛。他分明是那种大事业的首领们可以从他口中获得可靠情报的人。林加德确也问了他几个问题，只是这一回，贾菲尔自然说不上什么。说到卡特——他叫他“小的那个”——他说，那家伙一副白人自鸣得意的模样；随后不等明确的提问便补上一句——“外头那两条船，这会儿够安全了，啊，海拉者！”他的语调里没有半点得意。

===Original===

Lingard looked at him blankly. When the Greatest of White Men remarked that there was yet a price to be paid for that safety, Jaffir assented by a “Yes, by Allah!” without losing for a moment his grim composure. When told that he would be required to go and find his master and the lady Immada who were somewhere in the back country, in Belarab’s travelling camp, he declared himself ready to proceed at once. He had eaten his fill and had slept three hours on board the brig and he was not tired. When he was young he used to get tired sometimes; but for many years now he had known no such weakness. He did not require the boat with paddlers in which he had come up into the lagoon. He would go alone in a small canoe. This was no time, he remarked, for publicity and ostentation. His pent-up anxiety burst through his lips. “It is in my mind, Tuan, that death has not been so near them since that night when you came sailing in a black cloud and took us all out of the stockade.”

Lingard said nothing but there was in Jaffir a faith in that white man which was not easily shaken.

“How are you going to save them this time, O Rajah Laut?” he asked, simply.

“Belarab is my friend,” murmured Lingard.

In his anxiety Jaffir was very outspoken. “A man of peace!” he exclaimed in a low tone. “Who could be safe with a man like that?” he asked, contemptuously.

“There is no war,” said Lingard.

“There is suspicion, dread, and revenge, and the anger of armed men,” retorted Jaffir. “You have taken the white prisoners out of their hands by the force of your words alone. Is that so, Tuan?”

“Yes,” said Lingard.

“And you have them on board here?” asked Jaffir, with a glance over his shoulder at the white and misty structure within which by the light of a small oil flame d’Alcacer and Mrs. Travers were just then conversing.

“Yes, I have them here.”

“Then, Rajah Laut,” whispered Jaffir, “you can make all safe by giving them back.”

“Can I do that?” were the words breathed out through Lingard’s lips to the faithful follower of Hassim and Immada.

“Can you do anything else?” was the whispered retort of Jaffir the messenger accustomed to speak frankly to the great of the earth. “You are a white man and you can have only one word. And now I go.”

===Chinese===

林加德茫然地望着他。当“白人中最伟大者”指出这份安全还要付出代价时，贾菲尔应了一声“是，真主作证”，狰狰的镇定一刻不失。听说要他去寻他的主子和伊玛达小姐——他们在内地深处贝拉拉布的行营里——他声称即刻可以动身。他在双桅船上吃饱了、睡了三个钟头，不累。他年轻时偶尔也会累；可多年来他已不识这种软弱。他不需求那艘把他送进潟湖来的带桨手的艇。他愿独自乘一条小独木舟去。他说，眼下不是招摇过市的时候。他那按捺不住的焦虑从唇边迸了出来。“我心里想，老爷，死亡从没离他们这么近——自那一夜之后从来没有。那晚您驾着乌云般的夜色开来，把我们所有人救出栅寨。”

林加德没有说话，可贾菲尔心里对这白人的信，不是轻易摇得动的。

“这回您打算怎么救他们，啊，海拉者？”他直截了当地问。

“贝拉拉布是我的朋友。”林加德喃喃道。

焦虑之下，贾菲尔把话说得很直。“一个太平人！”他低声喊。“跟那样的人在一起，谁能安全？”他轻蔑地问。

“这又不是打仗。”林加德说。

“有的是猜疑、恐惧和报复，还有带枪的人的怒火。”贾菲尔回敬。“您只凭口舌之力，就把白人俘虏从他们手里拿走了。是不是这样，老爷？”

“是。”林加德说。

“您把他们都关在这条船上？”贾菲尔问，眼睛扫过那座白纱般的建筑——一盏小小的油灯火光里，达尔卡塞尔和特拉弗斯太太正在里面交谈。

“是，都在我这儿。”

“那么，海拉者，”贾菲尔低声道，“您把人交还回去，就一切安稳了。”

“我能那么做吗？”林加德对着哈西姆与伊玛达的这位忠仆，从唇间送出这句话。

“您还能做别的吗？”惯于对世上大人物直言的贾菲尔信使低声反问。“您是白人，您只能有一个字。我这就去了。”

===Original===

A small, rough dugout belonging to the *Emma* had been brought round to the ladder. A shadowy calash hovering respectfully in the darkness of the deck had already cleared his throat twice in a warning manner.

“Yes, Jaffir, go,” said Lingard, “and be my friend.”

“I am the friend of a great prince,” said the other, sturdily. “But you, Rajah Laut, were even greater. And great you will remain while you are with us, people of this sea and of this land. But what becomes of the strength of your arms before your own white people? Where does it go to, I say? Well, then, we must trust in the strength of your heart.”

“I hope that will never fail,” said Lingard, and Jaffir emitted a grunt of satisfaction. “But God alone sees into men’s hearts.”

“Yes. Our refuge is with Allah,” assented Jaffir, who had acquired the habit of pious turns of speech in the frequentation of professedly religious men, of whom there were many in Belarab’s stockade. As a matter of fact, he reposed all his trust in Lingard who had with him the prestige of a providential man sent at the hour of need by heaven itself. He waited a while, then: “What is the message I am to take?” he asked.

“Tell the whole tale to the Rajah Hassim,” said Lingard. “And tell him to make his way here with the lady his sister secretly and with speed. The time of great trouble has come. Let us, at least, be together.”

“Right! Right!” Jaffir approved, heartily. “To die alone under the weight of one’s enemies is a dreadful fate.”

He stepped back out of the sheen of the lamp by which they had been talking and making his way down into the small canoe he took up a paddle and without a splash vanished on the dark lagoon.

===Chinese===

“艾玛号”的一条粗陋小独木舟已转到绳梯边。甲板的黑暗里，一个影影绰绰的本地船夫恭候着，已经清了两次嗓子示意。

“去吧，贾菲尔。”林加德说，“做我的朋友。”

“我是一位伟大王子的朋友。”对方硬朗地说。“可您，海拉者，从前更伟大。只要您和我们同在——和我们这些海与陆地的人同在——您就永远伟大。可您的膂力到了您自己的白人面前，又算什么？我问您，它到哪儿去了？那么，我们只好信托您心里的力量了。”

“但愿它永不衰竭。”林加德说。贾菲尔满意地哼了一声。“不过，唯有真主能看透人心。”

“是。我们的庇护在真主。”贾菲尔应道——他常与那些公然的敬神之人往来（贝拉拉布栅寨里这样的人很多），学得了一口敬度的口头禅。其实，他的全部信托都押在林加德身上；在他眼里，林加德带着一种应劫而生的天降之人的威光。他等了一会儿，问：“我带什么话去？”

“把全部经过告诉哈西姆拉者。”林加德说。“叫他带着他妹妹小姐，秘密地、尽快地到这儿来。大难的时辰到了。咱们至少要在一起。”

“好！好！”贾菲尔衷心赞许。“孤身死在仇敌的重压之下，是可怕的命。”

他退出了两人说话的那盏灯光，摸索着下到小独木舟里，操起一支桨，无波无声地消失在漆黑的潟湖上。

===Original===

It was then that Mrs. Travers and d’Alcacer heard Lingard call aloud for Jörgenson. Instantly the familiar shadow stood at Lingard’s elbow and listened in detached silence. Only at the end of the tale it marvelled audibly: “Here’s a mess for you if you like.” But really nothing in the world could astonish or startle old Jörgenson. He turned away muttering in his moustache. Lingard remained with his chin in his hand and Jaffir’s last words took gradual possession of his mind. Then brusquely he picked up the lamp and went to seek Mrs. Travers. He went to seek her because he actually needed her bodily presence, the sound of her voice, the dark, clear glance of her eyes. She could do nothing for him. On his way he became aware that Jörgenson had turned out the few Malays on board the *Emma* and was disposing them about the decks to watch the lagoon in all directions. On calling Mrs. Travers out of the Cage Lingard was, in the midst of his mental struggle, conscious of a certain satisfaction in taking her away from d’Alcacer. He couldn’t spare any of her attention to any other man, not the least crumb of her time, not the least particle of her thought! He needed it all. To see it withdrawn from him for the merest instant was irritating﻿—seemed a disaster.

D’Alcacer, left alone, wondered at the imperious tone of Lingard’s call. To this observer of shades the fact seemed considerable. “Sheer nerves,” he concluded, to himself. “The man is overstrung. He must have had some sort of shock.” But what could it be﻿—he wondered to himself. In the tense stagnation of those days of waiting the slightest tremor had an enormous importance. D’Alcacer did not seek his camp bedstead. He didn’t even sit down. With the palms of his hands against the edge of the table he leaned back against it. In that negligent attitude he preserved an alert mind which for a moment wondered whether Mrs. Travers had not spoiled Lingard a little. Yet in the suddenness of the forced association, where, too, d’Alcacer was sure there was some moral problem in the background, he recognized the extreme difficulty of weighing accurately the imperious demands against the necessary reservations, the exact proportions of boldness and caution. And d’Alcacer admired upon the whole Mrs. Travers’ cleverness.

===Chinese===

正是在那时，特拉弗斯太太和达尔卡塞尔听见林加德高声唤约根森。那道熟悉的影子立刻立在林加德肘边，以超然的沉默听着。只在听完之后出声惊叹了一句：“瞧瞧这摊子。”其实世上没有什么能让老约根森吃惊了。他转过身去，对着小胡子咕哝。林加德依旧手支着下巴，贾菲尔最后那句话渐渐占满了他的脑子。随后他猛地拎起灯，去找特拉弗斯太太。他去找她，是因为他实实在在需要她肉身的在场，需要她的嗓音，需要她眼睛那黑色而清亮的凝视。她帮不上他什么。去的路上，他察觉约根森已把“艾玛号”上仅有的几个马来人叫起，分布在甲板各处，监视四面八方的潟湖。把特拉弗斯太太从“笼子”里唤出来时，林加德在内心的激战之中，竟还觉出一份快意——把她从达尔卡塞尔身边带走。他不能让她的一丝注意分给任何别的男人，她的一分一秒、她的一念一想，一星半点也不行！他全都要。哪怕一瞬看见它们离开自己，都叫他恼火——像一场灾祸。

被单独留下的达尔卡塞尔，对林加德那声呼唤的专断语调暗暗称奇。在这位察言观色的行家看来，这事实非同小可。“纯粹是神经。”他心里下结论。“这人绷得太紧了。准是受了什么打击。”可那打击能是什么呢——他自问。在那些等待的日子里紧张的凝滞中，最轻微的震颤也有天大的分量。达尔卡塞尔没有去睡行军床。他连坐都没坐。他两手撑着桌沿，向后倚着桌子。在这副闲散的姿势里，他保持着一份警醒的头脑，一时甚至怀疑，特拉弗斯太太是不是把林加德宠坏了一点。可在这种被迫的、突如其来的相处里——何况达尔卡塞尔确信背后还藏着某个道义难题——他明白，要精确掂量那些专断的索求与必要的保留、胆气与谨慎的恰当成数，何其之难。而总体上，达尔卡塞尔佩服特拉弗斯太太的聪明。

===Original===

There could be no doubt that she had the situation in her hands. That, of course, did not mean safety. She had it in her hands as one may hold some highly explosive and uncertain compound. D’Alcacer thought of her with profound sympathy and with a quite unselfish interest. Sometimes in a street we cross the path of personalities compelling sympathy and wonder but for all that we don’t follow them home. D’Alcacer refrained from following Mrs. Travers any further. He had become suddenly aware that Mr. Travers was sitting up on his camp bedstead. He must have done it very suddenly. Only a moment before he had appeared plunged in the deepest slumber, and the stillness for a long time now had been perfectly unbroken. D’Alcacer was startled enough for an exclamation and Mr. Travers turned his head slowly in his direction. D’Alcacer approached the bedstead with a certain reluctance.

“Awake?” he said.

“A sudden chill,” said Mr. Travers. “But I don’t feel cold now. Strange! I had the impression of an icy blast.”

“Ah!” said d’Alcacer.

“Impossible, of course!” went on Mr. Travers. “This stagnating air never moves. It clings odiously to one. What time is it?”

“Really, I don’t know.”

“The glass of my watch was smashed on that night when we were so treacherously assailed by the savages on the sandbank,” grumbled Mr. Travers.

“I must say I was never so surprised in my life,” confessed d’Alcacer. “We had stopped and I was lighting a cigar, you may remember.”

“No,” said Mr. Travers. “I had just then pulled out my watch. Of course it flew out of my hand but it hung by the chain. Somebody trampled on it. The hands are broken off short. It keeps on ticking but I can’t tell the time. It’s absurd. Most provoking.”

“Do you mean to say,” asked d’Alcacer, “that you have been winding it up every evening?”

===Chinese===

毫无疑问，局面在她手里。当然，这不等于安全。她把局面握在手里，像人捧着一种极易爆炸、变幻莫测的化合物。达尔卡塞尔怀着深切的同情和全然无私的关切想着她。有时在街上，我们与某种令人既同情又惊叹的人格擦肩而过，但我们并不会跟着他们回家。达尔卡塞尔克制着自己，不再追随特拉弗斯太太更深一步。他忽然察觉特拉弗斯先生在行军床上坐了起来。他一定是猛然坐起来的。就在一刻之前，他还像沉在最深的酣睡里，而四周的寂静久久未曾有半点破缺。达尔卡塞尔惊得险些叫出声，特拉弗斯先生缓缓朝他这边转过头来。达尔卡塞尔带着几分不情愿走近床前。

“醒了？”他说。

“忽然一阵寒。”特拉弗斯先生说。“可现在不冷了。奇怪！我觉出一股冰冷的风。”

“啊！”达尔卡塞尔说。

“当然不可能！”特拉弗斯先生继续说。“这凝滞的空气从不动弹。它黏在人身上，讨厌。几点了？”

“说实在的，我不知道。”

“我的表蒙，就是那晚在沙洲上被野人那么背信弃义地袭击时磕碎的。”特拉弗斯先生嘟囔道。

“我得说，我这辈子从没那样吃惊过。”达尔卡塞尔承认。“咱们停了下来，我正点雪茄，您记得吧。”

“不。”特拉弗斯先生说。“我当时刚掏出表。它当然从手里飞了出去，可还挂在链子上。叫人踩了一脚。针都折断了。它照旧滴嗒走，可我看不出时间。荒唐。气人。”

“您是说，”达尔卡塞尔问，“您每天晚上还照给它上弦？”

===Original===

Mr. Travers looked up from his bedstead and he also seemed surprised. “Why! I suppose I have.” He kept silent for a while. “It isn’t so much blind habit as you may think. My habits are the outcome of strict method. I had to order my life methodically. You know very well, my dear d’Alcacer, that without strict method I would not have been able to get through my work and would have had no time at all for social duties, which, of course, are of very great importance. I may say that, materially, method has been the foundation of my success in public life. There were never any empty moments in my day. And now this!﻿ ﻿…” He looked all round the Cage.﻿ ﻿… “Where’s my wife?” he asked.

“I was talking to her only a moment ago,” answered d’Alcacer. “I don’t know the time. My watch is on board the yacht; but it isn’t late, you know.”

Mr. Travers flung off with unwonted briskness the light cotton sheet which covered him. He buttoned hastily the tunic which he had unfastened before lying down, and just as d’Alcacer was expecting him to swing his feet to the deck impetuously, he lay down again on the pillow and remained perfectly still.

D’Alcacer waited awhile and then began to pace the Cage. After a couple of turns he stopped and said, gently:

“I am afraid, Travers, you are not very well.”

“I don’t know what illness is,” answered the voice from the pillow to the great relief of d’Alcacer who really had not expected an answer. “Good health is a great asset in public life. Illness may make you miss a unique opportunity. I was never ill.”

All this came out deadened in tone, as if the speaker’s face had been buried in the pillow. D’Alcacer resumed his pacing.

“I think I asked you where my wife was,” said the muffled voice.

With great presence of mind d’Alcacer kept on pacing the Cage as if he had not heard.﻿—“You know, I think she is mad,” went on the muffled voice. “Unless I am.”

Again d’Alcacer managed not to interrupt his regular pacing. “Do you know what I think?” he said, abruptly. “I think, Travers, that you don’t want to talk about her. I think that you don’t want to talk about anything. And to tell you the truth I don’t want to, either.”

===Chinese===

特拉弗斯先生从床上抬起眼，似乎也吃了一惊。“咦！大概是的。”他沉默了一会儿。“这不像您想的那样，光是盲目的习惯。我的习惯是严格条理的产物。我必须有条不紊地安排生活。您很清楚，亲爱的达尔卡塞尔，没有严格的条理，我别想做完我的工作，也根本腾不出时间尽社交义务——那自然是要紧得很的。我可以说，在物质层面上，条理是我公众生涯成功的根基。我的一天里从来没有空着的时刻。可如今这！……”他朝“笼子”四下看了看。……“我妻子呢？”他问。

“刚才我还同她说话来着。”达尔卡塞尔答。“我不知道时间。我的表在游艇上；不过还早，您知道。”

特拉弗斯先生以少见的利落掀开盖在身上的薄棉布单。他匆匆扣上躺下前解开的外衣，而正当达尔卡塞尔等着他一甩脚跳下地来，他却又躺回枕上，纹丝不动了。

达尔卡塞尔等了一会儿，开始在“笼子”里踱步。踱了两圈，他停下，温和地说：

“恐怕，特拉弗斯，您身体不大好。”

“我不知道生病是什么滋味。”枕上发出的声音答道，这让达尔卡塞尔大大松了口气——他实在没料到有回音。“好身体是公众生涯的一大本钱。一场病可能让您错过一个千载难逢的机会。我从来没病过。”

这些话发闷地传出来，仿佛说话人的脸埋在枕头里。达尔卡塞尔继续踱他的步。

“我想我问过您，我妻子在哪儿。”那闷闷的声音说。

达尔卡塞尔沉得住气，照旧踱步，仿佛没听见。——“您知道吗，我看她是疯了。”闷声继续说。“要不然就是我疯了。”

达尔卡塞尔又一次做到不中断他规律的踱步。“您知道我怎么想吗？”他唐突地说。“我想，特拉弗斯，您不想谈论她。我想您什么都不想谈。而且说老实话，我也不想。”

===Original===

D’Alcacer caught a faint sigh from the pillow and at the same time saw a small, dim flame appear outside the Cage. And still he kept on his pacing. Mrs. Travers and Lingard coming out of the deckhouse stopped just outside the door and Lingard stood the deck-lamp on its roof. They were too far from d’Alcacer to be heard, but he could make them out: Mrs. Travers, as straight as an arrow, and the heavy bulk of the man who faced her with a lowered head. He saw it in profile against the light and as if deferential in its slight droop. They were looking straight at each other. Neither of them made the slightest gesture.

“There is that in me,” Lingard murmured, deeply, “which would set my heart harder than a stone. I am King Tom, Rajah Laut, and fit to look any man hereabouts in the face. I have my name to take care of. Everything rests on that.”

“Mr. d’Alcacer would express this by saying that everything rested on honour,” commented Mrs. Travers with lips that did not tremble, though from time to time she could feel the accelerated beating of her heart.

“Call it what you like. It’s something that a man needs to draw a free breath. And look!﻿—as you see me standing before you here I care for it no longer.”

“But I do care for it,” retorted Mrs. Travers. “As you see me standing here﻿—I do care. This is something that is your very own. You have a right to it. And I repeat I do care for it.”

“Care for something of my own,” murmured Lingard, very close to her face. “Why should you care for my rights?”

“Because,” she said, holding her ground though their foreheads were nearly touching, “because if I ever get back to my life I don’t want to make it more absurd by real remorse.”

Her tone was soft and Lingard received the breath of those words like a caress on his face. D’Alcacer, in the Cage, made still another effort to keep up his pacing. He didn’t want to give Mr. Travers the slightest excuse for sitting up again and looking round.

“That I should live to hear anybody say they cared anything for what was mine!” whispered Lingard. “And that it should be you﻿—you, who have taken all hardness out of me.”

“I don’t want your heart to be made hard. I want it to be made firm.”

===Chinese===

达尔卡塞尔听得枕上一声轻叹，同时看见“笼子”外亮起一小团朦胧的火。他仍旧踱着步。特拉弗斯太太和林加德走出舱棚，就在门外停住，林加德把甲板灯放上棚顶。他们离达尔卡塞尔太远，听不见说话，可他辨得出他们的身影：特拉弗斯太太笔直如箭，对面那个沉重的身影低着头。他看见它的侧影衬着灯光，微俯着，仿佛恭谨。他们直直望着彼此。谁也没有一丝手势。

“我心里有一样东西，”林加德深沉地低语道，“本可以叫我的心比石头还硬。我是汤姆王，海拉者，这一带随便哪个人我都敢对视。我有我的名声要看护。一切都悬在它上面。”

“达尔卡塞尔先生会把这概括为：一切都悬在荣誉上。”特拉弗斯太太评论道，嘴唇并不颤，虽然她时时感觉得到心跳的加速。

“随你怎么叫它。那是一个人自由喘气所需要的东西。可是你瞧！——你眼见我站在你面前，我已不在乎它了。”

“可我在乎。”特拉弗斯太太回敬。“你眼见我站在这儿——我在乎。这是专属于你的东西。你有权持有它。我再说一遍，我在乎它。”

“在乎属于我的东西。”林加德贴近她的脸喃喃道。“你为什么要在乎我的权利？”

“因为，”她说，尽管两人的额头几乎相触，仍寸步不让，“因为倘若我还能回到我的生活里，我不想让真正的悔恨把它变得更加荒唐。”

她的声音温柔，那几个字的气息拂在林加德脸上，像一次爱抚。“笼子”里的达尔卡塞尔又作了一次努力，坚持踱步。他不想给特拉弗斯先生丝毫坐起来四下张望的借口。

“我竟然能活着听见有人说，在乎属于我的东西！”林加德低语。“而这个人竟是你——你，把我身上所有的硬都拿走了的人。”

“我不要你的心变硬。我要它变得坚定。”

===Original===

“You couldn’t have said anything better than what you have said just now to make it steady,” flowed the murmur of Lingard’s voice with something tender in its depth. “Has anybody ever had a friend like this?” he exclaimed, raising his head as if taking the starry night to witness.

“And I ask myself is it possible that there should be another man on earth that I could trust as I trust you. I say to you: Yes! Go and save what you have a right to and don’t forget to be merciful. I will not remind you of our perfect innocence. The earth must be small indeed that we should have blundered like this into your life. It’s enough to make one believe in fatality. But I can’t find it in me to behave like a fatalist, to sit down with folded hands. Had you been another kind of man I might have been too hopeless or too disdainful. Do you know what Mr. d’Alcacer calls you?”

Inside the Cage d’Alcacer, casting curious glances in their direction, saw Lingard shake his head and thought with slight uneasiness: “He is refusing her something.”

“Mr. d’Alcacer’s name for you is the ‘Man of Fate,’ ” said Mrs. Travers, a little breathlessly.

“A mouthful. Never mind, he is a gentleman. It’s what you.﻿ ﻿…”

“I call you all but by your Christian name,” said Mrs. Travers, hastily. “Believe me, Mr. d’Alcacer understands you.”

“He is all right,” interjected Lingard.

“And he is innocent. I remember what you have said﻿—that the innocent must take their chance. Well, then, do what is right.”

“You think it would be right? You believe it? You feel it?”

“At this time, in this place, from a man like you﻿—Yes, it is right.”

Lingard thought that woman wonderfully true to him and wonderfully fearless with herself. The necessity to take back the two captives to the stockade was so clear and unavoidable now, that he believed nothing on earth could have stopped him from doing so, but where was there another woman in the world who would have taken it like this? And he reflected that in truth and courage there is found wisdom. It seemed to him that till Mrs. Travers came to stand by his side he had never known what truth and courage and wisdom were. With his eyes on her face and having been told that in her eyes he appeared worthy of being both commanded and entreated, he felt an instant of complete content, a moment of, as it were, perfect emotional repose.

===Chinese===

“要让它坚定，你刚才那句话再好不过了。”林加德的低语流出来，深处带着某种温柔。“谁曾有过这样一个朋友？”他喊道，抬起头，仿佛请繁星的夜空作证。

“我也问自己：世上可能还有第二个男人，能让我像信你这样地信吗？我对你说：可能有！去吧，去救回属于你的权利，别忘了仁慈。我不必再提醒你我们是何等清白。地球一定小得很，才会让我们这样撞进你的生活。这足以叫人相信命数了。可我没法子像宿命论者那样行事，抱着手干坐。你若换了另一种人，我也许早就绝望了，或者不屑一顾了。你知道达尔卡塞尔先生管你叫什么吗？”

“笼子”里，达尔卡塞尔朝他们那边投去好奇的目光，看见林加德摇头，便略感不安地想：“他在拒绝她什么。”

“达尔卡塞尔先生给你起的名字，是‘司命之人’。”特拉弗斯太太说，微微有些气促。

“拗口。不要紧，他是绅士。也就是你……”

“我除了你的教名什么都叫过了。”特拉弗斯太太急急地说。“相信我，达尔卡塞尔先生了解你。”

“他是好人。”林加德插了一句。

“而且他是无辜的。我记得你说过的话——无辜的人也得各安天命。那么，做对的事吧。”

“你觉得那是对的？你相信？你感到？”

“在这个时辰，在这个地方，从你这样的人口里——是，那是对的。”

林加德觉得那个女人对他真得出奇，对自己也无所畏惧得出奇。把两个俘虏送回栅寨的必要，此刻已如此明白、如此无可回避，他相信世上再没有什么能拦住他这么做；可世上还有第二个女人，能把这件事承受成这样吗？他悟到：真实与勇气之中，自生智慧。在他看来，直到特拉弗斯太太站到他身边，他才算知道了什么是真实、勇气与智慧。他望着她的脸，得知在她眼里他既配受命令也配受恳求，一时感到一种完全的满足，一刻近乎完美的情感安歇。

===Original===

During the silence Mrs. Travers with a quick side-glance noticed d’Alcacer as one sees a man in a mist, his mere dark shape arrested close to the muslin screen. She had no doubt that he was looking in their direction and that he could see them much more plainly than she could see him. Mrs. Travers thought suddenly how anxious he must be; and she remembered that he had begged her for some sign, for some warning, beforehand, at the moment of crisis. She had understood very well his hinted request for time to get prepared. If he was to get more than a few minutes, *this* was the moment to make him a sign﻿—the sign he had suggested himself. Mrs. Travers moved back the least bit so as to let the light fall in front of her and with a slow, distinct movement she put her left hand to her forehead.

“Well, then,” she heard Lingard’s forcible murmur, “well, then, Mrs. Travers, it must be done tonight.”

One may be true, fearless, and wise, and yet catch one’s breath before the simple finality of action. Mrs. Travers caught her breath: “Tonight! Tonight!” she whispered. D’Alcacer’s dark and misty silhouette became more blurred. He had seen her sign and had retreated deeper within the Cage.

“Yes, tonight,” affirmed Lingard. “Now, at once, within the hour, this moment,” he murmured, fiercely, following Mrs. Travers in her recoiling movement. She felt her arm being seized swiftly. “Don’t you see that if it is to do any good, that if they are not to be delivered to mere slaughter, it must be done while all is dark ashore, before an armed mob in boats comes clamouring alongside? Yes. Before the night is an hour older, so that I may be hammering at Belarab’s gate while all the Settlement is still asleep.”

Mrs. Travers didn’t dream of protesting. For the moment she was unable to speak. This man was very fierce and just as suddenly as it had been gripped (making her think incongruously in the midst of her agitation that there would be certainly a bruise there in the morning) she felt her arm released and a penitential tone come into Lingard’s murmuring voice.

===Chinese===

沉默中，特拉弗斯太太眼角一瞥，注意到了达尔卡塞尔，像人在雾里看见一个人，一个黑影停在纱幕近旁。她毫不怀疑他正朝这边望，而且他看她，比她看他清楚得多。特拉弗斯太太忽然想到，他一定焦急极了；她记起他求过她，在危机来临的时刻，事先给他一个信号、一声警告。她非常明白他那暗示的请求——要一点准备的时间。若想多给他几分钟，此刻正是给他信号的时候——用他自己建议的那个信号。特拉弗斯太太朝后退了极小的一步，好让灯光落在自己身前，然后以一个缓慢而清楚的动作，把左手举到额前。

“那么，”她听见林加德那有力的低语，“那么，特拉弗斯太太，必须今夜办。”

人可以真实、无畏而智慧，可面对行动那简单的终极性，仍不免屏住呼吸。特拉弗斯太太屏住了：“今夜！今夜！”她低呼。达尔卡塞尔那黑雾般的剪影更模糊了。他看见了她的信号，退进“笼子”更深处去了。

“是，今夜。”林加德断言。“现在，立刻，一个钟头之内，此刻。”他狠狠地低声说着，随着她后退的步子跟上来。她觉得手臂被迅速攥住。“你还不明白吗，要这事有任何用处，要他们不至于被白白送去屠刀底下，就得趁岸上还一片漆黑时办，赶在一船船武装的暴徒吵嚷着靠上来之前。是。趁夜还不老一个钟头，好让我在全寨还睡着的时候，就去捶贝拉拉布的大门。”

特拉弗斯太太连想都没想抗议。这一刻她说不出话。这人凶得很，而她的手臂被攥住又忽然松开（慌乱之中她不合时宜地想到，明早那里准有一块青痕），随后她听见林加德低语的嗓音里透出了悔罪。

===Original===

“And even now it’s nearly too late! The road was plain, but I saw you on it and my heart failed me. I was there like an empty man and I dared not face you. You must forgive me. No, I had no right to doubt you for a moment. I feel as if I ought to go on my knees and beg your pardon for forgetting what you are, for daring to forget.”

“Why, King Tom, what is it?”

“It seems as if I had sinned,” she heard him say. He seized her by the shoulders, turned her about, moved her forward a step or two. His hands were heavy, his force irresistible, though he himself imagined he was handling her gently. “Look straight before you,” he growled into her ear. “Do you see anything?” Mrs. Travers, passive between the rigid arms, could see nothing but, far off, the massed, featureless shadows of the shore.

“No, I see nothing,” she said.

“You can’t be looking the right way,” she heard him behind her. And now she felt her head between Lingard’s hands. He moved it the least bit to the right. “There! See it?”

“No. What am I to look for?”

“A gleam of light,” said Lingard, taking away his hands suddenly. “A gleam that will grow into a blaze before our boat can get halfway across the lagoon.”

Even as Lingard spoke Mrs. Travers caught sight of a red spark far away. She had looked often enough at the Settlement, as on the face of a painting on a curtain, to have its configuration fixed in her mind, to know that it was on the beach at its end furthest from Belarab’s stockade.

“The brushwood is catching,” murmured Lingard in her ear. “If they had some dry grass the whole pile would be blazing by now.”

“And this means.﻿ ﻿…”

“It means that the news has spread. And it is before Tengga’s enclosure on his end of the beach. That’s where all the brains of the Settlement are. It means talk and excitement and plenty of crafty words. Tengga’s fire! I tell you, Mrs. Travers, that before half an hour has passed Daman will be there to make friends with the fat Tengga, who is ready to say to him, ‘I told you so.’ ”

“I see,” murmured Mrs. Travers. Lingard drew her gently to the rail.

===Chinese===

“即便现在也几乎太迟了！路是明明白白的，可我在路上看见了你，我的心就不行了。我那时像一副空壳，不敢面对你。你必须原谅我。不，我本没有权利怀疑你，一秒都不该。我觉得自己该跪下来，求你原谅我忘了你是谁，竟敢忘记。”

“怎么了，汤姆王，这是怎么说？”

“我像是犯了罪。”她听见他说。他抓住她的双肩，把她转过去，推着她往前走了一两步。他的手沉重，他的力道不可抗拒，虽然他自以为待她轻柔。“朝正前方看。”他俯在她耳边低吼。“你看见什么了吗？”特拉弗斯太太夹在那两条铁硬的手臂之间，被动地什么也看不见，只有远处岸上那一片堆积的、无形状的阴影。

“不，我什么也看不见。”她说。

“你准没看对方向。”她听见他在身后说。随即她觉得自己的头被夹在林加德的两手之间。他把她的头微微向右扳了扳。“那儿！看见了吗？”

“没有。我该看什么？”

“一点微光。”林加德说着忽然把手拿开。“一点微光，等我们的艇划到潟湖一半，它就会烧成一片大火。”

林加德话音未落，特拉弗斯太太就望见了远处一粒红色的火星。她曾多次凝望那处营寨，像凝望帘上一幅画，它的格局已牢牢记在她心里：那火星在海滩上，离贝拉拉布栅寨最远的一头。

“柴堆引着了。”林加德在她耳边喃喃道。“他们要有干草，这会儿整堆都烧旺了。”

“这说明……”

“说明消息传开了。那火在滕加的地界前，在他那头的滩上。全寨的脑筋都聚在那儿。这说明议论、激动，还有一大堆狡猾话。滕加的火！我告诉你，特拉弗斯太太，不出半个钟头，达曼就会到那儿去，跟胖滕加修好，滕加正等着对他说‘我早说过’呢。”

“我明白了。”特拉弗斯太太喃喃道。林加德轻轻把她引到栏杆边。

===Original===

“And now look over there at the other end of the beach where the shadows are heaviest. That is Belarab’s fort, his houses, his treasure, his dependents. That’s where the strength of the Settlement is. I kept it up. I made it last. But what is it now? It’s like a weapon in the hand of a dead man. And yet it’s all we have to look to, if indeed there is still time. I swear to you I wouldn’t dare land them in daylight for fear they should be slaughtered on the beach.”

“There is no time to lose,” whispered Mrs. Travers, and Lingard, too, spoke very low.

“No, not if I, too, am to keep what is my right. It’s you who have said it.”

“Yes, I have said it,” she whispered, without lifting her head. Lingard made a brusque movement at her elbow and bent his head close to her shoulder.

“And I who mistrusted you! Like Arabs do to their great men, I ought to kiss the hem of your robe in repentance for having doubted the greatness of your heart.”

“Oh! my heart!” said Mrs. Travers, lightly, still gazing at the fire, which had suddenly shot up to a tall blaze. “I can assure you it has been of very little account in the world.” She paused for a moment to steady her voice, then said, firmly, “Let’s get this over.”

“To tell you the truth the boat has been ready for some time.”

“Well, then.﻿ ﻿…”

“Mrs. Travers,” said Lingard with an effort, “they are people of your own kind.” And suddenly he burst out: “I cannot take them ashore bound hand and foot.”

“Mr. d’Alcacer knows. You will find him ready. Ever since the beginning he has been prepared for whatever might happen.”

“He is a man,” said Lingard with conviction. “But it’s of the other that I am thinking.”

“Ah, the other,” she repeated. “Then, what about my thoughts? Luckily we have Mr. d’Alcacer. I shall speak to him first.”

She turned away from the rail and moved toward the Cage.
